application said so. A photograph of correct behaviour that reads
exactly like a defect. The sets are now named to sort first, which is
deterministic rather than lucky. **What made this cheap to find was
putting the engine's return code in the alert**: `ret=-1 fam=0` said
in one frame that the application had never seen a family at all, where
the screenshot alone had already sent me to re-read the matcher twice.

## 2026-08-14 — the Macintosh application takes ALL the envelopes off

Asked for `Archive.sit.1.hqx` on the Mac, and found something else
first: the application had no envelope unwrap at all. Opened on
`boogers-152.hqx` it wrote `boogers152.sit` and stopped — measured, not
inferred — where the Linux CLI has always gone on to the contents.
README.md has promised the second behaviour since it was written, which
makes it the first user-facing claim in this repository that the
software did not honour (BLOCKERS.md, *Withdrawn claims*).

It also blocked what was asked for. A wrapped set is
`Archive.sit.1.hqx`: the envelope has to come off part 1 before anything
can notice that what is inside is a segment. No unwrap, no wrapped
parts.

**The loop is in the interactive path only.** The autotest keeps
single-layer semantics because every manifest it is graded against
describes one layer (`tools/vmac_check.py`), and 55/55 depends on that.
So `RunExtract` reports the SHAPE of what it wrote — one file and no
folders is what an envelope yields — and `ExtractOne` sniffs that file
rather than trusting its name, goes round, and deletes the layer it came
from once the next one has succeeded.

**And the bug that cost a run: a delete after the last flush is not a
delete.** `RunExtract` calls `FlushVol` before returning, so removing
the intermediate afterwards left the change in the volume's cache — the
harness kills the emulator where a person would quit, and the file was
still there on the image. The extraction was right and the tidying up
was invisible. Anything this application does to a volume AFTER an
extraction has to flush for itself.

**Wrapped parts then followed the CLI's shape**, with one difference
that is the whole reason it was ranked last: a decoded part needs
somewhere to live. On Linux that is a temp file beside the output; here
it is `MacExtract part N` on the OUTPUT volume, deleted when the join is
over — the one place this application knows it may write. The search is
rationed the same way, by `SegHaveAll`: envelopes are opened only while
the set is short of a part, so a complete set opens nothing. StuffIt
only, for the reason the CLI gives.

Verified through the real UI at 4 MB and at the 1 MB floor target: part
1 as `Apart.sit.1.hqx`, part 2 as `zzz-download.bin` (a different
envelope, a name that says nothing), part 3 as `Other.9.hqx` — joined,
extracted, and nothing of ours left on the volume, against the Linux
CLI on the same three files.

## 2026-08-14 — the detect-only claim had no test, and LZH stays out of scope

Asked why LZH is out of scope, and the answer in the documents was thin —
"the optional half of M8" — while the evidence for it is strong and was
never written down: **the corpus contains no `.lzh` at all**, measured
over every fixture. Macintosh software was essentially not distributed
that way; LHA belonged to the Japanese PC world and the Amiga. Compact
Pro's LZH is a different algorithm from LHA's, so nothing here is a head
start either: macutils keeps them in separate files (`cpt.c` +
`huffman.h` against `de_lzh.c`), and so does XADMaster. Extraction would
be ~550 new lines for a format the target platform did not use.

**What the question did turn up is that the detect-only claim was
untested.** Five formats are named but not extracted, and the promise —
`unc_sniff` names them so a UI can say what the file is — had nothing
behind it for LZH or StuffIt X. Worse, `run_diff.py` SKIPPED any fixture
whose format it does not extract, counting it as a "format pending", so
the 6 DiskDoubler and 1 Now Compress fixtures in the corpus were
asserted on in no way at all.

They are graded now, on exactly what the claim promises: the name agrees
with the oracle's, and extraction refuses without writing anything.
8 fixtures, and "formats pending" is 0.

**One real LZH file was added to the corpus on purpose**, and it is the
only fixture here that is not Macintosh software: an Amiga `-lh5-`
archive from Aminet. That is the point — the corpus has none because the
platform had none, so testing the probe needs one from outside, and
`fixtures/SOURCES.md` says so where somebody will find it.

**And a limit worth stating rather than discovering later.** The refusal
is guarded twice — `unc_format_extractable` in the CLI and the `default:`
arm of the engine's dispatch — so mutating either alone changes nothing
the test can see. It catches "this format silently became extractable and
succeeded", not "one of the two gates broke". Both mutations together do
fail it, which is how that was established rather than assumed.

## 2026-08-14 — one command to replay the segment work, and what it caught

Every segmented-archive scenario had been verified on the Mac by a
command typed once. `tools/vmac/segcheck.py` is those commands in one
place — seven scenarios across both harnesses, each saying what it would
mean if it failed. `sample.txt` cannot carry them: it is a flat list of
fixture names and the autotest opens a file called `archive.sit`, while
a set needs extra files beside it or its own real filenames.

**It failed on its first full run, twice, and both were right to fail.**

*The StuffIt case was checking the wrong thing.* That scheme yields the
JOINED ARCHIVE — one file — where the fixture's manifest describes what
is inside that archive, so the on-screen grid can never match it. The
disk is the only honest check there, and the scenario now reads the
joined file back off the Out volume and compares it to the archive it
was cut from. Compact Pro is the opposite: its reader emits the
archive's entries directly, which is exactly what the manifest lists, so
the grid is right for it. **Two schemes that look alike from the outside
want opposite oracles.**

*The zip and split cases had been overtaken by that morning's own work.*
Their payloads are archives, and the envelope unwrap added hours earlier
now extracts what the join produces and deletes it — so a phase looking
for the joined file found nothing. The application was right and the
expectation was a day old.

**And fixing that turned up a genuine defect: the two hosts disagreed.**
The Linux CLI unwraps only ENVELOPE formats — MacBinary, BinHex,
AppleSingle/Double, gzip, `.Z` — plus the segment schemes that yield the
file they were cut from. My Mac loop unwrapped anything that produced
exactly one file and no folders, whatever produced it. So a zip holding
a single `.sit` was extracted twice on the Mac and once on Linux:

    volume:  Demo Manual V1.6, EB Sounds, Empire Builder V1.6.1 Demo
    CLI:     payload.bin

The CLI's rule is the considered one — an archive that happens to hold
one archive is still an archive, and its contents are the answer. The
shell applies the same predicate now, and learns the format for free:
`unc_extract` sniffs internally, so sniffing explicitly and dispatching
with `unc_extract_as` costs nothing and tells the caller what the layer
was.

**Nothing would have found this except running both hosts against each
other.** The Mac phases compared against the Linux CLI, which is why a
divergence introduced on one side showed up at all — and it showed up
the same day rather than in a user's hands, because the scenarios were
written down as a script instead of left in a terminal.

## 2026-08-15 — measuring this project against StuffIt Expander 4.0.2

Everything this repository knows about "enough" has been self-referential:
the corpus, the oracle and the milestones are all ours. The one external
yardstick available is what a user of a real Mac Plus would otherwise
have run, and it had never been applied.

**A competitor's documentation is a primary source, and it ships inside a
file we can already read.** Expander 4.0.2's distribution is MacBinary
around a StuffIt self-extracting archive around a 400K HFS image; our own
CLI walked the chain and produced an image byte-identical to `unar`'s, and
its Read Me, its "What's new" file and its alert strings answered every
question the comparison needed. No web page was needed for a single claim
about 4.0.2's behaviour — the web was used only for dates. The tables and
their sourcing are in FORMATS.md, "Compared with StuffIt Expander 4.0.2".

Three things came out of it that were not visible from inside:

- **A rival's stated limitation is a claim about the problem, not only
  about the rival.** *"StuffIt Expander cannot expand encrypted or
  segmented Zip or Compact Pro files"* is its Read Me admitting the two
  hardest segment schemes — and those are exactly the two M11 built after
  ranking them as the ones a part cannot prove itself in. The ranking rule
  and the era's own tooling agree about which end of the problem is hard,
  which is the first outside confirmation the M11 ordering ever had.
- **The free tool's floor is a system version, ours is a machine.** Expander
  runs from System 6.0.5, but everything beyond `.sit`/`.cpt`/`.hqx`/`.bin`
  lives in an add-on its Read Me calls *"a System 7-only shareware
  product"*. So on the exact machine this project floors at, the standard
  tool is four formats and one segment scheme, permanently. That is the
  gap this project actually fills, and nothing in the repository had said
  so.
- **An oracle you share a blind spot with cannot show you the blind
  spot.** Expander claims multipart BinHex — several documents in one text
  file, the shape comp.binaries.mac produced. We take the first document
  and exit 0; `unar` does the same, so 179 BinHex fixtures and a
  differential suite could never have caught it. It was fixed the same
  day, with the reproduction — the entry below. The lesson generalises
  past BinHex: **the differential suite can only find defects the oracle
  does not share**, and the cheapest way to find the shared ones is to ask
  what some THIRD implementation claims. That is the same move that closed
  StuffIt method 6 with macunpack, arrived at from the other direction.

What this comparison is not: Expander was never RUN here. Every row in its
column is documentation, labelled as such in FORMATS.md, and the one number
it would have been nice to have — the memory partition the application asks
for — is unmeasured, because reading a `SIZE` resource off that HFS image
needs a reader this machine does not have.

## 2026-08-15 — several BinHex documents in one file, and the oracle's blind spot

**Claim:** a `.hqx` can hold more than one document; this decoder read the
first and returned success. **Evidence:** `cat a.hqx b.hqx` listed and
extracted one file, exit 0. **Consequence:** every posting that carried two
attachments silently lost all but the first.

The fix is small — after a document's terminating ':' , look for the next
banner and decode again — and it is transcribed rather than invented:
`reference/macutils/hexbin/hexbin.c` decodes a document and then does
`again = 1; goto nexttry`, its `find_header()` scanning on to EOF. Three
things about it are worth keeping:

- **The banner scan had a limit that was right once and wrong afterwards.**
  32 KB is a sane bound for finding the FIRST banner, since a file that is
  not BinHex should be given up on rather than read whole. Applied to the
  second document it would have made the fix work in extract mode and fail
  in list mode, where the previous document's stream is never consumed and
  the distance to the next banner is the size of the file. A bound derived
  from one caller's situation does not survive a second caller.
- **A banner with nothing usable after it is an error, not a quiet stop.**
  The tempting alternative — treat any failure after the first document as
  "end of file" — reintroduces exactly the defect being fixed, in a place
  nobody would look for it again.
- **`unar` shares the limitation, so the differential suite was blind by
  construction.** 179 BinHex fixtures and 3,740 corrupt variants could not
  have caught this at any point, and the size of that suite is precisely
  what made the gap feel impossible. The way out was a THIRD oracle:
  macutils' `hexbin` has handled the case since 1991, and it now grades the
  concatenated fixtures in `ci.sh` (TESTING.md). This is the macunpack
  lesson from the other direction — that one was *a limitation that names
  one tool is a limitation of that tool*; this one is **a limitation you
  share with your oracle is invisible until a different implementation is
  asked.**

Both new suites were shown to fail before being believed: a one-line mutation
(decode one document and stop) fails 6 unit checks and all four assertions of
`tools/run_multihqx.py`, including the `hexbin` one.

The other half of what StuffIt Expander 4.0.2 calls multipart BinHex — one
document broken across several postings — is NOT this. What this engine joins
is a document cut across files named `.001`, `.002`, which the plain-split
scheme already did (FORMATS.md, comparison table).

## 2026-08-15 — DiskDoubler in scope: three containers, four methods, and two traps

DiskDoubler and Now Compress were both out of scope by the brief and named
detect-only in five status files. The maintainer put them in scope; this is
what DiskDoubler took, and why Now Compress did not land with it.

**What is implemented is what the corpus can grade.** Three containers (a
single compressed file, `DDAR`, `DDA2`) and methods 0, 6/9, 8 and 10 — every
method the six fixtures use. Methods 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 7 are declined per
entry, because the reference marks two of them unimplemented and one untested,
and none appears in any fixture: code no oracle can grade is not a feature,
it is a liability with a version number. Same rule that keeps LZH extraction
out.

**Method 8 is Compact Pro compression in a DiskDoubler container**, so it is
`fmt_cpt.c`'s two stages with one parameter changed: the Huffman block is
0xfff0 here against 0x1fff0 there. That made three functions and two structs
move into `unc_internal.h` rather than be copied — the alternative was a
second copy of an LZH decoder that 108 fixtures already exercise.

Two traps, both worth carrying:

- **`unc_bufin_tell()` counts from the last `unc_bufin_init()`, not from the
  start of the file.** DDn addresses three sub-streams by absolute offset and
  seeks between them, and every seek re-bases the buffer — so the first
  version computed `literalstart`, `lengthstart` and `nextblock` against a
  moving zero and produced offsets like 42. The decoder now carries an
  `origin` and adds it. **A position helper is only absolute if nothing
  re-bases the thing it measures.**
- **A short read is invisible until the optimiser exposes it.** The `DDAR`
  entry header is 124 bytes and the walk read 120 of them, so `rsrccrc` came
  from uninitialized stack. At `-O0` that stack held zeros and the check was
  skipped; at `-O2` it held `"ttxt"` and the entry failed its checksum. The
  fixtures could not see it either, because every DiskDoubler file in the
  corpus takes the other branch — **the unit test written for coverage found
  it within a minute of existing**, which is the argument for fixture-free
  tests in one line.

**Now Compress is not the same job.** Its archive is SOLID — every fork of
every file in one compressed stream, entries addressed as (offset, length) —
which is a container model this engine has never had, and the codec is a
517-line handle against a single fixture. It stays detect-only, and the
detect-only claim stays tested (`run_diff.py` keeps it in DETECT_ONLY).
OPEN_ITEMS.md has what would close it.

**Neither format has multi-segment anything.** The request was conditional on
that, so it was checked rather than assumed: `XADDiskDoublerParser.m`,
`XADNowCompressParser.m` and macutils' `dd.c` contain no concept of a part, a
volume or a continuation, and no header field in either format numbers one.
DiskDoubler split *disks*, not archives — the multi-segment work of M11 has no
counterpart here.

## 2026-08-15 — Now Compress without the solid stream

The reference reads a Now Compress archive as one SOLID stream: every fork of
every file concatenated, entries cut out of it by (offset, length). That is
why this was written up as a container model the engine had never had, and
why it was ranked as a second job rather than more of the first.

**It does not have to be read that way.** The parser hands the decompressor a
list of per-file start offsets, and each file's stream holds exactly that
file's resource fork followed by its data fork — the solid offsets are just
the running sum of those. So this reader opens one stream per file and serves
two forks out of it, which is the shape the engine already has, and which is
what keeps the whole format to a **flat 128 KB**: history, block, staging, all
fixed by the format rather than by the archive. A solid reader would have had
to hold or re-decode the stream to reach a late entry.

**A rediscovered structure beats an inherited abstraction.** The reference's
solid-stream machinery exists because XADMaster has it and it is free there;
transcribing it here would have imported an architecture to solve a problem
this format does not actually have.

**One fixture is not coverage, and the mutation says so.** The corpus has a
single Now Compress archive; instrumenting the block loop shows it reaches two
of the five block shapes, with one file, one fork and no folders. The unit
test `test_nowcompress` builds the container synthetically — a folder, a file
with both forks, stored blocks — and its worth is measurable: **serve the two
forks in the wrong order and the unit test fails while the fixture still
passes 1/1**, because the fixture has no data fork to get wrong. What neither
reaches is written down in OPEN_ITEMS.md, including a checksum-driven search
for a wrong offset delta that has never once fired here.

## 2026-08-15 — the emulator harness graded fixtures against other fixtures

**Claim:** two Mini vMac tiers run in parallel, as this repository's own
harness README recommends, can grade a fixture against another fixture's
screen. **Evidence:** the 4 MB and 1 MB tiers started together; three fixtures
failed at 4 MB and two at 1 MB, and the failing rows reported file counts,
peak heaps and fork lengths that belonged to archives from the OTHER run —
`derived-MacPass390.sea` "failing" with `derived-DD_Expand.sea`'s 102,432 B
peak and 270,362 B resource fork. Re-run serially, all of them pass.
**Consequence:** every parallel run this project has ever done was liable to
it, and the same five fixtures pass 61/61 on both tiers once the cause is
fixed.

The cause is check-then-act. `Display.__init__` scanned for an `n` whose
`/tmp/.X<n>-lock` did not exist and then started Xvfb on `:n`. Two processes
both saw `:90` free; the second Xvfb exited immediately because the lock was
taken, and nothing checked that it had — the readiness probe,
`xdotool getdisplaygeometry`, succeeded against the FIRST run's server. From
there both runs pointed the emulator and `import` at one display.

Two things worth carrying:

- **A readiness probe that does not identify what it is talking to will
  happily confirm somebody else's server.** The probe was doing its job; it
  was asked the wrong question. Xvfb now picks the display itself with
  `-displayfd` and tells us which, so the answer cannot be another process's.
- **The failure mode was a wrong verdict, not an error.** Nothing crashed,
  nothing warned; a format that works perfectly was reported as broken, and
  the numbers in the failure message were the tell. Had two fixtures happened
  to share an aggregate, it would have been a false PASS instead. A harness
  whose two instances can silently share a channel does not have a flakiness
  problem, it has a correctness one.

## M13 — ARC and uuencode: a generator that agrees with you proves nothing

**2026-08-15.** The brief became "everything StuffIt Expander 4.0.2 expands",
which named four formats. Two are delivered (`.arc`, `.uu`); two are not
(AppleLink `.pkg`, StuffIt SpaceSaver), and the reason is worth separating
from the work.

**Neither delivered format occurs in the corpus.** Both are therefore graded
the way M11's segmented sets were: generate the archives, then hand them to an
implementation that is not ours. For ARC that is `unar`; for the UUTool shape
of `.uu` it is macutils' `hexbin`, the implementation the decoder was
transcribed from. Debian's `arc` 5.21q, fetched by `tools/get_arc.sh`, adds
archives whose bytes owe nothing to this repository at all — but it only emits
methods 2, 4, 8 and 9, and macutils says ArcMac used 1 to 7, so the generator
is not optional.

**Twice, our decoder and our generator agreed with each other and were both
wrong.** The crunch decoder (methods 5/6/7) read its 12-bit codes LSB-first;
`tools/synth_arc.py` mirrored the same reading, so every generated archive
round-tripped perfectly — and the CRC-16 agreed too, because ARC's checksum
covers the DECODED bytes, which were correct. `unar` refused all three
methods. The fix is one call: XADARCCrunchHandle reads `CSInputNextBitString`,
and in XADMaster it is the **LE-suffixed** variant that means LSB-first
(`reference/XADMaster/CSInputBuffer.h`), so these codes are MSB-first. The
second was the same shape: the compress encoder for methods 8/9 added a table
entry on every code, where the decoder adds one on every code *except the
first*, so it widened its codes one step early — again invisible in a round
trip, again caught by `unar` and by our own decoder agreeing with each other
against the generator.

**The lesson is the one this repository keeps relearning from the other
direction.** "Ask what evidence a tolerance rests on; if the answer is our own
output, it cannot fail" (2026-08-08) was about a skip. This is the same
sentence about a fixture: **a generator written from the same reading as the
decoder tests the reading, not the format.** A round trip is a self-portrait.
The oracle is the only part of the arrangement that can disagree, which is why
neither format was allowed into the suite without one.

**What is declined, and the difference between the two reasons.**

- ~~ARC methods 0x0a (Crushed) and 0x0b (Distilled) are PKARC's. Nothing
  reachable writes them, so an implementation could not be graded — the
  DiskDoubler rule (2026-08-15), unchanged.~~ **Both halves of that were
  wrong and both were withdrawn on 2026-08-25**: the methods are PAK's,
  from NoGate Consulting, and PAK 2.51 writes them here under DOSBox.
  They extract. See 2026-08-25 (*ARC 0x0a and 0x0b*), which is also
  where the reasoning lives.
- **ArcMac** is different and worth recording. Its entries ARE walked: the
  Mac header is documented in `reference/macutils/macunpack/arc.h`, offset by
  offset, so name, type, creator and both fork lengths come out and the entry
  is reported. What is refused is the payload, because the entry's single
  stream holds both forks and no source anywhere says which comes first.
  macutils would have said — `macbinary.c` calls `arc()` — but **`arc.c` is
  absent from the macutils distribution, upstream tarball included** (checked
  2026-08-15). Now Compress is the reason this is not guessable by analogy:
  its streams hold the resource fork BEFORE the data fork, the opposite of
  the order the header lists them in (2026-08-15). A coin flip with a 50%
  chance of silently producing swapped forks is exactly what rule #1 exists
  to prevent.
- **AppleLink `.pkg` and StuffIt SpaceSaver are not delivered at all**, and
  the searching is recorded so nobody repeats it: the only AppleLink format
  with a published layout is the Apple II `.acu` ("fZink", CiderPress2's
  format notes), which that documentation explicitly distinguishes from the
  Macintosh Package format; SpaceSaver has no specification and no open
  implementation, and Aladdin's own later Expanders dropped it. Neither can
  even be SNIFFED without inventing a magic number. The route that would work
  is the one Compact Pro segments took (2026-08-14): drive the original
  application under Mini vMac and derive the layout from specimens it
  produces. That needs a specimen and a copy of the tool, and this environment
  has neither.

**One implementation detail worth carrying.** `unc_bufin_tell()` counts from
wherever the buffer was initialised, not from the start of the file. fmt_uu.c
re-initialises a buffer per document (it reads each document twice — the
Finder info that gives a Mac file its type sits at the END of the document,
and the sink needs it before the first byte), so every offset it keeps has to
be made absolute. Getting that wrong looked exactly like a corrupt second
document in a multi-document file, and only in the second one.

## Multipart postings: the other half of "split", and what each format can prove

**2026-08-15.** "Every type that does split files should be decoded" turned
out to name two different things, and only one of them was already built.

**What is a split archive, per the references.** XADMaster ships exactly two
split parsers — `XADSplitFileParser` (plain `.NNN`) and
`XADStuffItSplitParser` — plus spanning inside the zip parser; Compact Pro's
scheme has no reference at all and was derived here (2026-08-14). Everything
else was checked and has nothing: **ARC has no volume, part or segment concept
anywhere** — not in `XADARCParser.m`, not in macutils' `arc.h` or its ArcMac
notes, not in the `arc` 5.21q man page — which is the same answer DiskDoubler
and Now Compress gave. So M11's four schemes are the whole of what the
REFERENCES describe, and no new joiner was needed. **A fifth turned up on
2026-08-21 and needed no new joiner either**: DiskDoubler's `SPLT`, which no
reference implements — the search above was of the references, and this file
should have said so at the time.

**What was missing is the TEXT formats' version of splitting**, which is not
an archive scheme at all: one file posted as several mail messages. Joining
the parts — by hand, or by the `.NNN` scheme this engine already has — leaves
each posting's header sitting in the middle of the encoded stream.

- A **clean** cut joined and decoded already: `hello.uu.001` / `.002` works,
  because a split's ground truth is whatever archive it joins back into.
- A **realistic** one did not, for BinHex *and* uuencode: `From:` ends a
  BinHex document, since `:` is its terminator, and a mail header is not a
  uu line. Both came back as errors.

**hexbin was doing it all along**, which is the recurring shape: a limitation
that names one tool. `hqx.c` checks each LINE for "intermediate garbage" and
skips the whole line when it finds any — measured here, it decodes a
posting-shaped file byte-for-byte identically to the unsplit one. The rule
works because the BinHex alphabet omits `n`, `o`, `s`, `t`, `u` and the space:
ordinary prose disqualifies itself within a few characters. Our reader
filtered invalid CHARACTERS instead, which keeps the valid ones out of a junk
line and then stops at its colon.

**Only half of hexbin's rule is transcribed, and the half left out is the
interesting one.** It also skips any line whose length differs from the first
line's, with a look-ahead exception for the last. That test can discard a
legitimate short final line — hexbin ships `-c` to turn it off, which is the
reference admitting the same thing — and what it would have caught here is a
junk line built only of alphabet characters. **BinHex can afford to drop that
half because it carries per-fork CRCs**: a wrongly-accepted line becomes a
per-entry checksum error, not silence. That is asserted, not assumed
(`tools/run_multihqx.py` feeds it `AB:CD` mid-stream and requires a non-zero
exit).

**uuencode cannot afford the same reasoning, and the difference is the point.**
It has no length and no checksum anywhere, so the line test has to come from
the format itself — and it does: a uu line's first character declares how many
bytes it encodes, so a line whose length disagrees cannot be data. That
disqualifies every header shape measured. What no test can recover is a part
that never arrived: a set missing its MIDDLE part decodes to a short file and
nothing objects. A missing LAST part is still caught, because the terminator
and `end` live there. **The limitation is asserted rather than described** —
`tools/run_uu.py` builds a gapped set and requires the short, silent result —
so the day uuencode grows a check nobody knew about, the suite says so.

**Both new assertions were run against a mutant** with the line rules removed,
and both fail there. A test for a tolerance is worth nothing until the
intolerant build has failed it.

## Now Compress: writing the archives the corpus never had

**2026-08-15.** The format shipped with one real fixture, and the entry above
says what that cost: three of the five block shapes, the offset-delta search,
folders, data forks and multi-file archives were transcribed and never run.
The remedy is the M13 one — `tools/synth_now.py` writes the archives and
`unar` grades them, three ways: the bytes that went in, what the oracle
extracts, what we extract (`tools/run_now.py`, in `ci.sh`). Fourteen
archives, byte-exact on Linux and under qemu-m68k; thirteen are graded three
ways and the fourteenth one way, because on that one the reference is wrong
(the entry below). One of them, `synth-now-mixed.now`, is committed as a
fixture, so the differential suite grades these paths even where `unar` is
not installed.

**What the fixture actually reaches, measured rather than assumed.**
Instrumenting the block loop: three blocks, flags `0x40` twice and `0x20`
once, `padding` 0 everywhere, `delta` 0, no stream-restart record, no
"extra length" list, and the deepest Huffman code 12 of a possible 31. Every
one of those is now a generated archive, including a code 17 deep, which is
the only way the length-over-a-nibble list is reached at all.

**The oracle constrains what can be generated, and the constraint is a fact
about the oracle.** The first generated Huffman blocks failed in `unar` while
decoding perfectly here. XADMaster tracks its bit position as
`currbyte*8 - (numbits & 7)` and raises end-of-buffer the moment `currbyte`
passes the end, so it needs the stream's padding to cover whatever its
register is still holding — up to 7 bits. Bisecting confirmed it: 3 spare bits
or fewer and a block it otherwise reads is refused. Our reader is bit-serial
and never looks past the bit it needs, so it is strictly more tolerant, and
there is nothing to fix — but a generated archive the oracle cannot read
grades nothing, so the generator leaves a whole spare byte. `endbits` can
express at most 15 bits of padding, which is what bounds the workaround.

**An assertion that could not fail, again, and the same tell.** A zero-length
table record means "the next block starts a stream", which makes the decoder
reload the dictionary cache. Put that marker on the SECOND block and deleting
the code that reads it changes nothing — the cache was written after the first
block and still holds exactly what the running window holds. The suite stayed
green against a build with the marker ignored. On the THIRD block the two
differ and the mutant fails. **When a flag's effect is "restore state X", the
test only bites where X has since diverged** — which is the 2026-08-13 ration
assertion in different clothing: both observed a consequence that was true
anyway.

Twelve mutations of `fmt_now.c` were run against the suite and all twelve
fail it: fork order swapped, the delta search disabled, the dictionary restore
and the dictionary save each removed, the restart marker ignored, LZSS-only
folded into stored, Huffman-then-LZSS folded into Huffman-only, the new-LZSS
window based at the block start instead of the history, `padding` dropped from
the block length, directory nesting flattened, the extra-length list ignored,
and the word alignment after the code table.

**What none of it proves.** XADMaster is the only implementation of this
format in existence, so for the three shapes no real archive reaches,
agreement means the transcription matches its source — not that its source
matches Now Software. The two shapes the fixture does reach are graded against
a real archive by the differential suite, and that is the whole of the
external evidence. Written down in TESTING.md rather than left implied.

## Now Compress: an empty file in the middle, and which reader is right

**2026-08-15.** Generating archives turned up a shape the two
implementations decode differently, and it is the reference that is wrong.

**Claim:** a zero-length file anywhere but last makes `unar` mis-decode the
files after it. **Evidence:** an archive holding `two forks`, `empty`,
`after` and `beside` — `unar` reports `Folder/after` as failed and writes
`beside` at the right length with the wrong bytes; we extract all four
byte-exact. Moving `empty` to the end makes `unar` agree, which is how the
boundary was located. **Consequence:** asserted both ways in `run_now.py` —
our extraction must be exact and the oracle must still fail — so a later
`unar` that fixes this reports itself instead of quietly agreeing.

**The mechanism.** An empty file's stream has no blocks at all.
`produceBlockAtOffset` asks "have I run out of blocks?" with an `if` and not
a `while`, so after loading a header that declares zero it indexes
`blocks[0]` regardless — and the array is only reallocated when a file needs
MORE entries than the last one did, so `blocks[0]` still holds the previous
file's first block. The next file is then decoded from a stale offset.

Two things worth carrying:

- **Reading per file made this unreachable rather than fixed.** Nothing in
  `fmt_now.c` handles it; the stream is opened per file, from that file's own
  table, so a file with no blocks is simply a file whose table is never read.
  The solid abstraction the entry above declined to inherit is exactly where
  the bug lives. **An architecture that cannot represent a bug beats a check
  for it.**
- **The oracle failed loudly on one entry and SILENTLY on the next.**
  `beside` came back the right length with the wrong bytes — no error, no
  warning. A differential suite that only compared exit statuses would have
  called that a pass, which is the same lesson the emulator harness taught on
  a different day: the dangerous failure is a wrong verdict, not an error.

## Two corpus lines that could not be restored, found by adding a third

**2026-08-15.** Committing a generated Now Compress archive as a fixture meant
writing a `synthetic:tools/synth_now.py` line in `fixtures/SOURCES.md`, and
the contract for those lines is in `harvest.py`: "the script must accept
`-o <path>` and be deterministic". Checking that the two existing lines of the
same shape worked found that **neither did** — `synth_arc.py` and
`synth_uu.py` take an output DIRECTORY and reject `-o`, so
`synth-arc-mixed.arc` and `synth-uu-mac.uu` could not be rebuilt at all. A
fresh clone would have restored 805 of 807 fixtures and reported the other two
as absent, which the diff suite counts and prints, but nobody had ever cloned
fresh.

**Why it survived:** `--restore` needs a network for every other fixture, so
it never runs in `ci.sh` — the one part of the corpus pipeline with no test
under it. Both scripts now take `-o` and reproduce their recorded sha256
exactly, and `check_docs.py` regenerates every `synthetic:` line and compares,
which is the half of the restore path that works offline. Shown to fail first:
with `synth_arc.py` reverted, the check names it.

**The generalisation is the useful part.** This repository's habit is to ask
what evidence a claim rests on; the sharper question here was *when does the
code that makes this claim true ever run?* A provenance line, a `--fix` flag,
a restore path, a migration — anything only exercised by a human doing an
unusual thing — is where a claim rots without contradicting anything.

## The second Now Compress archive, found by creator code

**2026-08-16.** OPEN_ITEMS.md asked for one thing: another real Now Compress
archive. It exists, it was on a public mirror the corpus already draws from,
and **no filename search would ever have found it**.

**What the name search says.** `.now` is the extension (the format's users say
so; there is no other public description). Crawled and searched by name:
funet's whole `/pub/mac` tree — 45,891 files, 30,596 of them `.hqx` — has no
`.now` anywhere; umich's 3,678 do not either; the Wayback Machine has never
archived a `.now` URL on any of six Mac hosts (`hyperarchive.lcs.mit.edu`,
`sumex-aim.stanford.edu`, `mac.archive.umich.edu`, `ftp.amug.org`,
`www.macgurus.com`, `ftp.apple.com`), and the same query for `.hqx` on the
first of them returns results, so the filter works. Now Software's own site is
in the Wayback Machine with no binaries. Their updaters are `TEXT/NwUD` and
`APPL/RMSP`, not Now Compress. Now Utilities 6.5's CD ships a 531-file HFS
listing with type and creator for every file and not one `NC` creator among
them, because Now Compress was 5.x-era and did not survive into 6.

**What found it is 4 bytes of metadata.** A Now Compress self-extracting
archive is an application with creator **`NCse`**, and BinHex and MacBinary
both carry type and creator in a header near the front of the file — so a
2 KB range request per file, decoded, gives the creator of every archive on a
mirror for a few megabytes of traffic. 241 `.sea`/`.bin` files into that
sweep: `science/SyntheticReferences2.0.sea.bin`, 1995, 2,960,000 bytes,
`APPL/NCse`. **The format is invisible in filenames and obvious in four bytes
of Finder metadata; search the metadata.**

**The specimen.** A folder, a `TEXT/ttxt` file with both forks (574 B data,
332 B resource) and a 9,949,696-byte FileMaker Pro database, in 2.9 MB. Two
streams, 306 blocks, all of them "new" LZSS with flag bit `0x100` also set
(which this decoder ignores and the reference ignores too), no padding, no
offset delta, and **two stream-restart records** — the zero-length table entry
that reloads the dictionary cache, which the corpus's first fixture never
uses and which had only ever been exercised by generated archives. Peak heap
on the 9.9 MB extraction is 131,072 B: the flat 128 KB holds.

**`unar` cannot read it.** Every fork fails with "Attempted to read more data
than was available", on the raw `.sea` and through the MacBinary envelope
alike. The cause is the overrun derived the day before from generated
archives: XADMaster tracks its bit position as `currbyte*8 - (numbits & 7)`
and raises end-of-buffer as soon as `currbyte` passes the end, so it needs the
padding to cover its register — up to 7 bits. **129 of this archive's 306
blocks end with fewer than 7 spare bits**, and the spare count is spread
almost uniformly over 0..15, i.e. the real encoder pads to the word and no
further. So the workaround in `tools/synth_now.py` — always leave a spare byte
— is a departure from what real archives look like, and the reason it exists
is now measured on a real one rather than inferred. What was written up as "a
property of the oracle rather than of the format" is stronger than that: it
makes the only implementation of this format unable to read the format.

**Why we believe our output, given nothing can grade it.** Three
independent things, none of which is our own say-so: the 574-byte `TEXT` fork
is coherent English prose describing the product it ships with; the 332-byte
resource fork's own first 16 bytes — data offset 0x100, map offset 0x11A, data
length 0x1A, map length 0x32 — account for exactly 332 bytes, and a resource
fork identifies itself that way; and the entry table's declared sizes are met
exactly. `tools/run_now.py` asserts the structural one, which can fail on its
own terms: swap the fork order and it reports a resource fork that is not one.
The hashes beside it are labelled for what they are — a regression anchor, not
ground truth.

**Consequences for the corpus.** Two fixtures joined it (the envelope and the
derived archive), the suite is 809, and **four forks are adjudicated by
nobody** — the manifest carries names, type and creator from `lsar`, which
walks the archive fine, and marks every fork unadjudicated with the oracle's
own error. That is the same shape as DiskDoubler's 11 (OPEN_ITEMS.md), and it
is why the differential suite alone cannot protect this decoder.

**The transferable part.** A format whose files are rare is not necessarily a
format whose files are gone: this one had been sitting on the mirror this
project has harvested since M0, three directories away from fixtures already
in the corpus. What hid it was that every search anyone would run — the
extension, the product name, the vendor — is a search over names, and the
thing that identifies it is metadata. **When a format is defined by its
header, look for it with its header.**

## Now Compress, written by Now Compress

**2026-08-16.** Three of the five block shapes had never existed as real bytes
anywhere this project could reach, so they were graded against XADMaster — the
implementation `fmt_now.c` was transcribed from — and OPEN_ITEMS.md said so.
They exist now, made by the encoder that defined the format:
`tools/vmac/nowcompress.py` installs **Now QuickFiler 5.0.1** under System 7
on an emulated Mac Plus and drives its Compress and Archive commands. Same
method as the Compact Pro segments (2026-08-14), same reason: the application
is the only authority left.

**What the encoder writes, measured** by patching `now_next_block` in a
throwaway build to print `flags`:

| what                        | flags       | shape             |
|-----------------------------|-------------|-------------------|
| Compress ▸ Faster           | 0x10        | LZSS only         |
| Compress ▸ Smaller, text    | 0x1f        | LZSS only         |
| Compress ▸ Smaller, mixed   | 0x3f        | Huffman then LZSS |
| Compress ▸ Smaller, random  | 0x00        | stored            |
| Archive ▸ Smaller (a `.sea`)| 0x100/0x140 | stored / new LZSS |

Every one of the seven specimens comes back **byte-for-byte identical to what
went in**, and `unar` decodes all of them too — so the three shapes are no
longer a transcription checked against its own source: they are the encoder's
own output, agreed on by two independent decoders.

**Four things the reference could not have told anyone.**

- **The encoder REFUSES incompressible files.** A 30 KB random file comes back
  untouched with "N file(s) are special uncompressible files". A stored block
  therefore only appears INSIDE a file that is compressible somewhere else —
  which is how the `stored` case is built: 33 KB of keystream followed by
  16 KB of prose gives a stored first block and a Huffman-then-LZSS second.
- **A stored block can carry flag bit 0x100**, which neither this decoder nor
  XADMaster looks at. Both are right by accident: the shape is chosen by
  0x20/0x40/0x1f, and 0x100 falls outside all three tests.
- **"Faster" and "Smaller" are the format's two compressors**, not two effort
  levels of one: Faster is always plain LZSS, Smaller picks per block.
- **The archive header's third and fourth bytes track the mode** — `00 10`
  for Faster, `00 3f` for Smaller, `01 60` for a self-extracting archive —
  a field this reader ignores and now has three known values for.

**Getting the application at all was the hard part**, and it is worth writing
down because it is a general shape. Now Compress was commercial, so unlike
Compact Pro (shareware, a corpus fixture from funet) it is on no public
mirror: what the mirrors carry is its free EXPANDER, which cannot create an
archive. It survives inside **Now Utilities 5.0.1**, whose Macintosh Garden
download is an **NDIF** disk image — Apple's undocumented compressed format.
Its `bcem` resource was derived from the one specimen, checked three ways
against what the file states about itself (chunk starts summing to the 2880
sectors its `vers` resource names, compressed offsets and lengths chaining
onto the data fork's exact length, each chunk decompressing to exactly the
sector count the next chunk's start implies), and confirmed by the only test
that matters: the result mounts as HFS. That decoder is in `nowcompress.py`,
not in the engine — disk images are out of scope, and this is tooling.

**The installer is driven by reading the screen, not by clicking coordinates.**
Its component list is eleven checkboxes; the first attempt clicked eight of
them blind and turned QuickFiler OFF while leaving three others on, which the
screenshots showed afterwards. The script now samples each row's checkmark
column out of a screenshot after every click, so it knows what it did. **A GUI
harness that cannot read the state it is changing is writing coordinates into
a file and hoping.**

**On the licence.** Now Utilities asks for a serial number on first launch and
offers a one-week demo instead; the demo is what this uses. Nothing from the
product is redistributed: `nowcompress.py` fetches the Garden image by URL and
checks its MD5, exactly as `setup.sh` does for the ROM, and the specimens live
beside the emulator rather than in `fixtures/`.

## The archive that carries its own oracle

**2026-08-16.** OPEN_ITEMS.md had one Now Compress item left: four forks of
`derived-SyntheticReferences2.0.sea` that nothing could grade. `unar` fails on
every one of them, `macunpack` never knew the format, and the entry above
records what defended our output instead — coherent prose, a self-consistent
resource fork, a structural check in the suite. All true, and all weaker than
an independent decoder.

**There was one all along, inside the file.** The archive is
SELF-EXTRACTING: the decoder Now Software shipped in 1995 is sitting in its
resource fork. `nowcompress.py --adjudicate` puts the `.sea` on a 25 MB volume
with room for what comes out, runs it under System 7 on the emulated Mac Plus,
and compares fork by fork.

**It agrees on every fork**, including the 9,949,696-byte FileMaker database
byte for byte. The one apparent difference — 11 bytes of the 332-byte resource
fork, at offsets 63..121 — is inside the region reserved for system use, which
the Resource Manager stamps with session scratch whenever it touches a file.
`tools/vmac_check.py::mask_reserved` has masked bytes 16..127 since M10 for
exactly this reason, and under that convention the forks are identical. The
suspicious part would have been the opposite result: bytes 16..127 matching
after a round trip through a real Mac.

**So the adjudication is not "nobody can check this".** It is: no decoder on
Linux can, and the decoder that came with the archive can, and it does.

Two things worth carrying:

- **A self-extracting archive is a format specimen and an implementation of
  the format, in one file.** Every `.sea` in this corpus is a decoder that has
  never been asked anything. That generalises past Now Compress — StuffIt,
  Compact Pro and DiskDoubler SEAs are all in `fixtures/`.
- **The oracle was not missing, it was unexamined.** The item said "a second
  implementation of Now Compress. None exists" — written while a copy of one
  sat in the corpus under a `.sea` extension. That is the same shape as
  "there is no oracle for StuffIt method 6", which was a fact about XADMaster
  with `macunpack` already in `reference/` (BLOCKERS.md). **Twice now the
  answer to "nothing can grade this" has been sitting in the repository.**

## ArcMac's fork order, measured rather than guessed

**Claim:** an ArcMac entry's single compressed stream holds the data fork
first and the resource fork second, and the header's two fork lengths are
little-endian. **Evidence:** ArcMac 1.3c, run under Mini vMac, archiving a
file with both forks non-empty (`tools/vmac/arcmac.py`); and `ARCMAC.DOC`,
*ArcMac File Structure*, for the byte order. **Consequence:** ArcMac payloads
extract, both forks byte-exact, instead of being declined per entry.

The route there is the transferable part, because the item had been closed as
evidence-limited and was reopened by a single wiki page.

**A format wiki is worth reading for what it CITES, not for what it says.**
justsolve.archiveteam.org/wiki/ArcMac carries no header layout at all — it
would have looked like a dead end. What it carries is two pointers: that the
specification is a section of the `ARCMAC.DOC` shipped inside every ArcMac
distribution, and direct links to those distributions. Both were decisive and
neither is a fact about the format.

**The specification answered the question nobody had asked.** `ARCMAC.DOC`
gives the Pascal record and marks seven fields with `{!` — "these values are
all stored in archive using Intel 80xxx byte sex". `arc.h`, which this
decoder was transcribed from, gives the offsets and says nothing about order,
so the fork lengths had been read big-endian since M13. Both real archives
disprove it instantly: little-endian, the two fork lengths sum to the header's
own uncompressed size on every entry; big-endian they sum to ~4 billion. **A
field whose byte order the reference does not state is an untested
assumption, not a transcription.**

**The archives in the wild decided a different question than expected.** Two
were found — `ARCMACRC.BIN` on cd.textfiles.com and, inside Macintosh
Garden's `ArcMac.hqx`, a self-extracting `ArcPop.Arc` — and neither settles
fork order, because *every entry in both has one empty fork*. That is not
chance: an ArcMac distribution is documents (data fork only) and applications
(resource fork only). A specimen search can be a complete success and still
not answer the question, and it is worth predicting which entries would
actually discriminate before going looking.

**So the encoder was asked instead**, as `nowcompress.py` asks Now Compress.
ArcMac 1.3c is not a dialog application at all — it opens a command shell,
"Martian Operating System", whose verb is `arc a<modifiers> archive files`.
Two archives of the same two-fork subject were made in one boot: `a` (which
chose crunching) and `as` (stored). Both decode to data fork then resource
fork, byte-exact both ways. **The stored one is the better evidence**: its
payload is the two forks laid down raw, so the order is legible without
invoking our decoder or any other, and the resource fork's own 16-byte header
appears exactly at the data fork's length.

Three smaller things this cost, all worth keeping:

- **The encoder came out of the archive it was needed for.** `ARCMACRC.BIN`'s
  first entry IS the ArcMac application — an APPL with an empty data fork —
  so decoding that one entry with our own CLI and re-wrapping it as MacBinary
  produced a runnable copy, and no self-extractor had to run inside the guest.
  The same shape as the `.sea` adjudication above: **the specimen and the
  implementation are one file.**
- **The Finder flags in a Mac header are not a blob to copy across.** Passing
  the ArcMac header's Finder info straight into MacBinary put the application
  on the volume with bit 0x40 of the high byte set — `invisible`. The disk
  showed 112K used and one item, which reads as "the copy failed" and is not.
- **Mini vMac only flushes a volume when the guest ejects it.** The Finder
  displayed the freshly written `Test.ARC` while the host's copy of the image
  did not have it, because the emulator buffers sector writes and the harness
  kills it. Special ▸ Shut Down ejects everything; `--make` ends that way.
  This is narrower than, and does not contradict, the withdrawn "write-back is
  lazy and partial" claim in BLOCKERS.md: writes DO land, on eject.

**An ArcMac header contains a whole PC ARC header at offset 59**, which is why
the wiki can say non-ArcMac-aware extractors sometimes cope. That makes a
one-entry `.arc` carvable out of any ArcMac entry, which is how the payloads
were first read — through the shipped decoder and its CRC, before any of this
was implemented. It is also a free cross-check: `XARCMAC.EXE` decoded out of
the 1988 archive is byte-identical to the copy in `macarc.zip` from a
different site, so method 8 has a real external oracle now.

## SuperDisk!, read out of SuperDisk!

**2026-08-20.** Alysis SuperDisk! was the one format the corpus held that
nothing here could name: two files, `Disk Expander .X` and `SD! Utilities.x`,
that came out of their BinHex envelopes and then sniffed as *unknown*. It was
filed as out of scope under rule #1 — magic numbers and layouts come from a
reference implementation or a spec, never from memory — because there is no
published layout, XADMaster does not implement it and `unar` and `macunpack`
both stop at the envelope. **The premise was wrong, and it was wrong in the
way this repository's smells predict**: both files are APPLs, both are
self-extracting, and a self-extracting archive contains an implementation.
The rule was never the obstacle. Finding the reference was.

There turned out to be three of them, each better than the last:

- **The self-extractor's `CODE 2`.** `SD! Utilities.x` carries 2,694 bytes of
  68k that walk the records, chain the blocks and decode methods 1..9. Every
  routine cited in `fmt_superdisk.c` — 0x2e4, 0x5f4, 0x6b4, 0x72e, 0x884 — is
  an address in that resource.
- **An Add-In Module in the data fork.** `Disk Expander .X` begins with 982
  bytes headed `SuperDisk! Add-In Module`, named `SlowFastB`, which is
  method 11's decoder: a self-contained LZSS with a parameter block at A0 and
  a selector at A0+36. The archive ships the codec its own payload needs.
- **The extension the second archive extracts.** Decoding method 11 yields a
  66,953-byte resource fork which is Disk Expander 1.2 itself, and its `Cmpr`
  resources are the whole codec set, one module per method, each with its
  method number at offset 0x1c and its name at 0x3c: 1 " Faster", 2
  " Tighter", 9 "30% Solution", 10 "SlowFastA", 11 "SlowFastB" (twice, a
  small implementation and a fast one), 13 " Faster" again, 20 "Tennessee".
  **Corrected 2026-08-29: 13 was listed here as "SlowFastC", which is its
  `Cmpr` RESOURCE's name and not the module's own** — one name in this list
  came from a different field from all the others, and the module numbered
  13 shares module 1's name. FORMATS.md keeps both, and what else answers
  to 13 in other Alysis products.
  **Implementing one method printed the method table for all of them.**

### What only running it could settle

The disassembly gives the layout; it does not give the fork order, because
both archives happen to hold one fork per file — the same gap ArcMac had.
Here the answer was in the code rather than on the emulator: `CODE 2` @0x3d0
opens the data fork with `_HOpen` and writes it, then @0x3f0 opens the
resource fork with `_HOpenRF` and writes that. **Data fork first.** The unit
test builds a two-fork entry precisely because no fixture can.

What the emulator settled is the whole of it at once. `tools/vmac/superdisk.py
--adjudicate` puts each self-extracting archive on a System 7 volume, runs it,
and reads back every fork: **5 forks across 2 archives, all identical to what
this decoder produces**. That is the only oracle the format has, and it is the
format's own.

### Three things the bytes say that no reading would have

- **The predictor's context is a lossy hash, not the last two bytes.** The
  reference shifts the 16-bit context left by 6 and then ORs the new byte into
  the low EIGHT bits, so bits 6 and 7 of each byte collide with bits 0 and 1
  of the one before it. Transcribing it as a clean concatenation compiles,
  runs, and decodes garbage.
- **The decoder over-produces by two bytes and the caller drops them.** A
  block's `ulen` counts only the bytes after the two raw ones that seed the
  output, so the loop yields `ulen + 2`; the writer (@0x65e) writes `ulen`.
  The two discarded bytes consume two literals that are not there — the
  reference reads off the end of its own literal buffer, every block, on
  purpose or by luck. Stopping at `ulen` gives identical output and no
  over-read. This was found by arithmetic, not by inspection: the literal
  count matched the flag bitmap exactly, and only one reading of the loop
  made both true.
- **A stored block is a length with bit 15 set — except 0x8002.** Bit 15
  marks "stored", so the length can say at most 32,767; a full 32 KB stored
  block cannot be expressed. @0x614 special-cases the one value that collides
  and reads it as 32,768. An implementation that masks bit 15 unconditionally
  is wrong for exactly one block size.

### Two things the SUITE got wrong, caught before it shipped

- **`--budget 4M` is four bytes.** The CLI parses the value with
  `strtoul(..., 0)`, which stops at the `M`, so all 24 corrupted variants ran
  with a four-byte allocation budget: every one of them was refused its first
  buffer and none of them ever reached the parser. They passed. Every other
  suite in the tree passes an integer (`fuzz.py` writes `str(4 << 20)`), which
  is what made the odd one out findable. The variants now run for real, and
  there are 116 of them.
- **An allocation failure was ending the archive.** The record loop treated
  `UNC_ERR_MEM` like a sink or I/O error and broke out, which contradicts the
  engine's own rule — allocation failure is per-entry, never per-archive — and
  `fmt_now.c`, which has always continued. The symptom is invisible unless you
  look for it: the entries after the oversized one are simply never mentioned.
  `run_superdisk.py` now runs each archive under a budget too small for any of
  its forks and requires every entry to still be NAMED; that check fails
  against the binary built before the fix.

### What is NOT implemented, and why that is the same bargain as LZH

Methods 10, 13 and 20 have modules sitting in `reference`-quality form inside
the corpus — they could be transcribed. **They are declined per entry
anyway**, because no fixture anywhere uses them and no encoder is reachable:
SuperDisk! itself compressed files, and only its expander survives here. Code
no oracle can grade is what OPEN_ITEMS.md already refuses for LZH and for
DiskDoubler's methods 1-5 and 7. `unc_sniff` still names the file and the
entry fails cleanly, and the block chain is walked past by length so the rest
of the archive still extracts — which is why the unit test puts an
undecodable entry BETWEEN two decodable ones.

## 2026-08-20 — DiskDoubler methods 1 and 7, and what the application itself could write

The engine declined six of DiskDoubler's eleven methods because "no corpus
fixture uses one and nothing reachable writes one". Only the first half had
ever been checked. **The way to find out what an application can write is to
run it**, which is what `nowcompress.py` and `arcmac.py` already do for two
other formats; `tools/vmac/diskdoubler.py` does it here.

**DiskDoubler 3.7's Settings dialog offers exactly two software methods**, and
they are method 1 and method 8:

| menu item | writes | note |
|---|---|---|
| Method A (fastest) | **1**, Unix compress | not implemented until now |
| Method B (usually smaller) | 8, Compact Pro | implemented since M12 |
| Smallest Guess (let DD choose) | 8 | on the subject used here |
| Smallest (try both) | 8 | "both" is A and B: there is no third |
| Sigma Hardware | **7**, Stac LZS | the item only appears with the card fitted |

All four were run. That accounts for every method a DiskDoubler writes that
this project can reach: 3.7 writes 1, 7 and 8, and 4.x writes 6, 9 and 10 —
which the corpus has had since M12, written by the application of the day.
Methods 2, 3, 4 and 5 belong to 1.x and 2.x, and no copy of either survives
anywhere this machine can reach (OPEN_ITEMS.md). (**Wrong on the second
half**, corrected 2026-08-21 in "the Salient disk" below: WinWorld carries
3.1.2, whose own distribution floppy holds a method 2 fork. Method 2 is
implemented and graded from it; 3, 4 and 5 are still specimen-less.)

The Sigma line is the interesting one: method 7 is a *hardware* method, so no
emulator here can produce one — and it did not have to, because Salient built
the 3.7 distribution disk on a machine that had the card. Two THINK C
libraries on it are method 7, and each carries the checksum that grades it.
**A distribution disk is a corpus.**

Three things about method 1 that a plain `.Z` reader gets wrong, all from
`XADDiskDoublerParser.m` case 1:

- the three-byte compress header is stored XORed with 0x5a, and **so are the
  decoded bytes — but not the code stream between them**;
- the fork's stored sum has those three header bytes added into it, so the
  value to compare against is `crc - m1 - m2 - flags`;
- **the fork's LENGTH ends the stream, nothing in the stream does.** Asking
  the LZW decoder for a full buffer when the fork owes fewer bytes makes it
  read the NEXT fork's first code and reject it as out of range. That failure
  reads as "archive structure is corrupt" on a file that is perfectly well
  formed, and it is a general shape: **a codec that stops on its own input
  cannot be handed a shared stream without a limit.**

Method 7 is a 2 KB-window LZSS with two twists worth carrying: the bit stream
is stored complemented **and so is the output** (macutils complements only the
input, which is one of the reasons its copy has never run), and a match length
is not bounded by the window — 2100 bytes at distance 9 is legal and repeats.
Copying byte by byte through the ring buffer reproduces that for free; the
reference splits it into window-sized emissions because its LZSS emitter
cannot express it.

**What graded what.** Method 1 is graded by the bytes that went INTO
DiskDoubler on the emulated Mac — compressed on a Mac, decoded here, compared
with the input, the loop `arcmac.py` closes the same way — and `unar` agrees.
Method 7 has no such loop, and does not need one: the fork's own XOR checksum
is computed by the compressor over the original bytes, and both specimens
match it, with `unar` agreeing byte for byte. `macunpack` cannot be the second
implementation for either: its method 7 segfaults (BLOCKERS.md).

**What the fixtures cannot see is in the unit test, and it is written by
hand.** Both method 7 specimens are 2,226 bytes — even, and far inside the
window — so neither reaches the odd-length checksum branch (the stored value
is complemented only when the fork's length is even) nor a match longer than
2048. `test_dd_stac` writes a stream that does both. Its encoder is ours, so
it grades the transcription against the reference and not against the format;
the specimens are what grade it against the format.

## 2026-08-21 — DiskDoubler Pro's stored entries, and a corpus that carries its own oracle

`ssokolow/diskdoubler-test-files` (CC0) is nine sets of the same seven small
files, compressed on a real Power Mac G4 by DiskDoubler 3.7.7 and DiskDoubler
Pro 4.1.1 in every method their menus offer, published **beside the originals**.
That last part is what makes it worth more than its size: the oracle is the
bytes that went in, the same loop `arcmac.py` and the method 1 work closed,
except that here somebody else's hardware ran the compressor.

**Five of the nine archives did not extract.** `DDA2` entry type `0x1000` is a
file DiskDoubler Pro could not shrink: it is stored in the archive with no
`0xABCD0054` header of its own, and its 90-byte record header carries the
metadata instead. Every set has two — the JPEG and the PNG, which is exactly
the pair nothing can compress. (**Half a condition**, corrected 2026-08-21 in
"the Symantec release" below: the file must also have no resource fork, and
these two are the only fork-less files in the set.) The reference has no branch for it and raises
on the missing magic (`XADDiskDoublerParser.m`, `parseArchive2`), which is why
`unar` calls every DiskDoubler Pro archive holding an incompressible file
corrupt, and why the repository's own README reports 4.1.1 as "hit-or-miss" —
the author had already localised it to the tools rather than the files by
unpacking them on the Mac. **This is the first place this project knowingly
extracts something XADMaster cannot.** `DDAR` has the same idea under a
different flag (finder flags `0x20`), which the reference DOES handle and we
already followed; the two containers were simply never read as one design.

The layout was derived from the ten specimens, not guessed: `+62` type, `+66`
creator, `+70` finder flags, `+78` data length, `+82` resource length, and the
two forks raw at `+90`. Each field is where the record's own `totalsize` and
the file's known content put it — `totalsize - 90` equals the original's
length in all ten, and the payload begins `FFD8` and `89504E47`. The two dates
at `+54` and `+58` are the exception and are labelled as such: every stored
specimen has them EQUAL, so nothing here distinguishes creation from
modification, and they are assigned in the order the reference reads them in
the folder branch of the same walk.

**`+86` is a one-byte XOR of the fork, not the 16-bit sum a file header
carries there.** Measured: it equals the XOR of all 220 bytes of the JPEG and
all 87 of the PNG, in all five archives, and matches none of the additive,
rotating, CRC-16/ARC or CCITT candidates. So the checksum is asserted rather
than skipped, unconditionally — a stored fork whose XOR is 0 is an empty fork,
and an empty fork never reaches the check. `+87` is read as the resource
fork's; no specimen has a stored file with one, and OPEN_ITEMS.md says so.

**The folder branch was reading uninitialised bytes.** `parseArchive2`'s walk
fills 46 bytes of its header buffer and the folder dates live at `+54` and
`+58`, so every `DDA2` folder took its dates from whatever the previous
entry's 80-byte file header had left there — and the first folder from an
unset stack buffer. Nothing could see it: no fixture is a `DDA2` with folders,
the differential suite does not compare dates, and ASan does not detect reads
of uninitialised memory (that is MSan, which nothing here runs). It is one `unc_read_full` and the unit test now pins
both dates, which is a smaller lesson than it looks: **a fixed-size read
followed by field offsets past its end is a class, not an incident** — the
compressed branch re-seeks and re-reads, which is why it was never wrong.

**What it graded.** 63 single-file `0xABCD0054` containers, 9 archives, 10
self-extractors and their 10 BinHexed copies, all extracted here and compared
byte for byte with the published originals: 54 data forks and 36 resource
forks exact, plus a TIFF that has no published original and comes out
identical from all nine independent compressions. That is the container the
corpus had no fixture for, tested 63 ways, and the first grading of methods 6,
9 and 10 against source bytes rather than against `unar`.

**DiskDoubler's `SPLT`, derived from the same four files, is the fifth
segmented scheme.** A 94-byte header per part — `+8` data length, `+12`
resource length, `+16` type, `+20` creator, `+24` Finder flags, `+32`
modification and `+36` creation, `+40` part count, `+42` index, `+44` this
part's payload length, `+48` a CRC-16/XMODEM of that payload, and `SPLT`
again at `+90` — whose payloads concatenated in index order give the data
fork followed by the resource fork. Verified byte-exact on both pairs,
including the 342-byte resource fork of the Pro archive. `unar` reads neither
a part nor the parts `cat`ed together (measured, 1.10.8), so there is no
oracle and the grading is against the bytes that were cut up.

Four things the specimens settled that a guess would have got wrong:

- **The fork order is the opposite of StuffIt's split** — data fork first,
  resource fork second. `unc_sitseg_emit` now takes the order as an argument
  rather than two near-identical emitters existing.
- **The dates are modification-then-creation.** No oracle for this: the
  AppleDouble sidecars beside the specimens carry Finder info and no dates.
  What decides it is that one specimen's pair DIFFERS, and only this
  assignment reads as a file modified after it was created — and it is the
  order DiskDoubler's own file header uses (`dd_parse_filehdr`). The `DDA2`
  folder record uses the other one, so the format is not self-consistent and
  the convention alone would not have been enough.
- **A part declares its own payload length, so the file's size is a bound
  and not a measurement.** Every other scheme here takes what the host hands
  it; this one can ignore bytes appended to a part, and re-proves at every
  open that the file belongs to this set and is this part. That is 20 lines
  in `seg_open` and it is the only scheme-specific branch in the joiner.
- **The header has to beat the filename.** Both hosts decide a plain
  `.NNN` split by NAME before the sniff has a say — deliberately, because
  part 1 of a split `.sit` sniffs as the truncated `.sit` it is a slice of
  (2026-08-10). A `SPLT` set named `Archive.dd.001` would therefore have
  been joined as a plain split, folding every 94-byte header into the file,
  quietly and byte-wrong. The rule that fixes it is the one this file
  already keeps for parts: **content disposes.** The suite's `numbered`
  case is exactly that collision and fails without it.

The identity at `+4` is parsed and unused: what it MEANS is unknown (it is
equal across a set and different between sets, which is all the joiner needs).
The payload CRC at `+48` was parsed and unused too, for the reason below it —
checking it would mean reading every part a second time, a disk swap each on a
one-drive Mac. **That was true of a SECOND pass and not of the first**, and
the entry after this one is where the running check that follows from it
lives. OPEN_ITEMS.md.

## 2026-08-21 (later) — the DiskDoubler corpus becomes fixtures, and three claims it brought with it

The corpus above was graded by a script that clones it (`tools/run_ddtf.py`)
and `ci.sh` never saw a byte of it. It does now: `run_ddtf.py --register`
copies all of it into `fixtures/`, writes a row per file in
`fixtures/SOURCES.md` — a raw URL **pinned to the corpus commit**, so a later
force-push cannot silently change what is fetched — and a manifest per file.
115 fixtures; the suite went 809 → 916.

**Nothing in those manifests is our own output**, which is the only thing that
made this worth doing rather than convenient:

- the forks are the published originals;
- `testfile.TIF` has no original — it was made on the Mac — so its two forks
  are `unar`'s decode of the four DiskDoubler 3.7.7 archives, which agree;
- the entry NAMES are what `lsar` reads out of the 63 single-file containers,
  which carry them, so every method contributes its own;
- the `sources/` folder the DiskDoubler Pro archives put those files under is
  `lsar`'s too, from the four entries it manages before giving up;
- type/creator are recorded only where `lsar` reads them off that very
  fixture, never borrowed from a sibling.

The six `SPLT` parts and the two AppleDouble sidecars beside them are
registered WITHOUT manifests: they are not archives to extract but what
`run_segments.py` grades a joined set against. That closes the gap those
generated sets could never close — a generator writes headers this project
derived, so it grades the reader against our own reading of the layout, where
these were cut by DiskDoubler and the file each joins back into is published
beside it. `make_manifests.py` now refuses to rebuild a manifest whose oracle
is not `unar`, because `--force` over this corpus would have replaced ground
truth with an opinion, or — on the five archives `unar` cannot read — deleted
it.

**The corpus is three `SPLT` sets, not two.** The earlier entry says four
parts across two sets; the clone has six across three, the third being the
`.sea` self-extractor with its 14 KB resource fork. The CRC-16/XMODEM at `+48`
is exact on all six (re-measured), and all three sets join back to the
published file byte for byte, forks included. BLOCKERS.md carries the
withdrawal.

**The payload CRC is now checked, for free.** The reason it was not is
re-read above: a SECOND pass over a part costs a disk swap. A FIRST pass costs
nothing, and joining a set is exactly a first pass — so the joiner accumulates
a CRC-16/XMODEM as the bytes go past and compares it at the part boundary. A
read that starts anywhere but where the running sum stopped abandons the sum
for that part (`crc_ok = 0`), so a backward seek costs the check rather than a
swap, and the part is then joined unchecked exactly as every other scheme here
is. Three lines of state in `SegJoin`, no allocation, no second read.
**What it cannot do is promise**, and the promise is the part worth being
careful about: this catches a corrupt part in the common case and says
nothing in the uncommon one, which is why the joined FILE still adjudicates
the set. The per-entry status a caller sees is `UNC_ERR_IO` while the archive
result is `UNC_ERR_CRC` — the same shape as a missing part, where the entry
reads as truncated and the archive says segmented, and for the same reason:
`UncIO` has no channel for a reason, only for a failed read.

**An AppleDouble sidecar with no name extracted as `_`.** Found by putting the
corpus's two sidecars in `fixtures/`: `unar` names such a file after its host,
we named it nothing, and the CLI sanitized nothing into `_`. Every other
name-less format in this engine already takes the host's name through
`opt.name_hint` — gzip, `.Z`, the DiskDoubler single-file container, `SPLT`,
uuencode — and AppleSingle/AppleDouble was the one that did not, because every
fixture in the corpus until now carried a real-name entry (id 3). Netatalk
writes sidecars without one, which is every `._X` beside an `X` on a Mac
volume. Four lines in `fmt_applesingle.c`, and a unit test that fails without
them. The sidecars themselves stay manifest-less: our hint rule drops the last
extension and `unar` keeps it, so a manifest for one would assert a naming
convention rather than a decode, and DECISIONS.md already records why the hint
is the way it is.
\n
## 2026-08-21 (later still) — the forks nobody could grade, graded by the archive itself

`derived-LHA_Expander_1.0.3.sea` and `derived-Terrainman_2.0__.sea` held 11
forks that `unar` fails on and `macunpack` fails on too. Both archives are
**self-extracting**, which is the fourth time in this project that the answer
to "no implementation exists" was inside the file that needed one
(BLOCKERS.md). Running them under System 7 compares all 33 forks of the two
archives: **all 33 identical.**

Three things this cost, all of them in the HARNESS rather than the format,
and all three the same shape — a path is not a path until something says
which tree it is in:

- **The Mac's copy of a resource fork is not ground truth and never can be.**
  Every resource fork the two extractors wrote differs from ours in bytes
  16..127 — the resource header's "reserved for system use" area, which the
  Resource Manager stamps whenever it touches a file. So `--write-manifest`
  records DATA forks as adjudicated and leaves resource forks
  `unadjudicated`, with the reason extended to say the Mac agreed with those
  bytes masked. `run_diff.py` counts the two kinds separately, because
  collapsing them would either overstate the gap or hide it.
- **A self-extractor expands INTO a folder it asks you to name**, so an entry
  at the top level of the archive is one level down on the volume. A global
  "strip the first component" rule looked right on the specimen that needed it
  and broke the other one completely — Terrainman's own folder happens to
  share the destination's name and merged with it, so its nested paths need no
  adjustment and only its two top-level entries do. The rule that works is per
  path: try it as it stands, then with the first component dropped, and take
  whichever exists.
- **`hls` prints Mac Roman bytes and Python hands the shell UTF-8.** A folder
  called `Terrainman 2.0 ƒ` was neither descended into nor copied, so the walk
  returned nothing and the run reported "0 forks compared" — which reads as
  *the application wrote nothing* rather than *this could not read it*, on a
  run whose own screenshot showed the folder sitting on the volume. `hcd` and
  `hcopy` now take the raw bytes.
\n
## 2026-08-21 (later still) — running DiskDoubler Pro, and what its own Split will not say yet

`ddpro.py` is `diskdoubler.py` for the other half of the format: DiskDoubler
Pro 4.1, which writes `DDA2` and whose Split command is where `SPLT` comes
from. Two open items need it. One is narrowed and one is not, and both are
worth writing down because the next attempt starts from here.

**The 4 bytes at `+4` of a `SPLT` header are the Mac's `TickCount`.** Three
files split 72 seconds apart wrote 7,581, 11,905 and 16,235 — differences of
4,324 and 4,330, or 60.06 and 60.14 per second against a 60.15 Hz tick, and
the first at 7,581 ticks is 126 s after the machine booted, which is when the
run reached the menu. Two of the three files were **byte-identical** with
different names, so it is neither a checksum of the content nor a hash of the
name; every checksum tried over every fork and the whole file had already
failed on the published sets, and this is why. It is written once per SET,
which is exactly the property the joiner relies on: equal across a set's
parts, different between sets. Nothing changes in the reader — the identity
was already matched as 26 opaque bytes, and the other 22 are the fork
lengths, type, creator, flags and dates — but a field that is understood is
one nobody has to re-derive, and the three published sets' values now read as
11 minutes, 45 minutes and 42.6 minutes into somebody's afternoon.

**A `DDA2` stored entry with a resource fork is still unseen, and it is not
for want of an incompressible file.** "Create Archive…" on a 20,000-byte
pseudorandom file with a 344-byte resource fork produced an ordinary
`0xABCD0054` header — data fork **method 0, stored, 20,000 → 20,000**;
resource fork **method 9 (AD1), 344 → 148** — and not the headerless
`0x1000` record the corpus's nine archives use for their JPEG and PNG. So
whatever selects that form is not "the data does not compress": it is 4.1.1
rather than 4.1, or an archive built from seven files rather than one. The
specimen is a real addition either way — the first archive here with a stored
data fork beside a compressed resource fork, and this engine reads it.

**Every way this failed photographed like a coordinate error.** A missing
application greys the whole menu out behind a dialog; a `key("Return")` at the
desktop opens the selected icon's NAME for editing, so the next keystroke goes
into the name box and the run renames its own subject to "a"; a subject
smaller than the split size comes out in one piece. Three different causes,
one screenshot each that says "the menu did not respond". It is the same
shape `superdisk.py` already records in its own source — *a screenshot of the
right window at the wrong moment looks exactly like success* — and the
practical answer is the same too: **photograph the step after the one that
matters**, since the frame that shows the damage is never the frame of the
click that caused it. Here the volume listing did: `Rough` had become `a`.
The checklist that follows is in `tools/vmac/README.md`, where re-running
lives.

## 2026-08-21 (last) — "out of scope" and "we have not found one yet" are different files

OPEN_ITEMS.md split its Part 1 into two kinds of thing and called both
*decided*: boundaries somebody chose (compression, encrypted archives, Zip64)
