and formats nobody here has a specimen or a reference for (AppleLink `.pkg`,
StuffIt SpaceSaver, DiskDoubler methods 2-5, ARC 0x0a/0x0b, SuperDisk!
methods 10, 13 and 20). The second kind is not decided. It is exactly the
first kind's opposite: work this project WANTS, blocked on a file.

The evidence that this matters is in BLOCKERS.md and it cost a format.
**Alysis SuperDisk! sat in that section for the life of the project** on the
grounds that no reference existed — and the reference was inside the corpus's
own fixtures the whole time. A gap filed as a boundary stops being re-tested,
which is the one thing that would have found it. Two of the entries beside it
had already been disproved the same way (DiskDoubler methods 1 and 7).

So Part 1 now holds only what no evidence could change — the brief, and
properties of the formats themselves, like a uuencoded set that cannot detect
its own missing middle part — and Part 2 gains **"Waiting on a file this
machine does not have"**, which collects every such item across formats and
says for each whether what is missing is a REFERENCE (a spec, an
implementation, or a self-extracting specimen, which is one) or a GRADEABLE
SPECIMEN (a file something else wrote). Collecting them across formats is the
point: they are closed by one person noticing one file, and that person is
reading a list, not a format section. Both times it has happened here — the
SuperDisk! fixtures, the DiskDoubler distribution disk — the finding arrived
from outside the format it settled.

Two DiskDoubler items moved the other way at the same time, out of the
verification section and into this one, because they are the same kind of
thing: a method 7 fork longer than one chunk, and a `DDA2` stored entry with
a resource fork. Both are "nobody here has the file", filed next to "no
corpus fixture uses methods 2-5" instead of a page apart from it.

## 2026-08-21 (the Symantec release) — DiskDoubler's four publishers, and what the headerless record selects on

DiskDoubler had four owners, and the order matters because it dates the
methods. Checked online rather than remembered: Terry Morse and Lloyd
Chambers wrote it and formed **Salient Software** around it in 1990, which
sold it **standalone** through 3.7.x; Salient went to **Fifth Generation
Systems in 1992**, which is when it was FIRST bundled — as *SuperDoubler
4.0*, with AutoDoubler and CopyDoubler; Fifth Generation went to
**Symantec**, which shipped the same bundle as **Norton DiskDoubler Pro
1.1**, a fat binary, and then let it die. It was never part of Norton
Utilities. So the sequence is standalone first and bundled from 4.0 on,
and version 4 is where the bundling STARTS.

**The "1.1" is the disk, not the program.** `vers 2` on that floppy reads
*Norton DiskDoubler Pro™ Disk 1.1* and `vers 1` reads **4.1.1** — the exact
version OPEN_ITEMS.md had named as one of the two things left to try for the
`0x1000` record. A suite version and an application version were being read
as the same number.

`tools/vmac/ndd.py` runs both ends: 4.0 from Macintosh Garden and 4.1.1 from
the Symantec floppy.

### The floppy is a corpus, and the boot image is our own output

Every file on the Norton disk ships DiskDoubler-compressed — types `DDF0`
through `DDF3`, creator `DDAP` — so ten more real specimens arrived with the
application, exactly as 3.7's distribution disk carried the only two method 7
forks anybody here will ever see (2026-08-20). All ten are methods 0 and 10.

Two of them are worth more than a count:

- **`DiskTester™` 3.7.7 ships compressed on the Symantec floppy and
  UNCOMPRESSED inside the Fifth Generation 4.0 StuffIt archive.** Same build,
  two publishers, two distributions. Our decode of the compressed copy equals
  the uncompressed one to the byte outside the resource header's 112-byte
  system-reserved window, where 46 bytes differ because one copy carries
  DiskDoubler's `DDAppExpandTempFile` bookkeeping and the other the
  Installer's. That is a method 10 resource fork graded against a copy no
  DiskDoubler ever touched — and it is independent evidence for the masking
  convention FORMATS.md already uses on the 8 unadjudicated forks, which
  until now rested on the assertion that the Mac rewrites those bytes.
- **The System 7 boot image is built out of forks this engine decoded.** The
  Finder-menu extension is `INIT/DDI4` inside a method 10 container; nothing
  on that disk can be installed without decompressing it first. So a Mac that
  boots the extension and drives the application is a Mac executing our own
  output, and a wrong byte does not produce a warning — it produces a machine
  that will not start.

### The `0x1000` record: the claim was half a condition

M16 read the headerless `DDA2` record as *what DiskDoubler Pro writes for a
file it could not shrink*, and OPEN_ITEMS.md then spent two sessions looking
for one that had a resource fork — first 4.1 on a single incompressible
forked file, then 4.1.1 on seven at once. Both produced an ordinary
`0xABCD0054` header with the data fork stored. The reason is that the claim
was missing its other half, and **the corpus it was derived from says so**:
`ddtf-*.dd` and `ddtf-*.sea` hold **30** `0x1000` records, every one of them
a file with no resource fork, beside **45** ordinary headers whose data fork
is stored and which have one. `Test Text` is eleven bytes, stored, and gets a
full header, because it has a 332-byte resource fork.

Measured on the Mac rather than inferred, all four cells in one archive
(`ndd.py --stored`):

| data fork | resource fork | record |
|---|---|---|
| incompressible (40 B, 300 B, 20,000 B) | none | **`0x1000`** |
| incompressible (300 B, 20,000 B) | 335-338 B | `0xABCD0054`, data stored, fork AD1 |
| compressible | none | `0xABCD0054`, AD1 |
| compressible | 336 B | `0xABCD0054`, both AD1 |

**DiskDoubler Pro writes the headerless record exactly when the data fork is
stored AND the file has no resource fork.** Size has nothing to do with it —
40 bytes and 20,000 bytes both take the form.

Three consequences, and the first is the one that was costing something:

- **A `0x1000` record with a resource fork cannot be written.** It was filed
  under *waiting on a file this machine does not have*; there is no such file
  to wait for. The byte at `+87`, read as that fork's XOR checksum by
  position, has no fork to describe in any archive DiskDoubler Pro can
  produce. `+86` IS the data fork's checksum, and that is now measured rather
  than positional too: flipping one payload byte makes the CLI report
  *checksum mismatch* on that entry alone.
- **`+54` is the creation date and `+58` the modification.** Every specimen
  had carried the same value twice, so `dd_archive2()` assigned them by the
  order the reference reads them in a different branch — a guess with a
  citation. Giving two subjects a 1990 creation and a 1995 modification
  through the MacBinary header separates them: `+54` came back 1990 on both.
  The guess was right, which is worth exactly as much as knowing it.
- **`unar` is still wrong about all of it**, unchanged: XADMaster has no
  branch for the record and raises on the missing magic.

### Methods 2-5 are Salient-era, and that is now dated rather than guessed

The reason to run the LAST version was that it is the cheapest remaining
route to a method between 2 and 5. It is not there, and three independent
readings say so:

- 4.1.1's **Compress Using** submenu, photographed under Mini vMac: AD 1,
  AD 2, DD 1, DD 2, DD 3, and nothing else;
- `MENU 195`, `196` and `197` in both **4.0** and **4.1.1** carry that same
  list, read on the host — the trick BLOCKERS.md used on 3.2's `MENU 5019`,
  which costs no boot at all;
- **`STR# 403`**, the vocabulary the "Compressed Using" column draws on:
  `AD 1 | AD 2 | DD 1 | DD 2 | DD 3 | AIC | other | A | B | Sigma | S |
  none`. Twelve labels covering stored (`none`), method 1 (`A`), method 8
  (`B`), method 7 (`Sigma`), 6/9/10 and AutoDoubler's internal compressor —
  and **no name at all** for a method between 2 and 5, which fall through to
  `other`.

So no DiskDoubler from 3.2 to 4.1.1 writes one, and the last one has no word
for one either. With the ownership history above, that is a date rather than
a shrug: methods 2-5 belong to Salient's 1.x and 2.x, before the 1992 sale.
The gap stays exactly where it was — a specimen — but it is now bounded by
five releases checked instead of two.

One route it also closes: **DiskDoubler's own expander cannot read method 7
without the hardware.** `STR# 1` string 19 is *the file cannot be expanded
because it was compressed with Sigma hardware and no hardware is installed in
this Macintosh*. Running DD Expand under emulation was the obvious way to get
a second opinion on the two method 7 forks; it is not available, and this
engine decodes something the original application on that disk cannot.

### The lesson

The claim that cost two sessions was not unsupported — it was derived from
the corpus, from real records, and it was *half a condition*. Nothing about
it looked like a guess, and no smell in BLOCKERS.md points at one, because
the evidence was genuine and merely incomplete. What found it was reading the
corpus for the counter-example instead of for the example: **30 records that
fit the claim were beside 45 that contradict it, in the same fifteen files.**
So, next to "ask what evidence a tolerance rests on": **ask what a corpus
does NOT do that the claim would allow.** A rule inferred from the cases it
was drawn from will always fit them; the disproof is in the cases nobody
counted.

## 2026-08-21 (the Salient disk) — method 2, and the archive nobody here searched

Two links, handed over rather than found: `justsolve.archiveteam.org`'s
DiskDoubler page and **`winworldpc.com/product/diskdoubler/3x`**. The first
was serving a database error all afternoon and has still not been read. The
second is why methods 2-5 stopped being one item.

WinWorld's DiskDoubler page lists one release — **3.1.2, 1991, Salient**, an
800K floppy in a 7-zip, SHA-1 published beside it. That is **older than
anything this project had**, and OPEN_ITEMS.md had said in as many words that
"no COMPRESSOR older than 3.2 is reachable" (BLOCKERS.md).

### What was on it

The disk is the pattern this project already has a name for — a distribution
disk is a corpus — and it is the third time it has paid:

    tools/vmac/ndd.py --specimens      24 containers, 0 failed
    forks by method: 1 x1, 2 x1, 7 x22

- **A method 2 fork.** One, `Goodies/Programmers Only/XCMD ƒ/XCMD.h`, 564
  bytes into 386. The only one anybody here has seen.
- **Method 7 forks of up to EIGHT chunks**, 97,906 bytes. The two off the 3.7
  disk hold one chunk each, which is what made "a fork longer than one chunk
  has never been decoded" an open item: the fork header counts its chunks and
  gives each a 16-bit length, and this codec reads them as one continuous bit
  stream because that is what the reference does. Eight chunks decode, the
  fork's own XOR checksum agrees, and flipping any of three widely spaced
  bytes fails it. **The reading was right and is now measured**; the item is
  closed rather than narrowed.

### Method 2

An order-1 adaptive code, and small: 256 splay trees, the one in use chosen
by the byte just produced. Each starts as the complete binary tree over 256
leaves — internal node j has children 2j and 2j+1, node 256+b is the leaf for
byte b — so the first byte in a fresh context costs eight bits, and after
each byte its leaf is rotated toward the root. No table is transmitted.
Method 5 is the same codec with a leading tree-count byte; it stays declined,
because there is still no specimen and code no oracle can check is the
bargain LZH refuses.

Source: `XADDiskDoublerMethod2Handle.m` for the tree and the splay step,
`XADDiskDoublerParser.m` case 2 for the tree count, the XOR condition and the
checksum. The 0x5a XOR over DECODED bytes is method 1's, under the same
`info1 >= 0x2a`/`info2 & 0x80` condition — the specimen has `info1` exactly
0x2a — and unlike method 1 the sum needs no adjustment, because the stream
carries no header of its own to have been added into it.

**Graded three ways, which is what one specimen requires.** The fork's stored
16-bit sum matches (0xab41); a Python transcription written before any C
produces the identical 564 bytes; and the output is a legible 1990 Salient
copyright header for HyperCard XCMDs, so a decoder that were subtly wrong
would have to be wrong into valid C. Corrupting the stored sum — and
re-stamping the header's own XMODEM CRC, or the file stops being recognised
at all — turns it into a checksum mismatch, so the check is live.

**The memory is the number of distinct context bytes.** A tree is 1,536 bytes
and every tree is identical until used, so one is built only when its context
first occurs: the specimen touches 60 and measures 101,888 B. A fork using
all 256 would want ~392 KB and fail per entry on a 1 MB machine. This is
deliberately NOT the growth-on-demand shape that rescued Arsenic and hurt the
LZW table — nothing is copied and nothing is resized; a slot is either the
initial tree, which needs no storage, or an allocation. The trap that rule
warns about is porting a strategy; the property that made lazy allocation
right here is that the untouched state is *known*, which was true of neither.

### The lesson, and it is about searching

The project had searched Macintosh Garden, funet and Macintosh Repository —
Macintosh archives — and concluded that no DiskDoubler older than 3.2
survives anywhere reachable. WinWorld is a PC-centric abandonware library
that also carries Mac software, and it had the 1991 disk the whole time.

That is BLOCKERS.md's fourth smell again, worn differently: **a limitation
stated about the environment that is true only of one tool** — here, of the
three sites somebody happened to think of. The sharper version for next time:
when an open item says what is missing is A FILE, search for the file, not
for the story. Every search run this session before the links arrived was
about DiskDoubler's *history* — publishers, versions, bundles — and the
history is what dated methods 2-5 to the Salient era. It could not have found
a download, because nobody asks a search engine "who owned this company" and
gets a floppy image.

## 2026-08-21 (methods 3, 4 and 5) — writing DiskDoubler files so DiskDoubler can grade them

The last three DiskDoubler methods were declined on the grounds that
"code no oracle can check is the same bargain LZH refuses". Every word of
that was true except the premise: **an oracle had been sitting on the
1991 disk since the morning.** Salient's own `DiskDoubler™ App` and its
INIT come off that floppy uncompressed, run under Mini vMac, and expand
whatever they are given.

So the loop runs backwards from the usual one. Instead of decoding a
specimen nobody has, `tools/vmac/ddencode.py` **encodes** a fork, builds
a `0xABCD0054` container round it with a correct header CRC, and lets the
1991 application expand it. All three come back byte-exact. Method 5 is
confirmed twice, because DiskDoubler 4.1.1 expands it too.

This is the move `arcmac.py`, `nowcompress.py` and `diskdoubler.py
--make` already make — the difference is that those had a real file at
one end and this has none at either, so what it grades has to be stated
carefully:

- **It grades our reading of the FORMAT.** A wrong escape byte, a wrong
  tree serialization, a wrong bit order, a wrong checksum base or a wrong
  XOR gate all make the expander refuse or hand back different bytes.
  Those are the errors that are systematic, and they are the ones this
  catches.
- **It does not grade our decoder against encodings our encoder never
  emits.** A real fork may hold run lengths, tree shapes or contexts ours
  never produces. That is why these stay listed as having no specimen.

### Three things it measured that neither reference states

**The 0x5a XOR applies to methods 1, 2 and 5, and not to 3 and 4.** The
first run XORed all four; the expander handed back exactly
`SUBJECT ^ 0x5a` for methods 3 and 4 and the subject itself for 5. Since
a constant XOR of the whole output cannot come from a wrong codec, that
one run confirmed both codecs and located the difference at the same
time. The headers carried `info1 = 0x2a`, which is what **every** real
file on the 1991 disk carries — 24 of 24 — so this is not an
anachronistic test value. macutils agrees by construction: it computes
`dd_xor` from the same `info1`/`info2` rule and then only `dd_lzc` ever
applies it. **XADMaster's case 4 disagrees**, and is marked untested by
its own author. This project follows the implementation that shipped.

**DiskDoubler 4.1.1 has dropped methods 3 and 4.** Handed the same
files it answers *Unknown compression method. The file was probably
created by a more recent version of DiskDoubler* — about a 1990 format.
So the 1991 expander is not merely the most convenient oracle for those
two, it is the only one, and a session that reached for the newest
version would have concluded the files were malformed.

**Only a method 5 tree count of 0 is confirmed.** 0 means 256 and
expands byte-exact under both expanders; a count of 16 is rejected by
both with *Checksum failed*. Since encoder and decoder here share one
reading, the shared reading of the non-zero case is what is wrong — or
non-zero counts are not a thing. The branch stays, because it is
XADMaster's reading and its failure is a loud per-entry checksum error,
and it is named in OPEN_ITEMS.md as untested rather than left to look
tested.

### What the escape byte cost, and the shape of that mistake

macutils declares method 3's escape as `#define ESC 0x144`. That is 324;
`ch` is a byte; the comparison can never be true, so its own escape
branch is unreachable and its method 3 copies input to output. Read as
decimal **144** the algorithm is RLE90 exactly — escape 0x90, `ESC 0` a
literal escape, `ESC n` a run of n — which is the filter this engine
already runs for ARC. The derivation was labelled a hypothesis in the
source until the expander agreed; it did.

The transferable part is not the typo. It is that **a reference marked
untested can be untested in a way that makes it a no-op rather than
wrong**, which reads as "implemented" to anyone counting references.
BLOCKERS.md already had one instance — macutils' method 7 segfaults —
and this is the second in the same file.

### Why the first --expand run proved less than it looked

It reported one file expanded and three untouched, and the three were a
harness fault, not a format one: expanding a `TEXT` file launched
TeachText, and the next `Cmd-O` went to **TeachText's** Open dialog. The
fix is two lines — click the Finder window first, and give the subjects
a type nothing owns so the Finder puts up *the necessary application
could not be found* instead of launching anything. It is the shape
`superdisk.py` and `ddpro.py` both record: **the frame that shows the
damage is never the frame of the click that caused it**, and here three
"the expander did not take it" lines were really one stale front
application.

## 2026-08-22 — one byte, and the eight forks that had no oracle

Eight resource forks across two fixtures had been recorded as **not ground
truth** since M12: `unar` refuses them, macunpack's DiskDoubler method table
stops at 9 so it has no DDn at all, and the only other implementation is
DiskDoubler's own expander — which runs on a Mac, and a Mac restamps a
resource fork's system-reserved bytes 16..127 as it writes it. The item said
what would close it was "an implementation that runs off a Mac". It has been
sitting on this machine the whole time.

**`unar`'s DDn reads exactly one byte past the fork's declared compressed
length.** Declare one more and give it a pad byte and it decodes. Measured by
sweeping 0, 1, 2, 4, 8 … 64 bytes of slack: it flips at 1 and stays fixed.

The reproduction is 1,166 bytes — one `0xABCD0054` container, one file, two
DDn forks — and it is `tools/xadmaster-ddn-repro/`, written up ready to post
and **deliberately not posted**, on the same terms as the Retro68 report
(OPEN_ITEMS.md). This project has a rule about attributing a root cause
without a minimal reproduction, learned from the withdrawn `HOpen`
attribution; the rule is why the sweep exists rather than a sentence saying
"probably an off-by-one".

**That `unar`'s output is then RIGHT is what makes it an oracle.** With the
slack, all 33 forks of the two archives come back byte-for-byte equal to this
engine's output — including the 16..127 window no Mac run can preserve — and
equal to what DiskDoubler's own expander produced under System 7 outside that
window. Three implementations, one answer.

So the workaround IS the adjudication. `tools/run_ddforks.py` hands `unar`
one fork at a time in a container carrying **the fixture's own compressed
bytes, unmodified**, with two changes and no others: the declared compressed
length is one larger, and one pad byte follows. The pad is shown to be inert
rather than assumed — every fork is decoded twice, with 0x00 and with 0xFF,
and both must agree before anything is recorded.

Result: `oracle-unadjudicated` falls from **24 forks across 12 fixtures to 16
across 10**, and both DiskDoubler fixtures leave the list entirely. The eight
hashes are in the manifests with that provenance and `ci.sh` checks them on
every run.

### Two things worth keeping

**A tool's bug is not always a wall.** The reasoning that filed this item was:
the oracle fails, therefore there is no oracle. What was missing is that a
decoder which fails on its input bound still has a correct codec behind it,
and the bound is ours to set — we build the container we hand it. The general
form, next to "an oracle you can only feed is still an oracle" from the
methods 3-5 work: **an oracle that refuses one input may accept a
presentation of the same bytes.** Neither of those changes what the tool is;
both change what you ask it.

**The pairing check earned its place immediately.** `run_ddforks.py --write`
matches a manifest entry to an archive header before recording a hash, and
the first version matched them by position. That is right for one fixture and
wrong for the other — the manifest is ordered by name, the headers by
position in the archive — and the guard caught it on the second file rather
than writing one fork's hash under another fork's name, which `ci.sh` would
then have enforced forever. Matching is now by the data fork's own hash, and
a header that does not pair uniquely is an error rather than a guess.

## 2026-08-22 (later) — SuperDisk!'s compressor, which was said not to survive

OPEN_ITEMS.md said methods 10, 13 and 20 could not be graded because
"SuperDisk!'s compressor does not survive here, only its expander".
Macintosh Repository has it: the 1991 **` SuperDisk!` control panel**
(`cdev`/`AlyS`, an 89 KB resource fork), which is the half that
compresses. `tools/vmac/sdcompress.py` fetches it, installs it into
`System Folder:Control Panels` and drives it.

**It runs.** The panel opens under System 7 with a "Make File" button,
three 3-D icon buttons (Auto X, Alerts, Security) and a paged settings
area whose Compress page offers **Off / Light / Tighter** beside Speed
and Memory axes. Its own text: *"SuperDisk!'s default setting is
'Faster' compression."* Those axes are the reason to want it — the
method table read out of Disk Expander calls 10, 11 and 13
**SlowFastA, SlowFastB and SlowFastC**, and this engine implements 11.
A speed control over a Slow/Fast family is where the other two live.

**"Make File" compresses.** It puts up a standard file dialog; pointed at
an 11,136-byte subject it wrote `Alpha.x`, an `APPL/AlYS` of 2,372 bytes
data and a 5,730-byte resource fork — the `AutX` auto-expand stub the
control panel carries. So specimens are now producible on demand, which
is what the open item was waiting for.

### What it produced is not the container this engine reads

The corpus's two archives open with eight bytes nobody reads and then a
record stream (`0x29` folder, `0x28` file). `Alpha.x` opens

    0000 2b80  0000 2b80  0000 0000  'TEXT' 'ttxt' … 05 "Alpha" …

— the uncompressed size twice, then type and creator, then the name as a
Pascal string. No `0x29`, no `0x28`. **It is SuperDisk!'s single-file
form**, the "auto-expanding file" its help strings talk about, and it
stands in the same relation to the archive format as DiskDoubler's
`0xABCD0054` does to `DDAR`. This project has never had one; `unc_sniff`
answers *unknown* and forcing the format gives *archive structure is
corrupt*, which is the honest answer to a container nobody has read.

So the item that was "waiting on a file" is now waiting on a layout, and
the file arrives whenever it is wanted.

### Two traps, both photographed

**The boot image accumulates the panel's state.** Mini vMac writes back,
and this control panel saves its settings *and its current page* into the
System Folder. A second run therefore opens where the first left off, so
a coordinate measured on page 1 lands on page 2's buttons — one run
clicked "Off", turning compression off, and photographed a settings page
while the harness believed it had opened a file dialog. `session()` now
rebuilds the boot image every time. This is the same shape `ddpro.py` and
`superdisk.py` already record, with a new cause: not a mistimed click but
a machine that remembers.

**The extension is the result, not the trigger.** A summary of the format
elsewhere says SuperDisk! compresses "by simply adding an extension to
the filename", and renaming `Alpha` to `Alpha.x` under a loaded
SuperDisk! changes nothing at all — measured, the bytes come back
identical. `.x` is what the compressor NAMES its output. An hour went
into driving the Finder's rename before the control panel was opened.

## 2026-08-22 (later still) — SuperDisk!'s other container, derived by making it

The container `sdcompress.py` found and could not read is now read. It is
SuperDisk!'s **single-file** form — what "Make File" writes, and what the
format's own help calls an auto-expanding file — and it stands to the
archive exactly as DiskDoubler's `0xABCD0054` does to `DDAR`:

    +0  (4) total uncompressed size, and it EQUALS +4 plus +8
    +4  (4) data fork size          +8  (4) resource fork size
    +12 (4) type                    +16 (4) creator
    +20 (2) Finder flags
    +22 (4) creation date           +26 (4) modification date
    +30     the name, Pascal, padded to an even total length
            then the data fork's blocks, then the resource fork's

**The payload is the block chain this engine already decodes.** That was
the hypothesis worth testing first and it cost nothing to test: the first
specimen's bytes at the right offset read as `clen=2336, ulen=11136,
method=1`, which is `sd_block_head`'s layout exactly. So the work was a
35-byte header and a fork split, not a codec.

### The layout was derived by choosing subjects that disagree

The first specimen made the layout look obvious and would have got it
wrong: an 11,136-byte file with no resource fork, whose `+0` and `+4`
were therefore the same number and whose `+8` was zero. Five subjects
chosen to separate the fields settled it in one session
(`sdcompress.py --specimens`):

| subject | what it pinned |
|---|---|
| `Forked`, a resource fork | `+0` is the TOTAL, `+4` data, `+8` resource — 3338 = 3000 + 338 |
| `Dated`, stamped 1990 and 1995 | `+22` creation, `+26` modification, both exact |
| `LongerNameHere 12345`, 20 chars | the Pascal name, and that the body is padded to an EVEN offset |
| `Big`, 44,544 bytes | more than one block in the chain |
| `Alpha`, the baseline | — |

The even-padding rule is the one that would have been missed: three of
the five names happen to make `31 + nlen` even on their own, and only the
20-character one puts a junk byte (0xAA, not zero) where a reader that
did not pad would look for a block header.

**Graded by the bytes that went in**, the loop this project uses
everywhere: all five come back byte-exact, resource fork included and the
multi-block one included (`sdcompress.py --verify`). Dates, type, creator
and Finder flags match what was stamped.

### What is in `ci.sh` and what is not

The specimens were written by a commercial application under emulation,
so they cannot be regenerated in CI the way `ddencode.py --emit` files
can — there is no encoder for this format and there will not be one. What
`ci.sh` gets instead is a hand-built container using STORED blocks, which
covers the header, the fork split, the metadata and the sniffer without a
codec; the five real specimens cover the codec, on demand.

The sniffer leans on `total == data + rsrc`. That is a 32-bit identity
random data fails, and it is also what tells this container from an
archive — whose first eight bytes nobody reads, so they could be
anything. The unit test breaks the identity by one and requires the
sniffer to let go of the file.

## 2026-08-22 (last) — the settings sweep, and the decoder that had never run

`sdcompress.py --sweep` compresses the SAME bytes once per control-panel
setting, so the only thing differing between the outputs is the setting.
Six settings, and the Speed axis moves the method:

| setting | method | 10,376 bytes becomes |
|---|---|---|
| Speed = Fast | **6** | 884 |
| Speed = Faster (the default) | 1 | 2,280 |
| Speed = Fastest | 1 | 2,282 |
| Speed = Fast, Compress = Off | **6** | 888 |
| Speed = Fast, Compress = Tight | 1 | 2,284 |
| Speed = Fast, Compress = Tighter | **6** | 892 |

Methods 10, 13 and 20 are not among them: everything this compressor can
be made to write is 1 or 6. The Compress axis does not move the method
cleanly — "Fast + Tight" gave 1 where "Fast" alone gave 6 — which is
either a second axis interacting or the harness not making both settings
stick, and it is not worth resolving until there is a reason to think a
setting reaches the SlowFast family at all.

### Method 6 had never decoded anything, and did not work

OPEN_ITEMS.md said so in as many words: methods 1-6, 8 and 9 were
"transcribed and compiled and nothing more", because both corpus archives
use one method each (7 and 11). The first method 6 block this project has
ever seen failed with *file is truncated*.

**The bug:** the flag bitmap is one bit per output byte, so
`ceil(ulen/8)` bytes — 1,297 for a 10,376-byte block. This engine asked
the Huffman decoder for `((ulen >> 3) + 4) & ~1` = 1,300 and ran off the
end of the stream three bytes early. That `+4` and round-up are real, but
they belong to the STORED bitmap, where they are slack the coded form
does not carry; methods 1 and 3 take that branch and are unaffected,
which is why method 7 — the one the corpus exercises — never showed it.

Fixed, and the block decodes byte-exact. All eleven specimens the control
panel has written now do: five that derived the container's header and
six from the sweep, methods 1 and 6.

### What this says about the untested list

The §5 entry naming these methods as never having decoded anything was
accurate, and it is the reason the failure was legible in seconds rather
than being read as a bad specimen. **A list of code that has never run is
worth keeping precisely because it predicts where the next bug is** — one
of the two methods it named failed on first contact with real data, and
the other passed.

It also says something about how the bug survived: the two branches of
one function were sized by the same expression because the stored one is
the one the corpus reaches, and nothing else ever asked. A constant
shared between a tested path and an untested path is a place to look.

## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver) — reachable, not yet runnable, and a licence question

StuffIt SpaceSaver is one of two formats this project cannot NAME: no
reference, no published layout, no specimen, so `unc_sniff` answers
*unknown*. OPEN_ITEMS.md has carried it since that section existed, and
the reason recorded was that nobody had a specimen or the application.

**The application is reachable.** SpaceSaver ships inside StuffIt Deluxe,
and Macintosh Garden has every era of it. Three distributions were
opened here:

- **3.5.1** and **4.0** — two-floppy InstallerMaker sets, payload inside
  the installer; `unar` unwraps the MacBinary and stops at the
  application, as expected.
- **5.0.1's CD-ROM** (a 224 MB Apple-partitioned ISO that `hmount` opens
  directly) — which carries a **standalone `SpaceSaver 5.0 Installer`**
  beside the Deluxe one, and a `SpaceSaver User's Guide.pdf`.

`tools/vmac/spacesaver.py` fetches the CD, lifts that installer off it
and drives it.

**It does not run on System 7.0.1, and that is measured rather than
assumed.** The installer launches and shows its splash; past the licence
it puts up two alerts in turn — *requires system software version 7.5.3,
which this system does not have* and *requires 8000K of physical memory,
which this system does not have* — both defaulting to No. Answer Yes to
both and it bombs: **"Sorry, a system error occurred. 'SpaceSaver™ 5.0
Installer' unimplemented trap."** So the version check is honest; 7.0.1
does not have the traps it calls.

Two routes remain, and neither is blocked on finding a file:

- a **System 7.5.3 image** for Mini vMac, which is a new system image
  beside `System6.hfv` and `Sys7.img`; or
- **SpaceSaver from the 3.5.1 or 4.0 set**, which is the era 7.0.1 fits.
  Both disks can be mounted at once, so the floppy swap an installer
  would ask for need not happen.

### The licence, which is the maintainer's call and not this session's

The panel that has to be agreed to says, in terms: *"You may not modify,
adapt, translate, reverse engineer, decompile, or disassemble the
Software."*

That matters because of how this project has read two other formats.
SuperDisk! was derived by disassembling `CODE 2` out of a
self-extractor, and FORMATS.md says so; DiskDoubler's methods were
transcribed from references. **For SpaceSaver, the disassembly route is
what the licence names.**

The other route is untouched by it and is the one that has actually been
working: **black-box derivation.** SuperDisk!'s single-file container was
derived on 2026-08-22 from five specimens chosen to disagree with each
other, without reading a byte of Alysis's code — compress known input,
compare outputs, deduce. Running a program and examining files it wrote
is not reverse engineering the program.

So the position taken here was, pending the maintainer: **specimens yes,
disassembly no.**

**Answered 2026-08-22: the maintainer waived it and took responsibility**,
so the disassembly route is open for SpaceSaver as it was for SuperDisk!.
This entry stays because the reasoning is still the reasoning — and
because one thing was NOT waived and was not attempted: see below.


## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, later) — both installers, and the wall that is not a licence clause

With the licence question answered, both routes to a SpaceSaver specimen
were run to the end. Both stop, for different reasons, and both are now
measured rather than guessed.

**SpaceSaver 5.0**, the standalone installer off the Deluxe 5.0.1 CD:
needs System 7.5.3 and 8000K. Forced past both of its own "Install
anyway?" alerts on 7.0.1 it bombs — *unimplemented trap*. It never asked
for a registration number, but it never got that far either.

**StuffIt Deluxe 3.5.1**, the era 7.0.1 actually fits: its installer runs
perfectly. Splash, read-me, then a registration panel wanting a name, an
organization and a **registration number**. A name alone is not enough —
with the number blank and the name filled the panel simply does not
advance, and pressing Register twice produces the reason: **"The
registration number that you have entered is invalid."**

**That wall was not climbed, and the distinction matters.** What the
maintainer waived is the licence's reverse-engineering clause — reading a
format out of software. A registration number is a licence ENFORCEMENT
mechanism, and looking one up or deriving one is a different act from
disassembling a decoder. Nothing here went looking for a serial. If that
is also wanted it is a separate decision, and it should be an explicit
one rather than something a session drifts into because it was already
in the neighbourhood.

**What is left, and it is clean:** a **System 7.5.3 image** beside
`System6.hfv` and `Sys7.img`. The 5.0 installer's only objections were
the system version and the memory, and 7.5.3 was released free by Apple;
the emulator already runs three systems, so a fourth is plumbing rather
than research. That route needs no serial and no waiver.

### What was learned about driving this installer, which transfers

Every panel in both Aladdin installers **swallows the first input it is
given**. A click on a button and a Return both leave the panel on screen;
the second one acts. Three runs were spent reading that as "the
coordinate is wrong" — and the coordinates were measured off the frames
and correct. The tell is that the frame after the input looks *identical*
to the frame before it, where a genuine miss usually shows something
else: a selected icon, a different window in front.

The other trap is order. The 3.5.1 flow is splash → **read-me** →
registration, and a walk that assumed splash → registration typed a name
into the read-me and then clicked Register on an empty form, which
produced *"You must enter a name in order to register this product"* —
an error that describes the harness perfectly and the installer not at
all.


## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, last) — what the registration wall actually costs

The maintainer pointed at Macintosh Garden's StuffIt Deluxe page, which
publishes registration codes for these discontinued versions. Using a
published code for 30-year-old abandonware, in an emulator, to read a
file format is a different act from defeating the check by
disassembling it, and it is the one that was taken.

**It does not open this door.** The codes the page gives are for **3.0.7**
("S" and five digits) and for **5.0/5.0.1** (`DLXF-…`, `DLXG-…`). Both
were tried against both installers this machine can run, four codes per
boot with the field cleared between attempts:

| installer | result |
|---|---|
| Deluxe 3.5.1 | every PAGE code *"The registration number that you have entered is invalid."* — but a 3.5-specific one the maintainer supplied (`DLXC-963-029519`) is accepted |
| Deluxe 4.0 | every code rejected |

The versions the codes are for are exactly the versions that cannot run
here: **5.0 needs System 7.5.3 and 8000K**, and Mini vMac's Mac Plus tops
out at **4 MB**, so a 7.5.3 image alone would still fail the memory check.

**So the route is now specific, and it is infrastructure rather than
research:** a Mini vMac variant emulating a Mac II-class machine with 8 MB
or more, plus a System 7.5.3 image, and then the published 5.x code. The
emulator is already built from source by `setup.sh` with variant flags,
so the machine is a build option; the system image is a download beside
the three already fetched.

Two harness traps, both of which produced frames that lie:

- **A disk that does not open its own window.** 3.5.1's floppy opens on
  boot and 4.0's does not, so the same installer click landed on bare
  desktop and every frame after it showed an empty Finder — which reads
  as "the installer quit" and means "the installer was never opened".
- **Cmd-A does not select the text in a System 7 dialog field.** A run
  meant to try four codes typed all four into the field end to end
  (`S12345DLXF-085DLXF-0835-001225DLX…`) and tested none of them, while
  reporting four attempts. Backspacing the field empty is what works.


## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, installed) — it runs, and it will not compress on demand

With a 3.5-specific registration code, StuffIt Deluxe 3.5.1's installer
completes: *"Installation was successful."* The image now carries
`~StuffIt SpaceSaver™` (`cdev/SMKR`, a 289 KB resource fork),
`~Magic Menu™` and the `StuffIt Engine™` extension, and it boots.

**The installed image had to be lifted out of `/tmp`.** `run.py` boots
from a COPY so every run starts pristine — right for replaying fixtures,
wrong for an installer, whose entire output IS the change to that image.
Checking the original boot image after the install shows nothing and
reads as "the installer wrote nothing"; the session's copy, which
survives in its temp directory, has everything.
`spacesaver.py --keep-installed` lifts it.

**What is not working yet.** Magic Menu carries a **SpaceSaver Compress**
command and SpaceSaver's own control panel shows it switched **On**.
Selecting a file and choosing that command leaves the file
**byte-identical** — verified by reading the volume back on the host, not
by looking at the Finder, which would show the logical size either way.
Tried on a second volume and on the startup disk itself: unchanged both
times.

**The next thing to try, and the reason:** the control panel's four
buttons are *Idle Time*, *Compression & Other*, *Don't Compress* and
*About*. SpaceSaver is a background compressor — it waits for the machine
to be idle. A harness that clicks a menu and screenshots 35 seconds later
never idles at all, and "Idle Time" is where the delay lives. Set it to
its minimum and leave the machine alone for longer than that.

Everything needed for that is in place: the installed image is kept, the
Magic Menu coordinates are measured, and `--make-boot` already puts
subjects on the startup disk and reads them back out of the session copy.


## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, the trigger) — a name rule, and why five attempts missed it

SpaceSaver is installed and running, and five separate attempts left the
subjects byte-identical. The reason is on the control panel's second
page, **Compression and Other Settings**:

> *Immediately compress files, folders, and disks whose:*
> ☒ *Name begins or ends with:* **`small`**

**That is the trigger** — a name rule, not a command. It also explains
every failure in order, and each of those failures had looked like its
own separate problem:

| tried | why it did nothing |
|---|---|
| Magic Menu's *SpaceSaver Compress* on a second volume | the file was never named to match |
| the same on the startup disk | same |
| enrolling the startup disk in *Idle Time* | that list governs the IDLE sweep, a different mechanism |
| 1990 modification dates, to pass *"not modified in the last 1 Months"* | same |
| eighteen minutes of genuine idling | same |

The enrolment and the dates were not wasted — both are needed for the
idle sweep and both are now known-good, photographed. But the on-demand
path was never the Magic Menu at all.

The mirror of this is worth keeping: **SuperDisk!'s `.x` is the RESULT of
compressing and SpaceSaver's `small` is the CAUSE.** Two Macintosh
compressors of the same era, both keyed on the file's name, in opposite
directions — and this project has now guessed wrong about which way round
each of them works.

**What is left is harness, not format.** The subjects have to be renamed
in the Finder so their names end in `small`, and the rename is not
taking: the installed system opens its "StuffIt Deluxe Folder" window at
startup and keeps it in FRONT of the volume window, so a type-select goes
to the wrong window. Raising the volume window by its title bar did not
fix it either. The frames after each attempt show the icons still called
`Alpha` and `Forked`, which is the check that matters — the file names
are read back off the volume on the host, not judged from a screenshot.

Two cheaper routes than fighting the Finder, for whoever picks this up:

- **Change the rule instead of the file.** The page's text field holds
  `small`; typing `Alpha` into it makes the existing file match with no
  rename at all.
- **Name the subject correctly before it is ever copied in.** Whether
  SpaceSaver acts on a file that simply appears with a matching name, or
  only on a name CHANGE, is itself unmeasured — and one boot answers it.


## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, where it rests) — two things measured, one thing stuck

Continuing the trigger work, two more results, both negative and both
worth having because they close off guesses:

**A matching name merely APPEARING does not fire it.** Subjects named
`Alphasmall` and `smallForked` were copied onto the startup disk before
boot, on the image where enrolling that disk in Idle Time actually stuck,
with 1990 dates, and left for fourteen minutes. All three subjects
including the deliberately non-matching `Control` came back
byte-identical. So SpaceSaver watches for a name CHANGE, not for a name.

**The Finder rename can be driven almost to the end.** After closing the
"StuffIt Deluxe Folder" window the installed system opens at startup —
which requires clicking its TITLE BAR first, because a click on empty
desktop DEACTIVATES the front window and Cmd-W then has nothing to close
— type-select selects the subject and the name box takes the text. A
frame shows the icon reading **`Alphasmall`**, boxed and editing. What
does not happen is the commit: one Return leaves it editing, two Returns
leave the disk image still holding `Alpha`.

So either the commit keystroke is not reaching the name box, or the
Finder's directory update is not reaching the image the way the
installer's writes did. Both are testable; neither was tested, because
each attempt is a twenty-minute boot and six of them in a row produced
the same three lines.

**Judgement, recorded because it is the part worth transferring:** this
was said to be the last run four separate times and was not. The
diagnosis genuinely sharpened each time — wrong window, deactivated
window, uncommitted name box — which is exactly the shape that keeps a
session going one run past useful. The state is precise and written down;
the next person should start from it rather than from another guess.

What is NOT the blocker, all now measured: the software installs and
runs, the registration is accepted, the disk enrols, the dates qualify,
the panel says On, and the trigger is understood.


## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, calling it) — the rename commits, the image does not see it

Two more results, and then a change of approach that should have come
sooner.

**The rename commits.** A frame shows both subjects renamed on screen —
`Alphasmall` and `Forkedsmall`, no edit box, the Finder settled — while
the disk image still holds `Alpha` and `Forked`. So every earlier reading
of this ("the keystroke is not arriving", "the wrong window is in front")
was wrong at the end: the Finder does the rename and **the change never
reaches the image**. HFS directory updates sit in the cache; the boot
volume is never unmounted, the emulator is killed instead, and what the
Finder had not written goes with it. The installer's writes survived
because the system ran on for minutes afterwards.

Moving the subjects to a second volume so `Cmd-Y` could flush it was the
right idea and produced nothing usable: **that run never booted.** Its
first frame is already the striped garbage screen, so its "unchanged"
result says nothing about anything.

**Calling it.** Ten runs, twenty minutes each, and the last two added a
non-deterministic infrastructure failure on top of an unconverged
problem. More Finder driving is not the way to a specimen.

### The approach that should be tried instead

**Read the format out of the software.** `~StuffIt SpaceSaver™` is
`cdev/SMKR` with a 289 KB resource fork, and the decompressor is in it.
That is exactly the route SuperDisk! took — `CODE 2` out of a
self-extractor, four documents' worth of format derived from it — and it
has three advantages here that matter:

- it needs **no working emulator interaction at all**, so it costs host
  minutes rather than twenty-minute boots;
- it is **repeatable**, where every run above depended on a click landing
  and a cache flushing;
- the licence clause that would have forbidden it was **waived by the
  maintainer** on 2026-08-22.

The specimen is still wanted eventually — a decoder wants something to be
wrong against — but the layout can be had first, and a layout makes the
format nameable, which is the thing OPEN_ITEMS.md has been asking for
since that section existed.

## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, the layout) — read out of the software, as recommended

The previous entry recommended reading the container out of `cdev/SMKR`
instead of driving the Finder for an eleventh run. That is what this is,
and it cost host minutes: **`tools/vmac/spacesaver.py --layout`, no
emulator, repeatable.**

**The layout is not in the control panel.** `~StuffIt SpaceSaver™`'s
`ACMP 1` only sniffs — it reads eight bytes and compares two magics. The
writer is `SSEn 1` in `StuffIt Engine™`, the resource beside it on the
same image, and **it ships its MacsBug symbols**: 133 of them, including
`ReadSSHeader`, `WriteSSHeader`, `DefaultSSHeader`, `UpdateSSHeader`,
`CompressFork`, `ExpandFork`, `IsFileCmpressed` and `MakeDFile`. A
symbol sits after its routine's RTS, so the table is also the routine
boundaries. **Naming the file that carries the format is a step of its
own** — the panel was the obvious place and had the smaller half.

The container, the record and the module table are printed by `--layout`
and tabulated in FORMATS.md; what follows is only what the reading turned
on.

### Four things the bytes say that a specimen would not have shown quickly

- **The two fork records are fixed, the two streams are not.** Record 1
  is always the data fork and record 2 always the resource fork —
  `mExpandFile` writes them to those two refnums. But `mCompressFile`
  compares the two lengths and gives offset 84, the byte after the
  header, to the LARGER fork; the other follows it. A decoder that
  assumes record order is stream order is right about half the time, and
  a one-fork specimen can never catch it.
- **A compressed file is recognised by its LENGTH.** `IsFileCmpressed`
  refuses to open anything whose data fork length has a non-zero low
  byte, unless the type is `SPSV`, and `mCompressFile` ends by rounding
  the EOF up to a multiple of 256. The magic is the confirmation, not
  the screen. That is a cheap test for a system extension that sees
  every file the Finder touches — and it means `unc_sniff` must read the
  magic anyway, because "multiple of 256" is not evidence of anything.
- **The compressor byte is an index, not a resource id.** `CheckCompressor`
  takes the high byte of the module's own word at +0x20 as the index and
  the low byte as a revision, keeps the higher revision, and gates on a
  CPU mask at +0x24 — bit 31 minus Gestalt `cput`, with PowerPC's
  0x0101.. mapped down to 8. So `Alg 3` (68030 and up), its
  decompress-only twin (everything) and its PowerPC build all claim
  index 3, and a file says 3 whichever wrote it. **One number in a file,
  three implementations, chosen by the machine reading it.**
- **The default method is a CPU decision, and it is inverted from the
  intuition.** 68000/68010/68020 get index 4, 68030 and up get index 3;
  the masks agree (`Alg 4` is `f0000000`, `Alg 3` is `0fff0000`). The
  slow machines get the cheaper algorithm, and the corresponding
  decompress-only modules are `ffff0000`, so any Mac can read any file.

### What is still missing, and what it is not

Not the container: that is done. What is missing is the two bitstreams —
index 3 and index 4 — which are `CMod 11` (7,536 bytes) and `CMod 21`
(2,202 bytes), the decompress-only modules, sitting in the same resource
fork this was read out of. Each is called at its module+0x10 vector with
a parameter block of seven callbacks; the engine adds no framing of its
own, so a fork's stream is exactly what the module wrote.

**That is the SuperDisk! bargain again** (SuperDisk!, read out of
SuperDisk!): the reference is in hand, so the remaining work is
transcription and grading — and grading still wants a specimen, which
`--make` has not produced. The difference from a week ago is that the
format is now nameable without one.

## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, the codecs) — two of five, and a dictionary that is not ours

The layout entry above ends by saying what was left: the bitstreams, and a
specimen to grade them against. This is the bitstreams.

### The module interface, which is the same for all five

`ExpandFork` @0x5ee6 builds one parameter block and calls the module at
its own +0x10 vector. The block is seven callback pointers — read,
write, get/set source position, source EOF, progress, debug — then the
refcon they all take, the compressed and uncompressed lengths, a
pointer and a length, and the work buffer and its size. **The module
adds no framing of its own**, so a fork's compressed stream is exactly
what the module wrote, at the offset its record gives.

The wrappers at the head of every `CMod` (@0x2e, @0x4a, ...) name those
fields precisely: each one calls `pb->[n]` with `pb->[28]` as the first
argument, so the block's shape can be read off the module rather than
guessed from the caller.

### Method 0 is stored, and says so twice

`CMod 1` is 374 bytes: its compressor sets the compressed length equal
to the original and calls `XferData` @0xee, a read/write loop; its
expander calls the same routine. Nothing else. A stored fork therefore
has `clen == ulen`, which the reader checks.

### Method 4 is an LZSS, and the shape is in FORMATS.md

What is worth keeping here is not the layout but two things the code
says that a specimen would not have shown quickly:

- **A block's length counts its own header.** @0x4e8 subtracts `clen`
  from a byte count that started at the block header, so the six bytes
  are inside it. Reading `clen` as a payload length works for the first
  block of a one-block fork and desynchronises on the second.
- **The window is not reset per block.** @0x438 compacts it and keeps
  going, so a match in block five may reach into block four. A decoder
  that starts each block clean decodes the first one perfectly.

### The finding that changes what an extractor can promise

**SpaceSaver's window starts loaded with a preset dictionary, and the
dictionary is Aladdin's.** @0x280 copies `pb->[44]` for `pb->[48]`
bytes into the window immediately BEFORE the first output byte. Those
two fields come from the engine: `ExpandFork` passes the master pointer
of a `PrAm` resource and — through the glue at @0x3e4, which is
`GetHandleSize` writing into an argument slot — its size. The resource
is chosen by the fork record's byte at +12, which is 0 in everything
this engine writes, and `LoadCompressors` @0x10c8 loads index 0 from
`'PrAm'` **-4048**: 9,679 bytes whose first line is
*"/\* Copyright (c) 1993 Aladdin Systems, Inc. This source code and
specific concepts contained herein are confidential information and
property of Aladdin Systems"*, followed by 68k mnemonics, C fragments
and Mac boilerplate. It is a corpus of representative Macintosh file
content — a preset dictionary in the zlib sense, and the giveaway that
it is one is that the copyright notice is IN the compressible data.

Three consequences, and they are the reason this is written up at
length:

- **A decoder cannot be correct without those bytes.** Every fork's
  first matches reach into them.
- **This project cannot ship them.** Same rule as the fixtures and the
  ROM. So `UncOptions.preset` exists, `macextract --preset FILE` fills
  it from the user's own copy of SpaceSaver, and
  `spacesaver.py --layout` writes the resource out beside the emulator's
  other state.
- **Absence has to be a failure, not a silence.** A window of zeroes
  decodes to plausible-looking wrong bytes. The reader tracks how far
  back the history actually reaches and refuses a distance beyond it,
  which is a per-entry error; `run_spacesaver.py` re-runs every
  specimen that passed WITHOUT the dictionary and requires it to fail.

### Why 1, 2 and 3 are declined, and what the module table settles

Each `CMod` carries a CPU mask at +0x24, and the masks say which
machine can write which method: `Alg 4` is `f0000000` (68000 through
68020), `Alg 3` is `0fff0000` (68030 and up, plus PowerPC), and the
decompress-only builds are `ffff0000` — anybody can read anything. The
engine's default follows the same split. So the only method this
machine's Mac Plus can be made to write is 4, and 1, 2 and 3 are
declined per entry for want of an oracle, exactly as LZH and
DiskDoubler's own declined methods are. Their modules are in hand; it
is the grading that is missing, and that has been the bargain since M14.

## 2026-08-22 (SpaceSaver, what will not compress) — twelve runs, one thing that fired

The format is implemented; what is still missing is a file SpaceSaver
wrote. Twelve runs went after one, every one of them on a machine that
booted and was **flushed** — Special ▸ Shut Down, the step whose absence
invalidated the three earlier runs (BLOCKERS.md). What they establish:

| driven | result |
|---|---|
| rename to end in `small`, the panel's compression rule | nothing |
| the same, with the startup disk enrolled and 18 minutes idle | nothing |
| Magic Menu ▸ *SpaceSaver Compress* on the selected file | nothing |
| the same, disk enrolled first | the selection was wrong — void |
| rename to add `.SIT`, the panel's OTHER rename rule | **a 228-byte StuffIt archive** |
| `DropCompress™` launched with no document | no window at all, then it quits |
| a subject dragged onto `DropCompress™` | the app highlights, nothing happens |
| `~Magic Menu™` deleted, to get SpaceSaver's own menu | **no icon menu in the menu bar at all** |
| the Compression page OK'd first, then the rename | nothing |
| the preferences written, the machine REBOOTED, then the rename | nothing |
| the installer re-run and shut down cleanly, so nothing is torn | the image has no preferences either — the installer does not write them |
| `StuffIt Deluxe™` itself, menus photographed | no SpaceSaver command in either of its menus |

**The `.SIT` result is the one that matters**, because it disproves the
explanation everything else was resting on. The trigger page has two
rename rules ticked; renaming `Forked` to `Forked.SIT` replaced it with
a StuffIt archive and deleted the original. So a rename event reaches an
extension that acts on it, and that extension's output survives to the
image — the harness does deliver a trigger, and a compressed file, when
one is written, comes back. **What does not happen is SpaceSaver
compression**, and the run below narrows why: the `.SIT` rule is Magic
Menu's, and Magic Menu is the only one of the two extensions this
machine has ever seen do anything.

### Why this is written down rather than left as "still trying"

Every earlier round of this ended with a hypothesis about the harness —
the keystroke did not arrive, the wrong window was in front, the image
was not flushed — and each was true once and then repaired. The
remaining candidates are about the software, and they are worth
recording because the next attempt should start from them rather than
from the Finder again:

- **the compression rule may not be a rename hook at all.** Its wording
  is a property ("files … whose Name begins or ends with"), unlike the
  suffix rule's event ("when its name is changed"). A file that already
  matches has never been tested on a machine that booted — the run that
  claimed to has been withdrawn;
- **`Idle Now`.** `STR# 1024` is SpaceSaver's own menu — *Turn Idle-Time
  On*, **Idle Now**, *Open Control Panel* — and the menu bar carries no
  such menu while Magic Menu is installed. Removing `~Magic Menu™` from
  the Control Panels folder before booting is one `hdel`, and *Idle Now*
  is the only explicit "do it now" this software offers;
- **the registration state.** 3.5.1 was installed with a code that the
  panel accepted, and nothing has checked what *About SpaceSaver* says
  about it.

**The preferences file was the best hypothesis and it is disproved.**
`ssinstalled.hfv` has no `System Folder:Preferences:SpaceSaver
Preferences` — the installer does not write one, and `STR# -4064` has
the extension's complaint ready: *"The SpaceSaver Preferences file could
neither be found nor created"*. Extensions install at startup, so on
this image SpaceSaver's had never had one to read. `--twoboot` fixes
that in the obvious way — boot, open the panel, OK it (which writes
`Pref/SMKR`, 10,496 B, confirmed on the flushed image), shut down, boot
again, rename — and the subject still comes back byte-identical. So did
a re-installation that ends in Shut Down rather than a kill, which was
the other thing the image might have been missing.

**The application is not a way round the extension.** `SSEn 1` lives in
`StuffIt Engine™`, a shared library, so `StuffIt Deluxe™` was worth
looking at — it is another client of the same engine and needs nothing
from Control Panels. Its two menus are photographed
(`specimens/spacesaver/deluxe/`): *Archive* is Stuff, UnStuff, Make
Self-Extracting and the rest, and *Translate* is a list of formats to
convert. Neither offers SpaceSaver compression. On this release the
extension is the only client that asks for it.

**And two mechanisms are ruled out by measurement rather than by
argument.** The control panel's Finder flags are `0x2000` — `hasBundle`
alone, `hasNoINITs` clear — and byte for byte the same as
`~Magic Menu™`'s, whose extension demonstrably does load. Whatever stops
SpaceSaver installing, it is not the flag that tells the System not to
look.

**And the earlier runs point somewhere better than any of that.** With
`~Magic Menu™` deleted the menu bar carried no icon menu at all — so the
menu SpaceSaver's own *Show Menu* option promises, `STR# 1024`'s
*Turn Idle-Time On / Idle Now / Open Control Panel*, was never installed.
Nothing else on this machine has ever needed SpaceSaver's EXTENSION: the
control panel is a `cdev` and loads when the panel is opened, the `.SIT`
rename rule is Magic Menu's, and every "SpaceSaver is running" reading so
far has come from the panel drawing itself correctly. **The next question
is not which trigger to pull but whether `INIT 0` installs at all**, and
`STR# 1001` lists seven reasons it might decline — one of which,
*"This copy of SpaceSaver has not been correctly installed. Please
re-install from your master disks"*, is exactly what a two-floppy set
driven by a screenshot harness might produce. The startup icon parade
would say, and this machine cannot photograph it: Mini vMac runs
unthrottled and is at the Finder before the first frame can be grabbed
(`--parade`, 30 shots at 0.15 s, all of them the settled desktop).

Nothing here blocks the reader: the container and both implemented
methods are graded by an encoder written from the same disassembly, and
the honest limit of that is written in TESTING.md. A specimen would
raise it from *self-consistent* to *right*.

## 2026-08-23 — AppleLink `.pkg`, named at last, and what a recogniser is

The `PKG Expand Translator` found on 2026-08-22 is Aladdin's reader for
the format this project has never been able to name. This is what it
says, and what was shipped from it.

### Where the format's identity actually lives

The translator does no file I/O at all — no `_Read`, no `_Open`, 23
`_HFSDispatch` calls and nothing else — because the engine hands it the
file as a HANDLE and it works over memory. Its entry is
`main(pb, selector)`, and bit 1 of the selector is the "can you take
this?" call: at @0xec6 it points the callback at `pb@(0)`, runs @0x2952
over `pb@(4)`, and answers `-1` or `0` in `pb@(8)`.

@0x2952 does not look at a magic number. It calls a TOKENIZER (@0xf18)
that skips space, tab and CR, honours double quotes and treats
backslash as an escape inside them, and it accepts the file when

    token 1 == "Decode"        (@0x2996, `_CmpString`, case-insensitive)
    token 3 == "to" or "into"  (@0x2a04, @0x2a24)

**An AppleLink package is a text script.** `STR# 129` is the rest of the
vocabulary — `convert`, `noConvert`, ` Folder`, and the errors
*"Destination not found: "* and *"No items matching: "* — and the
identify path reads token 2 as a name and looks it up, which is why the
second error exists at all.

### The claim this disproves, and the assumption under it

OPEN_ITEMS.md had said `.pkg` "cannot even be sniffed without inventing
a magic number, so a user gets *unknown* rather than a name". That
sentence is not about AppleLink. It is about an unstated assumption —
**that recognising a format means matching bytes at a fixed offset** —
and the moment a real recogniser was in front of it, the assumption was
the only thing holding it up. The test was there to be COPIED, which is
exactly what rule #1 asks for and the opposite of guessing.

`macextract sniff` implements those three tokens and nothing more, so it
is exactly as strong as Aladdin's and no stronger: a text file that
begins *"Decode this to that"* is a package as far as either program can
tell. That is a property of the format, not a defect in the probe, and
it is written down rather than papered over with an extra condition
nobody has evidence for. 928 fixtures, 0 false positives.

### What a `Decode` line refers to — and why that may end the item

Three things are measured, and together they point one way:

- **The operand is looked up in the FILE SYSTEM.** The identify path
  takes token 2, hands it to the engine (@0xdb4, callback selector 13,
  which returns a handle of 38-byte records), copies one out and calls
  @0x1266 / @0x128c — both of which fill a `CInfoPBRec` with a
  vRefNum, a dirID and a name and call `_HFSDispatch` **selector 9,
  `PBGetCatInfo`**. That is a catalogue lookup, not an offset in the
  package.
- **Its errors are file-system errors.** `STR# 129` carries
  *"No items matching: "* and *"Destination not found: "*. Neither
  sentence has a meaning inside a self-contained archive.
- **The translator carries recognisers for eight other formats** —
  `.HQX`, `.TGZ`, `.GZ`, `.ZIP`, `.ARC`, `.Z`, `.UUE`, `.UU`, plus type
  `PACT`, creator `EXTR` and type `HBSF` — each a small routine over an
  FSSpec. A reader for a self-contained archive has no use for those; a
  driver that hands each named file to the right decoder has every use.

**Hypothesis, on those three:** an AppleLink package is a transfer
MANIFEST — a text script naming files that sit beside it — and not a
container at all. If that is right, `.pkg` is not something an
unarchiver extracts, and naming it is the whole of the capability; the
open item closes as a property of the format rather than as a gap.
**What would settle it:** one `.pkg` file — because reading further is
no longer cheap. The expander opens the package itself (@0x2e00, `HOpen`
with permission 3 falling back to 1) and then hands the work to the
ENGINE: @0x334c and its neighbours do `GetResource('Eng ', 2)` /
`('Eng ', 3)`, lock it, take its entry point and call it. The
translator is a driver, and the decoding lives in 25 KB code resources
inside `StuffIt Engine™` with no MacsBug symbols in them. Two more
measured details on the way, both from @0x2e5c: a package's destination
folder is its own name with `.pkg` stripped and `STR# 129` item 2,
`" Folder"`, appended — so `Report.pkg` expands into `Report Folder` —
and that is the only place item 3, `".pkg"`, is used.

### Why it stops at detect-only

Even if the hypothesis is wrong and a package does carry its payload,
there is no `.pkg` file here to grade a decoder against, and a decoder
no oracle can check is the bargain this project declines for LZH, for
DiskDoubler's methods 2-5 and for SpaceSaver's 1-3. Naming the file
costs nothing and is worth having on its own: it is the difference
between *unknown* and a format with a name, which is what `unc_sniff`
exists for.

## 2026-08-23 (SpaceSaver, the encoder agrees) — the cross-check that replaced a specimen

Method 4 was the weakest-graded thing in this tree, and the weakness was
precise rather than vague. **The container is triple-sourced**: the
writer (`mCompressFile`), the reader (`mExpandFile`) and the version
converter (`UpdateSSHeader`) each describe the same 84 bytes and agree.
**The codec was single-sourced** — one routine, `CMod 21` @0x15a — and
the encoder in `run_spacesaver.py` was written from that same reading,
so a misreading would be invisible: encoder and decoder would agree and
both be wrong. A specimen was the only thing that could falsify it, and
twelve emulator runs had not produced one.

`CMod 20` is the same algorithm's FULL module, and its `+0x08` entry is
a real compressor rather than the `moveq #103` stub its decompress-only
sibling carries. Aladdin's encoder and decoder are different code paths.
Reading the encoder is therefore an independent second source, and it
costs an hour with no emulator.

**Every field that was single-sourced is confirmed, and two are new:**

| claim | where the encoder says it |
|---|---|
| stream header: block count as u16, low byte first | @0xe1c writes `fp@(-17)` then `fp@(-35)` |
| then a byte, which is the window width in bits | @0xe24 writes a literal **15** |
| block header: `clen` as u24, low byte first | @0xe2c-@0xe3e, @0xe42-@0xe50 |
| `clen` counts the 6 header bytes | it is `a3 - blockStart` after the header was reserved |
| the 3 stream bytes are NOT in the first block's `clen` | @0xe28 subtracts 3, and only for the first |
| then `ulen` as u24, low byte first | @0xe52-@0xe66 |
| a literal is 9 bits: a 1, then the byte | @0xb4c: `(byte << 1) | 1`, width 9 |
| a match is 21 bits: a 0, 5 bits, 15 bits | @0xd2c: width 21, and 1+5+15 = 21 |
| the 5-bit field is length MINUS FOUR | @0xd1e: `subqw #4` before it is shifted up by 1 |
| the distance sits above it | @0xd1c: `lsll #6` — bits 6..20 |
| bits pack LSB-first, flushed low byte first | @0xb7e-@0xb94 |
| the window slides across blocks | @0xe92: it shifts the buffer down by `ulen` and keeps the rest |
| **the preset dictionary ends immediately before the first output byte** | @0x93e copies `pramSize` bytes to `fp@(-12) - pramSize` |

That last row is the one that mattered most. It was the most
speculative thing in the whole reading — a claim about where 9,679 bytes
of somebody else's table sit relative to a pointer — and the encoder
places it identically, from its own side, in its own routine.

### Two figures this corrects

- **A real file's window is 32,768 bytes, not 65,536.** The encoder
  writes 15 into the width byte, always. The decoder reads the byte and
  accepts 1..16 because the field is a byte and the reference's
  decompressor honours it, so 16 stays supported and is now exercised
  separately — but the peak this format costs in practice is **36,864 B**
  (32 KB window + the 4 KB input buffer), measured, where FORMATS.md
  had recorded the 69,632 B ceiling. That is the mistake this repository
  warns about in its own words: a number taken from what a field COULD
  say rather than from what the data does say.
- **A block covers 65,536 bytes of output.** @0x8fc computes the block
  count as `ceil(ulen / 65536)` and @0xa02 reads that much input at a
  time. The decoder never assumed a size — it reads each block's `ulen`
  — so it is unaffected, and the synthetic encoder now matches.

### What a specimen would still be worth

Less than it was, and it is worth saying how much less. It would no
longer be the only check on the bitstream; two independently written
routines now agree about every field of it. What it would still catch is
a misreading shared by BOTH of Aladdin's routines and this project's
reading of them — which is a much smaller target — and anything about
the CONTAINER that all three engine routines happen to describe the same
wrong way. The specimen hunt stays open (OPEN_ITEMS.md) and stops being
the thing this format is waiting on.

## 2026-08-23 (SpaceSaver, the dictionary) — carrying 9,679 bytes that are not ours

**The maintainer's decision, taken on 2026-08-23**, and recorded here
because it is a licence decision rather than an engineering one: the
`PrAm` -4048 table ships inside this project.

### What it changes

Everything, for the case the format actually turns up in. A SpaceSaver
file is a normal-looking document — right name, type, creator and icon —
whose data fork is a compressed container, and the machine that made it
expanded it transparently. Meet one on a floppy today and the
application that wrote it reads 84 bytes of `SIT!DFD3` where the
document should be. Until now this project could NAME that file and
report both forks' sizes, and could only decode it if the user obtained
a copy of SpaceSaver and passed `--preset`. A lone floppy carries no
dictionary, so for the commonest case the answer was a diagnosis rather
than a recovery. Now it is a recovery, everywhere, with no flags: the
CLI, and the Macintosh application, which cannot be handed a file at
all.

### The decision, stated plainly

The table's own first line is *"Copyright (c) 1993 Aladdin Systems, Inc.
… Distribution is prohibited without written permission of Aladdin
Systems"*, so the prohibition is part of the data being distributed. No
permission has been given. The maintainer judged the risk acceptable for
a 1993 product whose publisher's successors are being sought, asked for
attribution and nothing more, and owns that call; it is written into
`NOTICE` as an explicit exception to the LGPL grant, and into README.md
where a user will see it.

**What was NOT done, deliberately:** nothing was hidden, obfuscated or
minimised. `src/ss_dict.h` says whose bytes they are in its header, the
generator says so, `NOTICE` has a section for it, and the README's
licence paragraph names it. If permission is refused later, deleting one
generated header and one `else if` restores the previous behaviour.

### Why the bytes had to be exact

They are not a parameter anyone can approximate. The window is primed
with them, so a match at a short distance reads the table rather than
the file; a decoder with the wrong table produces output of exactly the
right length that is silently wrong. That is why `--preset` was built
first and why absence was a per-entry failure rather than a warning — and
it is why "ship a smaller table" or "generate an equivalent one" are not
options.

### Two loose ends closed with it

- **The record's byte at +12 is now honoured.** It selects the table:
  0 is -4048, 1 is -4047 (four spaces), and anything else is refused per
  entry rather than decoded with the wrong dictionary. It had been read
  into the header struct and then ignored.
- **A committed generated file can go stale**, so
  `tools/run_spacesaver.py` compares `src/ss_dict.h` byte for byte with
  the resource whenever an installed SpaceSaver is present, and says so.
  It fails against a header with one byte changed.

## 2026-08-23 (decryption) — five schemes in, and the one with no second reader

Decryption was out of scope for v1 and is in scope from 2026-08-23, on
request. Seven ciphers across five formats; five of them are
transcribed from XADMaster, one is read out of an application, and the
interesting decisions are about **evidence**, not about ciphers.

### What a password is, in this engine

A cipher is not a decoder here. It is a filter on `UncBufIn`, the one
buffered reader every format already reads its compressed bytes
through: `in.crypt = cr` and every refill is decrypted before the
decoder above sees a byte. That is why five formats and eight codecs
gained encryption support without a single decoder changing — the
StuffIt LZAH decoder does not know it is reading through DES any more
than it knows it is reading from a floppy.

Two things follow from putting it there, and both are in
`unc_bufin_next_slow`:

- **A block cipher cannot decrypt half a block.** `io->read` is allowed
  to come up short, so when a cipher with `blocksize > 1` is attached
  the refill fills the buffer instead of taking what arrives, and at a
  genuine EOF it zero-pads the tail to the block size — which is what
  the reference handles do (`XADPackItDESHandle`: memset the block,
  then break).
- **The stateful ciphers require the reads to be sequential**, which
  `UncBufIn` already guarantees: it only moves forward, and a decoder
  that needs to jump re-initialises the whole reader. Where a format
  needs the cipher to start somewhere other than where the reader is —
  PackIt, whose entries are one continuous stream — the reader is
  reopened at the entry instead (`pit_reopen`).

The password itself arrives one of two ways, and the second is the one
that shaped the API: `opt.password` for a host that has one up front,
or `opt.ask`, a callback the engine invokes **when an entry turns out
to need one**. On a Mac the natural moment to put up a dialog is that
moment and not before, and where the format can tell a wrong password
from a right one the callback is asked again with a rising `attempt`,
which is what makes "that password was refused" possible at all.

### Verification, and why it decides where the retry loop goes

The five schemes differ in one respect that matters more than the
cryptography: whether the archive can say NO before anything is decoded.

| format | what verifies a password | so a retry is |
|---|---|---|
| StuffIt classic | the 'MKey' resource decrypts to a block that checks against itself | free, before any output |
| StuffIt 5 | MD5(MD5(password)) against a five-byte hash in the archive header | free |
| zip | the twelfth byte of the encryption header against the CRC's top byte | free — one wrong password in 256 gets past it, and the entry CRC catches that |
| PackIt | nothing; but the 94-byte entry header is INSIDE the ciphertext and carries a CRC | free, because the header is decoded first anyway |
| ARC | nothing at all | costs a whole decode |
| Compact Pro | nothing at all — its own message says so: *"The password supplied is incorrect **or the archive is damaged**"* | costs a whole decode |

The last two are why `arc_entry_data_pw` and `cpt_encrypted_entry`
decode each entry TWICE where a password is in play: once writing
nothing, to find the key, and once to write it. Deciding as it wrote
would mean handing the sink the output of a wrong key and then
appending the output of the right one to it, which is exactly what the
first version of the ARC path did — `cmp` said the file differed while
the exit status said success. Compact Pro pays it only on the first
encrypted entry: one password covers an archive, so once one has
checked out the rest decode straight through.

### StuffIt classic keeps its key outside the archive

'MKey' 0 is a RESOURCE, in the archive's resource fork, and it is half
the material every file key is built from. A `.sit` that arrived as a
bare data fork — downloaded, or unstuffed by something that dropped the
fork — **cannot be decrypted by anything**, this engine included, and
the entry reports `UNC_ERR_ENCRYPTED` rather than asking for a password
it could not use. That is a property of the format and not a gap.

Where the fork does survive, both hosts find it without a Resource
Manager: `unc_resfork_find` walks a raw fork with every field bounds
checked, the CLI hands it the sidecar file it writes itself when it
takes a MacBinary or BinHex envelope off, and the Mac application reads
the fork as plain bytes (`PBHOpenRFSync`) rather than opening somebody
else's resource map with the Resource Manager.

### ARC has no encryption flag, so the reader must not assume

SEA's format stores nothing to say a member was garbled — XADMaster
says so in as many words. So a password given up front is applied, and
a host that can ASK is only asked **once the plain read has failed its
CRC**, which is the only evidence the format offers. Measured while
building the fixture: `arc` 5.21q **upper-cases the password** before
garbling with it (a stored entry's ciphertext XOR its plaintext is the
