password in capitals), where XADMaster uses the bytes as given. Neither
is wrong for the files it wrote, so both are tried.

### Compact Pro: read out of the application, and graded by it

The Unarchiver's own format notes say "the algorithm for this is
unknown"; macutils skips the file; CptExpand's documentation says it
"will not extract encrypted files"; StuffIt's Compact Pro translator
answers "the file is password protected". No second READER exists — so
it was read out of Compact Pro 1.51's own `CODE` 3, both directions,
every address is in `src/unc_crypt.c`, and the application itself is
what grades the result:

- a **64-bit CRC** register, polynomial 0xEDB88320 in *both* halves
  (the table builder at 0x408e), seeded 0x0123456789ABCDEF (0x40fe);
- the **key** at 0x1892: nine bytes, a length capped at eight and the
  password's bytes ADDED into eight accumulators in rotation, so a
  password of eight characters or fewer is its own key; those bytes are
  then folded into the register one at a time;
- the **keystream** is the register's second byte, and the register is
  then stepped on the PLAINTEXT byte — the encrypt loop at 0x1152 and
  the decrypt loop at 0x1f08 are exact inverses, which is the one
  internal cross-check available;
- **scope**: `InitCrypt` is called once per FILE, immediately before the
  fork loop (0xac in the compress path, 0x14e0 in the expand path), so
  one register runs across the resource fork and then the data fork of
  an entry and is reset for the next.

**Graded by the application, both ways** — and the getting there is
the part worth writing down, because two of the three things that went
wrong were mine.

The plan was Salient's arrangement: write an archive with this cipher,
hand it to the application, see whether the bytes come back
(`tools/vmac/cptcrypt.py`). An unregistered Compact Pro refuses —
*"This version of Compact Pro cannot validate or extract encrypted
files"* — and the reading of WHY was wrong. The routine at 0x47ca
stores the personalisation string and 0x485e enables encryption when
that string ends with `*`; from that I concluded a registration would
not help unless its name ended in `*`, and said so. Registering with
the code Macintosh Garden publishes (`Registered` / 35427117) opened
the gate outright: the refusal went, the Add dialog's Encrypt checkbox
appeared (0x4864 patches DITL 312 to move it into view), and the
application both encrypted and decrypted. **Reading one branch is not
reading the feature**, and the cost of the mistake was a paragraph in
three files claiming the format could not be graded here.

Then it still failed, and the second wrong thing was also mine.
Compact Pro extracted our archive as *"The password supplied is
incorrect or the archive is damaged"*, and its own archives would not
decrypt under this engine either. The arithmetic said what the
disassembly had not: three archives the application encrypted with
data forks of 96, 100 and 103 bytes came back as 104, 108 and 111 —
**a constant +8**, so not a block cipher's padding but a fixed
preamble. An encrypted entry's stream opens with eight bytes that the
reader decrypts and DISCARDS (`CODE` 3 @0x1f5e moves the read cursor
past them), and their length is counted in the compressed size of the
first fork that has any bytes. They are a salt: the register steps on
them, so one password over the same bytes encrypts differently in two
entries — which is why the recovered keystreams of three archives
diverged at byte 0 and looked like a broken cipher.

With that one line, everything agrees:

- three RLE-only archives Compact Pro encrypted: every byte back;
- one LZH-compressed two-fork archive it encrypted: both forks
  byte-exact, which is also what proves the register runs across the
  two forks without resetting;
- an archive this project encrypted: Compact Pro extracted all three
  entries, and its own CRC-32 over both decrypted forks passed.

The third wrong thing was not ours and is worth recording so nobody
chases it again: every RESOURCE fork extracted on that machine comes
back with bytes changed a few dozen in. It is not Compact Pro — a
corpus archive extracted by the same copy does it too — it is the Mac
opening the extracted file's resource fork to draw its icon, and the
Resource Manager writing the map's live fields back. `cptcrypt.py`
compares data forks strictly and says so; what makes that sufficient is
that Compact Pro checks the entry CRC over BOTH decrypted forks before
it writes anything at all.

### WinZip's AES, which is out of era and in anyway

Method 99 with extra field 0x9901 is a 2003 extension, a decade past
every machine this targets, and it was written off as out of scope
until the rest of the work made the objection look thin: the file IS a
zip, this reads zips, and refusing one entry of it is a worse answer
than a slow one. So it is implemented — PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA1 over a salt in
the entry, AES in counter mode, and ten bytes of HMAC-SHA1 over the
ciphertext.

Three things kept it small enough to belong on a Mac Plus:

- **Counter mode encrypts to decrypt**, so only the FORWARD cipher is
  needed. No inverse S box, no inverse mix-columns, and the tables that
  make those fast are four kilobytes that do not ship.
- **One 256-byte S box** and a column mix computed with `xtime`, rather
  than the usual four kilobytes of T tables. Slower per block, and this
  is not the format anybody has a megabyte of.
- The **key derivation** is where the time goes: 1000 rounds of
  HMAC-SHA1, and for AES-256 that is four 20-byte blocks, so ~8,000
  SHA-1 compressions — seconds on a 68000, once per entry, before any
  output appears.

The oracle situation is inverted from every other scheme here.
**Debian's `unar` 1.10.8 cannot open a WinZip AES zip at all** — every
key length, stored or deflated, comes back "Missing or wrong password"
— so there is no second reader to agree with. What grades it instead is
the ENCODER: 7-Zip writes the archive, verifies it itself, and our
output has to equal the bytes that went in. The authentication code
does the rest, and the suite flips a ciphertext byte to prove it is
being checked.

### The fixtures, and what grades them

Nothing in the corpus is encrypted — measured, not assumed: `lsar -j`
over all 903 fixtures reports `XADIsEncrypted` 0 for every entry. So
every archive in the new suite is generated, and the point of
`tools/run_crypt.py` is that generation grades nothing on its own:

- `zip -P` and `arc g<password>` are somebody else's encoders, so those
  two fixtures owe nothing to this project's reading;
- StuffIt classic, StuffIt 5 and PackIt have no encoder here, so
  `tools/synth_crypt.py` writes them and **`unar -p` adjudicates** —
  a different implementation of each cipher, which is the check a
  shared misreading cannot survive. For StuffIt classic that means the
  fixture is MacBinary, because the oracle needs the resource fork too.

One divergence turned up between the two references, and it was
asserted rather than papered over: **`unar` 1.10.8 and current
XADMaster derive the StuffIt archive key differently once the password
reaches eight bytes.** It is now settled, and the answer is that
**neither of them is right** — see the next entry.

## 2026-08-23 (StuffIt's archive key) — the fold count, measured

The open question was which reference matched Aladdin: `unar` 1.10.8 or
current XADMaster. The answer is neither, and the way that came out is
the transferable part.

### What it is

The password is folded into the archive key eight bytes at a time, and
the number of folds is **ceil(len / 8)**. Three archives StuffIt Deluxe
3.5.1 wrote under Mini vMac say so — passwords of 8, 9 and 17
characters need 1, 2 and 3 folds (`tools/vmac/sitcrypt.py`; the table
is in FORMATS.md). XADMaster's loop gives 2, 2, 2; `unar` 1.10.8 gives
1, 1, 1. Each opens archives the other cannot, and this engine had
shipped XADMaster's.

### Why it survived a whole feature's worth of testing

Because **every encrypted `.sit` in the project was one the project had
generated**, and the generator was written from the same reading as the
reader. `unar` adjudicated those files and disagreed — which was
recorded as a divergence between two references rather than read as
what it was: two implementations disagreeing about a file that neither
of them wrote, with no third party in the room. The suite was green
throughout. It was green because it was asking the wrong question.

Under eight characters all three readings agree, and eight or fewer is
what a person picks when testing a password. The specimen that broke it
open was chosen the other way on purpose: nine characters, because nine
is where the two references were known to differ. Then 8 and 17 were
run to find the shape rather than to confirm the guess — and 8 is where
OUR reading turned out to be the wrong one.

### The rule this is an instance of

`OPEN_ITEMS.md` had this as "waiting on a gradeable specimen", filed
beside four others, and the entry was right. What it did not say — and
what the DiskDoubler and Compact Pro entries did not say either — is
that the software that WRITES the format is a specimen source, not just
a grader. Three of this project's last four format corrections came
from running the original application: Salient's expander for
DiskDoubler 3-5, Compact Pro for its own cipher, and now StuffIt
Deluxe. In each case the file that settled it did not have to be found;
it had to be MADE.

The StuffIt 5 entry in OPEN_ITEMS.md is now the same shape, and is
labelled with what this cost: two readings that agree with each other
for exactly the inputs anybody would test are not evidence about
anything.
## 2026-08-23 (btoa's licence) — a reference that may not be transcribed

Every other reference this project reads is LGPL or public domain, so
"transcribe it and cite the file" has never needed a second thought.
btoa is the first that does.

Its README: *"Btoa is in the public domain. You may use it, give it
away, and make improvements, as long as the names of the developers are
mentioned and you don't use it to earn money. It may NOT be used
commercially without my permission."* The first four words are wrong
about the rest of the sentence — a non-commercial restriction is not
the public domain, which is why Debian shipped btoa in `non-free` — and
it is incompatible with LGPL-2.1, under which somebody may charge for
what they do with MacExtract.

So the usual arrangement is split in two:

- **The format** — two header lines, base 85 offset by `'!'`, `z` and
  `y`, the three checksum recurrences, the per-line check, and
  `(len-1)&3` for the padding — is read out of `atob.c`, `btoa.h`,
  `chksum.h` and the man page, and implemented in `src/fmt_btoa.c`.
  What a format IS cannot be licensed; the developers' expression of it
  can, and none of it is in that file: no function, buffer or control
  structure is carried across, and each fact is cited to the routine it
  came from.
- **The code** stays in `reference/`, which is fetched and never
  committed, and is *used* as a test-time oracle — `tools/run_btoa.py`
  compiles it and requires our decoder to reproduce what it encodes.
  Using a program is not distributing a derivative of it, and this use
  earns nobody anything.

The names are mentioned, which is the one condition of that licence
this project can and does meet: in `NOTICE`, in `src/fmt_btoa.c` and
here.

**Why not just skip the format.** Because the licence covers their
program and not the wire format, and a Mac user with a `.ascii` file
from a Unix host has the same problem as one with a `.uu` — which this
already reads. Declining would have been a licence decision applied to
the wrong object.

## 2026-08-23 (Compact Pro comments) — a comment is saved, and it is still not a file

Compact Pro's index opens with a comment — one length byte and up to
255 MacRoman bytes, belonging to the archive rather than to any entry.
Until now the engine read it, folded it into the index CRC and dropped
it, and OPEN_ITEMS.md called that a decision: *"a comment is not a file
and the sink writes files"*. The second half of that sentence was true;
the conclusion did not follow. The sink writes files because nothing
had ever asked it to carry anything else.

**Where it comes out.** `UncSink` gains an optional `comment` callback,
delivered once before the first `begin_entry`. Not an entry with a
synthetic name: an entry has forks, a type and a creator, and inventing
one would put a file in every listing, every entry count and every
differential comparison — `unar` reports the comment as an archive
PROPERTY and writes no such file, so the corpus would have disagreed
with its own oracle on 108 fixtures to carry nine bytes. Not a new
entry point either, because the comment is found half way through the
index walk and a second entry point would mean a second walk.

**Why the sink and not the options.** `UncOptions` is where the other
host-supplied callback lives (`UncPasswordSource`), and appending there
would have been the smaller diff. But an archive comment is OUTPUT, and
every host that already distinguishes list from extract has to answer
the same question for it that it answers for a fork. Six sinks are
constructed in this repository; all six now say what they want, and a
`NULL` means exactly what it meant before the field existed.

**What each host does with it.** The CLI writes
`<archive>.comment.txt` beside the extracted items — a name derived
from the archive's, so it cannot collide with an entry of the
archive's own — and `l` and `t` print it instead, since neither writes
files. The Mac shell writes a `TEXT`/`ttxt` document called `Comments`
into the destination folder, which is where StuffIt Expander puts one.

**Printed text is decoded; saved bytes are not.** The CLI decodes
MacRoman to UTF-8 when it PRINTS the comment, like a filename, and
writes the archive's own bytes when it SAVES it — CR line endings
included. The alternative was to transcode the file too, which reads
better in a Linux terminal and would have made the two hosts save
different bytes for the same comment: `checkdisk.py` compares the Mac's
output against the CLI's file by file, and `vmac_check.py` sums their
CRCs, so an 8-bit comment would have failed both for no defect at all.
The comment is data, and the file is the comment.

**What grades it.** Six corpus archives have carried a real one for
thirty years and nobody had looked — no fixture had to be invented for
this, and five of the six were found by the half of the new check that
FORBIDS a comment file where the oracle reports none. The oracle is `lsar`'s `XADComment` (TESTING.md,
*Manifest format*); the Mac path is graded by the mock-Toolbox sink test
and on System 6.0.8 (FORMATS.md, *Real-hardware verification*). Compact
Pro's own name for it is a **Note**, and `tools/vmac/cptcrypt.py --note`
types one into the application.

## 2026-08-23 (LZH) — the format the corpus could not grade, graded by somebody else's corpus

LZH/LHA was out of scope, and the reason was written down carefully in
2026-08-14: **the corpus contains no `.lzh` at all**, measured over every
fixture, because Macintosh software was essentially not distributed that
way. The conclusion drawn from it — "~550 new lines for nothing the
corpus can grade" — was about the CORPUS, and that is the part that
turned out to be the wrong boundary. The maintainer put the format in
scope; what made it cheap was not effort but **finding a corpus that is
not ours**.

`reference/lhasa` — Simon Howard's LHA decompressor, in Debian — ships
**182 test archives written by fourteen different LHA implementations**:
MacLHA 2.24, LHa for Amiga/OS-2/OS-9/OSK/X68000, LHarc 1.13, LArc 3.33,
LHMelt, LHArk, UNLHA32, and generated cases for the methods no writer
still emits. That is a better specimen set than this project has for any
format it already had. `reference/lha` is LHa for UNIX 1.14i off
archive.debian.org, the original program, which compiles here and is
therefore an **encoder**.

**Three graders, and the difference between them is the whole point.**
`unar` is XADMaster, which `src/fmt_lzh.c` is transcribed from: where it
agrees with us we have established that the copy was faithful and
nothing else. lhasa is an independent reading of the format. LHa 1.14i
writes archives whose plain text is already known. The suite
(`tools/run_lzh.py`) runs all three, and the second one earned its place
within an afternoon:

- **`unar` cannot read LHA for OS-9/68k's level 2 headers.** That writer
  stores the length of the REST of the header, so every one of its
  archives is two bytes out; lhasa compensates by OS byte, XADMaster
  does not, and following lhasa turned three failures into three
  byte-exact extractions.
- **`unar` fails a 1.2 MB `-lh7-` file** ("Attempted to read more data
  than was available") that lhasa and this engine both decode, byte for
  byte identically.
- **`unar` cannot open an `-lzs-` archive** — its recogniser accepts
  `-lz0-`, `-lz4-` and `-lz5-` and omits `-lzs-`, while its own parser
  has a working `-lzs-` decoder behind that gate. This probe accepts it.
  A cross-file contradiction inside the reference, of exactly the kind
  BLOCKERS.md names: two halves of one program disagreeing about what
  the program supports.

**And two initial-window questions that only a specimen can answer.**
LZSS windows here are preloaded, and the three references disagree about
what is underneath the preload — XADMaster zero-fills, LHa 1.14i
space-fills a dictionary its own `decode_start_lz5` never touches, and
lhasa writes the same pattern rotated. The archives settled both, in
opposite directions:

- `-lz5-`: **zeros**. LArc 3.33's `initial.lzs` is an archive whose
  CONTENTS are the initial window, and its first 18 bytes are zero.
- `-lzs-`: **spaces**. There is no preload at all, and lhasa's
  `lzs.lzs` fails its stored CRC-16 zero-filled and passes space-filled.

Neither could have been settled by reasoning, and one of them cannot be
settled by any archive that never reaches back that far — which is why
`long.lzs` passes either way and `lzs.lzs` does not. **A difference that
only some files can see is a difference that needs the file that sees
it.**

**MacLHA is the Macintosh half, and it is a MacBinary stream.** A Mac
entry's decoded bytes are a 128-byte MacBinary header, the data fork
padded to 128, then the resource fork; the entry's name, type, creator
and dates come from that header. The trigger is the OS byte `'m'` and
nothing else — `unar` reads MacLHA's own LEVEL 0 archives (which have no
OS byte) as one 18,304-byte file where the level 1 copy of the same file
is 18,092 bytes plus metadata, and lhasa infers the same rule from the
same byte. So the peek runs in LISTING too, not only extraction: what
the entry IS — its name, its forks, its type — is what the Mac header
says, and a listing that disagreed with the extraction is a bug no diff
suite could see.

**One decoder was already here.** StuffIt classic's method 5 is
LHarc's `-lh1-`: XADMaster instantiates the same handle for both, and
this repository had transcribed it in M5. It moved to `src/unc_lzah.c`
behind a byte callback rather than being copied — the arrangement
DiskDoubler method 8 already has with Compact Pro's two stages, and for
the same reason: a second copy of a decoder is a second thing to fix.

**What is declined, and the shape of the argument.** `-lh2-` and
`-lh3-` are transcribable from `XADLZHOldHandles.m` and are NOT written,
because no archive that uses either is reachable — not in the corpus,
not among fourteen writers, and nothing here emits one. That is the same
bargain this project makes everywhere, and it is worth stating once more
now that the bargain has been paid off twice from outside: the ARC and
SuperDisk! entries in OPEN_ITEMS.md are waiting for exactly the kind of
find that this milestone was. **"No oracle exists" is a claim about a
search, and the search that matters is rarely inside the format.**

LHArk 0.4d is the counter-example worth keeping: it writes its own
algorithm behind an `-lh7-` label and marks itself with a space as the
OS byte. Reading it as `-lh7-` produces plausible rubbish that fails the
CRC; it is refused per entry instead, by the same OS byte lhasa uses to
recognise it.

**The specimen this milestone made itself found the one case both
references decline.** Every MacLHA archive in reach — sixteen of them —
holds a data-fork-only file, so the fork split had never been graded
against a byte MacLHA wrote. `tools/vmac/maclha.py` runs **MacLHA 2.00**
(which is already in the corpus, `derived-MacLHa_2.00.sit`) under Mini
vMac and archives a file with a real resource fork. Its MacBinary
header carries **zero for both dates**, and that is enough for every
reader here to refuse the unwrap: XADMaster's MacBinary-I heuristic
requires both dates non-zero, lhasa requires the modification date to
be sane AND to match the LZH header's. `unar` and lhasa therefore
return a 2,304-byte file with a resource fork inside it.

What replaces the dates is lhasa's own length check with the clock
taken out: the Mac header's name must be the entry's name, and the two
fork lengths each padded to 128, plus the header, must come to exactly
the uncompressed size. A data fork that passed that would have to agree
with its container about its own length to the byte and carry the
entry's name at offset 2. It fires on none of the 182 archives and none
of the 8 fixtures, and it makes MacLHA 2.00's archives extract as two
forks: 1,760 B and 333 B, byte-exact against what went into the
emulator.

**Two lessons, and the second is the reusable one.** The obvious one is
that a heuristic tuned on files that have dates fails on files that do
not. The other is that **the specimen was cheap because the encoder was
already in the corpus** — MacLHA arrived years ago as a fixture to be
extracted, not as a tool, and nobody had asked what it could write.
That is the same move that settled ArcMac's fork order and DiskDoubler's
methods 3, 4 and 5, and the question it comes from is worth writing
down: *of the software this corpus is made of, which pieces are
encoders?*

---

## 2026-08-24 (the performance pass) — four proposals measured, two of them dead

`PLANNED_WORK.md` arrived from a branch written at v0.12.0 and was worked
through in the order it proposed. Most of it held. The two items that did
not are the useful part of this entry, because both were disproved by a
measurement that took minutes and neither could have been disproved by
reading.

### The Arsenic block's unused tail is 29 KB, not 200

**Claim (withdrawn).** `PLANNED_WORK.md` section 2: a multi-block Arsenic
entry gets the declared 512 KB block while the largest fill in the corpus
is 323,384 B, so shrinking the block to its fill returns ~200 KB to the
sampled index and buys "~4x less scanning". It came with a named
fragmentation trap and needed a shrink operation the allocator does not
have.

**Evidence.** One instrumented run printing `blockcap - numbytes` at
`ars_bwt_prepare`. The largest block in `derived-Library_ISBN_Barcoder.sit`
is `cap=352,160 fill=323,384`, waste **28,776 B**; `derived-a2basic.dmg.sit`
is `cap=205,019 fill=197,515`, waste **7,504 B**. Neither entry ever
allocates 512 KB, because `ars_block_reserve` already sizes the first
allocation at `min(outlen, blocksize)` and both entries' whole output is
smaller than a block.

**Consequence.** The prize is 29 KB, and the index needs 225,528 B at
target 512 against 346,836 B at target 1024 — so 29 KB does not move the
dial by one step, let alone two. Section 2 is dead, and its trap with it.

**What the same run found instead**, which is the transferable part: the
index costs four bytes per sample and a sample is a position inside a
block, and `blockbits` is four stream bits plus nine, so a position is at
most `(1 << 24) - 1`. **Three bytes is exact.** A quarter off the index is
a quarter more density at the same budget, and 260,705 B against 290,391 B
of room is target 1024 where 346,836 B was not. That is half the scan
distance on the slowest fixture in the corpus, for a change that also
deletes the last unaligned word load in that structure.

**The lesson is the one CLAUDE.md already states and this file keeps
re-earning.** The section's arithmetic was a model of the allocator, not a
reading of it, and the model was two years out of date — the fix it
assumed was missing had been made in M9. *Instrument the number before
budgeting for it*; the run cost four minutes and deleted a day's work.

### 61 KB of deferred code is worth under a second at launch

**Claim (withdrawn).** `PLANNED_WORK.md` section 7.4: segmenting the CODE
resource keeps ~61 KB out of the launch path, and "startup gets cheaper,
not dearer". The section labelled itself unverified and said, correctly,
that *no launch-time baseline is measured anywhere in this repository*.

**Evidence, and the method matters more than the number.** Ballast: a
block of resident data compiled into the Mac shell behind a temporary
define, referenced from the startup path so `-gc-sections` keeps it, with
the application otherwise unchanged. Three runs each on `mv-4M-1x`
(authentic 7 MHz), System 6.0.8:

| build | app | relocations | boot |
|---|---|---|---|
| shipping | 150,144 B | ~2,300 | **8.9 s** (3/3) |
| + 64 KB as `const unsigned char[]` | 215,680 B | ~2,300 | **8.9 s** (3/3) |
| + 64 KB as 16,384 pointers | 232,064 B | 18,682 | **10.0 s** (3/3) |

**The first ballast measured nothing, and that is the finding.** A byte
array carries no relocations, so it costs the Segment Loader a read and
nothing else — 64 KB of it is below the 0.1 s the harness resolves. The
cost is entirely the RELOCATIONS: 16,306 more of them are 1.1 s, about
**67 µs each** at 7 MHz. The whole shipping application has **2,376**, so
everything it pays at launch for its own size is ≈ 0.16 s of an 8.9 s
boot.

**Consequence.** Segmentation cannot buy what it is not spending. It would
also need every format's `probe` moved into one translation unit —
~20 files — or opening any archive would touch every segment. Dead, and
recorded here rather than in `OPEN_ITEMS.md` because nothing is open: the
question is answered.

**Generalise it: an unmeasured cost is not a small cost, it is an unknown
one, and this repository has a harness that could have said so at any
point in the last three weeks.** The ballast trick is reusable — it prices
a resource by adding some, which needs no refactor to try.

### The same fix, twice, in opposite directions: inlining the bit reader

`unc_bitin_bit` was an out-of-line call taken once per bit. Making it
`static inline` with the refill left behind an out-of-line
`unc_bitin_bit_slow()` measured **3.1 to 6.6 % WORSE** on Compact Pro,
DiskDoubler and Now Compress. Inlining the refill as well measured
**1.0 % better** on the same fixtures and a further 1.0 point on Arsenic.

**Why**, and it is not obvious from the source: `unc_canon_decode` lives
in the translation unit `unc_bitin_bit` lived in, so gcc was *already*
inlining the whole of it there with `bitbuf`, `nbits` and `le` in
registers. Introducing an opaque call for the refill forced all three back
to memory around every eighth bit. The formats that gained were the ones
calling it from another file — which is the case the proposal was written
for and the only case it was right about.

**The rule this yields:** before moving a function to a header for the
callers that cannot inline it, check what it costs the callers that
already could. A function's cost is not a property of the function.

### A shared decoder's tables belong wherever the deepest caller can afford

`unc_canon_decode`'s fast table is 320 B on an `UncCanon`. DiskDoubler
held three `UncCanon` and Now Compress two, **on the stack**, and the
measurement that had to be taken before adding a byte to them found the
rule already broken: `m68k -Os -fstack-usage` over the call graph put
`unc_dd_extract -> dd_fork -> dd_ddn` at **6,664 B** and Now Compress at
**5,632 B**, against CLAUDE.md's own 4 KB call-depth limit.

**Not against the machine, and the correction is worth recording because
the first draft of this entry got it wrong.** `mac/macextract_mac.c` opens
with `SetApplLimit(LMGetApplLimit() - kStackBump)` and `kStackBump` is
24 KB, so the application runs on **32 KB of stack**, not the 8 KB
default. Nothing was ever close to overflowing; a 1 MB Mac Plus extracts
both formats byte-exact today and did before. What was broken was the
RULE, which exists so the engine stays portable to a host that did not
ask for more — and the way to check it was to read the host, not to
assume the platform default.

Both sets moved to the heap and the deepest chain in the engine is now
**3,290 B**. The zip end-of-central-directory scan window stopped at 1 KB
for the same reason rather than the 4 KB that would have made its reads
17 instead of 65: at 4 KB that one buffer would have been the deepest
frame there was.

**The transferable half:** a limit with no measurement behind it is not a
limit, and the cheapest time to measure one is when something is about to
push against it. `-fstack-usage` plus the relocations in the linked
objects gives the whole call graph's worst path in one command; there is
no reason this was not known.

---

## 2026-08-24 (the performance pass, part two) — what re-profiling found that the plan had not

`PLANNED_WORK.md` was written from one profile, and every item it named was
real. The three biggest wins of this second pass were **not on it**, and the
reason is worth keeping: the plan profiled the tree once and then ordered
itself, but each landed change re-orders what is left. Re-profiling after
the first pass put three functions on top that the original run had ranked
below the noise it was chasing.

- **`unc_huff_decode` at 48 % of `derived-EmpireBuilderDemo.sit`.** The plan
  fixed `unc_canon_decode` and stopped, because that is the decoder four
  formats share. There is a SECOND Huffman decoder — the serialized tree
  used by StuffIt method 3, DiskDoubler method 4 and PackIt — and once the
  canonical one was cheap, it was the largest single cost in the corpus's
  StuffIt fixture. Same remedy, one difference: an arbitrary tree has no
  arithmetic that recovers a symbol from its code, so its table stores the
  symbol where the canonical one stores only a length.
- **`unc_bitin_bits` at 9–13 %** of DiskDoubler and Now Compress, looping n
  times through the single-bit reader. It became a masked shift the moment
  `unc_bitin_fill` existed — which the canonical-decoder work had added for
  its own reasons. **A tool built for one job made a second one trivial, and
  nothing would have noticed without a second profile.**
- **BinHex's `% 3` per output byte**, in the same class as the three divides
  the plan did name, in the file it did not look at.

**The rule: re-profile after each landed change, not once per plan.** A
profile is a snapshot of a tree whose shape the next commit changes.

### Two inline fast paths that measured worse, for the same reason

Twice now, giving a hot function an inline fast path with the rare cases
behind a call has measured SLOWER than leaving it alone: `unc_bitin_bit`
(3.1–6.6 % worse) and BinHex's `bh_get_bits` (−5.5 % against −6.6 %
without). Both times the cause is the same — the opaque call forces the
state the fast path just loaded back to memory around it, and the compiler
was already doing better with the whole function in view.

**So: "make the hot function inline" is not a transformation, it is a
hypothesis.** The version that worked, in `unc_bitin_bit`, was the one with
NO call left in it at all.

### Filling the disk on purpose

`run.py --out-kb` sizes the destination volume, which is how the full-disk
path was reached for the first time. It immediately disproved the plan's
description of it: 9 files written, **one** error, eight entries never
attempted, because thirteen of the fourteen extractors end the archive on a
sink failure. The write-up had described that path in four numbered steps
and traced three of them to lines.

The lesson is narrow and useful: **a described behaviour with no file:line
beside it is a guess in the shape of a reading.** The step that was wrong
was the only step with no citation, and one run against a full disk settled
it in two minutes.

---

## 2026-08-24 (PLANNED_WORK.md, closed) — the file is gone, and what it left behind

The plan file opened on 2026-08-23 with seven numbered items and closed a day
later with none. Four landed, **five were disproved by measurement**, and the
three biggest wins of the pass were not on it at all (see the entry above).
It is deleted rather than annotated, which is what its own preamble asked
for: a plan file that keeps completed rows stops being readable as a list of
what is left.

### Why three of its five window items were closed rather than done

The last item was "size five windows from the entry, not the format's
ceiling". Two were done — SuperDisk's and zip's — and both because they
changed **what a machine can extract**, not what it reports: an entry in
`alysis-disk-expander-12` that a 50,000 B budget used to refuse now comes
out, and a small-entry zip halved its peak.

The other three were closed on one command — `tools/run_budget.py` over the
whole corpus at a 400 KB cap, which says only StuffIt 5 ever refuses
anything — and **one of them was reopened within the hour**, because that
command was run in the wrong configuration.

**The correction, and it is the useful part of this entry.** 597,036 B is
what a 1 MB Mac Plus leaves the engine with the machine to itself. Under
MultiFinder, and on System 7 and later, the application gets a PARTITION
that the user sets in Get Info; `macextract.r` declares a 400 KB minimum, and
150 KB of that is the application's own code. Measured on System 7.0.1 with
the preferred size built down to 400 KB: **`FreeMem()` 212,056 B, budget
130,136 B** — four and a half times tighter.

At 130,136 B **Now Compress opened none of its three fixtures.** Its
131,072 B of block buffers are allocated once per archive, before an entry
is read, so the failure was not even per-entry: the archive was refused
whole, with a high-water of zero. The entry table carries each fork's
uncompressed size, and one stream carries both forks, so the bound is their
sum; sized that way the small fixture peaks at 84,248 B and opens. Listing
skips the allocation entirely now, because `l` needs no buffers and is the
one thing a user in a small partition can still usefully do.

**So the order of operations was right and the input was wrong.** "This
allocation is bigger than it needs to be" is true of all five and is not, by
itself, a reason to change any of them; *does anything actually fail for
want of this memory?* is the right next question. But a "nothing fails"
answer is only as good as the configuration it was taken in — and the
configuration a user can choose is not the one a test harness defaults to.
**Ask which machine the green result came from, and whether the user can
pick a worse one.**

ARC (33,732 B) and SpaceSaver (36 KB) still fit at 130,136 B and stay on
their ceilings, but that is now recorded as a fact about today's corpus and
today's minimum partition rather than as a property of those decoders.

### Why zlib stays

`NO_GZIP` and `Z_TESTW=0` take 8,704 B out of the application between them
and neither modifies the vendored source: the engine uses zlib for raw
inflate only, reads the gzip header and trailer itself, and computes its own
CRC, so the gzip-wrapper state machine and the braided CRC tables were never
reachable.

The transcribed replacement is **declined, not deferred**. It would recover
perhaps 10 KB more, and `inflate_fast` is 52.65 % of a gzip extraction
(measured) while the compact inflates worth transcribing are deliberately
bit-at-a-time and roughly an order of magnitude slower. That trades this
project's second priority for its third on the one format where the engine
does almost nothing itself. The thing to write, if it is ever revisited, is
not a smaller decoder but a raw-only one that keeps a table-driven fast
path — a project with a risk budget, not a cleanup.

### Checked and empty — recorded so nobody looks again

Each of these is a place a reader will otherwise re-propose.

- **The launch path.** Between double-click and the file dialog the
  application does six Toolbox inits, `InitCursor`, `FlushEvents`, one
  trap-availability test, and goes straight into `SFGetFile`. No resource
  preloading, no volume scan, no window built before the dialog — and the
  Segment Loader's own cost is ≈ 0.16 s of an 8.9 s boot (FORMATS.md,
  *Launch*).
- **The progress path.** `prog_tick` throttles to ~10 Hz off `TickCount` and
  returns before any drawing, so `ARS_PROGRESS_STEP` at 256 bytes costs a
  trap and a compare.
- **Opening an archive** deliberately calls `unc_segment_describe` rather
  than a full `unc_sniff`, to avoid walking a Compact Pro index twice on a
  7 MHz machine.
- **The compile line** is `-O3 -DNDEBUG -mcpu=68000 -Os ...`, where `-O3`
  comes from `CMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release` and `-Os` from the target options.
  **Last wins, so the application is built `-Os`**, whatever `flags.make`
  looks like at a glance.
- **Narrowing hot-path arithmetic to 16 bits** would help a 68000, and
  CLAUDE.md forbids it deliberately.
- **`ars_next_symbol`'s linear symbol search** averages **10.4 iterations
  per decoded symbol**, which is what kills replacing its division with a
  multiply per iteration: ten `__mulsi3` calls cost a 68000 more than one
  `__udivsi3`. The search is 9.9 % of the slowest run and has no obviously
  cheaper shape — a running cumulative array would have to be updated on
  every `ars_model_bump`.
- **`adler32.o`, 994 B.** Raw inflate never calls it; inflate.c still
  references it from the zlib-wrapper path, and cutting that would mean
  editing the vendored source.

---

## 2026-08-24 (the reserve) — a constant that suited one machine of the two

`kMemReserve` was a flat 80 KB from M10 and nothing recorded why. It is now
a quarter of `FreeMem()`, clamped to [32 KB, 80 KB].

**Claim (measured).** The two configurations this application runs in are
four and a half times apart: 678,956 B free with a 1 MB Mac Plus to itself,
211,960 B in the 400 KB minimum partition `macextract.r` declares. A flat
80 KB is 12 % of the first and **38 %** of the second.

**Evidence for what the reserve is worth.** A probe recording the lowest
`FreeMem()` seen during writes, across all 67 fixtures of the 1 MB sample:
the tightest moment left 121,124 B free, and the Toolbox and File Manager
together took **under 1 KB** while the engine sat at its peak.

**Consequence.** A quarter, capped at the old 80 KB, changes nothing on the
tier where every figure in FORMATS.md was measured — a quarter of 678,956
exceeds the cap — and gives the minimum partition 158,970 B instead of
130,136. That is what takes Now Compress from opening none of its three
fixtures there to opening all three, and StuffIt 5 from 10 to 15.

**Why a floor of 32 KB and not the kilobyte measured**, which is the part
worth transferring: the measurement is of the best case and the reserve
exists for the worst. A stripped System Folder, a RAM-backed disk and no
fragmentation are exactly the conditions under which a safety margin looks
unnecessary. **Measuring how much of a safety margin is used tells you what
it costs, not what it is for** — so the number sets the shape of the rule
(proportional, not constant) and the caution sets its floor.
MANUAL_TESTS.md carries the two things only iron can settle: a floppy and
an AppleShare volume with an extension loaded, and MultiFinder at the
declared minimum.

---

## 2026-08-24 (the icon) — four things the resources alone do not do

The icon family arrived as a generator: `tools/icon/make_icon.py`, which
draws an open carton lettered EXTRACT with a document and a block, and emits
`ICN#`, `ics#`, `icl4`, `ics4`, `icl8`, `ics8`, `FREF` and `BNDL` at ID 128.
Correct resources, and on their own they produced **nothing** — the Finder
drew the generic application diamond. Four separate things were missing, and
each one alone is enough to leave it looking exactly the same.

**How they were found is the point: by booting a real Finder and looking.**
`interactive.py --e2e` photographs the boot volume's window
(`e2e-2-volume.png`), and the icon was still the generic diamond after the
first fix, and after the second, and after the third. Nothing in the build
output ever said a word. A resource file that compiles, an application that
launches and an icon that never appears are perfectly compatible.

1. **`#include "Multiverse.r"`.** Retro68's Rez carries no built-in type
   declarations; without it the file stops at `Can't find type definition
   for 'ICN#'`. The only RInclude declaring these eight types.
2. **The creator code.** `add_application()` defaults it to `'????'`, and
   the Finder binds an icon by matching the `BNDL` owner to the creator. The
   generator stamps `'MExt'`, so the build has to as well.
3. **`hasBundle` (0x2000) in the file's Finder info.** Rez takes `-t` and
   `-c` and has no option for the flags, so a built application has
   `0x0000` and the Finder never looks at the `BNDL` at all.
   `tools/icon/set_bundle_bit.py` sets it after the build: MacBinary byte
   73, with the header CRC at 124 recomputed because byte 73 is inside the
   range it covers, and the `.dsk` **rebuilt** from the patched MacBinary
   rather than edited — `hcopy -m` carries the flags across, and hfsutils
   cannot set them in place (`hattrib -b` blesses a system folder, which is
   a different thing entirely).
4. **The signature resource** — type = the creator code, ID 0. The Finder
   wants it present to register an application's bundle. This was the last
   one, and with the other three already right the icon still did not
   appear until it was added.

And a fifth, on the harness side rather than the application's: the stock
System 6 image carries a **`Desktop` file** at its root, the Finder's cache
of which application owns which icon. `mkboot.py` deleted every root item
except the System Folder and left that one, because it enumerates with
`hdir` and `hdir` hides invisible files unless given `-a`. A user does the
same thing by holding Cmd-Option at startup; the harness now deletes the
file.

**The transferable lesson is about what "it built" is worth.** Every one of
these five produced a clean build, a working application, and the wrong
icon. The only instrument that could see any of them was a screenshot of a
real Finder — which is why `README.md` already requires that its screenshots
be real frames, and why this project keeps a harness that can take them.

**The COLOUR members were drawn for the first time on 2026-08-27**, and by a
tier that was being re-run for another reason entirely: Mini vMac emulates a
1-bit Mac Plus, so `ICN#` and `ics#` were all that had ever appeared. Mac OS
9.2 runs at 32-bit colour, and the Finder picks the deepest member it has —
`icl8` in the volume window (`docs/screens/0b-icon-os9-color.png`) and `ics8`
in the Standard File dialog's own list, both out of the sweep's ordinary
per-fixture screenshots. **The two 4-bit members `icl4` and `ics4` have still
been drawn by nothing, and no emulator here can draw them** — which was
measured rather than assumed, the same day and by the obvious two steps.
The guest's own Monitors control panel offers **one** colour depth,
"Millions", with "Grays" disabled: the depth cannot be changed from inside.
And booting the same disk with `-g 1024x768x4` does not reach the Finder at
all — it stops in OpenBIOS after *"unable to find a usable NVRAM
partition"*. A Finder draws the deepest member the screen can take, so at
32-bit it will always choose `icl8`. That pair therefore moved to
MANUAL_TESTS.md, where the things this machine cannot do live, rather than
staying an open item it could close.

---

## 2026-08-24 (the low-memory message) — saying the number, and where it can be graded

"Out of memory" told a user nothing they could act on: the memory size in
Get Info is a box wanting a number, and the application knew the number and
did not say it.

**Claim (measured).** `mal_alloc` refuses when `size + 16 > budget`, and the
difference is exactly how much larger the application's memory partition
would have had to be at that moment. It is kept as a run-level MAXIMUM,
because that is the one that has to be covered. The Mac alert rounds it up
to the next 8K — round enough to type, and never short:

    Needs 64K more memory. Give MacExtract more in Get Info.

**The two refusals are kept apart, because their fixes are opposite.** Over
budget means the partition is too small. A `NewPtr` that fails while the
budget still had room means the heap has no single piece that big, which a
bigger partition need not cure and quitting something else will — so that
one says so instead.

**Why it is measured rather than predicted, which is the whole point.** A
pre-flight check would have to model each format's sizing a second time, in
a second place, from header fields that can lie. This number comes from the
allocator at the moment it said no. It cannot disagree with what happened.

### Where the verification landed

The arithmetic is graded in `ci.sh`: `mac_sink_test --shortfall` asserts the
figure **exactly** rather than merely non-zero, so a wrong sign, a missing
16-byte header or a first-instead-of-largest would all fail it. It was
sabotaged to confirm it can: a 16-byte error takes the suite to exit 1.

**The alert is photographed** (2026-08-24, `interactive.py` default phase,
System 6.0.8, `derived-AuthoRize_2.0.sit`): *Needs 72K more memory. Give
MacExtract more in Get Info.* The method is in `tools/vmac/README.md`,
*Forcing a memory refusal on screen* — and the method is the transferable
part, because the obvious route does not work. Shrinking the `SIZE`
partition only bites under MultiFinder, i.e. System 7, and the default
phase's Standard File clicks are calibrated for System 6; on System 7 they
open the wrong item, so the application reports `UNC_ERR_FORMAT` on a file
that is not an archive (fixed 2026-08-25 — the clicks are per-system now, and
the wrong item turned out to be the wrong DIRECTORY: see that day's entry). Capping `ma.budget` in a throwaway build creates
the same refusal on the machine the harness can actually drive.

**The lesson is about instruments, not about memory.** Four emulator runs
went into this, and every one of them returned a plausible frame:
a *generic* failure alert looks exactly like the new one not firing, and so
does an alert about a file that was never opened. Before reading a
screenshot as evidence about a code path, make the harness *say* which path
it took — the run that settled it printed `ret`, `gShortBy`, `gShortPiece`,
`entries` and `errors` into the alert box, and `entries = 0` gave the answer
in one frame after three runs of guessing.

## 2026-08-24 (`Icon\r`) — an instrument that quietly normalised what it measured

**Claim (measured).** `checkdisk.walk`, which reads every name off an HFS
volume for both `run.py` and `interactive.py`, called `.rstrip()` on each
line of `hls` output. A carriage return is a legal HFS filename character —
and `Icon` + CR is what every custom folder icon on a Macintosh is named —
so a file that was present and correct was reported under a different name
and counted as missing. Reduced to a repro without an emulator: `hcopy` a
file to `T:Icon\r`, and `walk` returns `('Icon',)`.

**Consequence.** `interactive.py` had been reporting "phase 2 output
INCOMPLETE" for `derived-AuthoRize_2.0.sit`, naming `:AuthoRize 2.0:Icon`.
With the strip removed it reports COMPLETE — which also establishes, for the
first time, that the Mac application writes a filename containing a control
character correctly. Nothing else moved: dot-files, `/` inside a name, 8-bit
MacRoman and nesting were re-checked against the same repro.

**The lesson is the one this repository keeps relearning, in a new place.**
`.rstrip()` is defensive habit — remove whatever the line ends with — and it
is exactly wrong when the thing being measured may legitimately END with
whitespace. The suite could not fail on this and could not pass on it
either: it just reported a name that was never on the disk. **Ask of any
comparison what it NORMALISES before comparing, and whether the input is
allowed to contain what is being normalised away.**

**Why it survived.** No fixture in the graded 927 has a control character in
a name, so the suite that would have caught it was never handed the case.
The one fixture that does was picked, for something unrelated, today.


## 2026-08-25 (the System 7 UI) — the dialog is a different dialog, and where it opens

**Claim (measured).** The interactive application's cancel paths now run on
System 7.0.1 as well as 6.0.8: `ProgressPump()`'s `WaitNextEvent` side
repainting a percentage and an estimate, the Cancel button through
`DialogSelect`, Cmd-. through the keyDown check beside it, and the SFGetFile
repaint after the alert. Photographed 2026-08-25 with
`derived-Education7.1.cpt`, and the run's own name assertion — every output
name off the volume the Mac wrote to — reports COMPLETE.

**Two things stood between the harness and that tier, and only one of them
was known.** The known one is geometry: System 7 draws its own Standard File
dialog, with a directory pop-up above the list, and its first row sits ten
points higher, so every System 6 coordinate selects the second item. The
other is the current directory. System 7's Process Manager gives each
application a default directory of its own, so the boot-shell arrangement —
which is how every phase here installs the application — opens the dialog
inside the **System Folder**, listing `Finder`, `System` and the application.
The first row was not "the wrong row"; it was the right row of the wrong
directory, and the application dutifully reported `UNC_ERR_FORMAT` about
`Finder`. Desktop, then the volume, is two clicks and no menu tracking.

**Consequence beyond the UI.** The same run photographs System 7's Standard
File drawing `spinning brain ƒ` — a folder the application created from
MacRoman bytes — which is the only tier below Mac OS 9 where how a name
LOOKS had never been seen. The bytes were already checked everywhere; the
glyphs were not checked anywhere but System 6.

**The transferable part.** "The clicks are calibrated for System 6" was
written down (2026-08-24, the low-memory message) as a reason not to run
this tier, and it was true — but it named the cheaper half of the problem.
A harness that navigates by coordinate has TWO assumptions about every
dialog: where the items are, and what is in them. The first fails visibly;
the second produces a plausible frame of the wrong directory.


## 2026-08-25 (SuperDisk!'s second implementation) — the oracle was inside the corpus, and it runs on Linux

**Claim (measured).** Alysis's own decoders run under `qemu-m68k` on this
machine, in milliseconds, with no Macintosh anywhere: Disk Expander 1.2's
`Cmpr` resources are one Add-In Module per codec, 68k code that touches no
Toolbox, and `tools/sdmodule/` lifts all seven out of a corpus fixture and
calls them. Every method 1..9 now goes through two implementations in
`./ci.sh` — 4 corpus blocks, 14 blocks Alysis's own 1991 compressor wrote,
and synthetic blocks for the six methods no reachable file uses.

**It found a defect the moment it ran.** Method 9's Huffman region starts at
the block's LENGTH word, not after the six-byte header, and the method is the
low BYTE of the word above it — the high byte is that region's `startbits`.
Read as a 16-bit field a real method 9 block says 0x0809, so this engine
refused every one of them. BLOCKERS.md has the row and the cost.

### Why this was reachable and nobody reached it

The modules were FOUND on 2026-08-22 — DECISIONS.md that day lists the whole
method table, read out of these same resources — and used as a table of
names. What was not asked is whether they would RUN. They are
position-independent, they take a parameter block in A0, and the only thing
between them and a Linux process is that one of them ("Tighter") calls its
caller back to decode a sub-region: a `jsr` through a field of the parameter
block, which is address zero unless somebody puts a dispatcher there. That
callback is thirty lines.

**The general form is worth keeping:** a reference implementation for a 68k
format is often a subroutine, not an application, and a subroutine does not
need an emulated Macintosh — it needs a calling convention. `qemu-m68k` was
already in this repository's CI for the cipher vectors.

### What the harness cannot see, and why it says so out loud

A synthetic block is built from THIS engine's reading, so the Huffman regions
carry a flat 8-bit table nothing real would emit: what is graded is the
framing, the flag bitmap and the context predictor, not the code assignment.
And agreement is not correctness where both sides share one reading — a
framing error shows, a misunderstanding of what the original COMPRESSOR emits
does not.

**The one place the two references disagree is written down rather than
smoothed over.** When a coded flag bitmap ends exactly on a 32-bit refill,
the 1991 self-extractor and the 1992 module part company over where the
literals begin, by one refill. This engine follows 1991, which is what every
reachable specimen was written by. The harness counts those cases separately
and names them; a harness that had quietly skipped them would have looked
cleaner and known less.

**And the first thing that came of naming it was that its own "what would
close this" line was wrong.** It said: ask the 1991 compressor for a block
whose `ceil(ulen/8)` is a multiple of four. Four subjects were sized to do
exactly that (`sdcompress.py --boundary`), the panel wrote all four as method
6, and **both readers decode all four byte-exact**. The reason is the
predicate, not the format: the harness's synthetic Huffman is a flat 8-bit
code, so its bit length is eight times the byte count, where the real panel
codes each flag in ONE bit and the length is data-dependent. A byte count
that looks like a boundary is not one. The gap is still open; what would
close it is a real block measured to end on a 32-bit refill, and choosing a
subject size does not produce one.


## 2026-08-25 (SpaceSaver's INIT) — it installs, and it always did

**Claim (measured).** A boot of the installed image that never opens the
control panel leaves `System Folder:Preferences:SpaceSaver Preferences`
(`Pref/SMKR`, 10,496 B) on an image that had none. Two runs, one with the
extension's own install gate patched open and one without it, and both wrote
it. So `INIT 0` runs, and gets far enough to write its preferences, on this
machine — which is the opposite of what OPEN_ITEMS.md, PROGRESS.md's M24 line
and BLOCKERS.md had been saying since 2026-08-22.

### What the code says, which is what made the measurement worth taking

`~StuffIt SpaceSaver™` is a `cdev` carrying `INIT 0` and `INIT 1`. The INIT's
install path opens:

    2a50  link   a6,#-26
    2a5c  _GetKeys           ; the KeyMap at fp@(-22)
    2a66  _Button
    2a6a  bne.s  2a74        ; ...and 2a74 shows ICN# -4063 and RETURNS
    2a6c  btst   #0,fp@(-23) ; KeyMap byte 7 bit 0 = key code 56 = SHIFT
    2a72  beq.s  2a8c        ; not held -> on to the named checks

That is the ordinary "hold the mouse or Shift at startup to disable" bypass,
and it is the ONLY way this extension declines without saying so. Everything
else it can refuse for — no Gestalt trap (`STR# 1001` #1), no `PERS` resource
(#20), a 'SLip' Gestalt already registered (#8), a locked volume (#9), a full
one (#10), anything else (#3) — ends at @0x2c8a in **`NMInstall`** with the
message as `nmStr`, which is an alert once the Finder is up.

**So the twelve runs tabulated on 2026-08-22 already contained the answer.**
Every one of them photographed a clean desktop; not one showed an alert. A
refusal with a name could not have happened, and the only silent exit is a
bypass nobody was triggering. `--patch-init` turned that branch into a BRA on
a copy of the image to check the last possibility — that the emulator looked
like Shift was held — and the run is indistinguishable from the unpatched
one, down to the preferences file.

### The lesson, which is not about SpaceSaver

The question this repository had written down was *"whether `INIT 0` installs
at all"*, and it had been carried for three days as the thing to find out
next. It was answerable from resources already on the disk — a 13,638-byte
INIT and a `STR#` — without booting anything, and the boot that settled it
measured a FILE rather than a screenshot. **When a harness keeps coming back
with "nothing happened", ask what the software would have done if it had
refused.** Software that refuses usually says so; if the screen is clean, the
refusal is not where you are looking.

**What is left is the trigger, and it is now the whole question.** The
extension is installed and the subjects still come back byte-identical after
being renamed to end in `small`, which is the rule its own Compression page
states. The preferences file is written FRESH at every boot from an image
that has none, so what those defaults actually say — and whether the rule is
even enabled in them — is the next thing to read, and it can be read off the
image with `hcopy` rather than driven.


## 2026-08-25 (SuperDisk! method 10) — a codec with no data, graded three ways

**Claim (measured).** SlowFastA is implemented and correct against Alysis's
own decoder: 20 cases where the bytes a test encoder started from, this
engine's decode, and the module's decode are all equal, including the
wide-offset escape. `ci.sh` carries the check.

The format: a 32-bit control word ahead of every 32 items, MSB first, a set
bit being one literal byte and a clear one a two-byte match — high nibble the
length, low twelve bits the offset — copying `nibble + 2` bytes from
`out - offset + 1`, with a zero offset ending the block and a field of 0xF00
or more escaping to a wider one.

### Why this was worth doing at all, when nothing writes method 10

Because "an implementation could not be graded" had stopped being true. The
module is a decoder that runs on Linux (`tools/sdmodule/`), so a transcription
has an oracle whether or not a single archive in the world uses the method.
What is left is a scope question — three codecs no reachable file uses — and
10 is the one whose module runs end to end, so it is the one that landed.

### The two things the oracle caught, both invisible without it

- **The match length is `nibble + 2`, not `2*(nibble+1)`.** The copy is one
  unconditional `MOVEB` at 0xfc followed by a `DBF` whose target is the
  SECOND move at 0xfe — so the loop body is one byte. Read as a pair, the
  decoder agreed with this project's own test encoder on every case and with
  the module on none.
- **The wide offset is sixteen bits, not twenty.** `ASL.W #8` shifts the
  marker nibble out of the word, so the escape joins the field's LOW byte
  with the next one. Carried along, the marker would put every wide match
  about a megabyte behind the output — which no window reaches, and which a
  reading that never ran would have shipped.

**The lesson is the one this repository already had, in a new place: an
encoder and a decoder written from one reading agree with each other by
construction.** Three-way agreement is the cheapest way to make that
worthless — and it cost nothing here, because the third voice was a 302-byte
resource inside a fixture the suite already extracts.


## 2026-08-25 (SuperDisk! method 20) — the method that turned out to be an encoder

**Claim (measured).** There is no method 20 bitstream. Module 20
("Tennessee") answers `badCommandError` to the decompress command and
compresses on command 2, and every block it writes declares **method 11** in
its own header — five inputs from 4 KB of zeros to 30 KB of text, each
decoded back to the bytes that went in by this engine. It declines random
data with `inadequateCompressionError`, which is the code Alysis's published
header gives for exactly that.

So "implement method 20" was never a task. A block that carried 20 could not
be read by Alysis's own expander either: its dispatcher hands method 9 and up
to the module of that number (INIT 32 @0x50c8), which for 20 is this one, and
it refuses.

### What the work produced instead

**A compressor for method 11**, which nothing else in reach is. The corpus
has four SlowFastB blocks; this writes as many as anybody wants, and
`tools/sdmodule/` now grades the decoder against blocks Alysis's own encoder
made. A decoder that had only ever seen four blocks now sees any number.

### The shape of the mistake, which is the transferable part

The entry said "methods 10, 13 and 20 are declined because no fixture uses
them", and every re-reading of it — including this project's own, twice —
took *method* to mean a bitstream. It is a **module number**: the identity of
a codec in a library, and a codec may implement compression, decompression or
both. Two of the three turned out not to be what the word implied: 20
compresses only, and 13 does too. The list was three codecs with no data; it
was really one codec with no data, one codec with no decoder to need, and one
that was implementable all along.

**Ask what a number identifies before asking why nothing uses it.** Alysis's
own published header had it — the module table is `thisModuleNumber`,
`compressAddress`, `decompressAddress`, so half of an entry may simply be
absent — and this project had been reading that table as a method list since
the day it found it.

## 2026-08-25 (ARC 0x0a and 0x0b) — the wrong attribution, and an oracle that was wrong

Both methods now extract, graded against the CRC-16 their compressor wrote in
1988 and 1990. Three things about how that happened are worth carrying.

### The search named a program that never wrote them

`OPEN_ITEMS.md` asked for "a `.arc` written by PKARC or PKPAK using method
0x0a or 0x0b", and no such file exists: Crushed and Distilled are **PAK's**,
from NoGate Consulting, the second introduced in PAK v2.0 in July 1989.
PKARC's contribution to this list is method 9. The attribution was in four
files, and re-reading them confirmed itself each time.

One search for the FORMAT — not for the file — settled it, and the specimens
followed in minutes: `ARC2PAK1.PAK` (three Crushed members, 1988) and
`IE111SEP.ZIP` → `INTRECHO.PAK` (ten Distilled ones), both named by the
ArchiveTeam file-format wiki's PAK page, both a plain HTTP GET from
`cd.textfiles.com`. **A search that names the wrong tool returns nothing for
as long as anyone runs it, and the nothing reads as scarcity.**

### The oracle was wrong, and only the archive's own CRC could say so

`unar` — this project's primary oracle, and the reference `fmt_arc.c` is
transcribed from — decodes Distilled to the **right length and the wrong
bytes** on 6 of the 10 members of a real archive. XADMaster's
`XADARCDistillHandle.m` starts the 8 KB window at zero; PAK fills it with
spaces, as LZHUF.C does, and the compressor emits matches into it. Every
match that reaches back past the start of the data therefore returns NULs
where the file has spaces — which is why it is text members that fail and
binaries that pass.

deark implements the method independently and agrees with the stored CRC on
all 21 Distilled members in reach, so it is the reference this decoder
follows and the oracle the suite grades it with. Upstream XADMaster is
byte-identical to our `reference/` copy and untouched since 2018, so
transcribing the obvious reference would have shipped the bug.

**What makes this different from an ordinary transcription error is what
would have hidden it.** The engine's other ARC methods are graded by
generating archives and handing them to `unar`; that arrangement is what
caught the crunch decoder reading its codes LSB-first (2026-08-15). Here it
would have failed the other way round: a generator written from the same
reading, or `unar` itself, would have agreed with a wrong decoder, and the
entry's CRC — computed over the DECODED bytes — is the one participant that
could not be talked into it. **When the reference and the oracle are the same
codebase, the format's own checksum is not a formality; it is the only
independent party in the room.** The same sentence, from the other side, is
2026-08-08's: ask what evidence a tolerance rests on.

### The compressor still runs, and that is the corpus

Nothing on Linux writes either method, but the program that wrote both does:
**PAK 2.51 runs headless under DOSBox**, unpacked without root, and takes
`/CR` and `/O` to force Crushed and Distilled respectively
(`tools/pak.py`). That turns a two-specimen format into one with a corpus:
seven generated archives whose contents are chosen to reach the paths the
real specimens do not. Measured on the generated corpus, `pak-crushed-big.pak`
alone fires Crushed's table-full replacement scan **49,751 times** and its
inverted-literal mode **13,009 times**, neither of which either real
specimen reaches at all; `pak-distilled-text.pak` and the real archive
between them cover all seven tiers of the offset ladder and 12 matches into
the pre-filled window.

It also extracts, so the 1990 compressor grades its own output: the nine
members of the PAK distribution come back byte-for-byte identical whether
PAK 2.51 expands them under emulation or this engine does on Linux.

**The transferable part is that "nothing reachable writes it" is a claim
about reach, not about the format** — the same shape as DiskDoubler 3.7
under Mini vMac (2026-08-20) and SuperDisk!'s control panel (2026-08-22).
The third instance in five days; the question to ask first is now "what wrote
it, and where does that program still run?"

### What was NOT done, and why

Method 0x0a is ambiguous — ARC 7.x uses it for Trimmed, a different
algorithm. This engine decodes it as Crushed, and a Trimmed member fails its
CRC instead of extracting. deark disambiguates by looking for PAK extended
records versus ARC archive-info items, which is implementable, and it is not
implemented here: there is no Trimmed specimen to test either the decoder or
the heuristic against, and a discriminator that has never seen the case it
discriminates is a guess with a test suite. It is in OPEN_ITEMS.md as a
specimen wanted.

## 2026-08-25 (ARC 7.x Trimmed) — the gap filed without looking, and the codec already in the tree

Method 0x0a's other meaning now extracts too, hours after the entry saying it
could not was written. Four things are worth keeping.

### The open item was a claim, and it had no evidence

`OPEN_ITEMS.md` gained "ARC method 0x0a written by ARC 7.x… **what is missing
is a gradeable SPECIMEN**" on the same day, in the same session, as the
withdrawn claim directly above it — which was about a search that named the
wrong tool and therefore found nothing. Nobody had searched for a Trimmed
specimen at all.

Once asked, the answer took two minutes: the ArchiveTeam wiki lists ARC+Plus
7.10 and 7.12 with direct links, `ARC712.ZIP` is one HTTP GET, and **the
distribution is itself the specimen** — its own installer archive holds four
Trimmed members, and the nested `ARCDOS.ARC` holds three more, including
`ARC.EXE`, the writer.

**A gaps file is not exempt from the evidence rule.** Everything else in this
repository is labelled measured, hypothesis or withdrawn; an OPEN_ITEMS entry
asserting what is missing is a claim of the same kind, and "I did not look" is
not a finding. The mechanism is in BLOCKERS.md.

### The decoder was already here, twice over

Trimmed is LHarc's `-lh1-`: the same adaptive-Huffman tree of 314 leaves, the
same 64-entry distance code, the same 4 KB window. It differs in three
details — 0x100 is a stop code rather than a match of 3, so lengths run 3..59
instead of 3..60; and the window starts as **zeros** where `-lh1-` carries
XADMaster's preload of value runs, ramps and spaces. `src/unc_lzah.c` gained a
mode argument and nine lines; StuffIt method 5 and LZH `-lh1-` pass
`UNC_LZAH_LH1` and are untouched.

**That is the second time in one day that a "new" codec was a parameter on an
old one** — Distilled needed a window filled with spaces, Trimmed a window
filled with zeros, and both were single constants inside decoders this project
already shipped. Ask what an unfamiliar method is a DIALECT of before writing
it: deark's own structure said so out loud (`is_arc_trimmed` is a field in its
lh1 parameter block), and reading that saved the work.

### One method decides per ARCHIVE, and it is pushed rather than pulled

`arc_dialect()` walks the headers once and answers Crushed or Trimmed for the
whole file, on the two signals deark uses. The result rides in the `method`
int above the header byte, which is what lets it survive the password path —
that path decodes the same entry up to four times, and a per-entry decision
taken twice could differ.

The decode itself is the only PUSH path in `fmt_arc.c`: every other method is
pulled a byte at a time by `arc_byte()`, but `unc_lzah.c` drives its own loop
and is shared with two other formats. **Inverting a decoder three formats
depend on, to suit one method, would have put 930 fixtures at risk to save
fifteen lines** — so RLE90 is written a second time, in push form, next to the
pull version it mirrors.

### A copy with no decoder in it beats every oracle

The strongest single check on Trimmed is not deark and not the CRC. ARC+Plus's
distribution carries `XARC.EXE` compressed with Trimmed; a different CD
(`swsii/072/XARC.ZIP`) carries the same 21,174-byte file **uncompressed**. The
two are byte-identical, which is a statement no implementation had a hand in.

The same archive gave a lesson about what a CRC failure means: three of its
eight members fail, and **two of those are STORED** — a straight byte copy no
decoder can get wrong. deark fails exactly the same three. That copy of
`ARC712.EXE` is damaged, and the way to know it was to notice that the failing
set includes members with no codec in them at all. **When several entries fail
at once, look for one whose decoder does nothing**; if that one fails too, the
argument is about the file rather than the code.

## 2026-08-25 (zip entry counts) — a count that wraps into success

A forum thread claimed no Mac OS 9 application can unpack a full mirror of
`ftp.apple.com`, not even StuffIt Expander 7.0.3. Asking what OUR ceiling is
turned up four limits, three of them harmless and one a defect.

**The defect: a zip with 70,000 entries listed and extracted 65,535 of them
and exited 0.** `unzip` and CPython's `zipfile` both say 70,000. The
end-of-central-directory record counts entries in 16 bits, and past 65,535 the
real count lives in a Zip64 record — so the field does not overflow into an
error, **it wraps into a smaller archive that is internally consistent**. Every
other limit measured the same day fails loudly: a container past 4 GB stops
the walk with `UNC_ERR_CORRUPT` and a non-zero exit, a tar member past 2 GB is
refused by an explicit guard. This one produced a clean success with 4,465
files missing.

**Why nothing here could have caught it.** The largest zip in 932 fixtures has
**29 entries**. No robustness variant reaches 65,536 either, because the
variants perturb a fixture rather than grow one. A suite built entirely from
harvested files cannot fail on a property no harvested file has, and the
corpus is 65,506 entries short of the boundary.

**Two different projects wear the name "Zip64", and only one of them was
taken.** Recovering a COUNT needs no 64-bit arithmetic: the entry total, the
central directory's size and its offset are read as two `uint32`s each, and
the high half is only ever compared against zero. Addressing a file that is
actually larger than 4 GB is the other project — it puts 64-bit offsets in
`UncIO` and every format pays for them, on a 68000 where each 64-bit operation
is a libgcc call. So the record is read for its counts, and any field whose
high half is set returns `UNC_ERR_UNSUPPORTED` cleanly. Zip64 proper stays out
of scope (OPEN_ITEMS.md, part 1), and the floor target pays nothing.

**What was deliberately not implemented.** XADMaster carries a kludge for
writers that exceed 65,535 entries WITHOUT emitting a Zip64 record: if the
central directory has room for another 65,536 records, it assumes there are
some and keeps going. It is a heuristic about broken files, and nothing here
has one to grade it against — the same bargain this project declines
everywhere else. Named here so the next reader knows it was seen and passed
over, not missed.

**The general lesson, and it is not about zip.** Ask of any width in a format
whether exceeding it produces an ERROR or a smaller valid structure. A length
that overflows is caught by the next checksum; a COUNT that saturates is
consistent with everything downstream of it, so nothing catches it. The other
16-bit count this engine reads is Compact Pro's `nentries`, and there the
field IS the format's ceiling: no second record exists to consult, so 65,535
is a limit of `.cpt` rather than a reading of one. This is the same shape as the
gzip/.Z fallback NAME defect (CLAUDE.md): an aggregate that agrees cannot fail
on what it never counted.


## 2026-08-25 (SpaceSaver's codecs, off the Macintosh) — the specimen problem, solved by the compressor

Methods 1, 2 and 3 were declined per entry on one claim: nothing
reachable WRITES them, so an implementation could not be graded
(2026-08-22, the codecs). That claim was about the ENGINE's choice of
method — index 4 below a 68030, index 3 above, and 1 and 2 never — and
it was read as if it were about the codecs. It is not. **The `CMod`
modules compress as well as expand, and they run on Linux.**

`tools/ssmodule/` is that, and it is `tools/sdmodule/` applied to a
second format three days later: lift the modules out, call them under
`qemu-m68k`, and use them as both the specimen generator and the second
implementation. Every method the format has now round-trips through
Aladdin's own code in **3 seconds**, with no Macintosh in it.

### What had to be read rather than guessed

- **The seven callback slots are NAMED by the engine's own MacsBug
  symbols.** `ExpandFork` installs `cReadSource`, `cWriteDest`,
  `cGetSourceFPos`, `cSetSourceFPos`, `cGetSourceEOF`, `cProgress` and
  `cDebug` at +0..+24 — so the block's shape is Aladdin's own
  vocabulary, not an inference from how many arguments a thunk pushes.
- **`cReadSource` takes its count by reference and `cWriteDest` by
  value.** Reading both as FSRead-shaped is the first crash.
- **Every vector takes two arguments**, the parameter block and the
  private block the module's own `init` allocated (`LoadCompressors`
  @0x11a0 keeps one per module; `mExpandFile` @0x57e6 passes it).
  `CMod 10` reads five fields out of it immediately, so Alg 3 called
  with one argument dereferences the stack.
- **Alg 3 needs the preset dictionary as much as Alg 4 does** — with a
  null pointer it faults outright, and a stream compressed with it
  decodes wrong without it. Methods 1 and 2 never touch those fields.

### Two properties of the "Original" modules, and why neither is patched over

**Their store-this-block path is an F-line word.** `CMod 3` @0x5e4 and
`CMod 4` @0x6ba execute `0xFFFF`, which no 68000 can, on the branch that
gives up on a block; about a third of the subjects reach it. What that
word stands for cannot be read off the code, so nothing is emulated for
it — the run is counted and every other kind of fault is a failure.
The path is covered from the other side instead: a stored block built
by hand is returned verbatim by both expanders, which is how its layout
is known (flag byte 1 at +0, big-endian payload length at +4, payload at
+8). Guessing at `0xFFFF` would have been inventing a format detail;
asking the expander what a stored block looks like is not.

**`CMod 3` does not terminate on a corrupted stream.** It spins rather
than refusing, which is worth knowing twice over: every module call
needs a timeout, and the decoder this project will write must bound
itself by the fork's declared length rather than trusting the blocks.

### The lesson, which is about the CONTROL and not about SpaceSaver

The negative controls were built by picking a case out of the sweep, and
the pick was wrong twice in a row:

| picked | why it could never fail |
|---|---|
| the largest stream | that is the random subject — all literals, so it decodes identically with no dictionary at all |
| the best-compressing stream | that is 40,000 zeroes, which decode to zeroes however the stream is damaged |

Both printed a passing control. The fix is not a better heuristic: the
control subject is now one deliberate file — ordinary prose,
compressible, no long runs — and the phase refuses to trust either check
unless that subject actually compressed. **A control whose subject is
chosen by an extremum is choosing the degenerate case**, which is the
same failure as a tolerance whose evidence is our own output
(CLAUDE.md).

## 2026-08-25 (SpaceSaver methods 1 and 2) — two codecs the day the specimens arrived

The entry above ends with the modules running. This is what that was
for: methods 1 and 2 are implemented, and 34 forks Aladdin's own
compressors wrote read back byte-exact (FORMATS.md has the shapes).
Both were transcribed from `CMod 3` @0x634 and `CMod 4` @0x6e8 and
graded by generating specimens with the matching compressor — the loop
M32 built for SuperDisk! and this format could not use until today.

Three things are worth keeping, and none of them is the codec.

### The oracle finds what a reading cannot

Method 1 was right first time. Method 2 was wrong twice, and neither
bug was visible in the disassembly:

- **A code whose table entry was never filled copies the module's own
  copyright string.** The table is initialised with a pointer to
  `CMod 4`'s last eighteen bytes, and five of eight subjects reach one.
  Reading that as "cannot happen" would have been the natural
  assumption; the compressor disproved it in a second.
- **A flag word of exactly `0x0001` looks like an exhausted one.** The
  sentinel lives above the sixteen flag bits, and the module tests it
  once per item at the loop head — never again for the item that
  follows a reload. Testing it again costs sixteen items, and the
  OUTPUT STAYS CORRECT until the first of those items is a match, which
  is why it survived 387 bytes of agreement before failing.

### How the second one was actually found

Reading harder would not have found it: the table maintenance and the
hash were both confirmed correct by direct probe (build a stream of
sixteen literals and one match, sweep all 4,096 codes through the
module, and compare what each one copies — it agreed everywhere).

What found it was **`qemu-m68k -one-insn-per-tb -d exec`**: log every
instruction the module executes, keep the two program counters that are
the literal branch and the match branch, and diff that string against
the same string from our decoder. The first difference was event 290 of
306, and it named the bug exactly — the module decoded a match where we
reloaded a second flag word. An execution trace is a cheap oracle for
"which branch did it take", and this repository now has the recipe.

### The general shape, which is not about SpaceSaver

**A decoder that agrees for a while is not a decoder that agrees.**
