Both of method 2's bugs produce correct output until a specific,
uncommon input turns up: an unfilled dictionary entry, or a flag word
whose value is 1. Neither is rare enough to be safe and neither is
common enough to show up in a small test. The sweep that caught them
runs eleven subjects through every method in three seconds, which is
the whole argument for making specimens cheap rather than precious.

## 2026-08-25 (SpaceSaver method 3) — the codec whose code assignment is a quicksort

Alg 3 is implemented, so every method StuffIt SpaceSaver has now
decodes. FORMATS.md has the shape; this is the two things in it that
would not have been guessed, and the one that changes how the next
codec should be approached.

### The code assignment is a sort, and the sort is not stable

`CMod 11` @0x27c assigns canonical codes in the order @0x15a's
quicksort leaves the code lengths in — a Hoare partition pivoting on
the first element, recursing into the smaller half. Among symbols of
equal length, which one gets which code is therefore a property of
that partitioning and nothing else. Both sides of the format run the
same routine, so it is self-consistent; **any other implementation has
to reproduce the sort itself.** This one does, iteratively, because the
engine forbids recursion (CLAUDE.md) — the smaller half goes on an
explicit 32-entry stack.

It is worth naming the trap because it is invisible from the outside: a
decoder that sorts stably decodes most blocks correctly, because most
blocks have few ties.

### The tables are rules, not data

The length and distance bases look like the sort of thing that gets
transcribed as a table of magic numbers. They are not: the module's own
init computes them (@0x1a04, @0x1aa0), `extra[i] = i < 4 ? 0 :
(i - 4) >> 2` from a base of 0 for lengths and the same shape from 1
for distances. Reading the init rather than dumping the arrays is what
makes them checkable, and it is why nothing new had to be carried in
`ss_dict.h` for this method.

### The order that made it cheap

**Prototype in Python against the module, then port.** The prototype
decoded ten subjects byte-identically before a line of C was written,
and the C then worked on its first run — which is not how method 2
went, at two bugs and an execution trace. The difference was not the
codec's difficulty. It was that a Python prototype can be rewritten in
a minute while a hypothesis is still cheap, and that the module was
sitting there to grade each attempt in three seconds.

The generalisation for the next one: **when a reference implementation
can be RUN, the expensive step is no longer reading it — it is the
round trip between a guess and a verdict.** Shorten that first.

## 2026-08-26 (an encrypted StuffIt 5 archive) — the pointer that was said not to work

The open item asked for a specimen written by Aladdin, because the two
readings adjudicating our RC4 were `unar` and this project's own generator,
which is not two readings at all. It is closed: **DropStuff 5.0.1 wrote
three** on Mac OS 9.2, and all three come back byte-exact (FORMATS.md,
*StuffIt 5's RC4*).

Four things are worth carrying forward.

### Position and click are different primitives, and conflating them cost the tier

The 2026-08-02 probe measured five clicks in Mac OS 9 and got 0.0000 from
every one, and the conclusion written down was "the pointer does not work".
Every one of those clicks fired with the cursor pinned in a screen corner,
because the harness made an ABSOLUTE `usb-tablet` the active device — which
Mac OS 9 has no driver for — and because a relative mouse saturates at ±63 px
per ADB packet, so one big delta never arrives anywhere. The probe never
established that it could POSITION the cursor before it tested clicking, so
its zeros were a fact about the cursor's position and not about the button.

The rule this suggests is narrow and reusable: **establish the primitive one
level down before measuring the one you care about**, and make the first
target something that MUST respond — here, a modal alert's default button,
whose disappearance is unambiguous. Two of this run's own click tests landed
on a menu bar that a modal was suppressing, and those zeros meant nothing
either.

### What defeats frame-differencing, and what beats it

Locating a cursor by diffing screenshots fails on anything else that moves:
the menu-bar clock ticks, a text caret blinks, a progress bar fills. Masking
"pixels that changed between two idle reference frames" is not enough — a
caret's phase can alias with any fixed sampling interval, which it did, and
the locator then reported "cursor not seen" inside every dialog with a text
field. What works is **shape**: cluster the changed pixels and take the
largest blob, because an arrow is ~100 pixels and a caret is a sliver.

A corollary the harness now depends on: positioning costs ~40 s, which is
longer than a classic sticky menu stays open. Command keys have no timeout;
menus are not worth driving.

### An application can refuse the FILE SYSTEM, not the OS version

Deluxe 5.0.1 is documented for System 7.5.3 through Mac OS 8.5, so the
expectation was that Mac OS 9.2 might reject it. It installs there without
complaint. What fails is the Deluxe application at launch: *"an update to
this software is required for compatibility with the improved Mac OS file
system"* — it predates HFS+, and the boot volume is HFS+. **DropStuff, from
the same installer and using the same StuffIt Engine, runs unaffected**, so
the specimen was written by the tool that had no such problem. Reading the
version requirement would never have predicted either half.

### A nag whose buttons move is an automation hazard, not an inconvenience

DropStuff's evaluation notice shuffles its four buttons on every appearance.
A fixed "Not Yet" coordinate therefore hits "Order Info" sooner or later,
which opens an order-form save dialog that swallows every subsequent
keystroke — including the ones meant for the password prompt. One specimen
cycle was lost that way, and it looked like a timing problem. The harness now
matches the button's LABEL against a template before clicking it.

## 2026-08-26 — base64 and MIME, and an oracle that is wrong three times

Asked for — and **already filed**, which the session that implemented it did
not notice until afterwards. OPEN_ITEMS.md's part 1 does not mention base64,
so it was never out of scope; part 2's then-section 6 ("Not implemented, and
nothing blocks it" — emptied and deleted on 2026-08-26 when its last entry,
the disk images, was closed) had named it since 2026-08-25, raised from the Macintosh
Garden forum thread *Archive formats* — with the reference already identified
(deark's `modules/xfer.c`, in `reference/` for ARC) and the grading bargain
already proposed. That entry is now deleted, the format being read.

**The lesson is CLAUDE.md's own, and it still cost a search.** "Read
OPEN_ITEMS.md before starting work on a format" was followed for part 1 and
the head of part 2; the one section that had the answer was below a truncated
grep. Everything in it was then re-derived from scratch — the same reference,
the same oracle, the same `.b64` framing. **Cost:** no wrong code, but an
afternoon of searching for what one paragraph already said, and a status file
that spent one commit contradicting itself (section 2 waiting on a specimen
for a format that section called unimplemented).

The brief has grown three times before on request (ARC and uuencode,
SuperDisk!, btoa), and this is the fourth.

**Why it belongs here rather than in the "modern PC formats" row.** base64 is
what BinHex, uuencode and btoa all became. A Macintosh file that travelled by
mail after about 1993 travelled as `multipart/appledouble`: an
`application/applefile` part carrying the resource fork, the real name and
the Finder info, then the data fork. That is a classic-Mac transport encoding
with a resource fork in it, which is exactly the line OPEN_ITEMS.md draws
around what a modern extractor is not the right tool for.

**The reference, and what it costs.** mpack/munpack 1.6 (John G. Myers,
Carnegie Mellon, 1994) is where the format's Macintosh side came from — its
own tree holds `macfile.c`, `macos.c` and `README.mac`, the port that wrote
these messages. The CMU licence is permissive, so unlike btoa (2026-08-23,
*a reference that may not be transcribed*) this one may be **transcribed**,
and `src/fmt_mime.c` is: the header rule, the parameter parser, the boundary
rule, `from64`, `fromqp`, and the order the filename is looked for in.

### An explicit stack where the reference recurses

`handleMessage` calls `handleMultipart` calls `handleMessage`. The engine may
not recurse at all (CLAUDE.md), so the walk keeps a stack of at most four
boundaries and loops: parse a part's headers, find the next boundary line,
emit or skip, pop the levels that boundary closed. The file is walked
linearly either way — a part ENDS at the next boundary — so nothing is lost,
and the reference's own rule that an enclosing boundary closes everything
inside it falls out of scanning the stack outermost-first, as `pendingboundary`
does.

The walker is ~2 KB of struct, which on the stack would have been the
deepest frame in the engine. It goes in the same heap block as its buffers
(`unc_mime_extract`, one allocation), and the chain the format adds is under
1 KB — measured, not assumed, with `-fstack-usage` (FORMATS.md, *Stack*).

### The oracle is wrong in three places, and each one has a reproduction

Grading against munpack found three divergences. Each was reduced to a
minimal case before being called a finding, which is rule #2:

1. **It never uses a `Content-Disposition` filename.** `getDispositionFilename`
   matches the token `filename` and then requires the next non-space
   character to be `';'` — where the `'='` is. It falls through to the next
   `';'`, finds none, and returns null, so every such part is written as
   `part1`. Reproduction: one part, `Content-Disposition: attachment;
   filename="b.bin"`, base64 body; munpack writes `part1`. We follow mpack's
   own documented order (disposition first, `name` second), which is also
   RFC 2183's.
2. **It cannot read the CRLF form a mailer writes.** `ParseHeaders` ends the
   headers on a line that is `"\n"`; with CRLF that line is `"\r\n"`, so the
   blank line is pushed back and becomes the first two bytes of the body, and
   a quoted-printable soft break `=\r\n` is decoded as a bad hex escape,
   inventing one byte per line. mpack is a text-mode reader on a system where
   CRLF has already become LF. This engine reads bytes and handles both, so
   the suite gives each implementation the form it was written for.
3. **It drops a decoded `\r`.** `fromqp` writes the byte only `if (c !=
   '\r')`. But `=0D` is an explicit encoding of a byte, and dropping it
   corrupts any binary attachment that contains one. We keep it, and
   `run_mime.py` asserts it with a subject munpack is not asked to grade.

None of the three is a bug in the transcription; all three are the reason
"graded by a second implementation" has to mean *and the disagreements are
adjudicated*, not *and they matched*.

### deark and mpack disagree about a group that ends short, and deark is right

A base64 stream that stops mid-group implies bytes: two characters are one
byte, three are two. deark writes them with a warning; mpack drops them
("Premature EOF"). deark's reading is followed, because the alternative
loses real data from a file that is otherwise fine — and a truncated stream
is not detectable anyway. base64 carries no length and no checksum, so a
message cut in half decodes short and silently, exactly as a uuencoded set
missing its middle part does (OPEN_ITEMS.md). The suite asserts that outcome
rather than describing it.

### base64 unwraps as an envelope; MIME does not

`.sit.b64` should come out as the archive, so `UNC_FORMAT_BASE64` joins
MacBinary, BinHex, AppleSingle/Double, gzip and `.Z` in both hosts'
envelope lists. `UNC_FORMAT_MIME` deliberately does not, though a `.mim`
usually holds an archive too: the unwrapper DELETES the layer it consumed,
and a MIME part can carry a resource fork that would go with it. base64 has
one anonymous stream and cannot.

### The probe claims a message only when there is something to extract

Mail headers are not evidence: a BinHex posting and a uuencoded one both
have them, and both are files this project already reads. The MIME probe
therefore wants a `Content-Type` **and** a boundary, a filename, or a
base64/quoted-printable encoding; and both new probes run LAST in
`unc_sniff`, after BinHex, uuencode and btoa. A mail message with a `.hqx`
in its body stays a BinHex file — asserted in `run_mime.py`, because probe
order is the kind of thing that changes by accident.

Bare base64 has no marker at all, so its probe leans on shape: four or more
full lines of one width between 40 and 76, every character in the alphabet,
and a last line that closes the stream — head AND tail, because a file whose
head is base64-shaped and whose end is not is something else with an
attachment in it. Measured at **0 false positives across all 940 fixture
files**, and the diff and robustness suites (932 fixtures, 9,320 variants)
are unchanged with both probes in place. The cost is that a base64 file too
short to have a shape cannot be recognised bare; with the `begin-base64`
marker it can.


## 2026-08-26 (the btoa translator, by Apple event) — the door nobody tried

The btoa decoder had been graded against one encoder since it was written:
`reference/btoa` 5.2.1, compiled here. The independent encoder was known and
was 30 cm away — Aladdin's `btoa/atob Translator`, installed on the same disk
image StuffIt Deluxe 3.5.1 runs from — and three attempts to reach it had
failed, because all three asked the same question: **how do I click that
menu item?** The item is in a hierarchical submenu, submenu items cannot be
selected under Mini vMac, and the answer to the question was correctly "you
cannot". The item was never the only way to run the code behind it.

### What the application says it will do is in its own resources

Four readings off the disk image with `hcopy`, no emulator, in about ten
minutes:

- `MENU` 132 (`Translate`) holds two items and neither is a translator: the
  translator entries are built at RUNTIME from the files in
  `Extensions:Aladdin:Translators`, so there was never a `MENU` resource to
  patch a Command key into either.
- The `aete` declares a `translate` event — `SIT!`/`TRAN`, direct object an
  alias, `trnm` the translator's name, `encd` the direction, `dest` the
  destination — beside `stuff`, `unstuff`, `segment`, `join` and `do script`.
- `SIZE` -1 is `0xd8e8`: isHighLevelEventAware **and** localAndRemoteHLEvents.
  The application accepts events; nothing had asked it to.
- Aladdin's own `Translate Sample`, a compiled script in `Scripting Tools`,
  contains the literal bytes `.SIT!TRANnull … alis … trnm`. The vendor's
  sample sends exactly the event that was wanted.

The generalisation is the entry's title. **A route that a harness cannot
drive is not the same list as a route the application does not have**, and
the second list is written down inside the application. This project already
reads resource forks for a living; it had simply never pointed that at the
question of how to make a program DO something.

### Apple events need no scripting language

The image runs System 7.0.1 with no AppleScript on it, and the reflex answer —
install AppleScript, or drive the Script Editor — would have added an OSA
dependency to a 68000 Mac Plus. It is not needed: the Apple Event Manager is
part of System 7 itself, and any application can send an event. `tools/aetran/`
is a ~400-line Retro68 application that finds its own folder through
`GetProcessInformation`, launches StuffIt Deluxe with `LaunchApplication`,
sends `SIT!`/`TRAN`, and writes the reply's `errn` to a text file. One
type-select and one ⌘O in the Finder is the entire on-screen part of the run.

### The dictionary says what the terms mean, not what the handler accepts

The first send came back `-1700`, `errAECoercionFail`, for an event built
exactly as the `aete` describes. Rather than guess again at a cost of one boot
per guess, the sender was given a table of six shapes and sent them all in one
boot, logging each reply. The answer: `dest` as an `fss ` naming the
not-yet-existing output FILE is the only shape that fails; every shape naming
an existing FOLDER and every shape naming no destination at all succeeds. A
destination is a place. And `encd` is the direction with a default of DECODE —
the shapes that omitted it left a zero-byte `Subject.dec` behind, which is the
sort of evidence a single-shape run would have had to infer.

**Send the matrix, not the guess**, whenever the round trip is minutes and the
parameter space is small. It is the same economy as `--layout` reading a
container out of a control panel instead of booting it.

### What the specimen was worth

The stream that came back uses the older `xbtoa Begin` framing, MacBinary-wraps
the file before encoding it, and is byte-identical to `reference/btoa -o` over
the same payload **except for the width of the End line's `R` field** — eight
hex digits where this host writes sixteen, because `Crot` is a `long` and the
reference's rotate assumes that is 32 bits. FORMATS.md had that difference
written down as reasoning; it is now measured, and `run_btoa.py` asserts the
narrow form in `ci.sh`.

It also showed something the suite could not: a real btoa file from a Mac is
`X.MBin.ascii`, an armour around a MacBinary wrapper, and `macextract x` stops
at the wrapper because btoa is not in the envelope auto-unwrap list — open
then, closed since, btoa and uuencode both being envelopes now.
Every layer reads; the chain does not. That is
what one file from outside your own toolchain is for.

## 2026-08-26 (disk images) — read the volume, not the extension

The open item asked for seven extensions to be named: `.dmg`, `.iso`, `.cdr`,
`.toast`, `.img`, `.smi`, `.cpgz`. It reads as seven jobs and it is not: an
extension is not evidence, this engine has never sniffed one, and the file
behind six of those names is the same thing — a byte-for-byte image of a
volume. So the probe reads the volume. Four structures cover the list: an HFS
master directory block, an HFS+/HFSX volume header, an Apple Partition Map,
and an ISO 9660 descriptor pair in any of its four sector layouts (FORMATS.md,
*Disk images*, has each one and its source).

**Every one of the four came out of `reference/deark`,** whose `hfs`, `apm`
and `iso9660` modules already identify exactly these structures — the same
tree that has been the third oracle for ARC since M33. The reference was in
the repository before the item was filed; what the item called "a reference
this project does not yet have" was a directory nobody had grepped. That is
the second time this month (base64, the same file, the same week).

**The item's own framing was wrong in a useful way.** It said `.smi` and
`.cpgz` "have something to match" while a raw `.iso` or `.img` "has no header
of its own at all". Backwards: `.cpgz` is gzip and was already named, `.smi`
is NDIF whose data fork carries no signature anywhere, and the raw images are
precisely the ones with a structure to find — because the volume is the
structure. **An item that reasons about file NAMES is worth re-reading before
it is worked on.**

### What is corroborated, and what a 16-bit signature is worth

Three of the four branches carry a second field, because deark's own identify
functions do: HFS wants an allocation block size that is a nonzero multiple of
512, APM wants a map entry in sector 1 *and* in sector 2, ISO 9660 wants a
descriptor at sector 16 *and* at sector 17. HFS+ has nothing — deark reads
`"H+"` and parses no further, and rule #1 forbids inventing the field at
offset 40 from memory. What corroborates it here is not a format detail at
all: **an image of a disk is a whole number of 512-byte sectors**, so the
file's own length has to be one. That is a claim about images, not about
HFS+, and it is labelled as ours in the source.

### The negative case has to be written the way the probe is entered

`run_diskimage.py`'s APM negative first zeroed the block at 1024 — and a
mutant with the sector-1 read deleted passed it, because 1024 is where the
branch *starts*: spoil it and the branch is never entered, so the missing
check is never reached. Zeroing 512 instead kills that mutant. All four
corroborations were then mutation-tested one at a time, which is the only
reason this is known. **A negative test proves nothing about a check the
input never reaches**, and the way to find out is to delete the check and
watch.

### The specimens were already on the machine

138 real disk images: Retro68's 800K test volumes, every emulator tier's HFS
and Apple-partitioned disk up to the 2 GB Mac OS 9 image, two 1.44 MB
Macintosh Garden floppies, and a 224 MB Toast hybrid CD that turns out to be
Apple-partitioned rather than ISO 9660. No download, and no synthetic image
standing in for a real one — the only generated positives are the ISO cases,
because nothing here writes a CD (`reference/pycdlib` does, and deark and
`lsar` both confirm what it wrote).

### The tier figures were describing a smaller sample than the file held

Re-running the three Mini vMac tiers for a binary change is routine here, and
this time it returned **69/69** where every status file said 67/67. Nothing
regressed: M33 and M34 added two ARC fixtures to `sample.txt` on 2026-08-25
and re-ran only those two, so the sweep figures had quietly become claims
about a 67-fixture subset of a 69-fixture list. Corrected in place
(BLOCKERS.md, the tier table; FORMATS.md, the three tier sections).

**A sample list is a number two places carry**, the file and the write-up,
and only one of them changes when a fixture is added. The cheap guard is the
one that caught it: the sweep prints its own denominator, so run the whole
list rather than the fixture you added.

The two things still unnamed are **compressed** images, and the NDIF one is
measured rather than assumed: the one NDIF file here opens straight into
compressed data, its chunk map is a `bcem` resource, and its fork is not even
a whole number of sectors. UDIF wants a reference — and the entry below
then took disk images out of scope altogether (OPEN_ITEMS.md, part 1).

## 2026-08-26 — a name is only worth something inside the frame the user came with

Yesterday's disk-image probe was removed the day after it shipped, and nine
PC-side archive formats were named in its place. Both halves come out of one
observation, which arrived as a question about the first: *a known format that
is not extracted is treated exactly the same way an unknown one is — nothing
is extracted either way, and it does not change what the user can do about
it.*

**That was true as stated, and measurable.** On the Macintosh application the
two paths were byte-identical: no output, and *"This file could not be
extracted."* — the same sentence a corrupt archive gets. The envelope-unwrap
chain behaves the same for both (`!unc_format_extractable(inner)` breaks the
loop either way). The whole difference was one line of CLI text and the error
code a library caller sees, `UNC_ERR_UNSUPPORTED` against `UNC_ERR_FORMAT`.
640 bytes of 68000 code for that.

**Where it stops being true is the user's frame.** Somebody who opens an
unarchiver is telling you they believe they have an archive. Inside that
frame a name is an instruction: *"MacExtract cannot open RAR files"* says the
download is fine and sends them to a program that opens it, where *"not
recognized"* says the file is broken and sends them to download it again.
Outside that frame a name is trivia: a disk image is not an archive, so
naming one leaves the user exactly where silence does — a different program
either way, and nothing this one can do.

So the test for detect-only is not "can we identify it?" but **"is the user
already in the frame where the name is an instruction?"** Archives yes, disk
images no. Disk Copy 4.2 had been detect-only since M9 and went out with the
new probe, for the same reason and on the same day.

### Naming the archives is nine `if`s, and the references were already here

`reference/XADMaster` recognises RAR (three generations), 7-Zip, xz, bzip2,
Cabinet, Zoo and ARJ; `reference/deark` recognises ar, cpio, Zoo and ARJ.
Every constant in `src/fmt_foreign.c` is transcribed from one of those, with
the function it came from named in the comment. Five formats stayed out for
having no reference in the tree — zstd, lzip, brotli, lrzip, WIM — and rule #1
does not bend for a magic number that would be easy to look up (OPEN_ITEMS.md,
part 2).

**A specimen that the oracle cannot parse proves nothing about a probe.**
bzip2, xz, 7z, cpio and ar are written here by their own tools, and the suite
then asserts that **p7zip opens each one** before believing the probe's
answer about it. Zoo, ARJ and Cabinet are hand-built headers that deark must
identify. RAR and RAR 5 have no oracle in `ci.sh` at all — no writer here, no
deark module, and p7zip declines to name a stub it cannot parse — so they were
measured once against real archives out of libarchive's test suite, which this
engine's own uuencode reader decoded, and the docs say so rather than implying
a suite covers it.

### The alert is what makes any of this real

The argument above only holds if the sentence reaches the user, and until
today it did not: the Mac shell had never said what a detect-only file was,
for any of the five formats it could name, since M9. It does now, and it is
photographed on System 6.0.8 — **"MacExtract cannot open RAR files."** The
string is built from `unc_format_name`, so the longest name in the table draws
46 characters against the ~60 the alert wraps at.

**The code was moved rather than deleted.** `attic/` keeps the disk-image
probes, their unit tests and their suite verbatim, with a README saying what
putting them back would take, because nothing about them was wrong: 135 real
images named, 0 false positives, four branches each mutation-tested. What
changed was the answer to whether they belonged in the product, and that is a
different fact about the same code. `UNC_FORMAT_DISKCOPY_RETIRED` stays in the
public enum as a reserved slot so that no enumerator after it moved.

## 2026-08-26 — the project is renamed MacExtract, and the API is not

The project name became **MacExtract**. Everything a person reads changed with
it: the documents, the Linux CLI (`macextract`), the application as the Finder
shows it (`MacExtract`), the two downloads (`dist/MacExtract.bin`,
`dist/MacExtract.dsk`) and the alert strings the Mac shell builds.

**The `unc_*` / `UNC_*` / `Unc*` prefix deliberately did not change**, and the
reason is not compatibility. Nothing installs a header — the Makefile has
`all`, `test` and `clean`, and a release ships `MacExtract.bin` and
`MacExtract.dsk` — so anyone building on the engine has vendored the tree at a
pinned commit and is untouched until they choose to update. The reason is that
**the prefix does not spell the old name.** Nowhere in this repository is
`unc_` expanded, and `unc_extract()` reads as "uncompress" or "unarchive" at
least as readily as "unclassic"; `unclassic_extract()` would have had to go.
Against that: 6,145 occurrences across every source file, 211 more cited by
name in these documents — many inside dated entries, so renaming them rewrites
the record of what was decided — and `git log -S` archaeology buried in a
project whose docs lean on it. The split is the decision, not an oversight:
`libmacextract` in prose and `unc_extract()` in code are the same library.

**The header filename was the exception, and moved on 2026-08-26.**
`include/unclassic.h` spelled the old name outright and was what five
`#include` lines said, which is the one place the argument above does not hold.
Renaming it to `macextract.h` touched 26 lines and could not change the
binary: with no `__FILE__` and no `assert()` anywhere in `src/`, `mac/` or
`cli/`, the Mac payload rebuilt byte-identical, so `dist/` was left matching
the published v0.24.0 assets exactly rather than churned for new timestamps.

**The creator code moved `'UNCL'` → `'MExt'`, which is not free.** The Finder
binds an icon family to an application by matching the `BNDL` owner to the
creator (M31), so the code, the `BNDL`, the signature resource and
`tools/icon/make_icon.py` all had to move together; `mac/macextract_icons.r` was
regenerated rather than hand-edited, and the bitmaps came out byte-identical.
The cost lands on anyone who already has the old application: their desktop
database still binds `'UNCL'`, so the icon goes generic until it is rebuilt —
which is what the Command-Option restart note in `dist/README.txt` covers.
The `UNCL` that `tools/synth_now.py` and `tools/vmac/ddencode.py` stamp into
synthetic containers was left alone: it is payload the manifests record as
expected output, so moving it would rebuild fixtures to say nothing new.

**A rename can change a fixture.** `tools/synth_exo.py` fills its corpus with
the project's own name, so four fixtures (`synth-exo-tar`, `-tar-Z`,
`-zip-deflate`, `-zip-store`) regenerated 1 byte per repetition longer and
`check_docs.py` caught it. They were rebuilt, their manifests re-derived from
`unar` 1.10.8 — the same oracle string they already recorded — and
`fixtures/SOURCES.md` corrected. The 9-byte literal in `tests/unit_formats.c`
was NOT renamed: `NLIT` hard-codes its length, and it is payload rather than a
name.

**The rename found a variable with two spellings.** `tools/run_spacesaver.py`
and `tools/ssmodule/run_ssmodule.py` read `UNCLASSIC_VMAC_HOME`; the other
thirty-odd call sites read `UNC_VMAC_HOME`. Nothing failed, because both fall
back to the same default — so pointing `UNC_VMAC_HOME` at a different emulator
tree moved every harness except those two, silently. Unified on
`UNC_VMAC_HOME`. The `UNC_` prefix stays for the same reason the C API's does.

## 2026-08-26 — the tag that did not fire

v0.24.0 was tagged and pushed and no release appeared. Everything checkable
from this machine was right: the annotated tag on the remote at `928e00c`,
`main` pushed before it so the workflow sat on the ref Actions reads,
`release.yml` parsing and differing from the copy that shipped v0.23.0 only in
the two asset filenames, both assets committed and non-empty. **Deleting the
remote tag and pushing the same tag object again published it**, assets and
notes intact.

**Why the first push produced nothing is a hypothesis, not a finding.** The
repository had been renamed to MacExtract shortly before, and that is the only
variable that changed since v0.23.0 shipped — but this is one observation,
reproducing it would mean renaming the repository again, and no release is
worth that. Free-plan Actions minutes on a private repository fit the timing
equally well and were never ruled out.

What is worth carrying is smaller and does not depend on the cause. **A pushed
tag is evidence that a tag exists.** This machine authenticates with a deploy
key against a private repository, so every unauthenticated API call is a 404
(2026-08-07, *releases are published by Actions*) — which means the step
between "tag pushed" and "release published" is unobservable from here in both
directions: it cannot be confirmed, and its failure cannot be noticed either.
It was reported as a release published, and it was not one. The remedy is one
line in CLAUDE.md's release procedure — re-push the tag if the page does not
show the new version — and the discipline of saying which of the two happened.

## 2026-08-26 (zip comments) — the second format with one, and the ceiling that moved

An end-of-central-directory comment is up to 65,535 bytes at the very end of
a zip. `fmt_exo.c` parsed its length in order to step over it and dropped the
bytes; OPEN_ITEMS.md excused that as *"a zip comment is usually boilerplate
and `unar` does not write one out either"*. The corpus disagrees in one
place: `derived-MUSFMT10.ZIP` carries 495 bytes of author, address and format
list from 1994, and that is the only place in the archive where any of it is
written down.

**It leaves by the route Compact Pro's does** and nothing was invented for
it: the same `UncSink.comment` callback, the CLI's `<archive>.comment.txt`
and printed `comment:` line, the Mac shell's `TEXT`/`ttxt` document called
`Comments` (2026-08-23, *Compact Pro comments*). A second format's comment
reached both hosts with no host change at all, which is the argument for
having put it in the sink rather than in `UncEntryInfo` in the first place.

**What did have to change is the ceiling.** `UNC_COMMENT_MAX` was 255 because
Compact Pro writes the length in a byte; zip writes it in two, and the corpus
fixture is already twice the old maximum. The constant is now 65,535 and **a
host may no longer size a fixed buffer by it** — the CLI printed through a
`UNC_COMMENT_MAX * 3 + 1` array on the stack, which at the new value is
192 KB, and now flushes in 384-byte pieces. That is the transferable half: a
constant named for one format's field width goes wrong the moment a second
format has the same concept, and what breaks is a buffer sized from it, far
from where the constant lives.

**The comment gets an allocation of its own, taken and released before the
extraction buffers.** A 64 KB ceiling is a real fraction of a 1 MB Mac's
heap, so it must not stand alongside the 12 KB of I/O buffers and the central
directory; measured on the fixture, the allocator's high-water mark for that
archive is 54,856 bytes before and after the change. An allocation that
fails, or a length running off the end of the file, drops the comment and
extracts the archive — the trade both hosts already make when they cannot
write the comment out.

**Graded by the suites that already existed.** `run_diff.py` compared
`lsar`'s `XADComment` against the CLI's file and printed line for every
fixture except a named set holding `Zip`; deleting that set was the whole
change there, and both halves fail when the delivery is taken back out
(verified by taking it out). `tests/run_mac_sink.sh` gained the fixture, so
the Mac shell's `Comments` document is now graded byte-for-byte against the
oracle on x86-64 and under qemu-m68k. The unit test covers what no fixture
has: a comment longer than Compact Pro could express, a length that runs past
the end of the file, and a host with no callback at all.

## 2026-08-27 — the last two armours become envelopes, and MIME still does not

btoa and uuencode extracted perfectly and chained not at all. A real btoa
file off a Mac is `Subject.MBin.ascii` — Aladdin's translator MacBinary-wraps
before it encodes (FORMATS.md, *btoa*) — so `macextract x` decoded the armour,
wrote the MacBinary layer, and stopped one layer short of the file the user
came for. Both layers had always read; only the recursion was missing, because
`env_is_envelope` did not name them.

**The list is the decision, not the decoder.** Nothing in `src/` changed.
`UNC_FORMAT_BTOA` and `UNC_FORMAT_UU` join `env_is_envelope` in `cli/main.c`
and `IsEnvelopeFormat` in `mac/macextract_mac.c`, which are one list kept in
two places because the two hosts must agree on it and `dist/` is built from
the second.

**The test for membership is the one base64 was admitted under**: an envelope
layer is DELETED once the layer beneath it comes out, so a format may join
only if a fork it carries belongs to the layer being deleted rather than to
something else. MacBinary, BinHex and the UUTool shape of uuencode all carry
the wrapper's own forks, and those are litter once the wrapper is consumed.
MIME still does not qualify and is still out: an `appledouble` part can be the
resource fork of a SIBLING part, and deleting the message would take it. That
is why this is two lines and not a rule change.

**uuencode went in on shape rather than on a specimen**, which is worth saying
out loud. None has ever been seen here — the argument is that it is the same
armour around the same wrapper, and the guard that makes it safe is one the
chain already had: several documents in one file yield several files, and the
recursion needs exactly one. The newsgroup shape therefore cannot chain at
all, which `run_uu.py` now asserts rather than assumes.

**Measured on the Macintosh, not inferred from the CLI.** `interactive.py
--chain` drove both armours through the real application at 1x, three layers
deep — btoa or uuencode around MacBinary around a DiskDoubler archive — and
each came down to the one file the Linux CLI produces, with both intermediates
deleted (FORMATS.md, the interactive tier table). It had to be that harness:
`run.py` drives the autotest build, which calls `RunExtract` once and has no
unwrap loop, so no sweep this project has ever run could see a chain on the
Mac at all. `tools/vmac_check.py` said the opposite in a comment until today.

### The second grader had the same stale claim, and it was load-bearing

Correcting `vmac_check.py`'s comment was worth doing again next door.
`interactive.py`'s `expected_names` — "the names the Mac should have written",
used by `--wd`, `--dest` and the default phase — also graded with `-nr`, and
its docstring said it made "the same mapping checkdisk.py makes". Both halves
were false. checkdisk.py grades the AUTOTEST build, where `-nr` is right;
these phases drive the SHIPPED application, which unwraps. And checkdisk.py
maps the CLI's `<archive>.comment.txt` onto the `Comments` document the Mac
shell writes, which `expected_names` had never learned.

Measured, not reasoned: on `boogers-152.hqx` the old reference wanted
`boogers152.sit` on the volume, and `--wd` with the fixed one reports the
volume holding `:Archives:boogers1.5.2ƒ:BOOGERS! 1.5.2` and
`:Archives:boogers1.5.2ƒ:read_boogers.txt` with no intermediate beside them —
so the phase would have called a correct application WRONG for any chained
fixture. `--chain` had been working around this the whole time by building its
own reference without `-nr`, twenty lines further down the same file.

**"The same mapping X makes" is a claim, and it decays like any other.** Two
copies of one grader drifted apart in the same file; what found them was
asking what a harness's reference actually rests on, not reading either
comment.

**Both suites were mutation-tested by taking the two names back out**: the
four chained cases fail without them and pass with them, so what they measure
is the chain and not the decoders that already worked (CLAUDE.md — a test that
passes may not be testing anything). The other three new cases are controls
and pass either way: `-nr` stops at the wrapper in both suites, and two
uuencoded documents in one file do not chain.

## 2026-08-27 (a member over 2 GB) — the entry is not the archive

`fmt_exo.c` refused a tar member over 2 GB by ending the walk, which cost
every member behind it: a tar of `[4 KiB, 2.5 GiB, 11 B]` listed one file of
three, and the third was lost though its position was written in a header the
reader had already parsed. It is now a per-entry refusal — the member is
reported through the sink with a new code, `UNC_ERR_TOOLARGE`, and the walk
resumes where that member's own header says the next one begins.

**A cap that ends a walk is only justified when the walk cannot continue.**
Those are separate questions and this guard had answered them together. The
one that genuinely ends a tar is the NEXT position leaving 32 bits — the 4 GB
container limit every format shares — and that check moved to the advance,
*after* the member is reported, so the last member a header describes is still
named. `[1.5, 1.5, 1.5 GiB, small]` lists three of four and exits 1, which is
the older documented behaviour preserved rather than re-derived. The
advance is computed in 64 bits, which on a 68000 is a libgcc call — once
per 512-byte header, in the same expression that already parsed a 12-digit
octal field into an `int64_t`, and nowhere near a decode loop.

**Why a new error code rather than `UNC_ERR_UNSUPPORTED`.** The obvious reuse
would have printed "this compression method is not supported yet" over a
stored file, and the CLI's follow-up asks the user to send the specimen in —
here, a 2.5 GB file, for a limitation no specimen can advance. Thirteen small
negatives were already public API; a fourteenth is cheaper than a message that
sends somebody down the wrong road.

**Only tar can pose the question** — corrected 2026-08-27, same day: a zip
carrying Zip64 extra fields poses it too, and the sentence below was written
without checking (*the Zip64 escape at 2 GB*). Every other format in scope
declares an entry's size in a 32-bit field, so no value it can hold is out of
reach: without a Zip64 field zip's ceiling is the format's own, and the
Macintosh formats inherit HFS's 2 GB. The
other `0x7FFFFFFF` guards in `src/` read as the same rule and are not — probe
rejections decide whether a file is recognized at all, and the segmented-set
descriptors in `fmt_sitseg.c` and `fmt_ddseg.c` describe one file, so
refusing the set IS the per-entry answer. Checked rather than assumed, by
editing a StuffIt fixture's second entry to declare 0xFFFFFFFF and repairing
the header CRC: it lists as `?`, fails alone, and the other eight entries are
untouched. Repairing the CRC was the whole point — the first attempt left it
stale and the reader refused the archive at the header, which measures the
header check and not the size (FORMATS.md, *Size limits*).

`tools/run_sizelimits.py` holds all of it in `ci.sh`, on sparse subjects
graded by GNU tar: a 4.5 GB archive costs a few kilobytes of disk because
every oversized member is a hole. That also bounds what it proves — the holes
are never read, so the read path over a multi-gigabyte entry remains
unexercised, and on the Mac target it is unreachable anyway.

## 2026-08-27 (the Zip64 escape at 2 GB) — a ceiling that was half what it said

A question about behaviour over a network filesystem — many small entries, a
large container — turned up something the suites could not: a 3.4 GB zip of
23,400 entries **listed all 23,400 and then failed 21,352 of them**, one per
entry at or past 0x80000000. The documented ceiling was 4 GiB, the container
was well under it, and every offset in the file fit in 32 bits.

**The boundary belongs to the WRITER, not to the format.** A central-directory
record whose local-header offset passes CPython's `ZIP64_LIMIT` — 2³¹ − 1, not
2³² − 1 — is written as `0xFFFFFFFF` with the real offset in extra field
0x0001. `fmt_exo.c` read extra field 0x9901 (WinZip AES) and no other, so it
seeked to 0xFFFFFFFF, found no local header, and returned `UNC_ERR_CORRUPT`.
Where the threshold sits is the writer's choice, and the two measured here
differ: CPython escapes at 2³¹ − 1, while Info-ZIP 3.0 writing the same three
files does not escape at all at 2.2 GB — its entries past 0x80000000 carry
plain 32-bit offsets, so an Info-ZIP archive of that size always read
correctly, and 7-Zip sidesteps the question by ordering the large member last.
"We read a zip up to 4 GiB" was therefore true or false depending on which
tool had written the file, and nothing here had asked.

**Reading the escape costs no 64-bit arithmetic.** Any archive this engine can
open at all is under 4 GiB, so the escaped value's high half is zero in every
case that can arise; it is read as two `uint32` halves exactly as the
end-of-central-directory record has been since 2026-08-25, and a high half
that is set refuses the entry. The whole fix is one extra-field walk shared
with the AES one — APPNOTE 4.5.3 fields appear only where the matching
directory field holds its escape, and in a fixed order, so the record has to
be walked rather than indexed (XADMaster's `XADZipParser.m` reads it the same
way, and its comment records that stopping at the first recognised field is a
bug somebody else already found).

**Per entry, and the same error code for a size and a position.** The
directory is intact in both cases, so the neighbours are readable and the
refusal belongs to the entry — the argument in the entry above this one. A
size past 4 GB and a position past 4 GB are one condition worth one code, and
`UNC_ERR_TOOLARGE`'s message became *"entry is past this engine's 32-bit
reach"*: the old wording named the tar case only, and would have said "larger
than 2 GB" over a 40-byte entry that merely sits too far along.

Two things the miss is worth remembering for:

- **A limit measured on subjects you generated measures your generator.**
  `run_sizelimits.py` proves a 3 GiB zip entry lists and verifies — one entry,
  at offset 0, where no writer emits an escape. `run_ziplarge.py` builds
  70,000 entries and a few megabytes. Neither could produce a large OFFSET,
  which is the only thing that triggers this, and both looked like coverage of
  "large zips".
- **The scope smell fired twice on the same decision.** "Zip64 is out of scope"
  named the expensive project (64-bit addressing) and was read as covering
  everything with Zip64 in the name; on 2026-08-25 that hid an ordinary
  archive with too many entries, and it hid this one for two days more. Asking
  which USES a scope decision refuses is worth doing once per use, not once.

## 2026-08-27 (the name cap) — a clamp the host cannot see is a rename nobody asked for

A sweep for limits applied by CLAMPING rather than by refusing — asked
for after the Zip64 escape turned up the same shape — found the biggest
one in `UncEntryInfo` itself. `name` is `UNC_NAME_MAX` (255) bytes; a
457-byte zip path listed and extracted as 255 bytes and **exited 0**,
while `unzip` showed all 457. The file written was a different file from
the one in the archive, and nothing anywhere said so.

**Why not just carry the name.** `UncEntryInfo` is 298 bytes on the
68000 target (measured; the "280 bytes" that stood in `fmt_lzh.c` was
never right, and the "296" this entry stated until 2026-09-03 stopped
being right a few hours after it was written — 2026-09-03, *the struct
that grew after it was measured*) and it travels on a 4 KB call stack.
A name field big
enough for zip's own maximum would be 65,535 bytes. The cap is the
right decision; being silent about it was not.

**Why the ENGINE does not refuse.** The obvious fix — fail the entry
where the name is cut — is wrong on the target platform. The Macintosh
application shortens every name to 31 characters per component anyway,
because that is what HFS takes, and a folder tree eight deep with
full-length names sums past 255 bytes without anything being unusual
about it. Refusing in the engine would deny a Mac user files that the
Mac shell would have named correctly by its own rule. So the engine
reports — `UncEntryInfo.name_truncated`, and `name_full_len` where the
format can say how long the name was — and the host decides.

**Why the CLI does not refuse either, decided the same day.** The first
version of this made a cut name a per-entry failure in the CLI, on the
argument that a file written under a name that is not the archive's is
worse than no file. The maintainer's call went the other way, and it is
the better one: **the Linux CLI should be constrained by the files and
the filesystem it writes to, exactly like the Mac client.** A filesystem
that cannot take a name has always shortened it and carried on — that is
what an 8.3 volume did, numbering `~1`, `~2` where shortening made two
names one — and a user who wanted the file is not served by being handed
nothing. So: extract under the shortened name, cut components on UTF-8
boundaries at `pathconf(_PC_NAME_MAX)` rather than at a constant of
ours, number a collision that the shortening caused, and list every
shortened name in a notice at the end of the run. Exit 0: nothing
failed. The one refusal left is a path past `_PC_PATH_MAX`, because no
shortening of a NAME makes a path shorter than the directory it goes in.

**The notice lists the original names, because a notice is text.**
Corrected the same day, on the maintainer's point: what a screen can
show is not the filesystem's business, and the first version of the
notice gave only the name as written plus a length. The bytes are gone
from `UncEntryInfo` — that is the point of the cap — but they are not
gone from the FORMAT at the moment the cut is made: zip's directory is
in memory, tar's `path[256]` holds all 256, AppleSingle's name entry is
still on disk in front of the reader. So `UncOptions.long_name` is a
buffer the HOST provides, the engine fills it where it can, and
`name_truncated` reads 2 rather than 1 to say it did. The CLI passes
64 KB — the largest name zip or tar can state, and it is only ever
displayed; the Mac shell passes NULL and pays nothing.

**uuencode looked like the exception and was not.** Its `begin` line goes
through a 96-byte line buffer, so the name was gone before anything
could read it — but that buffer is ours, not the format's, and the bytes
are streaming past at the moment they are dropped. They are now kept in
the host's buffer instead, from the part already in the line buffer
onwards, and the name is lifted out of the line and held there while the
data lines behind it are read. **A limit worth reporting is worth asking
whether it is the format's or your own first**; this one was ours, and
three formats' worth of "we cannot know" turned out to be one line
buffer.

What genuinely cannot be recovered is a name whose bytes were dropped
before the engine ever held them: a zip whose central directory does not
fit the allocator's budget is read one record at a time, and MIME's
512-byte header and btoa's 256-byte line do the same to a name that sits
inside them. There the notice gives the length where the field states it
and says *the rest was cut before it reached us* — the one thing it must
never do is present the shortened name as the archive's.

**The cap is not the only cap.** Three line buffers cut a name before
`UNC_NAME_MAX` is reached — uuencode's 96-byte line (a `begin` line's
name dies there first), btoa's 256, and MIME's 512-byte header. Each now
sets the same flag, because a host cannot tell them apart and has no use
for the difference. The one clamp deliberately left silent is a
segmented set's DESCRIPTOR name, which comes from the host's own
filename rather than from the archive, and the archive comment, which is
dropped by an older decision that says why.

**Saying so is not the same as refusing.** The first attempt at the
over-4-GB diagnosis refused the file where the CLI opens it, which reads
as the tidy answer and cost a capability: a tar is walked sequentially,
so its members under 4 GB list and extract, and `run_sizelimits.py`
failed within the hour on exactly that. The message stands and the walk
goes on, with `0xFFFFFFFF` — the largest size `UncIO` can state —
handed over in place of a wrapped one.

**The host had three of its own.** The CLI turned a name into a
filesystem name through a 512-byte buffer, and 255 bytes of MacRoman
become up to 765 of UTF-8 — so a path of accented components was cut
AFTER the engine had carried it whole. That one is sized from
`UNC_NAME_MAX` rather than reported: a cap that cannot be met is better
than a cap that is announced. `outdir/name` sat in a 1024-byte buffer,
which is a quarter of what this filesystem takes; it is `PATH_MAX` now,
and the limit that remains is the filesystem's own. And AppleSingle's
real-name entry was read into 64 bytes — a cap the first sweep missed
because it is spelled `sizeof tmp` rather than `UNC_NAME_MAX`, which is
worth remembering about greps: **the constant you are looking for may
not be the constant that is written.**

**The general shape, since this is the third instance.** A limit that
CLAMPS produces a result that is internally consistent, so nothing
downstream can notice: the zip entry count saturating at 65,535
(2026-08-25), the Zip64 escape read as a literal offset (2026-08-27),
and this. A limit that REFUSES cannot hide. When a constant meets data
from the archive, the question is not "is the constant big enough" but
"what happens to the archive that exceeds it, and who is told".

---

## 2026-08-27 (the scaffolding audit) — the codec is the format, the code around it is not

A review asked why there is so much per-format code. Reading `src/` for
that question rather than for a bug found the answer in a different place
than expected: the **codecs** are as format-specific as they look and
should stay that way, and it is the **scaffolding around them** — the
thing that stages decoded bytes, checksums them and hands them to the
sink, the thing that maintains a folder path, the thing that decides
whether an error is fatal to the walk — that is written once per format.
The inventory and what closed each is the two entries that follow; this
entry is the why.

**How it accumulated, which is not carelessness.** Every format arrived
as a milestone graded against an oracle, and the cheapest correct move
each time was to write the fork writer next to the codec that fed it.
Nothing in that loop ever asks whether the writer already exists — the
fixtures pass either way. The proof that it is habit rather than
necessity is the ORDER: `unc_entry_path_append` and `unc_entry_set_path`
were already in `unc_util.c` when `DdPath`, `NowPath` and `SdPath` were
written, and all three do the same job beside them rather than through
them.

**The lesson, and it generalises past this repository: a shared helper
only helps the formats that call it.** The name-cut reporting landed
2026-08-27 (*the name cap*) in `unc_entry_set_name` and
`unc_entry_path_append`, and every format that routes through those
reports `name_truncated = 2` with the whole name behind it. The three
that keep their own path stack got the half that is `name_truncated = 1`:
they say a name was cut and cannot say what it was, while holding the
components that would answer. So when a feature lands in a shared helper,
**the question is not who calls it — it is who does not**, and that list
is the blast radius. The same shape put a format property in host code
twice: `env_is_envelope` and `IsEnvelopeFormat` are one predicate about
`UncFormat` living in two shells, which is why they could disagree, and
did for a day (2026-08-14).

**Four things that look like the same duplication and are not.** Filed
here so nobody spends a session unifying them:

- **Now Compress's whole-buffer decoders.** `now_lzss`, `now_newlzss` and
  `now_huffman` take `const uint8_t *src` / `uint8_t *dst` where every
  other format streams. That is the format: a table of blocks, each at
  most 32 KB of output against a 32 KB history, which is exactly why its
  peak is a flat 128 KB. Putting it on the streaming contract would trade
  a measured constant for a worse one.
- **`SdLz`.** SuperDisk!'s LZSS bit reader is LSB-first WITHIN a 16-bit
  big-endian word, which is not what a byte-wise LE reader produces —
  `unc_bitin_init_le` would decode garbage. Its neighbour `SdHuff` is
  MSB-first over bytes in order and IS the shared reader; the two sit
  four hundred lines apart and only one of them is duplication.
- **`ss_a3_sort`.** SpaceSaver's symbol order comes from the module's own
  non-stable quicksort, not from `(length, symbol)`, so the sort has to
  be transcribed. What need not be is the builder around it.
- **The codecs.** Arsenic, DDn, sit13, SlowFastA/B, the SpaceSaver
  methods: different algorithms that happen to share a bit reader.

**Why it is worth doing at all, and the one thing to watch.** Most
refactors of this size are unverifiable, which is why they are usually a
bad trade. Here 932 fixtures byte-exact plus 9,320 robustness variants
mean a scaffolding change either passes or does not, which makes this one
of the few places where the trade is good. The thing to watch is
FORMATS.md's peak-RAM matrix: a shared fork writer changes allocation
shape, so those figures must be re-measured rather than assumed to carry
over — and a figure that does NOT move after the change is the smell
CLAUDE.md already names, not a reassurance.

**What landed the same day.** Five of the items, at once: `unc_copy_fork`
(three copies of a stored-fork loop), `unc_fatal` (the triage, nineteen
sites), `UncPath` (three folder-path stacks and their three identical
leaf builders), `UncStage` (the staging half of two decoders) and
`unc_format_is_envelope` (the list that was living in both hosts). 460
lines out, 380 in, 932 fixtures byte-exact. None of it touches an
allocation, so the peak-RAM matrix is unaffected and was not re-measured;
what WAS measured is the stack, because moving a function out of line
moves a frame. The largest frame is unchanged, the deepest chain's
members (`dd_fork`, `dd_ddn`) were not touched, and the two walks that
had `*_path_leaf` inlined into them lost ~250 bytes of frame and gained
one 288-byte call below them — a chain ~40 bytes deeper, against a 4 KB
rule and a 3,290-byte worst case that is somewhere else entirely.

**The near-miss, which is the part worth keeping.** `ss_emit` and
`sd_emit` matched for eight lines under a duplicate scan that normalises
identifiers, and that scan is what put them in the inventory as the same
function. They are not: SpaceSaver's also advances its sliding window,
three lines past where the match ends. Folding them together compiled
clean, sniffed clean, extracted a plausible-looking file, and failed
three unit tests on the preset dictionary. **A duplicate detector reports
a PREFIX, not a function** — read to the closing brace of both before
deleting either. It is also the clearest case this project has had for
the rule about a test being able to fail: nothing else in the run noticed.

**Two behaviour changes, and they are why `unc_fatal` was worth more than
the lines it saved.** gzip propagated no per-entry status at all and `.Z`
omitted `UNC_ERR_IO`, so `unc_gzip_extract` returned `UNC_OK` for a
decode that was cancelled or failed on I/O. Both date to M8, neither was
recorded as a decision, and they are exactly the two sites out of
nineteen where the predicate was written rather than copied. Neither was
observable through either host — both learn of a per-entry failure
through `end_entry`, and the Mac shell tracks its own cancel flag — which
is why nothing caught it in a year. They now say what the other seventeen
say. **A predicate spelled out at nineteen sites has nineteen chances to
be spelled wrong, and no way for the odd one out to be seen as odd.**

---

## 2026-08-27 (the fork writer and the filter) — a shared hot path, measured three ways

The two large items from *the scaffolding audit* landed the same day:
`UncForkOut`, which is now the only thing in the engine that stages
decoded bytes and hands them to the sink, and `UncRle90`, which is the
only place the 0x90 run filter is written. 932 fixtures byte-exact,
9,320 robustness variants clean. What is worth writing down is not that
they landed but what they cost, because the first version of each was
slower and the fixtures could not see it.

**The inventory was one short at each end.** The audit counted eight fork
writers; ARC had a ninth written inline inside `arc_entry_data`, found
only by reading for `arc_emit`'s callers. And the audit called
`ss_emit`/`sd_emit` the same function, which cost a CI cycle on the day
before. **Count by reading, not by scanning.**

**What is deliberately not in `UncForkOut`: the cap.** Four formats stop
at the entry's declared length, and the obvious design puts that in
`put()`. In every one of the four the same counter is ALSO the decode
loop's own condition — `o->left`, `r->remaining`, `s->want`,
`t->outsize` — so hoisting it would have bought nothing and cost every
other format a compare per byte in its hottest loop. The writer holds
staging, the checksum, the write guard and the two-fork routing; the cap
stays with the decoder that already had to know it.

**The filter went through three shapes and the fixtures liked all
three.** A callback (`src`/`emit` function pointers, the obvious shape)
cost a `.hqx` run **27 %**: BinHex puts every decoded byte through the
filter, and gcc would not devirtualise the pointer. Inlining the filter
but keeping the callback got that to 15 %. What worked was removing the
callbacks entirely — `unc_rle90_feed` and `unc_rle90_take`, both inline,
with each caller reading its own source and writing its own output. Two
further shapes were tried on top and both were WORSE, because they grew
`bh_next` past the point where gcc inlines it into `bh_fork`: straight
-lining it (two inlined `feed` bodies) cost 10 %, and a fast/slow split
of the kind `UncBufIn` uses cost 4 %. The loop that survives costs 2-3 %.

**One instruction per byte was 2 % of a Compact Pro run.** The first
`cpt_rle_emit` decremented `r->remaining` before calling the writer,
where the original had stored the byte first. Same work, same
instruction count on paper; +2.14 % measured, and reordering the two
lines brought it back to +0.04 %. **On a per-byte path, "the same
instructions in a different order" is not a rhetorical phrase — measure
the order too.**

**The net, and it is a cost.** +0.86 % across a 14-fixture set
(FORMATS.md, *Decode cost*): BinHex +2.3 to +3.6 %, LZH +1.4 %, ARC
+0.7 %, StuffIt and Compact Pro unchanged, and DiskDoubler **5.4 %
faster** because `dd_out_byte` came out better than it went in. Stack:
`dd_fork` +44 B, which moves the deepest chain in the engine to 3,334 B
against a 4 KB rule. This project has killed optimisations on
measurement before; it is only fair to record that this simplification
was bought, not free.

**Two of the four audit items turned out to be correctly unshared, and
both were settled by a number rather than by taste:**

- **SpaceSaver's Huffman builder must not become `UncCanon`.**
  `sizeof(UncCanon)` is **2,064 bytes**, most of it a 1 KB fast table
  whose own comment says the build cost is `1 << K` writes per table and
  that it pays for itself on alphabets of 256 symbols and up. `SsA3`
  holds SEVEN Huffman tables — five meta levels of at most 32 symbols
  each, rebuilt every block, plus the literal and distance pair — so the
  swap would add ~14 KB to a method whose peak is a measured 41 KB, on a
  format that has to fit the 1 MB floor. `ss_a3_sort` was always going to
  stay (the format's symbol order comes from a non-stable quicksort);
  what this settles is that the builder around it stays too.
- **`sd_huff_bit` must not become `UncBitIn`.** It refills 32 bits at a
  time, and `sd_huff_end` derives *where the next region begins* from the
  cursor that refill leaves — `h->pos + 2`. A byte-granular reader would
  put that cursor somewhere else. This is the same boundary OPEN_ITEMS.md
  records as an open question, where Alysis's own 1991 and 1992
  implementations disagree by one refill; changing the reader would move
  the engine off the 1991 answer it was deliberately pinned to.

---

## 2026-08-27 (the shapes that were left) — one probe signature, one scheme table, one buffer size

The last of the scaffolding audit, and the part that is about SHAPE
rather than about copied code: four places where the engine knew a thing
per format and expressed it as a search instead of a table.

**Twenty-eight probes, three signatures, and one reason.** `unc_sniff`
was a 120-line chain of `if (unc_X_probe(...)) return UNC_FORMAT_X;`
because the probes could not be put in an array: most took
`(head, len)`, nine also took `io`, AppleSingle took a `UncFormat *` to
say WHICH of two it had found, and `unc_foreign_probe` answered for ten
formats by returning one. That last pair is the whole explanation — a
probe that can answer for several formats cannot return a flag. So the
signature is now

    UncFormat probe(const uint8_t *head, int32_t len, UncIO *io);

for all twenty-eight: the format it claims, or UNC_FORMAT_UNKNOWN. The
probes that do not need `io` take it and ignore it, which is the price
of the array, and `unc_sniff` is a walk over `unc_sniff_order[]` with the
ordering argument kept as comments on the rows it argues about. The same
table now dispatches extraction: `unc_formats[]` gained an extractor
column, so `unc_extract_as`'s 26-case switch is a lookup, and the three
functions that scanned that table separately share one `unc_desc()`.

**`unc_appledouble_extract` lost an argument nobody read.** It took the
format to distinguish AppleSingle from AppleDouble and its first line was
`(void)which;` — the layouts differ only in what is present, which the
entry table already says. Both rows now point at the same function.

**The segment joiner had six `desc->scheme ==` tests and every one of
them was a property with no name.** They are named now, in one row per
scheme: `addressed` (the archive stores part/offset pairs),
`data_first` (which fork the joined stream starts with), `anonymous` (a
part carries no header at all, so the descriptor is the host's to
build), and function slots for `describe`, `match`, `payload`,
`stored_crc` and `extract`. Three of those slots are filled by
DiskDoubler alone and NULL everywhere else, which is exactly the shape
the `if (scheme == UNC_FORMAT_DDSEG)` tests were hiding. `unc_segment_match`
moved to `unc_segment.c` where the dispatch belongs, and the StuffIt
half of it became `unc_sitseg_match` beside the header it parses.

**The audit was wrong about the hosts, and the correction is the
interesting part.** It said both hosts dispatch on `scheme` too "though
the per-scheme functions already exist". They do dispatch on it — and on
a DIFFERENT question. What `cli/main.c` and the Mac shell decide is
which sibling FILE to go looking for: `.zip` for a spanned set's last
part, `.z01`, `.001`, a `SPLT` part by its own header. That is naming on
a filesystem, and the engine has never touched a file — it is the rule
the whole `UncVolumeSource` design exists to keep. **Two dispatches on
the same enum are not necessarily the same dispatch**; the question is
what each one is asking. This one stays where it is.

**`dd_fork` had its prologue nine times.** Every branch of the method
dispatch repeated the same `unc_alloc` / `unc_bufin_init` /
`dd_out_start`, differing only in how much working memory it wanted
behind the two buffers. That difference is now `dd_extra()`, a five-line
table, and the prologue happens once — nine `unc_bufin_init` calls in
that file became three. Method 8 came OUT of the dispatch rather than
into it: it decodes through Compact Pro's stage, which carries its own
writer and its own checksum, so it never shared the prologue in the
first place and now says so as `dd_fork_cpt`.

**And `4096` is `UNC_IOBUF`.** It was a bare literal in eleven files and
named in one. A size nobody chose is a size nobody can change.

**Free, measured.** +0.01 % instructions over the same 14-fixture set —
sniffing runs once per file and dispatch once per archive, so there was
nothing there to cost. `dd_fork`'s frame came DOWN 4 bytes, putting the
deepest chain in the engine at 3,330 B (FORMATS.md, *Stack*). With this
the scaffolding audit's list is empty and OPEN_ITEMS.md's section 4 is
gone, which is what a gaps list is for.

---

## 2026-08-27 (the fast table StuffIt could not reach) — a gate that read as a property of the format

`unc_canon_decode` has had a 10-bit lookup table since M29, and it was
gated on `!bi->le`. The comment beside it said StuffIt method 13 "reads
LSB-first and takes the walk below, which is what it always did", which
is true and reads like a fact about the format. It is a fact about how
the table is indexed.

**What it cost, measured on the same function.** 58 instructions a
symbol for Compact Pro, which reaches the table; **189** for StuffIt
method 13, which does not. On `derived-Ultimate_Copland_1.1.sit.1`,
`unc_canon_decode` was 27,809,569 Ir over 146,952 symbols — 59 % of the
whole run — and callgrind showed the fast-path lines executing **zero**
times. The codes are the same canonical codes either way; only the order
the stream hands the bits over differs.

**So the window is turned round, not the table.** One reversal of the
peeked ten bits through a 256-byte const table, and everything after it
— `fastlen[]`, the shift, `first[]`, `cbase[]` — is the code that was
already there. zlib solves the same problem by storing a symbol per
table entry, which needs no reversal and would be right on a machine
with memory: here it is 2 KB more per `UncCanon`, and DiskDoubler holds
three at once and Now Compress two, on the heap, inside budgets
FORMATS.md states to the byte. The reversal table is 256 bytes of const
shared by every `UncCanon` there is, and the struct does not grow.

**The first version was slower for everybody else, and that is the part
worth keeping.** Folding both directions into one expression — a ternary
for the peek and an `if` for the consume — put two branches per SYMBOL
on the MSB path where there had been one per CALL. Now Compress lost
2.56 %, Compact Pro 1.87 %, LZH 2.65 %, and the whole change came to
−0.24 % overall: a 38 % win on one format paid for out of everyone
else's pocket. Hoisting the direction to a single test at the top of the
function — two fast-path blocks, one branch — turned that into −2.26 %
overall with **every** `UncCanon` user faster. **A shared hot path has no
cheap `if`: the question is not how many branches you added but how
often each one runs.**

**Two of the three formats I expected to benefit do not use this decoder
at all.** The finding was first written up as "StuffIt method 13,
SpaceSaver method 3 and ARC read LSB-first and all fall back to the
walk". They do all read LSB-first — and `fmt_spacesaver.c` contains no
`UncCanon` at all (it has `SsHuff`, transcribed from Aladdin's own
non-stable sort, 2026-08-27 *the fork writer and the filter*), while
ARC's Squeezed, Crushed and Distilled walk explicit node arrays of their
own. `fmt_sit.c` is the only file that both reads LSB-first and calls
`unc_canon_decode`. The measurement caught it: two of the three fixtures
moved 0.00 %. **"Reads LSB-first" and "uses the shared decoder" are two
lists, and I had intersected them by eye.**

**What is left, measured and not done.** `arc_raw_byte` is 81 % of the
largest single run in the corpus (`derived-INTRECHO.pak`, 146 M Ir) at
129 instructions per output byte, because Distilled walks its Huffman
tree one bit at a time — ~10.5 bit-steps a symbol, of which the bounds
check alone is 8.4 % of the run. The same style of table would fit, but
those trees are explicit node arrays rather than canonical codes, so
nothing here drops in, and how often each tree is rebuilt has to be
measured first: per entry the build is free, per small block it may cost
more than it saves. That is the question `UNC_CANON_FAST_BITS`'s own
comment records asking about `keyAhead.cpt`, and it is why this entry
stops here.

---

## 2026-08-27 (the tree that was walked a bit at a time) — ARC's Squeeze and Distilled get a first-bits table

The other half of the same reading. `unc_canon_decode` had a lookup
table StuffIt could not reach; ARC's Squeeze and Distilled have no
canonical codes at all — they store an explicit tree of node pairs and
walked it one bit at a time, with a bounds check and a node load per
bit. `arc_raw_byte` was **81 % of the largest single run in the corpus**
(`derived-INTRECHO.pak`, 146 M Ir) at 129 instructions per output byte.

**The measurement that authorised it is the one the previous entry said
to make.** A table is only worth building if it is built rarely enough.
Instrumented on that fixture: **10 trees for 295,185 symbols** — the
trees are read once per ENTRY, not per block — and the code lengths are

| bits | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 | 10 | 11+ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| share | 13.2 % | 6.7 % | 4.9 % | 9.9 % | 18.3 % | 16.6 % | 19.9 % | 8.6 % | 1.8 % |

7.0 bits a symbol on average and **98.2 % of them ten or fewer**, so a
ten-bit table resolves nearly all of them in one lookup and the walk
stays as the fallback for the rest.

**Indexed raw, and that is the opposite of the StuffIt case decided an
hour earlier.** Both readers are LSB-first. `unc_canon_decode` assembles
its codes MSB-first, so its window has to be reversed before it indexes
the table; ARC's walk consumes the first bit as the first branch, so the
bit that leaves the stream first is bit 0 of both the window and the
index and nothing is turned round. **The bit order of the STREAM and the
bit order a code is ASSEMBLED in are different questions**, and the two
changes needed opposite answers on the same day.

**The builder runs the decoder's own walk.** Each tree got a `step`
function — child, leaf, or bad — and the walk and the table builder both
go through it, so a table cannot disagree with the walk it replaces. The
table is filled by walking every one of the 1,024 window values rather
than by traversing the tree once: a thousand short walks per tree is
nothing against the two million steps it saves, and a builder that
cannot diverge is worth more here than a clever one.

**One divergence nearly did get in.** The first Squeeze fast path
returned the table's symbol after checking only for the 256 end code,
where the walk also refuses anything above 255. Caught by reading the
two side by side before it ran — but it is the second time in two days
that a fast path silently dropped a refusal the slow path made, and the
first one (`ss_emit`) shipped as far as CI. **When a fast path replaces
a walk, list what the walk REFUSES, not just what it returns.**

**Result, over the 48-fixture set:**

| | before | after | |
|---|---|---|---|
| `derived-INTRECHO.pak` | 146,326,346 | 89,159,573 | **−39.07 %** |
| `synth-arc-mixed.arc` | 2,040,944 | 1,960,599 | −3.94 % |
| ARC's LZW methods | | | 0.00 %, no tree to walk |
| **TOTAL** | 1,078,346,090 | 1,021,099,385 | **−5.31 %** |

**Cost.** +654 B of `fmt_arc.o` text; +2,560 B of heap for a Distilled
entry (two tables) and +2,048 B for a Squeeze one; +8 B of stack. ARC's
documented peak of 46.5 KB is **unmoved**: re-measured by bisecting the
allocation budget over every `.arc` and `.pak` fixture, the ceiling is
46,686 B both before and after, and it is set by `arc2pak1.pak`, which
uses one of the LZW methods this change does not touch.

**Where the three tiers plus the two decoders leave the engine:**
1,098,529,621 Ir before the harmonisation, **1,021,099,385** after —
**−7.05 %** over the two largest fixtures of every format in the corpus.

---

## 2026-08-27 (what the tables left behind) — the profile moves, and the next thing is never the thing on the list

Asked whether `unc_bitin_bit`'s per-bit `if (b->le)` was the last
optimisation left. It was third on a list written before two tables
landed, and re-profiling answered the question differently: **it is no
longer worth doing, because the traffic that made it visible is gone.**
It cost ~1.1 % of a Compact Pro run when three decoders walked their
codes a bit at a time; `unc_canon_decode` and ARC's trees now resolve
almost every symbol in one lookup, and the single-bit reader does not
appear in the top of any profile. **A ranked list of optimisations is
only ranked against the profile it was made on. Re-profile after every
one you take.**

**What re-profiling actually found.** The largest run in the corpus is
no longer ARC — it is Now Compress at 424 M Ir, and 4.5 % of it was the
CONDITION of its two decode loops. `now_bit_offset()` sits in
`while (now_bit_offset(&b) < numbits)`, so it ran once per symbol —
1,993,952 times on `derived-SyntheticReferences2.0.sea` — and computed
an absolute bit position by calling `unc_bufin_tell()` and multiplying.
A `NowBits` is always memory-backed (`now_bits_start` →
`unc_bufin_init_mem`), and there `lenfed == len`, so
`lenfed - (len - pos)` is `pos` and the call is two subtractions to
recover a field it already had. One line: **−2.33 %** on that fixture,
−0.73 % on `synth-now-mixed.now`.

**Two that are measured and NOT taken, both for the same reason.**

- **A symbol per `UncCanon` fast-table entry, as zlib has.** It would
  take `unc_canon_decode` from 64 instructions a symbol to roughly 55 —
  three array loads become one — which is ~4 % of a Now Compress run.
  It costs 2 KB per table, and Now Compress holds two at once and
  DiskDoubler three, against peaks FORMATS.md states to the byte for a
  machine with 1 MB. Speed bought with the scarce resource is the trade
  this project has refused before and should refuse by default; it is
  the maintainer's to reverse, not a decision to make quietly in a
  profile.
- **Slice-by-4 CRCs.** `unc_crc32` is 6.8 % of a Compact Pro run and
  `unc_crc16_arc` 9.2 % of a StuffIt one, and a four-way table is 2-3×
  faster for 4× the table — 1 KB to 4 KB. That is code space rather
  than heap, which is the usual objection and does not apply, but it is
  still 3 KB on a 177 KB application for a few percent of two formats.

**Where the whole sequence lands.** Over the two largest fixtures of
every format in the corpus: **1,098,529,621 Ir before the harmonisation,
1,011,192,657 after it and the three decoder changes — −7.95 %** — with
the engine 2 KB smaller on the target than when it started.

---

## 2026-08-27 (the path builder's own cap) — the bytes were still in the walk

`unc_path_leaf` reported a cut name without the name, and reported the
wrong length for it. A nine-deep Now Compress tree of 31-byte components
is a 296-byte path; the notice said *the archive's name is 287 bytes;
the rest was cut before it reached us*, which is two claims and both are
false — the length is 296, and nothing had been cut before it reached
us. The helper had cut the path into its own 255-byte buffer one
component at a time on the way DOWN, so by the time a leaf asked, the
bytes were gone and the length was the length of what survived.

**Where the fix goes is the whole point.** The three formats that use
this — DiskDoubler, Now Compress, SuperDisk! — hold every component in a
header the walk is still reading when the cut happens, so nothing is
unrecoverable here the way MIME's 512-byte header is. `UncPath` now
carries the uncut LENGTH, and writes the uncut BYTES straight into
`UncOptions.long_name` as it descends: `full` is both the length and the
write offset, so a pop restores the text as well as the count, and a
host that passed no buffer (the Mac shell) pays one addition per
component. No second engine buffer: the one place with room for a
65,535-byte name is the host's.

**It uncovered a second defect one layer down, and that is the part
worth keeping.** `unc_extract_as` builds its `UncCtx` on the stack, and
where the caller passes no `UncOptions` it zeroed *a list of the
fields*. The list was written when the struct had nine members and was
never revisited, so `long_name` — added earlier the same day — was a
pointer read off an uninitialised stack for every in-tree caller. It had
been harmless only because nothing had yet asked for the buffer on that
path; the moment three formats asked once per entry, the unit tests
segfaulted at `-O2` and passed under ASan at `-O1`, which is how a
stale initialiser looks from the outside. It zeroes the whole struct
now. **A per-field initialiser is a list that has to be maintained;
nothing tells you when it stops being complete.**

**What it cost to find, and what that says.** Nothing found it for
months, because the three formats' own fixtures have short paths and the
only suite that reaches the cap generates zip, tar, uuencode and
AppleSingle — carriers where ONE name is long. A path that sums past the
cap is a different shape of the same defect, and the suite that could
