not see it said so in its own docstring (*"nothing here synthesises such
a container"*). **A named blind spot in a passing suite is a bug report
with no reproduction attached.** `tools/synth_now.py` writes the
container; `unar`, which has no cap, gives the path that was meant.

## 2026-08-28 (the OS 9 tier's last three keystrokes) — a run that stopped early still reported every step it took

The Mac OS 9 harness had never driven the application past the destination
dialog, and said nothing about it. After the launch it sent Return, Return,
Cmd-Q. The second Return is what accepts the **destination** dialog, so
`14-result.png` was a photograph of SFPutFile under the name of the result,
the step printing "extraction reported" had timed that dialog opening in 7 s,
and Cmd-Q was typed into a modal dialog that never sees it — Standard File is
where Cmd-. means Cancel, and Cmd-Q means nothing at all. The application was
still running, with its result alert up, when the emulator was killed.

**Nothing about the extraction was wrong**, because this tier reads its
verdict off the volume rather than off the screen; the 20/20 of the day
stood. What was wrong was every claim the run made about where it had got to.
**A harness that stops early still reports every step it did take**, each with
a plausible number beside a plausible name — which is why the labels were
believed for as long as the tier existed. The fix is not more waits, it is
naming a shot after what is in it and adding the steps that were missing: the
alert dismissed, SFGetFile checked to repaint, and Cmd-. to leave.

**The completion wait had to change with it, and the reason generalises.**
`wait_change` cannot wait for the result, because the progress dialog repaints
ten times a second and is therefore the first thing to change after the
destination dialog. The obvious replacement — wait for the screen to go STILL
— was written, and it misreported a 30 s extraction as finished 14 s in: a
progress dialog reading "0%  estimating time left" is perfectly still until
the first percent lands. **Stillness is a proxy for "done", and a proxy is not
the thing** — the same lesson `settle()` learned in 2026-08-02 against a
startup splash. What replaced it is a state signature: the dialog's TITLE LINE,
the one part of it that does not repaint while the extraction runs, so it
changes when the dialog is replaced and at no other time.

**One keystroke is not a test of a keystroke — and the second archive was
not a second try, it was the control.** The first cancel run sent a single
Cmd-. two seconds after the progress dialog appeared and the archive extracted
to completion, which reads as "Cmd-. does not cancel on Mac OS 9". Sent again
on a DIFFERENT archive, the first chord cancelled and the volume proved it: 1
of 34 entries written, byte-exact, 33 never started. Sent three times over
twenty seconds on the original archive, still nothing. So the variable was the
archive, not the keystroke — and the two differ by format.

**The cause was in the engine, and it had been there since the format was
written.** `ProgressPump()` runs only from `prog_tick`, which runs only from
the engine's progress callback; `src/fmt_now.c` pulls each compressed block
with `unc_read_full` — the raw `UncIO`, not the shared buffered reader that
drives the pump — so **Now Compress never called the callback at all**. The
progress dialog therefore sat at "0%  estimating time left" for the whole
extraction (photographed), no events were pumped, and both Cancel routes were
unreachable for as long as it took. On a Mac Plus, that 9.9 MB entry is not
twenty seconds. One `unc_progress_add` per block fixes it; the interval is
then one block, at most `NOW_INBLOCK` = 32 KB, which is now what
include/macextract.h says. **Confirmed the way it was found**: the same
archive, the same harness, the fixed engine — the first Cmd-. cancels, 1 of 2
entries written and byte-exact.

**Nothing here could have seen it, and that is the part worth keeping.** Every
suite in `ci.sh` grades bytes, and a decoder that never yields produces
perfectly correct bytes. The contract was in the public header, the rule was
in CLAUDE.md, and 946 fixtures passed without either being checked once.
`tools/tick_probe.c` and `tools/run_ticks.py` now measure it — every fixture
the engine extracts, one line each: it fails on the pre-fix engine and passes
on this one, and it reports the widest gap in the corpus (30,634 B with Now
Compress's block tick; 16,172 B without it, which is what the other twenty-odd
formats have always been doing). **A property nothing measures is not a
property the suite protects, however many times it is written down.**

The harness changed with it: it sends Cmd-. until the dialog goes away, prints
the count, and prints "Cmd-. DID NOT CANCEL" when none lands. **A test whose
negative result is indistinguishable from a lost keystroke is not a test; make
the count part of the output.**

**The pass condition is what makes the cancel gradeable.** A cancelled run has
no oracle — there is no correct number of entries to stop at — so what is
asserted is the shape: a **strict, non-empty subset** of the entries, every
file in it byte-exact, and nothing left behind by the entry that was in flight.
Both degenerate outcomes fail as a broken TEST rather than passing, and say
which archive to use instead: a cancel that arrives after the last entry has
interrupted nothing, and one that arrives before the first cannot tell a
working Cancel from a dead keystroke.

**And the cheap thing that was sitting there the whole time.** Every tier
compares the BYTES of an 8-bit MacRoman filename; only the two Mini vMac ones
had ever photographed the glyphs. The fixture was already in the OS 9 sample —
PackIt's `App` + MacRoman `CC A9` — and the shot cost one crop of a screenshot
the run was taking anyway, at the moment the run had never reached before:
after the application quits, with the Finder window open behind it. Mac OS 9
draws it as `AppÃ©` in the Finder's icon view and again in Standard File's
list, the same two characters the Linux CLI prints as UTF-8. **The step that
was missing from the harness and the observation that was missing from the
tier turned out to be the same step.**

## 2026-08-28 (clicking a dialog that does not exist yet) — park the pointer before the thing you mean to hit appears

The Mac OS 9 tier's last open item was the progress dialog's Cancel BUTTON,
never clicked here: Cmd-. is one branch of `ProgressPump()` and the button is
the `DialogSelect` beside it, and the keyboard route a fixture run uses cannot
reach the second. What kept it open was a timing argument that looked like a
wall — `goto()` is a closed loop costing ~40 s, and the dialog it would aim at
exists only while the extraction runs, which is seconds here.

**The wall is only there if the cursor has to be aimed at the dialog.** It
does not: the button's position is known from `mac/macextract.r` before the
application is even launched (`DLOG` 128 does not autocentre, so item 1 of
`DITL` 128 is at (340,168) on screen), and the modal dialog BEFORE it — the
destination dialog — waits forever. So the pointer is parked on the button's
coordinates while SFPutFile is up, the Return that starts the extraction moves
nothing, and the click is then instantaneous. **When positioning is expensive
and the target is short-lived, position early against a stationary predecessor
rather than faster against the target.**

Two smaller things had to be right, and each would have failed as "the button
does nothing":

- **The press is HELD, 0.9 s.** `DialogSelect` calls `TrackControl`, which
  asks whether the button is still down; an application that pumps events only
  from its progress callback dequeues the mouseDown a tick late, by which time
  a 60 ms tap has ended and reads as a click released outside the control. The
  Finder is fast enough that os9mouse's taps never exposed this.
- **The guest gets a relative `usb-mouse`.** The tablet a fixture run attaches
  is inert here (BLOCKERS.md, *Withdrawn claims*), so the click configuration
  is os9mouse's, not os9.py's default.

**The control is the whole result.** A cancel that fires proves nothing on its
own — "the extraction stopped early after a mouse press" is equally consistent
with any press waking a stalled application. So `--click-at 150,168` presses
the same dialog's blank background, 190 px away, with everything else
identical: the button cancels on the first press (1 of 34 entries, byte-exact)
and the background press does not (34 of 34, and the run fails as a cancel
that arrived too late). One extra boot, and it is what separates "the button
works" from "a click works". Numbers in FORMATS.md, *Cancelling, and an 8-bit
name*; how to re-run in `tools/vmac/README.md`.

The positioning code moved rather than being copied: it is `os9mouse.Pointer`
now, given a "send these monitor commands" and a "give me a frame", and both
guests hand it their own. A second copy of the four measured facts in that
module's docstring would have been a second copy to get wrong.

## 2026-08-28 (the password dialog on Mac OS 9) — a change threshold is only as good as the rectangle it is measured over

The last thing the Mac OS 9 tier had never done was decrypt something. It was
filed as a harness limitation — the application asks for the password in a
dialog and a fixture run drives the Finder — but the dialog is keyboard work:
`PassFilter` maps Return to OK and Escape to Cancel, and everything else
typed goes into the buffer. What actually stood in the way was the SUBJECT.
**Nothing in the corpus is encrypted**, measured (FORMATS.md, *Encryption*),
so there is no fixture to add to the sample; `--encrypted` therefore generates
its archive the way `interactive.py --password` already did, and StuffIt
classic's DES is the one worth carrying here because its verifier `'MKey'` 0
lives in the archive's RESOURCE fork. On Linux that fork arrives inside a
MacBinary envelope; on the Mac the shell reads it through the File Manager.
Both decrypt, and the three entries came off the volume byte-exact, resource
forks included.

**The wrong password goes first.** An application that ignored what was typed
and decrypted anyway would pass a run that only ever gives the right one, and
the retry is what a user meets. It is photographed: eight bullets for
`wrongone`, then the dialog again reading "That password was refused."

**And the part worth transferring.** That refusal step reported *"NO CHANGE,
continuing anyway"* on the first run — 0.43 % of the rectangle changed, over a
120 s timeout — while the screenshot taken two lines later showed the refusal
plainly. Nothing was wrong with the application or with the 2 % threshold: the
rectangle was `WINDOW_RECT`, 600x500, and the whole password dialog is a small
part of it, of which the refusal moves one rewrapped paragraph. Measured over
the message area from `DITL` 132 instead, the same event is **19 % in 4 s**.
A threshold answers "did enough of THIS change"; when the answer disagrees
with a photograph, suspect the rectangle before the guest. The general form is
this repository's oldest smell: **an aggregate cannot fail on a property it
averages away.**

## 2026-08-28 (the exception one front end had) — a rule implemented in one shell is not implemented

Closing the Mac OS 9 tier's last gap meant replaying SpaceSaver there, which
had never happened anywhere: its only specimens are the ones Aladdin's modules
wrote under `qemu-m68k`, they cannot be committed, and the sample list can only
name files in `fixtures/`. They can be run by path, and one boot was all it
took — 76,800 B byte-exact (FORMATS.md).

**The run found a defect in the Macintosh shell.** Formats that store no name
get a hint from the archive's filename, "minus its last extension", which is
what `unar` does. SpaceSaver is the exception: it compresses a file IN PLACE
and adds no extension, so the whole name is the name and stripping one eats a
real `.txt`. `cli/main.c` has said exactly that, in a comment, since SpaceSaver
went in. `mac/macextract_mac.c` stripped unconditionally. So a `Report.txt`
came out of the Macintosh application as `Report` and out of everything else as
`Report.txt`, and no suite could see it: the engine is identical on both sides,
the Mini vMac tiers compare summed CRCs and counts rather than names, and the
one tier that DOES compare names had never been given a file of this format.

**The lesson is the shape of the bug, not the bug.** A rule written down in one
front end is not a rule the project implements — it is a rule that one program
implements. The two shells compute this hint independently, ten lines apart in
two files, and the second one has been wrong for as long as the exception has
existed. **Where two front ends must agree, the agreement needs a test that
fails, and the format that exercises it is the one nobody has a fixture for.**

**And the reconciliation it forced is a strengthening, not an accommodation.**
An in-place format's output is named after the archive, so on the Mac — which
extracts BESIDE the archive — the shell's collision numbering writes
`archive.sit~1`, while the Linux reference writes `archive.sit` into an empty
directory. `verify_output()` now maps the one to the other, and the NOT_OUTPUT
guard that used to refuse the whole fixture as ungradeable no longer fires.
Both failure modes it protected are still failures: a shell that overwrote the
archive reads as no output at all, and the extension-stripping one reads as a
missing file and an unexpected one — which is how the defect above was seen.


## 2026-08-28 (the walk nobody had descended) — writing the container is what tests the walk

`src/fmt_dd.c` has two directory walks: `DDAR`'s isdir/enddir records and
`DDA2`'s dirlevel counter, one folder deep between the nine real archives in
the corpus. Both were transcribed from XADMaster and neither had ever descended
a tree, which OPEN_ITEMS.md had said in a bullet whose remedy it also named —
"`unar` reads the first, so DiskDoubler's is a writer away from being graded".

`tools/synth_dd.py` is that writer, and the writing is where the work was. The
records themselves are twenty lines of `struct.pack`; what the reference does
NOT document, and what a reader of the format never has to compute, is the
CRC-16/XModem over the first 76 bytes (`DDAR`), 60 (`DDA2`) or 82 (a
0xABCD0054 file header) that `recognizeFileWithHandle` checks. Without it
`unar` answers *Couldn't recognize the archive format* — so the generator
would have graded nothing while looking like it worked, and this engine, which
recognises by magic alone, would have read every archive perfectly. **A
generator has to satisfy the oracle's recogniser, which is a part of the format
the extractor is free to ignore.** The algorithm came out of the specimens:
XADMaster's table is the 0x1021 table byte-swapped and it byte-swaps the stored
value before comparing, which is a plain XModem CRC stored big-endian, and it
verifies on all nine.

**What the exercise found was in the other half of the same bullet.** It named
SuperDisk!'s up-directory record as untested too — and `super-disk-utility.hqx`
walks two folders, pops between them and is adjudicated path by path against
its own self-extractor (`tools/run_superdisk.py`). The record was tested before
the bullet was written. What is untested there is only DEPTH, which is a
different sentence with a different remedy (BLOCKERS.md). **An "untested" claim
is a claim about the corpus, so it expires when the corpus does; this one was
disproved by a fixture that had been in `ci.sh` for a week.**


## 2026-08-28 (the front end that was already doing it) — read the shell, not the flag

The same audit that closed DiskDoubler's walks left OPEN_ITEMS.md naming a
remedy for the Macintosh shell: "`~1` numbering in `mac/macextract_mac.c`".
That code has been there since M10. The shell numbers off the File Manager's
`dupFNErr`, which catches every collision the 31-character cut creates without
needing the engine's flag at all — so the bullet, written from the flag's point
of view, described a gap the shell did not have (BLOCKERS.md).

**What the shell really lacks is the notice, and that is a screen decision**
rather than a code one, which is why it stays open. Two behaviours it has no
way to report were measured while establishing this: two FOLDERS whose names
shorten alike MERGE (`EnsureDir` descends into what already exists, which is
also what the Finder would do), and a path the engine had to cut lands on its
own parent folder and is numbered. Nothing is lost either way; the path is
simply not the archive's, and nobody is told.

**The lesson is where the test went.** `tests/mac_sink_test.c` has run the real
shell against real archives since M10, so the shell's name path could have been
tested for as long as the harness has existed; what was missing was an archive
that reaches the caps, and by then `tools/synth_dd.py` existed to write one. **A front end
without a test is not tested by reading it** — three one-line breakages of the
shell fail this suite in three different ways, and the first of them is the
line the bullet said to write.


## 2026-08-28 (the refill boundary) — the specimen that could not be made, made

OPEN_ITEMS.md wanted "a real block whose coded flag bitstream is a multiple of
32 bits" and said in the same breath that sizing a subject does not produce
one, "because the 1991 panel codes the flags one bit each". Both halves came
from four subjects compressed on purpose in August and sized
`ceil(size/8) % 4 == 0`.

**That rule describes the harness's own encoder.** It writes a flat 8-bit code,
so its bit length is eight times its byte count; the panel writes a real
Huffman code, and what it codes is each flag BYTE — 0xff, eight predictions
right, in one bit. Measuring the four blocks (`tools/sdhuff.py`, the same
reader as `fmt_superdisk.c` with a counter in it) gives 130, 133, 260 and 261
bits for 125, 128, 255 and 256 flag bytes: **nflags + 5**, the five two-bit
codes being this subject's only other flag bytes, all of them in its first 40
bytes. So the bit length is a function of the SIZE after all, and
`ceil(ulen/8) + 5 ≡ 0 (mod 32)` is the aim. 1240 bytes lands on 160 bits and
2008 on 256; 1248 and 2016 are one bit past, as controls.

**The panel wrote all four, and this engine got the two on the boundary wrong
from byte 0.** So the reading was ours, not the format's: the two Alysis
implementations agree with each other and always did (BLOCKERS.md).

**The fix is in the READER, not in the `+ 2`.** `sd_huff_bit` fetches a long
only when a bit is asked for; the compressor's reader has already fetched it.
So the cursor `sd_huff_end` adds 2 to is four bytes further on whenever the
last symbol ended exactly on a refill, and that is the whole correction. It
was tempting to write the four bytes into the constant instead, which would
have been right for these blocks and wrong for every other one.

**Two things this is a lesson about.** A tolerance that rests on our own output
cannot fail: `run_sdmodule.py` SKIPPED every synthetic block that reached the
boundary, and that skip was the one case that would have caught this. And a
measurement missing its target is not evidence about the target — four
subjects sized by the wrong rule missed by 2 to 5 bits, and the miss was
written up as a property of the format rather than of the arithmetic.


## 2026-08-28 (the tree count) — a probe outside the legal range tests the range check

Method 5's leading byte was confirmed at 0 and nowhere else, and the evidence
for the doubt was that "a count of 16 is rejected by both expanders". Encoder
and decoder here share one reading of the non-zero case, so the rejection was
read as *possibly our reading is wrong* — a live branch in shipping code with
no way to tell.

**It was neither.** `CODE` 11 of the 1991 `DiskDoubler™ App`, on the 3.1.2
floppy this project already keeps, is the splay codec, and it validates the
count before decoding anything:

    1dfc:  cmpiw #32   -> valid      1e10:  cmpiw #128  -> valid
    1e08:  cmpiw #64   -> valid      1e18:  cmpiw #256  -> valid
                                     else   moveq #0    -> invalid

The decompressor at 0x228c turns 0 into 256, calls that validator, answers
-20999 when it says no, answers -20998 when the count exceeds the trees it
managed to allocate — it allocates `count * 1536` and HALVES the count down to
32 while the allocation fails — and masks the next context with `count - 1` at
0x1fbc, which is `byte % count` for all four legal values and is exactly what
`enc_splay` writes.

So 16 was never a count this format can carry. A 128-tree fork written here
came back **byte-exact from that same expander** (`ddencode.py --expand`), and
the 16-tree one is now asserted to be REFUSED — the case passes by being
turned down.

**The lesson is about what a failing probe measures.** A value outside the
legal range exercises the range check, and says nothing about the reading it
was aimed at. The check was there to be read: the same disassembly that
answered this took twenty minutes, on a disk image the repository has had
since 2026-08-21, and the item had been open with "what would change it: a
method 5 fork with a non-zero count" — a specimen nobody could produce — while
the answer sat in the expander that was already being used as the oracle.

## 2026-08-28 (`--only`) — an exemption keyed to a proxy is an exemption that misfires

`--only NAME` restricts `l`, `x` and `t` to the entries named. Three
decisions in it are worth keeping.

**The selector is the name `l` prints, not the archive's bytes.** The
comparison happens after `name_to_fs`, so a row copied out of a listing
is a selector and the two commands compose. It also means a selector can
carry whatever the name carries: the Finder's custom-icon file is
`Icon\r`, and selecting it needs the CR.

**A selector that matches nothing is an error.** Otherwise a mistyped
name extracts nothing, says nothing and exits 0 — the worst of the three
possible answers. Every match is recorded rather than the first, because
the flags are what make the report possible.

**The exemption for envelope layers is the unwrap GATE, not the
format.** `x` peels `.hqx`/`.bin`/`.gz` on the way to the archive, and
filtering a layer that holds one file on that path would extract nothing
at all — so a layer about to be unwrapped is exempt. The first version
asked `layer_unwraps(fmt)`, which is the format alone; but `l` and `t`
never unwrap anything, so for them the envelope's single entry IS the
listing, and the exemption made `--only` silently inert there. A typo'd
selector on `macextract t archive.sit.bin` exited 0. The condition is
now the same expression the auto-unwrap uses, and both call one helper
so they cannot drift. **An exemption must be keyed to the condition it
exists to protect, not to something that is usually true when that
condition holds.**

One claim was corrected before it was built on. "No host has ever
returned >0 from `begin_entry`" was wrong twice over: listing returns it
for every entry, and BOTH hosts return it for every directory
(`cli/main.c` `sink_begin`, `mac/macextract_mac.c` `sink_begin`), so the
branch was live in all 25 format files. What had never happened is the
MIXED case — >0 for some entries and 0 for others while writing — which
is the shape a selection has, and which is what found Now Compress and
DiskDoubler calling `end_entry` for a skipped folder against the
contract in `macextract.h`. The question "has anything ever returned
this?" is one grep of two files, and it was worth spending before
planning around the answer.

## 2026-08-28 (three ideas, two numbers) — a constant nobody named cannot be searched for

The engine had **42 raw `0xFFFFFFFF` / `0x7FFFFFFF` literals across 14
source files** and exactly one named ceiling, `UNC_SIZE_UNKNOWN`. A
search for "every place with an opinion about a limit" therefore could
not be run: `grep 0xFFFFFFFF` returned CRC-32's initial value, a zip
escape, a StuffIt 5 marker, three algorithm register masks and the
engine's addressing ceiling, undifferentiated.

They are three ceilings, and they are now named apart:

| name | value | the idea |
|---|---|---|
| `UNC_OFF_MAX` | 0xFFFFFFFF | what `UncIO` can address — `seek`/`tell`/`size` are `uint32` |
| `UNC_MAC_MAX` | 0x7FFFFFFF | what a classic Macintosh file can be — HFS caps a fork at 2 GB, and the File Manager's position parameter is a signed long |
| `UNC_READ_MAX` | 0x7FFFFFFF | the largest single read the engine expresses — `UncIO.read` takes an `int32_t` |

Plus three things that are not limits at all and were indistinguishable
from one: `UNC_CRC32_INIT`, `ZIP64_ESC32` (the format's own escape) and
`SIT5_DATALEN_NONE` (the marker after every directory entry).

Two things the naming showed that the literals had hidden:

- **`UNC_SIZE_UNKNOWN` and `UNC_OFF_MAX` are the same number.** "This
  entry's length is not known" and "the largest offset this engine can
  address" are indistinguishable at every comparison in the engine.
  Nothing depends on the coincidence today, and it is the first thing a
  widened offset type would break — silently, because the two would stop
  being equal while every `==` still compiled.
- **`0x7FFFFFFF` meant two unrelated things inside one file.** In
  `fmt_exo.c` it bounded a tar member (a fork ceiling) and a
  central-directory buffer (a read length). Same number, opposite
  reasons to change.

Behaviour is unchanged by construction, which is the point: the 932-fixture
suite can prove this refactor and structurally could not prove the
widening it prepares for. A `unc_off_t` that is 64-bit on Linux and
32-bit on 68k yields **two engines and one differential suite** — every
fixture exercises the 64-bit build, the Mac tiers sample 69 and 20
fixtures nowhere near a boundary, and each `high half is set` guard
becomes a branch that is compiled out on the machine that tests it and
live on the machine that ships.

One measurement worth keeping for that decision: **no codec file
mentions `UncIO` at all** — not `unc_inflate.c`, `unc_lzw.c`,
`unc_lzah.c`, `unc_lzdyn.c`, `unc_huffman.c`, `sit_arsenic.c` or
`unc_crypt.c`. All ~64 `seek`/`tell`/`size` call sites are in the format
parsers, a handful per entry rather than per byte, so the 68000 cost of a
wider offset type is not in the decompressors. That is a narrower claim
than the one OPEN_ITEMS.md's Zip64 row rests on, and the scope decision
does not turn on it: the verification cost above is the expensive half.

Four literals stay raw on purpose: SuperDisk!'s three `& 0xFFFFFFFF`
register masks and Arsenic's range-coder overflow guard are transcribed
algorithm internals whose width is fixed by the algorithm. If the offset
type ever widens, those are exactly the ones that must NOT follow.

## 2026-08-28 (what a width costs) — the offset type is a compile-time choice, not an architecture

`UncIO` now carries a container offset in `unc_off_t`: `uint32_t` by
default, `uint64_t` when a host defines `UNC_OFF64`. **The abstraction
costs the 68000 nothing, and the wider type costs it 0.5 %.**

`m68k-apple-macos-size`, same toolchain and flags:

| build | text | data |
|---|---|---|
| before, `uint32_t` spelled out | 138,868 | 31,440 |
| `unc_off_t` = 32-bit — what ships | 138,864 | 31,412 |
| `unc_off_t` = 64-bit, on 68k | 139,576 | 31,436 |

Both widths pass 683 unit checks, 932 fixtures against the oracle and
9,320 robustness variants under ASan+UBSan. A 6 GiB tar of four 1.5 GiB
members: GNU tar lists four, the 32-bit build lists three and exits 1,
the 64-bit build lists four and exits 0.

**The objection this replaces was wrong, and worth recording as wrong.**
"Two widths give two engines and one differential suite" assumed the
32-bit configuration could only be tested on a Macintosh. It cannot be:
width is a compile-time choice, so the configuration that ships to a
68000 builds and grades on Linux against the same fixtures and the same
oracle. `ci.sh` variant 5 does exactly that, and without it two widths
would not be defensible.

Four things the work established:

- **`-Wconversion` finds only half of a widening.** It flagged 24 sites
  where a widened value narrows into a 32-bit local — a clean worklist —
  and cannot flag a 32-bit local passed INTO a widened `seek`, which
  still wraps. The compiler gives interface conformance, not addressing
  correctness. tar's walk was widened by hand for that reason.
- **Only tar gains.** Its member positions are a running sum, so it
  addresses as far as the type does with no format change. zip's
  directory fields are 32-bit and its Zip64 escape is read as halves, so
  a zip past 4 GiB is Zip64 ADDRESSING, which stays out of scope
  (OPEN_ITEMS.md part 1).
- **Widen only what addresses.** An entry's own size stays 32-bit — no
  target here can write a fork past 4 GiB. The classic Macintosh formats
  keep 32-bit addressing behind one helper, `unc_io_size32()`, which
  clamps: a container past 4 GiB reads to them as one whose length the
  host did not state, the same leniency a streamed container already
  gets, and none of them can address far enough to be misled by it.
- **A test that asserts a ceiling must ask the binary which ceiling it
  has.** `run_sizelimits.py`'s over-4-GB case asserted the 32-bit
  boundary and duly failed the 64-bit build. `--version` now derives the
  width from `sizeof(unc_off_t)` and the test grades against that. Being
  TOLD the expectation by ci.sh would have been weaker: the binary is
  the thing that knows.

Run on the hardware it is for: **69/69 on an emulated Mac Plus at 4 MB
and 69/69 at the 1 MB floor target**, System 6.0.8, the same
`tools/vmac/sample.txt` the shipped build passes — so the 32-bit
configuration is verified where it runs, not only where it compiles.

**And it runs on a 68000.** `UNC_OFF64` on an emulated Mac Plus:
**69/69 PASS**, with **identical peak heap on every one of the 69** and
identical result grids — file counts, summed CRCs, data and resource
byte totals. Those come off the application's own grid as integers, so
that comparison is exact rather than poll-quantised. 64-bit arithmetic
on a 68000 means libgcc helper calls in a no-libc, 4 KB-stack
environment, and the claim that it would never be run there was a scope
statement wearing a technical one: the question was cheap and worth
asking. `mac/CMakeLists.txt` carries the switch so it can be asked again.

**Time is the one thing not measured, and the instrument is why.**
`macburger-helper.hqx` at 1x, four runs alternating 32/64/32/64:
**104.0, 104.0, 104.1, 104.1 s** — the spread between widths equals the
spread within one. But `run.py` timestamps at poll instants, so the
figure is quantised to the 3 s poll however reproducible it looks:
**the result is "no difference above 3 %", not a stopwatch.** Every
subject this harness can reach is decode-dominated — the parser touches
offsets a few dozen times per archive while the codec loops millions of
times — and the one shape that would isolate the parser, an archive of
tens of thousands of entries, does not fit on a 1440 KB floppy. On the
host, 500,000 entries LISTED (parser only, no decode) cost 0.17-0.18 s
at either width; that says the widening is free algorithmically, and
says nothing about m68k, where a 64-bit add is not one instruction.
So the true 68k cost sits somewhere under 3 %, with +0.51 % of text as
the only hard number pointing into that range.

One build hazard surfaced and is not fixed here, and the first diagnosis
of it was wrong. It is NOT that the Makefile ignores header
dependencies — `ENGINE_HDR` lists `include/macextract.h` and every
generated header, and has since 2026-08-23. It is that **make tracks
timestamps, not flags**: objects compiled without `UNC_OFF64` are not
rebuilt when the flag is added, so a width switch without `make clean`
links a mixed build. It cost one build whose warnings belonged to the
previous compilation. `ci.sh` cleans between variants, so CI never sees
it; an interactive build does, and the Makefile now says so where
`OFFDEFS` is defined.

## 2026-08-28 (the certificate painted first) — a completion marker that goes down first certifies nothing

Two of thirteen fixtures reported FAIL at `--poll 3` on a 4 MB Mac Plus
while passing at `--poll 20` and all out. The harness's README already
carried a hypothesis for a similar 1 MB failure — a poll landing while
the app was still painting — and said the short 4 MB runs were "a
different regime" where `--poll 3` is fine. Both halves were wrong: the
regime is the same, and the cause is not a race that a slower poll
avoids.

`at_report` erases the screen to white, paints three calibration squares,
then the 16-bit sync word, then eight 32-bit values MSB-first. **The sync
word went down first.** So a screenshot taken part-way through decoded
perfectly: calibration squares found, sync valid, early values final, and
every cell not yet reached reading as zero because the erase left it
white.

The kept frame is the proof, and it is exact. `derived-PackIt.pit`
reported `totalRsrcLen` **0x00008000** where the truth is **0x0000DB05** —
that value with painting stopped after its 17th bit — and `peakBytes` 0
because painting never arrived. `ret` was already 0, which is why the
host took the frame as final.

**The sync word is now painted last**, same grid layout, deliberate
order. It becomes a completion certificate rather than a header: a
mid-paint screenshot has no sync, `decode_grid.py` rejects it, and the
host looks again. Both fixtures now pass at `--poll 3` and at `--poll 1`.

Three things worth carrying:

- **A marker that says "this is complete" must be written after the thing
  it describes.** Written first it is a header, and a header cannot
  distinguish complete from interrupted. The same shape appears wherever
  a length or a magic precedes its payload.
- **"Poll more slowly" was a workaround that hid a defect for weeks**,
  and it cost accuracy in the direction that mattered: the two rows it
  forced to `--poll 20` are the coarsest in FORMATS.md's timing table.
  A tolerance widened to make a symptom go away is worth re-opening.
- **The image was worth looking at.** The values alone said
  "part-painted" and that was already in the docs as an inference. What
  turned it into a mechanism — erase-to-white plus forward paint plus
  sync-first, with 0x8000 as the arithmetic to check it — came from
  decoding the one frame the harness had kept all along.

## 2026-08-29 (the yardstick) — the free tool, run at last

Every Mac Plus figure this project publishes was absolute, and absolute is the
wrong shape for the one number a user actually weighs. The comparison with
StuffIt Expander 4.0.2 had been Aladdin's documentation against our
measurements for two weeks, and said so honestly, while what was missing was a
download and about forty minutes of emulator. Results: FORMATS.md, *Timed on
the same Mac Plus*. Three things are worth keeping.

**The clock has to be outside both applications, and the quiet period is not
part of the answer.** Ours can be asked what it took — it already keeps
`startTicks` for the progress ETA — and Expander cannot be asked anything. A
comparison that used our own clock for our side and screen polling for the
other would have credited us with the difference between an instrument that
costs nothing and one that costs ~50 ms a poll, which is a measurement of the
harness. So both are timed the same way, by screenshot. What each run reports
is the timestamp of the **last frame that differed from the one before it**,
not the total wait: proving that the work stopped takes a minute of unchanged
screen, and counting that minute would make the number an artefact of how
patient the harness is. The residual error is one poll interval, paid equally
by both, which is why the ratio is quoted and the seconds are not.

**"The volume is empty" was a fact about our own sink.** Expander expanded a
278 KB archive for 56 seconds, with its progress window photographed at
"Items remaining to UnStuff: 3", and the disk image afterwards held nothing at
all. Our sink calls `FlushVol` after closing each fork, so its output is on
the host image the moment the alert goes up; Expander leaves its writes in the
File Manager's cache, and SIGKILLing the emulator throws them away. The
harness had never had to ask whether output reaches the image, because the
only program it had ever measured answered the question by construction —
**an instrument inherits its assumptions from the one subject it has always
had**, and this one presented a working program as a broken one with the
evidence sitting in the same directory. Command-Q after the measurement fixed
it and costs the comparison nothing. The same cache is already documented one
screen away, in the shipped application's own envelope loop, which flushes
after deleting an intermediate for exactly this reason.

**Mixed was the useful outcome.** Expander unstuffs StuffIt in three quarters
of our time; we expand Compact Pro in five sixths of its, and a BinHex
envelope with an archive inside it in under two thirds. Neither number would
have been safe to guess from the other, and one fixture would have supported
whichever conclusion it happened to land on — which is the argument for the
set being one per format rather than one that is representative. What makes
the times comparable at all is that both applications wrote the same output
names on every fixture; a speed comparison between two programs doing
different amounts of work is arithmetic, not evidence.

## 2026-08-29 (SuperDisk! method 13) — the specimen that would have settled nothing

OPEN_ITEMS.md filed method 13 under *"waiting on a file this machine does not
have"*, and named what would close it: **"a file that actually contains one"**.
That line was wrong twice over, and the lead beside it — *"Disk Expander 1.2's
`INIT 32` has a compress path walking a method table"* — is what showed it.

**The method table is real, and 13 is in it.** INIT 32's compress path
(@0x5232) reads a setting word, indexes an eleven-entry table at +0xf2e, eight
bytes per entry, and calls the module of each number in turn: 1, 11, 17, 2, 18,
12, 1, 11, 11, 1 and **13**. So the premise held — Alysis's own compressor can
be asked for method 13, and a file could carry one. Three of the numbers in
that table (12, 17, 18) have no module in this product at all, which is a
second reading of the same lesson method 20 taught: **the number identifies a
codec in a library, and the library is not this product's.**

**But the file would have settled nothing, because there is nothing to grade it
against.** Module 13 answers command 2 and executes `_Debugger` on the
decompress command, at its own 0xd4 — reproduced as SIGILL under the runner.
Alysis's extension hands method 9 and up to the module of that number, so no
expander that ever shipped in reach can read a method 13 block. Four streams
written here decode as no method this engine knows and as none of the other
modules' either. A specimen is only worth having when a second reader can say
whether what came out is right; for this codec there is no second reader, and
after today there is no shortage of specimens either — they can be made at
will.

So the item moves to Part 1, out of scope. Not because it is impossible:
module 13 IS a reference implementation of its own format, and inverting a
compressor is legitimate under rule #1. It is out because the work would
produce a decoder for a codec no Alysis expander can read, that no archive in
the corpus uses, whose framing is not this container's — with nothing to check
the result against at any point.

### Verified afterwards: the number is a slot, and eDisk proves it

The entry deleted from OPEN_ITEMS.md carried one more claim — that eDisk 1.2
and More Disk Space 1.1 "carry only methods 1, 11 and a 13 named ' Copy'" —
cited to DECISIONS.md 2026-08-25 and `tools/vmac/rescomp.py`. **Neither
source mentions either product.** Checked by downloading both (Macintosh
Garden, md5s in FORMATS.md) and reading their resources:

- **eDisk 1.2's module 13 is real and is a byte copier.** `Cmpr` 0 and 1,
  resource name `JC`, module name " Copy", 310 B and 290 B, working buffer
  zero, an unrolled `MOVE.B (A0)+,(A1)+`. Nothing like Disk Expander's
  5,030-byte module 13.
- **More Disk Space 1.1 is where the claim was wrong.** Its application
  carries one module, and its `Alysis Preferences` file carries 1, 2, 9, 10,
  11 twice and a `Cmpr` 26 named SlowFastC declaring 5,030 B — Disk
  Expander's module 13, not eDisk's.

So the substance held and the sourcing did not, and the sourcing is the part
worth noticing: **a claim can be true and still have no evidence behind it
where it says it does.** A citation is checkable in seconds and this one had
been wrong since it was written; the fact it named was worth keeping and had
been sitting in a file nobody could confirm it from.

The finding it supports is the same one twice over now. In 1992 Alysis wrote
a byte copier into slot 13; by 1993, in a different product, slot 13 held a
codec wanting 229,618 bytes of working buffer. A block header carrying "13"
means nothing without knowing which library wrote it — and this project read
that field as a method for six days.

### The transferable part: a guard is not a requirement

Getting the module to run at all meant undoing a wrong number, and the wrong
number came from the right place. FORMATS.md said module 13 "wanted a 64 KB
working buffer (it compares `workingBufferSize` against 65536 and DEBUGGERs
below it)" — which is true, and is a floor written into the module's own code.
What the module actually needs is the long at +0x1E of its header, 229,618
bytes, and that is the field INIT 32 checks before calling anything (@0x51a2,
refusing with -4 below). Given 64 KB it ran past the mapping and died on every
input.

**A guard says "not below this"; a manifest says "this much".** Reading the
requirement out of the code's own assertion satisfied the assertion and not the
module — and the crash then read as the module's fault, because the previous
sentence in the same document had already blamed the module ("13's traps on an
A-line instruction under the runner"). Two accounts of one module in one
section of one file, both wrong, four days apart. The runner now takes the
larger of its own heuristic and what the module declares, which changes nothing
for methods 1-11 and is the whole of the difference for 13.

## 2026-08-29 (methods 3, 4 and 5, graded across the format) — the gap was in our encoder

OPEN_ITEMS.md wanted *"a DiskDoubler fork of method 3, 4 or 5 that this
project did not write"*, because what nothing graded was "the encodings our
encoder does not emit — other run lengths, other tree shapes, other
contexts". Both halves of that were true and the conclusion drawn from them
was wrong: **the missing encodings were missing from our ENCODER, and the
expander does not care who wrote the stream.**

`ddencode.py` has handed forks to Salient's own 1991 DiskDoubler since
2026-08-21. Every shape it can be made to write is a shape that expander
will grade. So the fix was not to find a file; it was to enumerate what our
encoder had never emitted and emit it. Five cases, one boot, and the
results are in FORMATS.md (*What the 1991 expander accepts*): method 5 at 32
and 64 trees — the two legal counts nothing had ever written — and a method
4 tree with a leaf at depth 1, all byte-exact; `ESC 2`, the shortest run the
format expresses, byte-exact; `ESC 1` refused twice.

**A specimen would have graded one set of shapes. This grades the range.**
That is the general form, and it is the second time this month the
*"what would close it"* line was the thing that was wrong rather than the
thing that was missing (SuperDisk! method 13, above).

### The control is the whole reason the first answer is not in the docs

The first run refused BOTH new method 3 cases, and the obvious reading —
`ESC 2` is illegal — would have been wrong. Both forks had GROWN: 564 bytes
in, 767 and 567 out, which is a thing no DiskDoubler ever wrote and an
expander is entitled to refuse on sight. That is the Splay16 mistake exactly
(DECISIONS.md 2026-08-28): a range check exercised and reported as a
disagreement about the codec.

So the subjects were given a 3,000-byte run to make them shrink, and
`RleGrow` was added: a fork that grows and uses **no token the
already-accepted case does not**. Second run: `Rle2` byte-exact, `RleGrow`
refused, `ESC 1` still refused. Three facts where there had been one guess.
**When a probe and a control disagree about which property is being
measured, the control is cheaper than the argument.**

### And the one that was a real divergence

`ESC 1` was refused at offset 0. That is two hypotheses, not one — the token
might be illegal, or an escape before any literal might be. `RleEsc1Mid`
puts the same token 1,800 bytes in; it is refused too, so it is the token.

`dd_rle` passed `strict = 0` to the shared RLE90 filter, lenient on `ESC 1`
after macutils, **and it was the only one of six call sites that did**.
StuffIt, ARC twice and BinHex twice all pass 1. The comment above it named
its source, which is what made it look settled: a comment that cites a
reference reads as sourced, and nobody asked whether that reference was the
vendor. Salient's is, and it refuses. The engine now refuses too, and 91/91
DiskDoubler fixtures are unchanged — no real fork carries the token, which
is exactly what a vendor expander rejecting it predicts.

## 2026-08-29 (four more names) — one of the five references was already here

OPEN_ITEMS.md carried *"Five archive formats are still unnamed for want of a
reference: zstd, lzip, brotli, lrzip and WIM"*, with the note that rule #1
does not bend for a constant that is easy to look up. Four are named now and
the fifth never can be. Two things are worth keeping about how that went.

**lzip's reference was in `reference/deark` the whole time.** deark's
`unsupported.c` — a module whose entire job is naming formats it will not
open, which is exactly this file's job — tests `LZIP` at offset 0 and has
since deark was cloned for ARC's Distilled method on 2026-08-25. The item
said no implementation or spec was in `reference/`; for one of its five that
was false on the day it was written. **A "we have no reference for X" list is
a search, and a search is a claim about somebody's looking** (BLOCKERS.md
names that mechanism; this is the fourth time it has fired here).

**brotli cannot be identified, and that is a finding rather than a gap.** RFC
7932 section 9.1 is the whole stream header: one to seven bits of WBITS. No
magic, no signature, no version, no length. Every other format in
`fmt_foreign.c` was waiting for somebody to look up a constant; this one has
no constant to look up, so it moves to part 1 with the RFC kept as evidence
for a negative. Filing it beside four that WERE lookups is what made it look
like the same kind of thing for three days.

### What the weakest probe is allowed to be

lzip's magic is four ASCII bytes with nothing behind them, and deark rates it
50 against xz's 90 for that reason. This engine reports no confidence — a
sniff returns one answer — so the confidence had to be spent somewhere, and
it was spent on ORDER: lzip is tested after every corroborated magic in the
file, so only a file that matched nothing else reaches it. The false positive
is real and is measured rather than feared: a text file opening with the word
LZIPPER is claimed as an lzip archive. That is the honest cost of naming it
at all, and naming it is still worth more to somebody holding a `.lz` than
"not recognized" is.

The other three each had something to corroborate the magic with, and each
corroboration is tested by removing it: zstd's skippable-frame range has one
value above and one below it, lrzip's `MAGIC_LEN` rejects a 23-byte file that
starts with `LRZI`, and WIM's header-size field rejects 207 where wimlib
accepts only 208.


## 2026-08-29 (the end-of-run report) — what the run cost, on a screen with no scrollback

The Macintosh application ended every run in one sentence. "Extraction
complete." was true and said nothing about the names it had changed to get
there; "Some entries could not be extracted." named neither the entry nor
what was wrong with it. The CLI had listed both since 2026-08-27. The
request was to give the Mac the same thing — renames AND the failures that
do not stop the archive — in one box.

**A box, and only when there is something to say.** A run with nothing per
entry to report still ends in the alert it always did: a list holding one
line, behind a button the user has to find before the familiar one, is a
worse answer to the common case. What the two share is the SENTENCE — the
outcome chain that picks it is one `if` ladder now, and the box and the
alert are two ways of showing its answer.

**The outcome is the list's last line, and it is pinned.** It reads as one
document ending in its verdict, which is what it is. The first attempt was an
ordinary last line, and a Mac Plus photographed the cost: an archive of
eighteen photographs put "Extraction complete." nine rows below the fold,
behind a scroll bar nobody has a reason to drag — on a screen with no
scrollback the conclusion cannot be somewhere the reader has to go and find.
The second attempt moved it out of the list into a static item below the
frame, which fixed that and broke the reading: the box stopped being one
document. What ships is both — after a blank line, in the same frame and
font, bold; drawn where the text ends when everything fits, and held on the
bottom row when the list is longer than the box. The saved document simply
ends with it.

**The extension is KEPT and the stem is cut**, and the collision number goes
before it: `LongDocumentName.txt` → `LongDocumen.txt`, `dup.txt` → `dup~1.txt`.
Cutting at 31 bytes leaves `...ForTheBoard.rt`, which reads as a corrupted
name rather than a shortened one, and `dup.txt~1` no longer reads as one of a
numbered pair — so something had to give either way. **This was decided
backwards first**, for a few hours on the same day: the argument was that a
Macintosh opens a file by the type and creator `sink_end` stamps on it, which
makes a suffix decoration and the first thing to spend. The correction is that
it is not this machine's convention that matters, it is the FILE's: a name
that arrived carrying a suffix generally came off a system that has no
resource forks and no Finder types, so the archive holds neither for the
shell to stamp, and the suffix is the only thing left that says what opens it.
A `.txt` from a zip is the case, and it is not a rare one. The CLI never had
the question: 255 bytes rarely needs the room.

**The verdict is worded for whether the list is there.** "Some entries could
not be extracted." was the whole of what a user was told, so it had to carry
the news. Above a list that names each failure and what is wrong with it, it
restates that list in the vaguest terms available; what is left worth saying
is the one word that tells this run from a clean one, so the box reads
"Extraction finished." and the alert — where there is no list, and the
sentence is still all there is — is unchanged.

**A name goes in whole, and the box wraps it.** The lines were fixed-width
records with the names elided at 36 characters, which is fine until an
archive holds eighteen photographs whose names differ at character 34: the
list then says the same thing eighteen times and cannot answer the one
question it exists for, which is *which file became `~11`*. So the text is
packed end to end in one pool — a line costs what its names are, not a
per-line ceiling every short line pays for — and the box measures and wraps
it at draw time, preferring to break after the name so one row is what the
archive called it and the next is what became of it. Wrapping is worked out
once, when the box opens, because `TextWidth` is a trap call and scrolling
must not pay for it.

**Failures have 16 of the 64 lines reserved, and 1 KB of the pool.** The pool is fixed (the
engine's budget is taken from `FreeMem()` once, so anything allocated during
a run comes out of the Toolbox's reserve), and the first version simply
filled it in order. An archive with 100 alike names then filled all 64 with
renames and pushed out the two lines that mattered — the entries that could
not be written at all. A report that drops the actionable line to make room
for the hundredth cosmetic one is worse than no report.

**A folder MERGED into another is reported, but only where the archive
declares the folder.** Two folders whose names shorten alike become one, and
nothing is lost but the path is not the archive's. Telling that from a
folder merely revisited needs to know the name the folder was CREATED for,
so the shell remembers the first 63 bytes of the archive's name for up to 24
folders it made itself; a folder that no entry declares — the formats
without directory entries, where folders exist only inside the paths of the
files in them — cannot be told apart and is not guessed at. Exact where it
fires, silent where it cannot be.

### Two defects, found by driving the box rather than by testing the lines

`interactive.py --report` builds an archive designed to make the report TALK
— names too long, one that fits only without its suffix, a duplicate, two
folders that shorten alike, eighteen photographs alike for 31 characters,
one damaged entry — extracts it on an emulated Mac Plus, photographs the
box, scrolls it, saves it as a document, and reads that document back off
the volume. The first run of it found:

- **the tenth alike name ended the extraction.** `BumpName` numbered
  `~1`..`~9`, and the loop that calls it returned -1 on running out, which
  the engine reads as *abort the archive*. Live since M10; the archive of 26
  entries produced 10 files (BLOCKERS.md). Numbering goes to `~99` now, and
  a name that still cannot be placed fails **its own entry** — which is the
  same rule this project already had for allocation failures, applied to the
  other resource an entry needs.
- **the mock Toolbox never refused anything.** `PBDirCreateSync` tolerated
  `EEXIST` and handed back a fresh directory id; `PBGetCatInfoSync` returned
  `noErr` over an untouched param block. So `EnsureDir`'s dupFNErr branch —
  the one the real File Manager takes whenever a folder is already there —
  had never run under test, and the merge it stands for could not be
  detected at all. Both are honest now, and the folder-collision case that
  has passed since 2026-08-28 was passing for the wrong reason.

The transferable half: neither defect was reachable by the corpus (932
fixtures, no two names alike for 31 characters, nothing damaged) or by the
mock (which refused nothing). What reached them was **an archive built to
make the interface say every sentence it has**, which is a different
instrument from a fixture built to be decoded correctly.


## 2026-08-29 (a cut archive) — where the archive ends and where the file ends

The end-of-run box lists what did not come out. That invites the question the
maintainer asked of it — *is there a way to know what hasn't been extracted?*
— and answering it honestly meant checking, rather than asserting, that every
entry the engine declines reaches the sink. Every one does. What does not is
an entry the walk never reaches, and two formats were ending their walks at
the wrong place:

- **DiskDoubler DDAR ends where the FILE ends**, because the format has no
  end marker, so a header that would not read was taken for the end. A cut
  download therefore extracted the entries before the cut and reported
  success. The end is now the file's END exactly: arriving anywhere else —
  short of it, or past it because an entry's data was stepped over — is
  truncation. Inside the file every entry position must hold a `DDAR` magic,
  so a position that does not is damage as well.
- **DiskDoubler DDA2 has an end marker and never checked for it.** Falling out
  of the loop any other way now returns `UNC_ERR_TRUNCATED` — with one
  exception the corpus supplied within the hour: `derived-DD_Expand.sea` ends
  after its last entry with 38 bytes and no marker at all, and `unar` reads
  it, so a walk that runs out of file INSIDE the file with less than a record
  left has reached a real ending. Being sent PAST the end is what a cut looks
  like. The residue is a cut that lands in that same 46-byte window, which is
  indistinguishable from the valid ending and is written down as such
  (TESTING.md) rather than guessed at. The first version of this rule called
  that `.sea` truncated and `./ci.sh` failed on it — 931/932 — which is the
  whole reason the fixture corpus is the arbiter of a rule like this and not
  the format documentation.
- **StuffIt clamps the archive's stated length to the file's actual length.**
  That clamp is what keeps the walk inside the file, and it is the same step
  that erased the evidence: the loop then ended tidily at the new end. It
  clamps and REMEMBERS now, and a walk that finished under a clamp returns
  truncated.

The general shape, which is what transfers: **a walk's end condition has to
distinguish "the archive said it was over" from "the bytes ran out".** Every
format here answers that differently — an end marker, a stated length, an
entry count, or simply EOF — and wherever the two answers were folded into
one branch, a cut file became a complete run.

### The suite that could not have caught it, and the one that does

`run_robustness.py` has truncated every fixture at four fractions since M2. It
requires "a normal exit code", and exit 0 is one — so it passed 9,320 variants
over both defects. That is the tenth smell in BLOCKERS.md's list (an aggregate
that cannot fail on a property it never aggregates) in the suite whose whole
subject is damaged input.

`tools/run_truncation.py` asks the oracle instead: where `unar` calls a cut
archive broken, so must we. It found the StuffIt defect immediately, which the
hand scan that found the DiskDoubler one had missed — **because the hand scan
picked its fixtures alphabetically**, and for DiskDoubler that is two
single-file archives with no walk in them. The suite was written with the same
bug, passed against a deliberately broken build, and was fixed to choose the
most-entried fixtures. A check that cannot fail is worth exactly what it
costs to run.

## 2026-08-29 (two MIME gaps) — the item described our own binary, and nobody had run it

OPEN_ITEMS.md's *"Waiting on a file this machine does not have"* carried two
MIME entries from M36. Between them they made four statements about what mpack
does and what this engine does. Three are false, and every one was one command
from being checked.

**`x-uuencode` inside a multipart.** The entry read *"mpack decodes those; this
does not"*, and both halves are wrong.

- munpack does not decode it. Its encoding enum is `enc_none, enc_qp,
  enc_base64` (`reference/mpack/decode.c`), an unknown Content-Transfer-Encoding
  warns and falls through as `enc_none`, and `handleUuencode` is reached only
  for a depth-0 `text/*` part carrying no encoding, no `filename` and no
  `name`. Fed a multipart whose part is `Content-Transfer-Encoding:
  x-uuencode`, it prints *"ignoring unknown content transfer encoding"* and
  writes the uuencoded TEXT out as the file.
- This engine decodes it, and always did: the uu probe wins the sniff and steps
  over the boundary lines, so `macextract x` writes the 36-byte payload
  byte-exact.

The real defect is the one the entry did not state. Where a multipart holds a
uuencoded part AND something else, the uu probe shadows the MIME walk and the
rest is dropped — measured on a two-part message, this engine lists
`uupart.txt` alone where munpack writes both it and the base64 `wanted.bin`.
Which means the oracle exists for the half that matters: munpack reads every
part a uu part is hiding, and this machine writes the specimen.

**`message/partial`.** The entry said the specimen was missing. mpack writes
it: `mpack -m 10000 -o out.mime payload.bin` cuts 30,000 bytes into
`out.mime.01`…`.04`, each a `message/partial`, and munpack reassembles them
byte-exact. Two things a harness on that has to know, both of which cost a run
here:

- munpack reassembles through a fixed directory, `/usr/tmp/m-prts-$USER`, and
  does not create it;
- mpack stamps `total=` on EVERY part, and `handlePartial` creates that
  directory's `CT` file with `os_createnewfile` — `O_EXCL` — so parts 2..n die
  with *"CT: File exists"* unless `CT` is removed between invocations. This is
  mpack failing to round-trip with its own twin, not a defect in either.

The entry also described this engine as decoding "each file on its own", a part
that is half an attachment yielding half a file. None of the four parts decodes
at all: part 1 sniffs as a MIME message and then fails *"not a recognized
archive"*, parts 2–4 are *"format not recognized"*, and `src/fmt_mime.c` does
not contain the word *partial*.

**What transfers.** BLOCKERS.md already keeps *"ask what evidence a tolerance
rests on; if the answer is our own output, it cannot fail"*. This is its
neighbour and it is now in that list: **an open item that describes what our
own binary does is the cheapest claim in the repository to check, and is
therefore the one most likely to have been written rather than run.** Both
items had been filed under *waiting on a file*, which is a decision to stop
looking; neither was ever blocked on a file. The section they were moved to
names its oracle instead of its shortage.

## 2026-08-29 (a Macintosh wrote a MIME message) — the port was in the reference tree

`src/fmt_mime.c` is transcribed from mpack 1.6, and every message it had ever
been graded against was made on Linux. OPEN_ITEMS.md filed the gap as a wait:
what was missing was a message a MACINTOSH wrote, because the half no Unix
tool has is the layout of the `application/applefile` part and what a real Mac
put in the Finder-info entry.

The entry named the thing that would settle it — "mpack's own Macintosh port"
— and that port is in `reference/mpack`, in the tree `tools/build_mpack.sh`
had been building munpack out of since M36. `tools/macmime/` compiles
`encode_applefile()` and `encode()` out of it unmodified for a 68000, adds the
host glue THINK C used to supply, and `tools/vmac/macmime.py` runs the result
on System 6.0.8. The message is 1,912 bytes and passes 18/18, both forks
byte-exact through this engine and readable by munpack.

**What is mpack's and what is ours.** Everything that decides a byte is
mpack's: encode.c, macfile.c, codes.c, magic.c, md5c.c, xmalloc.c, compiled as
they lie. Ours is the Toolbox glue and two `os_` entry points. The boundary
moved a byte in exactly one place, and it is named in FORMATS.md rather than
here: line endings are the C library's, so this specimen cannot settle them.

Three things cost a run each, and all three transfer.

### Retro68's newlib cannot open a file on System 6.0.8

mpack speaks nothing but `FILE *`, so the first build used stdio. It hung —
silently, with the window drawn and no bomb. Two runs went into finding out
where, and the answer was the first call: a bare
`open("Probe1.tmp", O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_TRUNC, 0666)` **never returns**,
photographed at 40 s and again at 80 s with only the banner line on screen.
Not stdio, then — the POSIX layer under it.

**This is a symptom and not an attribution.** Retro68's `_open_r`
(`libretro/syscalls.c`) does `HCreate(0,0,…)`, then `HOpenDF(0,0,…)`, then
`HOpen` if that answered `paramErr`; which of the three hangs has not been
reduced to a reproduction, so it is not a finding and it is not filed as one.
It sits beside something this project HAS reduced: `OpenDF`/`HOpenDF` return
`paramErr` on System 6 (`tools/retro68-hopen-repro/`).

The fix needs none of that settled. newlib's `funopen()` builds a `FILE *` out
of four callbacks, so every byte mpack reads or writes goes through the same
fully-zeroed HFS param blocks the shipping application uses — and mpack reads
the LIVE forks, which is what macos.c's `Macopen()` gave it by reaching into
THINK C's `FILE` internals. The engine's "no libc" rule bought the same
immunity for the application years earlier, for reasons nobody had connected
to this.

### A harness that could not put a Finder flag on the volume

The first complete run failed one check of eighteen: the Finder flags in the
applefile were `0x0000` where the subject was built with `0x0100`. The subject
was wrong, not the applefile — **`hcopy -m` does not carry the Finder flags**,
measured off the Mac in one command: a MacBinary whose byte 73 is `0x01`
round-trips out of the image as `0x00`. So the flag was zero before the
Macintosh ever saw the file, and a run that trusted the transfer would have
been grading the encoder against a word nothing had set.

They are set on the Mac now, with `PBHSetFInfoSync`, and the catalogue is read
AGAIN afterwards so that what reaches mpack is what the volume holds rather
than what the harness intended. The general form is the one this file keeps
finding: **a fixture is not what you built, it is what arrived** — and the
transfer is part of the experiment.

### The subject has to be in the blessed folder

`mkboot.py` puts a shell application in the System Folder, and a shell
application's default directory is its own. The subject went to the volume
root, which is where every other harness here puts one, and `PBGetCatInfo`
answered −43. Two runs; the frame said so the first time, which is the whole
argument for a harness that draws its progress on screen.

### What it does not do

One message from one encoder. It establishes what mpack's Macintosh port
writes; Eudora 1.2 is in the corpus and Claris Emailer is not, and neither has
been run. That is a different specimen and not an open item — the layout
question this one was filed for is answered.

## 2026-08-29 (a part that hid its message) — the strongest scan is not the strongest claim

`unc_sniff` runs the uuencode, btoa and BinHex probes before the MIME one,
because a posting whose body is one BinHex document is a BinHex file that
happens to have mail headers, not a message with an attachment. That rule is
right for a posting and wrong for a multipart: those three probes scan for a
document ANYWHERE in the file, so a message holding a uuencoded part AND a
base64 attachment was claimed whole by the uuencode walk, which stepped over
the boundary lines, decoded the uu part and never saw the rest. Measured, and
the measurement is the previous entry's: `macextract l` listed `uupart.txt`
alone where munpack wrote `wanted.bin`.

**What changed.** `unc_mime_probe_multipart` — the same probe, refusing
anything that does not declare a `multipart/*` WITH a boundary — runs before
those three. A boundary is the evidence that this walk can enumerate the
parts; without one, nothing is taken away from the format that has a marker of
its own, and a `.hqx` or `.uu` posting still belongs to it. That is asserted
both ways in `tools/run_mime.py`.

**The half that could have cost a capability.** Claiming the message means the
MIME walk has to produce everything the uuencode walk used to, and mpack's own
rule drops it: a `text/*` part with no filename is the note the sender typed
(`handleMessage`). So the walk now runs mpack's OTHER rule at every depth
instead of only at the top. `handleUuencode()` fires for a depth-0 text part
with no encoding, no `filename` and no `name`, and looks at the BODY; the same
test at any depth is `mime_body_nested()`. A uuencoded part is decoded through
the two helpers `fmt_uu.c` already had — exported rather than transcribed a
second time, because two transcriptions of one format disagree eventually —
and named from its `begin` line.

`Content-Transfer-Encoding: x-uuencode` is not what decides. mpack has no such
value (`parseEncoding` warns and falls through to `enc_none`), this engine now
follows it there, and the body decides in both. A header that lies costs
nothing either way.

**A BinHex or btoa document in that position is kept as it lies**, undecoded,
named `partN`. This walk has no decoder for either and is not getting one; what
it must not do is drop the bytes, which is exactly what the sniff change would
otherwise have done to the one shape the BinHex probe used to claim. The saved
part is a `.hqx` and `macextract` says so when it is handed back — two steps
where there was one, against a part that used to be dropped entirely.

**What transfers.** The probe order was documented as a hierarchy of
CONFIDENCE — "a stronger claim on the same kind of file" — and a scan that
finds a document is indeed strong evidence that a document is there. It is no
evidence at all about what ELSE is there, and the format that can answer that
question was running last. **Ask what a probe's evidence bounds, not just how
strong it is**: a marker found by scanning bounds a region, never a file.

## 2026-08-29 (a message in several files) — the sixth join scheme, and the first whose parts are text

`message/partial` (RFC 2046 5.2.2) was the last item in OPEN_ITEMS.md's
*"not written, and not waiting for anything"*: the oracle was on this machine
already — mpack writes the sets with `-m`, munpack puts them back — and no
part of one yielded anything here.

**Why it is a join scheme and not a shape of the MIME reader.** The parts are
FILES. The engine never opens a file (engine discipline), so the host has to
produce them and the joiner has to sequence them, which is exactly what the
five existing schemes do. What made this one fit at all is that the joined
stream needs no intermediate file: part 1's body already begins with the
enclosed message's own headers, so the concatenation IS a message and the
scheme's reader is `unc_mime_extract`. mpack composes something larger for its
own output — the enclosing headers minus `Content-*` and `Message-ID`, then
the inner ones — because its result is a file somebody may read. Ours is a
stream somebody parses.

**Two properties no other scheme has**, and both cost a change to the joiner
rather than a row in its table:

- **Every part's header is a different length**, because a part's header is
  its own mail headers. `UncSegmentDesc.head_len` is one number for the set,
  and five schemes were right to keep it there; the joiner now asks a `hdr`
  hook per part where a scheme has one, keeps the answer for the OPEN part
  only (`SegJoin.curhdr`), and reads the payload from it. The hook re-proves
  the part's `number` and `id` while it has the header in hand, which is the
  structural check this scheme gets in place of a fingerprint comparison the
  host could not make.
- **The set declares a part COUNT and no byte total.** The flat walk stops at
  `total`, and a `total` of UNKNOWN made it run off the end of the map and
  report a set that had simply ENDED as one missing a part. So the joiner
  learns the total when it opens the last part, which is the first moment the
  number exists.

**Identity by content, through a different call.** A part names its set in an
`id` parameter every part repeats — content, not filename, like StuffIt and
Compact Pro. But `unc_segment_match` is given 100 bytes and mail headers are
longer than that, so both hosts identify a candidate with
`unc_segment_describe` instead. That is the second existing shape reused
rather than invented: only the LAST part must carry `total`, so a set opened
anywhere else asks around for its count exactly as a Compact Pro set asks
which segment holds the index — the Mac shell's `SegFindLast` now answers for
both.

**What the oracle cost.** munpack reassembles through a fixed directory it
does not create, and refuses to overwrite its own `CT` file, so a set fed to
it part by part dies on part 2 unless that file is removed in between. Both
quirks are in `tools/run_mime.py` and TESTING.md; neither is a defect in this
engine, and both would have been read as one by a harness that only checked
the exit status.

**It joined on a Mac Plus the same day**, at 4 MB and at the 1 MB floor, and
that run is a case in `tools/vmac/segcheck.py` rather than a command somebody
typed once. The check is the Out volume, not the on-screen grid: what is being
tested is that four TEXT files became one Macintosh file with a resource fork,
and an aggregate cannot fail on that.

**Stack.** This is the only chain in the engine that goes through the engine
twice — a joiner calling a format reader that reads back through the joiner —
so it was measured rather than assumed: 2,796 B on a 68000, second only to
DiskDoubler's 3,386 B, against a 4 KB rule (FORMATS.md, *Stack*).

---

## 2026-08-29 (the chain that was walked twice) — the postfix byte was the last one the walk wrote

Asked why StuffIt Expander 4.0.2 unstuffs `derived-EmpireBuilderDemo.sit` in
three quarters of our time (FORMATS.md, *Timed on the same Mac Plus*). The
answer to THAT question is not this entry — but profiling the archive to ask
it found two things.

**The fixture the comparison lost on has no method 13 in it.** Its three forks
are methods 2 and 3, out of the entry headers, and the profile agrees: 58 %
`unc_lzw_read`, 26 % `unc_huff_decode`, 7.7 % `unc_crc16_arc`. FORMATS.md's
decode-cost table had called that row "StuffIt (13)" and its prose reasoned
from the label, while `unc_huff_decode`'s own comment three files away names
the same fixture as "a StuffIt method-3 run". **Two files disagreed and the
one nobody was reading was right** — corrected in place.

**And LZW walked every symbol's parents twice.** `lzw_firstbyte` walked the
chain to find the byte the new table entry gets as its postfix; the expansion
that follows walks the same parents to emit the symbol. The chain is written
reversed, so its LAST element is the root — which is exactly the byte
`lzw_firstbyte` went looking for. Build the chain first and the postfix is
`chain[n-1]`, free.

**Why it survived M29's pass.** It is a faithful transcription of `LZW.c`'s
`NextLZWSymbol`, where the two walks sit in different functions and neither
looks redundant beside the other. The profile named `unc_lzw_read` as the hot
function and it WAS the hot function; what was wrong was the order of two
steps inside it, and a profile does not report ordering. **A duplicated walk
reads as one walk each in every measurement that attributes cost to
functions.**

Three details the change turns on:

- **KwKwK costs a slot, not a walk.** When the code is the one about to be
  added, its expansion is the previous symbol's followed by that expansion's
  own first byte. Reversed, that byte comes FIRST, so the previous symbol's
  chain goes in one slot up and `chain[0]` takes the root the walk found. One
  walk, one extra store.
- **`lzw_grow` had to move up with it.** It reallocates the block the chain
  lives in and resets the chain, so a chain built before it would not survive.
  Its condition is unchanged and nothing between it and the table add reads
  what it moves, so it now runs before the walk rather than after.
- **Both hot loops read the struct on every byte.** The walk reloaded
  `z->parent`, `z->chr` and `z->capsymbols` per step and the drain updated
  `z->chainpos` and `produced` per byte. Locals and two pointers: that half is
  worth more than the walk on the short chains this corpus actually has
  (2.9 bytes a code on the StuffIt fixture).

**Measured, and it is the shape that makes it safe.** −16.3 % on
`popper-1.7.tar.Z` down to −1.4 % on `synth-arc-mixed.arc`, and **0.00 % —
not "within noise", identical instruction counts** — on six fixtures whose
formats never reach this decoder. The full table is in FORMATS.md, *Decode
cost*. Heap is unmoved: no table was added, and `unc_lzw`'s allocation is the
same layout it was.

**The branch is graded.** A counter build over all 946 fixtures reports the
KwKwK path taken in **111** of them, `.Z`, DiskDoubler method 1 and StuffIt
method 2 alike — so the restructured half is inside the differential suite
rather than beside it. Establishing that before trusting the green run is the
same rule as *a test that passes may not be testing anything*.

---

## 2026-08-30 (the flush that was not the answer) — a screen cannot see a commit

*"Expander's 56 s and our 76 s do not cover the same work"* was the best
candidate for the one row the yardstick loses (FORMATS.md, *Timed on the same
Mac Plus*): our sink flushes after every fork and Expander's output was known
to be absent from the volume until it quits, so up to 415 KB of writing sat
outside its number and inside ours. Tested on 2026-08-30. **It is real, it is
under a second, and it explains none of the gap.**

**The first instrument measured nothing and said "3 s".** The obvious test —
send Command-Q, time it the way the extraction is timed — came back at exactly
the poll floor for both applications and for a 1.6 KB control, which read as
"quitting is free". It is not: Expander's frames either side of Command-Q are
**byte-identical**, so the timer was watching an event that never reaches the
screen. The reading was consistent, reproducible, and about nothing. **An
instrument that can only see repaints reports the floor for everything that
does not repaint, and the floor looks like a small number rather than a
missing one.**

**The image is the instrument.** The emulator writes `boot.hfv` on the host
while it runs, so a copy of it can be mounted mid-run without touching the
machine. Three reads — at the last changed frame, after the quit key, after
the kill — settle it directly: our three files are on the volume when the
clock stops; Expander's four are **not there at all** and appear within a
second of the keystroke. The claim this project had carried since 2026-08-29
was right about WHAT and had never been asked HOW MUCH; the answer is that on
this emulator a commit is a host `memcpy` with no seek time behind it.

**Which is the honest limit of the result.** A real Mac Plus writing 415 KB to
a floppy or a SCSI disk would not be paying zero, so the ratio a user on
hardware sees is not this one. That is a MANUAL_TESTS.md question and stays
there rather than becoming a hypothesis in a status file.

**And it cost one wrong inference of mine on the way**, worth recording
because the shape recurs: identical frames plus a Finder on the boot volume
said "Command-Q did not quit". The image probe then found the files landing at
exactly that keystroke. **Two explanations fit "the screen did not change" —
nothing happened, and something happened invisibly — and only the second
instrument can tell them apart.**

**What is left of the original question.** Expander unstuffs that archive in
57 s against our 73 s, both applications' output equally on the disk, so the
remainder is decode and I/O rather than accounting. On Linux that archive is
58 % `unc_lzw_read`, 26 % `unc_huff_decode` and 8 % `unc_crc16_arc` — and the
last of those is the 2-3x table this project has twice measured and twice
declined to buy with 3 KB of code (2026-08-27, *what the tables left behind*).

---

## 2026-08-30 (the flag that was never measured on these files) — 70.4 s to 52.6 s, and a kilobyte

Asked to make this at least as fast as StuffIt Expander 4.0.2 on the one
archive it wins. It is level now — 56 s each — and not one line of decoder
changed. What changed was which functions the optimiser was allowed to work
on.

**First, the probe, because the profile everyone had was the wrong machine.**
Linux callgrind sees decoders and nothing else; the screenshot clock sees the
