whole run and nothing inside it. `UNC_AT_TIMING` bills File Manager time by
kind on the Mac itself and leaves the engine as the remainder: reads 0.6 s,
writes 0.3 s, create/`SetEOF`/close/`FlushVol`/catalog 0.2 s, **engine 69.4 s
of 70.4** (FORMATS.md, *What -O3 is worth, and where*). **Every I/O idea on
the list was worth 1.1 s in total** — including dropping the per-fork
`FlushVol`, which would have spent the guarantee that our output is on the
disk when the alert goes up to buy a fifth of a second.

**Then the flag.** `mac/CMakeLists.txt` built everything at `-Os`, and said
so with a measurement: `-O2` on `sit_arsenic.c` cost 1,536 B and was SLOWER,
1,304 s against 1,284 s. That measurement is sound and still is. What was
wrong is that a conclusion drawn on one file had been applied to forty, and
the note beside it said the question could not be re-asked "without a harness
that resolves better than 20 s on a 22-minute run" — which the tick probe now
is. On the four files a StuffIt run actually lives in, `-O3` is **−25.4 %**.

**A rule proved on one file is a rule about that file.** The engine has ~40
files and one of them was ever measured. The cost of not re-asking was a fifth
of every StuffIt, DiskDoubler and PackIt extraction, for as long as those
files have existed.

**The knee is real and it argued for a list.** `-O2` on the whole engine bar
Arsenic is 56.0 s for +40,192 B against 56.2 s for +5,504 B on four files:
0.4 % more speed for 35 KB more application, on a machine whose engine budget
is stated to the byte. A blanket flag would have been the easy call and the
wrong one.

**The maintainer's question was the best move of the day.** Asked whether
buying speed with size argued for hand-written assembly. It argued instead for
aiming the optimiser: `__attribute__((optimize("O3")))` on six functions is
**52.6 s for +1,024 B**, against 52.5 s for +8,320 B. The file-level flag had
been paying for `unc_huff_read_tree` — +2,428 B for 0.44 % of the run — and
for every cold path beside it.

Three things fell out that were not the point:

- **Marking functions puts them under the oracle.** A flag in
  `mac/CMakeLists.txt` optimises code that no Linux run ever executes, so the
  most aggressively compiled code in the project was the one thing the
  932-fixture differential suite could not see. `UNC_HOT` lives in `src/`, so
  every fixture now decodes through it on both machines.
- **The attribute made a function smaller.** `unc_sit_decode_fork` is 226 B
  under its `-Os` size, because GCC will not inline across differing
  optimisation attributes and the two marked method decoders stopped being
  copies inside it.
- **Assembly is still on the table and the case for it has to be made
  differently.** It would now have to beat a `-O3` inner loop rather than an
  `-Os` one, and it would cost exactly the coverage the point above just
  bought. The `qemu-m68k` harnesses that already run Alysis's and Aladdin's
  modules are the shape a graded asm routine would need, and that harness has
  to exist before the first instruction, not after.

**What is NOT claimed.** That `UNC_HOT` is the right list — it is the
measured list for one archive plus the two shared primitives, and every other
format's inner loop is still at `-Os`. And that `sit_arsenic.c` should join
them: that file's own measurement says otherwise and was not re-taken, which
is the same discipline this entry is about.

## 2026-08-30 (the shell name System 3.0 does not read) — the oldest system a Mac Plus boots, and a NOGRID that meant nothing

**Claim: the application runs unchanged on System 3.0 / Finder 5.1, the system
the Macintosh Plus shipped with in January 1986.** 69/69 byte-exact at 4 MB and
69/69 at 1 MB, plus the interactive application driven end to end
(FORMATS.md, *System 3.0 tier*). No source change; the same binary.

The first run said the opposite, and the way it was wrong is the transferable
part.

**A harness assumption, inherited by every tier.** `run.py --system Sys30.img`
reported `NOGRID` — "the app never painted its 'alive' grid, so it did not
launch or bombed before starting". That sentence is true about the frame and
false about the application. Every harness here installs the application as the
boot shell by rewriting the boot blocks' `bbShellName` (`mkboot.py`, offset
0x1A), which works on System 6.0.8 and 7.0.1 and had therefore never been
questioned.

**The disproof cost one boot.** Set the shell name to `NoSuchApp` and boot: if
the field is read, a missing shell must produce *something* different. The
framebuffer was byte-identical to the `MacExtract` boot — same MD5 — and so was
a third boot with the application at the volume root rather than in the blessed
folder. System 3.0 does not act on that field at all. Nothing had been launched
in any of the three, so the first result was evidence about `mkboot.py` and
about nothing else.

**Consequence: it is launched the way a person launches it** — boot to the
Finder, double-click the volume, double-click the application
(`tools/vmac/sys30.py`). *(This entry first said the Finder therefore stays
resident while the engine works. It does not: launching an application unloads
it. The claim and what it cost are in BLOCKERS.md, `the Finder that was never
there`.)*

**And a second chord that "did nothing".** Cmd-. does not cancel Standard File
on System 3.0: the frame after it is the Open dialog unchanged. This file
already records a failure that looked exactly like that and was really a
chord the emulator never delivered (`interactive.py`, `command_key`), so the
claim needed a discriminator rather than a repetition. The one to hand was our
own code: the same chord, in the same run, cancelled an extraction through the
application's progress dialog and produced *"Extraction cancelled."* The chord
arrives; old Standard File ignores it. The Cancel button quits normally, and
the frame after it is byte-identical to the Finder window the application was
launched from.

**What is NOT claimed.** That System 3.0 is the oldest system that could work,
only the oldest a Macintosh Plus can boot. Anything older than the Plus means a
64K ROM, which has no HFS and no `NGetTrapAddress` — both of which this
application uses — so that is a different question and not a smaller one.

## 2026-08-30 (512 KB, and the machine that cannot be reached) — the RAM was never the boundary

**Claim: 512 KB of RAM is enough, and a 64 KB ROM is not.** A Macintosh 512Ke
runs the application under System 3.0, 65/69 of the sample byte-exact, the rest
refused per entry (FORMATS.md, *512 KB machines*). A true Macintosh 512K never
reaches the application at all.

**The interesting number is which SYSTEM.** On the same 512 KB machine System
3.0 passes 65 and System 6.0.8 passes 58, its failures a strict superset. The
mechanism is the SYSTEM HEAP: free heap when extraction starts is 218,208 B
against 122,808 B, a 95,400 B gap, against System files of 181,913 B and
1,221,287 B. The lesson is not "old is small" but that **the floor was set by
the system, not by the machine**, and nobody had varied the system.

**The negative was attributed before it was reported**, which is the part worth
keeping. The 512K boots to a Sad Mac `0F0064` on our System 3.0 floppy. Cheap
A/Bs said what that meant: the same ROM with no disk gives the blinking
floppy icon and a framebuffer byte-identical to the Mac Plus control, so the
machine is healthy; Apple's own 128K ROM (`28BA61CE`) and the 512K's
(`28BA4E50`) fail identically, so it is not the ROM revision; and a blank
800 KB HFS volume — same media, same filesystem, no boot blocks — gives the
no-disk frame rather than the Sad Mac (added 2026-08-31), so the drive reads
the disk and it is the boot code that dies. What is left is the volume — and
the reason that ROM cannot boot it is HFS, which arrived with its successor.

**Which codecs survive 512 KB is decided by adaptivity, not by appetite.**
`derived-PuzzleGetter_0.8.sit` asks for 270,043 B and fits, renegotiating its
block down; `derived-a2basic.dmg.sit` asks for 247,687 B and does not, having
reached 37,372 B when it gave up. That is 2026-08-03's finding arriving on a
smaller machine: growth-on-demand rescued the Arsenic block and did nothing
for the LZW table, and 512 KB is where the difference stops being academic.
*(This entry first made the point with `popper-1.7.tar.Z`, which was refused
at 224,264 B. It is no longer refused — the entry below tightened the bound
that sized that table, and the corrected figures are in FORMATS.md.)*

**What is NOT claimed.** That the Sad Mac's cause has been reduced to a
minimal reproduction: it has not, and the trap-level explanation is labelled a
hypothesis in FORMATS.md. It does not change the outcome, because
`TrapAvailable` calls `NGetTrapAddress` and every file operation here goes
through an HFS parameter block, and neither exists in a 64K ROM. A machine
that could boot would still not run this.

## 2026-08-30 (a byte buys fewer symbols the further in it sits) — a third off the LZW table, and a fixture back

**Claim: the Unix compress table was a third larger than the input can
justify, and `popper-1.7.tar.Z` extracts on a 512 KB Macintosh now.** 225,170 B
of budget before, 148,408 B after, measured by binary search on Linux; on the
machine itself 224,232 B became 147,796 B and a refusal became a byte-exact
extraction, taking that tier from 64/69 to 65/69 (FORMATS.md, *Unix compress:
the `.Z` peak*, *512 KB machines*).

**The old bound assumed every code is the minimum width.** `compress(1)`
declares `maxbits=16` whatever it compressed, so the table was sized from the
input instead — `256 + reserved + insize * 8 / 9`, one symbol per 9 bits. But
codes are 9 bits only while the table is under 512 entries, 10 under 1024, and
so on: a byte late in a big stream buys a sixteenth of a symbol, not a ninth.
Walking that schedule is the same bound done exactly, and on 59,323 B of input
it is 33,870 entries rather than 52,988.

**It is still strict, for three reasons that all cut the same way.** Creating
the symbol that takes the table to k costs one code at the width in force,
which is what the walk sums; the first code of a stream creates nothing; and a
block-mode clear resets both the table and the width, so the bits are split
between generations and one generation holding every bit dominates any split
— the schedule is increasing in the bits it is given.

**What made it cheap to attempt.** The table already had `lzw_grow` behind it
as a net for an under-estimate, and `ci.sh` already builds a
`UNC_LZW_TINY_TABLE` variant that under-sizes the table deliberately so that
net runs on every stream. A bound that got this wrong would produce slow
output, not wrong output, and the 932-fixture suite adjudicates it either way.

**What is NOT claimed.** That this helps the archives that actually matter on
a small machine. The three fixtures still refused at 512 KB are StuffIt 5, and
their limit is a BWT block whose size their compressor chose; this touched
none of them. Small `.Z` streams that never widen past 9 bits are unchanged,
because they were already bounded exactly.

## 2026-08-30 (the Desktop file the password phase needed) — a harness reported a working feature as broken, and a bisect agreed with it

**Claim: encrypted StuffIt archives extract on the Macintosh, and always did.
What broke on 2026-08-24 was the harness that checks them.**
`interactive.py --password` began failing when `mkboot.py` started deleting the
boot volume's invisible `Desktop` file — one line, added so the Finder would
stop drawing a stale icon (`d3907fc`). Under the boot-shell arrangement, where
no Finder runs at all, deleting its cache stops the encrypted archive's
resource-only entry reaching the volume. Fixed by dropping the file only when
a Finder is going to run.

***The mechanism is not understood, and that is written into `mkboot.py`.***
No Finder is running; why its cache should matter is exactly the question. The
fix is justified by the bisect, not by a causal story, and the next person is
told so rather than handed an invented explanation.

**What the isolation cost, and why it was worth it.** Bisect alone named a
commit; it did not say whether the application or the harness had broken.
Holding the application constant at the bad commit and reverting `mkboot.py`
alone answered that in one run — and the answer inverted the finding, from "an
advertised capability is broken" to "the test is". **Always ask which side of
the instrument moved.**

**And the bisect before it was worthless, for a reason worth carrying.**
`git bisect start <bad> <good>` takes the GOOD end **on trust**. Mine came
from a DECISIONS entry recording that the password path worked — on the
**Mac OS 9** tier, while I was testing **System 6**. Evidence about a
different machine, used as a starting axiom. The bisect then converged, over
five emulator runs, on a commit that touches only documentation and one new
harness file: an answer that was confidently wrong and looked authoritative.
The re-run, with both ends measured rather than assumed, found the real commit
108 revisions earlier. **A bisect is only as good as its good end, and the
good end is the one thing bisect will never check for you.**

**What let it hide for six releases.** No emulator phase runs in `ci.sh` —
none can, they need an X server and minutes per fixture — and `run.py`'s
69-fixture sample contains no encrypted archive, because nothing in the corpus
is encrypted (FORMATS.md, *Encryption*). So the only check on this path is a
phase somebody has to run by hand, and between 2026-08-24 and 2026-08-30
nobody did.

## 2026-08-31 (the overlay that works and is not worth it) — a lifetime argument, and a 6 KB prize

**Claim: loading part of the engine on demand works, and buys about one
archive.** Two branches carry it, both built and both run on a 1 MB Mac Plus:
`selective-loading` makes the ciphers an overlay fetched from a resource when
a format wants them, and `overlay-eager` loads the same overlay at launch
instead. **Parked, neither merged**, and the branches are kept as a working
mechanism rather than as a proposal.

**The evidence, in the order it changed the answer:**

- **The prize is 5,988 B**, for six formats and not for StuffIt. Against a
  corpus whose headroom study puts roughly one archive per 6–7 KB of budget,
  that is one archive in 922, and it is not
  `derived-EasyFind.dmg.sit` — neither branch reaches the ones still out.
  For scale, re-pricing one constant in the memory reserve the same week was
  worth 49 KB (FORMATS.md, *Budget*).
- **It stood at 6,140 B for a day, from a pair of builds that were not the
  same build** — the resident row a plain build, the overlay row reproducible
  at no commit on either branch. Both sides from one build at one commit give
  5,988 B, or 5,720 B if both are autotest builds. Corrected 2026-08-31 while
  merging main into the branches; every version of it rounds to "about 6 KB",
  which is exactly why nobody re-measured it. **A figure that cannot change
  the conclusion is a figure nothing checks.**
- **And the download does not shrink, it grows.** "7,016 B off the download"
  was backwards: the `OVLY` resource ships inside the application, so
  `MacExtract.bin` is 191,104 B against 188,800 B — **2,304 B bigger**. The
  overlay buys resident heap and pays for it in disk, which is the opposite
  trade from the one written down.
- **StuffIt cannot be one of the six, and that is inherent.** A format is
  disqualified if it may carry an encrypted entry, or if it is an envelope
  whose contents are not yet known — a `.sit.hqx` sniffs as BinHex, so
  releasing on the outer format strands the StuffIt inside it. Freeing the
  memory and guaranteeing no disk access are in direct opposition unless you
  know before extracting whether an entry is encrypted, and for StuffIt you
  cannot: it is per entry, discovered as the entry is read, and the only
  signal is `UNC_ERR_PASSWORD`, which arrives too late.
- **The disk dependency cannot be removed, only moved.** A lazy `GetResource`
  touches the application's own floppy AFTER the archive is chosen, and a Mac
  Plus with one drive may have had it swapped by then. Loading at launch
  confines that to a moment when the boot disk is certainly in — but the
  release is per archive while the application lives per session, so a later
  archive reloads and pays the same access, later and less predictably.
  `selective-loading` pays it on the first encrypted archive; `overlay-eager`
  pays it on the second.

**Consequence: an end user trades a disk swap, or a stall, for one archive's
worth of memory.** That is the wrong side of a trade this project has always
taken the other way — an archive that will not open beats an archive that
opens 0.4 s later (FORMATS.md, *Size flags, priced in both currencies*), but
neither branch OPENS anything new. Scope status: OPEN_ITEMS.md, Part 1.

### The transferable part: a release lifetime is an argument about scope, and it can be wrong silently

`overlay-eager` shipped a bug for one commit that is worth keeping. The
release ran after each archive's sniff while the "already tried" flag was
global, so the overlay never reloaded: extract an LZH, then an encrypted
`.sit` in the same session, and the ciphers were gone — cipher kind cleared,
the entry failing, **a capability disappearing because of what was opened
before it**. Not a prompt and not a delay; a silent loss. The reasoning was
about the wrong scope, and the comment said so plainly: *"if it was released
the format cannot want it"* is true within one archive and false across a
session. The fix — allow the reload — concedes the branch's own argument.

**So: if something later needs tens of kilobytes, the loader exists.** Nobody
should have to rediscover `-mpcrel`, the `.bss`/`.data` distinction, the three
dispatch tables or the `--gc-sections` correction; `BRANCH_NOTES.md` on either
branch has them, along with the three ways to know whether an entry is
encrypted and what each costs.

## 2026-08-31 (the message that failed a successful extraction) — the allocator cannot know which refusals matter

**Claim: a memory shortfall is not a failure, and the shell had been treating
it as one.** `derived-Troi-ClipSave-Plug-in1.1.sit` extracts **completely** on
a 1 MB Mac Plus and ended with *"Needs 48K more memory."* Measured through
`interactive.py`, no segments involved, the harness reporting *phase 2 output
COMPLETE* in the same run.

**Why it happens.** `mal_alloc` sets `gShortBy` whenever a request exceeds the
budget, and it has no way to know what the engine will do about it. StuffIt 5's
inverse BWT asks for `numbytes * 4` **expecting** to be refused on a small
machine: declined, it builds a sampled occurrence index instead, which is
slower and byte-identical, and is the whole reason the format fits at 1 MB
(2026-08-07). So the archives that exercise the designed fallback are exactly
the ones that were told they had failed.

**The fix is where the knowledge is.** The allocator cannot tell an optional
request from a required one; the outcome can, because a request that mattered
leaves an errored entry behind. The message is now gated on
`errors > 0 || ret != UNC_OK`.

**Consequence, and it is the reusable half:** the shortfall was reported by the
component that could measure it rather than the one that could interpret it.
`gShortBy` is a true number — the largest amount by which some request exceeded
the budget — attached to a sentence it does not support.

### How it was found, which is not how it was looked for

It was found while investigating something else, and the something else was not
real. OPEN_ITEMS.md carried *"joining a segmented set costs far more memory
than the same archive whole"*, filed with two numbers: 472,137 B for the
archive whole, and a refusal 64 KB beyond budget for the same archive joined
from three parts.

**Those two numbers came from different instruments.** The first is peak heap
from the autotest replay — what the engine USED. The second is an alert
reporting `gShortBy` — what it ASKED for and was refused. Nothing was measured
twice the same way, and the conclusion was drawn from the difference.

Two checks settled it, both cheap and neither needing the emulator first:

- **On Linux, join and whole are identical.** `macextract x --stats` on a
  three-part set prints two high-water marks — **8,192 B** for the join and
  **661,173 B** for the inner extraction — and the whole file prints
  **661,173 B**. The join costs 8 KB and nothing else.
- **The sizes name their own source.** `derived-Library_ISBN_Barcoder.sit`
  peaks at 1,683,101 B with memory to spare, and 1,683,101 − 615,348 is the
  "over a megabyte more" the item reported. That is the transform vector,
  which exists on both paths.

Then one emulator run: the same archive, whole, through the same UI. It
extracted and it printed the same message. **The variable was never the
join.**

**What to take from it:** when two figures disagree, check that they are the
same measurement before believing the difference. And a withdrawn claim can be
worth more than it cost — this one was pointing at joining, which few users
do, while hiding a defect on the path almost every StuffIt 5 user on a small
Mac takes. BLOCKERS.md, *Withdrawn claims*.

### `gHasWNE` does not mean MultiFinder, and the first fix used it

The advice attached to that message was *"Give MacExtract more in Get Info"*,
which names a control that does not exist without a partition — wrong on
precisely the machine this application is designed around. The first attempt
chose between wordings on `gHasWNE`, reasoning that `WaitNextEvent` is what
MultiFinder adds.

**It is not.** On System 6.0.8 booted straight into this application, with no
MultiFinder anywhere on the volume, `TrapAvailable(_WaitNextEvent)` is TRUE —
the trap is in the System file whether the Process Manager is loaded or not.
The screenshot that proved the message bug also showed the new wording taking
the wrong branch, in the same frame.

What answers it is the SHAPE of the heap rather than a trap: an application
with the machine to itself has a zone reaching most of RAM, one in a partition
has a zone far smaller than RAM. Both configurations are far from the line —
~700 KB of a 1 MB MemTop against ~400 KB of 4 MB — and a 512 KB Macintosh
(~220 KB of 512 KB) is on the correct side too.

## 2026-08-31 (the joined archive that was never meant to survive) — a harness expectation, three hours old and already documented

**Claim: the Macintosh segment join is fine, and the check on it was asking
for a file the application is supposed to consume.**

`interactive.py --segments` drives the "locate part N" dialog correctly, ends
on *"Extraction complete."*, and then failed its own byte check with `NOT
FOUND (50205 B expected)`. It reproduced identically at the commit this
session started from, so it was not today's memory work — and for a few hours
it sat in OPEN_ITEMS.md as *"completes and writes nothing where it is
checked"*, with both readings still open.

**What the volume actually held settles it.** Not the joined `derived-Cal.sit`
the phase looked for, but its CONTENTS:

    f  DFIL/DMOV     27545         0  Cal 3.02
    f  TEXT/MACA         0      2739  Cal.Intro
    f  APPL/C0tl     39884         0  Calendar Tools

Both forks, type and creator intact. The Mac joined three parts, unwrapped the
joined archive and extracted it. **The shipped application has unwrapped
envelope chains since 2026-08-14**, so a joined `.sit` is an intermediate that
never reaches the disk, and a check that looks for it can only fail.

**The lesson was already written down, in the file the fix belongs in.**
`expected_names()`'s own docstring says a single-level reference *"says a
chained fixture should leave `boogers152.sit` on the volume, where the
application leaves the two files inside it — so every phase using this would
have failed on any chained fixture, in the direction that looks like an
application bug"*, dated 2026-08-27. Every phase was converted to that helper
then except this one, which kept its bespoke check and so kept the defect the
conversion existed to remove. `phase_segments` now uses `expected_names()`
like the others.

### What it cost, and the part worth transferring

An hour, a worktree bisect against `bba32c5`, and a false entry in
OPEN_ITEMS.md naming a user-facing failure that did not exist — the second
time in one session that a phantom defect was filed from an instrument rather
than from the machine (BLOCKERS.md, *Withdrawn claims*, the segmented-join
memory row).

**Both had the same shape: a check whose expectation was written before a
behaviour changed, failing in the direction that looks like an application
bug.** A harness that is not run for three weeks does not merely go stale, it
starts LYING, and it lies in the most expensive direction — nobody
investigates a passing test. This phase's last recorded run was 2026-08-09 and
the behaviour it mis-describes arrived on 2026-08-14, five days later.

## 2026-08-31 (the drop the Finder does not make, and the version it does show) — a gesture measured on three Finders

Two questions about the Macintosh shell were answered from memory in the same
session, so both were put on the machine. `tools/vmac/interactive.py --drop`
drags an archive onto the application's icon and photographs what the Finder
does, then reads Get Info.

**The drop is a System 7 gesture, and an `FREF` is what enables it.** Six runs,
one variable at a time, on `derived-ScrollbarPattern.sit` under `mv-4M`:

| system | `FREF` for the type | the file's type | what happened |
|---|---|---|---|
| System 3.0 / Finder 5.1 | yes | `SIT!` | icon moved, nothing launched |
| System 6.0.8 | yes | `SIT!` | icon moved, nothing launched |
| System 7.0.1 | no | hfsutils' default | icon moved, nothing launched |
| System 7.0.1 | no | `SIT!` | icon moved, nothing launched |
| System 7.0.1 | yes | hfsutils' default | icon moved, nothing launched |
| System 7.0.1 | **yes** | **`SIT!`** | **the application launched** |

So on System 7 the `FREF` is necessary and sufficient, and it is a RESOURCE —
free in the only currency that is scarce here.

**And `'****'` is a wildcard.** With one `FREF` declaring that type and the
archive's type set to `'????'` — matching nothing anyone declared — the drop
launched the application. That matters more here than it would anywhere else:
this program exists because names and types LIE, its own front door is
`unc_sniff`, and the files its users have are the ones that arrived as `TEXT`
(a `.hqx`) or `'????'` (a raw FTP download). An enumeration of `SIT!`, `CPT!`
and the rest would have made exactly the well-behaved files droppable and
refused the awkward ones the program is FOR. One line of Rez covers all of
them.

A single `'*'` is not a wildcard and not even a mistake Rez reports: it
compiles to the OSType `\0\0\0*` — right-aligned, null-padded, checked in the
built `FREF` — which matches nothing, and the drop was refused. Below System 7 no arrangement
of resources produces the gesture at all: it is the Finder that does not have
it. Which means the ~344 B of code that would read `AppParmHandle` (measured
by building it and reverting) buys nothing on the floor target, and the pair
is worth adding only together — a `FREF` without the receiving code makes the
Finder launch the application and hand it a file it does not look at.

**Neither is committed.** What is committed is the harness, so the next person
to wonder does not re-derive it.

### The control is the whole experiment

Every frame here is "the icon moved and nothing else happened", which is also
what a drag that was never delivered looks like — the failure this repository
keeps meeting, most recently when a mouse harness pressed 140 px from its
target and photographed a plausible screen (BLOCKERS.md, *a coordinate in a
vmac harness is right if the run completes*). So each run drags the archive to
empty space FIRST and photographs that it went there. Without that frame the
three negatives are worth nothing, and with it they are worth as much as the
positive.

The first System 7 run was a false negative for a reason worth keeping:
`mkboot.py --data` copies a data fork and leaves hfsutils' default type, so the
file was not a `SIT!` at all and the `FREF` under test could not match it. The
phase now sets the type with `hattrib` — **the subject of the experiment has to
be the thing the experiment is about**, and a file that merely has the right
NAME is not.

### And the version

`vers` 1 and 2 shipped earlier the same day with nothing having seen them.
Get Info displays them **twice on System 6.0.8 and on System 7.0.1** — once
under the name, once on the `Version:` line — and **System 3.0's "Information
about MacExtract" has no version line at all**: Kind, Size, Where, Created,
Modified, Locked, comment box, and nothing else. The System 7 box proves the
field reads `vers` rather than the file: Get Info on the archive beside it
says `Version: n/a`.

Both boxes read *"MacExtract 0.29.0"* when they were first photographed,
because the long string carried the name as well as the number. The box is
titled "MacExtract Info" and draws the name on the line above, so it said the
name twice and the version once; the strings are now the version alone, and
System 6.0.8's box was re-photographed to say so — `MacExtract` on one line,
`0.29.0` on the next, `Version: 0.29.0` below.

## 2026-08-31 (the drop, taken) — 128 bytes, one Rez line, and a partition that stopped asking

The measurement is the entry above; this is what was done with it.

**The wildcard is documented, not just observed.** Inside Macintosh: Macintosh
Toolbox Essentials, Finder Interface: *"You can create a file reference
resource that specifies '****' as the file type to allow users to drag all
file types--including applications, system extensions, documents, and so on,
but not including disks or folders--to your application icon."* The same
chapter says the Finder reads `FREF`s *"in system software versions 7.0 and
later"*, which is the emulator result in Apple's own words. `'disk'` and
`'fold'` are the other two wildcards and neither is wanted: this application
extracts one file.

`'****'` is the right shape here rather than a list of archive types, because
the files this program is FOR are the ones whose type is wrong — a `.hqx` is
`TEXT`, a raw download is `'????'` — and an enumeration would have refused
exactly those. `fType` is read and thrown away: the Finder matched it to
decide whether to offer the drop, and what the file HOLDS is `unc_sniff`'s
business.

**128 bytes, and the alternative cost 344.** `AppParmHandle` is read where it
lives; `CountAppFiles`/`GetAppFiles`/`ClrAppFiles` are glue rather than traps
in the Multiversal libraries, and linking them costs 216 B more for less —
`GetAppFiles` also spins forever at index ≥ 2 (`glue.c`, `while(index)` with
no decrement), which a walk of our own does not. Every document is taken, one
per pass of the existing loop, so dropping three archives extracts three; the
loop falls back to Standard File when the list runs out rather than quitting.
The ratchet in `tools/build_mac.sh` moved 147,296 → 147,424 to pay for it, and
the 512 KB machine pays those bytes for a gesture its Finder does not have.

Driven end to end on System 7.0.1: a file typed `'????'` dropped on the icon,
no Open dialog, the destination dialog straight away, *"Extraction complete."*
and both entries of `derived-ScrollbarPattern.sit` on the desktop behind it.

### The partition that had been asking all along

`SIZE` asked for 4 MB, so a 4 MB Macintosh — a generous one for this program —
met *"MacExtract prefers 4,096K of memory. 3,089K is available."* on every
launch. **Nothing here had ever seen it**, because every harness in this
repository installs the application as the BOOT SHELL, which gets all of
memory; it turned up in the first Finder launch this project has done on
System 7, while testing something else entirely. Preferred is now 2 MB, which
clears the alert with about a megabyte of margin.

**The baseline is the part worth keeping.** The obvious sentence to write was
"this costs `derived-EasyFind.dmg.sit`, which needs 2,658,721 B" — and it is
false. Measured at 4 MB preferred, on the same machine, EasyFind is refused
there too: a 4 MB Macintosh has ~3 MB free, so the partition was never 4 MB
and the archive never fit. What the change actually costs is archives needing
between ~1.9 MB and ~3.8 MB on a machine with more than 2 MB free — an 8 MB
Macintosh, which no emulator build here can boot. The heaviest fixture in the
sample passes: `derived-Library_ISBN_Barcoder.sit`, byte-exact at a peak of
1,683,101 B inside the 2 MB partition.

**A cost you cannot measure is not a cost you may assert.** One extra run
against the OLD value turned a confident regression note into an honest
"unreachable from here", and it is the same discipline that killed the stack
watermark: change the thing being measured and see whether the number moves.


## 2026-08-31 (the medium, not the machine) — the Macintosh 512K boots, and both reasons it could not were wrong

**Claim: "a true Macintosh 512K cannot be reached at all" was a claim about
HFS.** That machine boots Apple's 400K MFS System 1.1 floppy to the Finder,
and boots System 3.0 and Finder 5.1 — the floor system — off an MFS volume
built here. It then launches this application and bombs, `ID = 03`
(FORMATS.md, *A true Macintosh 512K*).

**The negative was one A/B short, and the missing axis was the one the
conclusion named.** Yesterday's three A/Bs varied the ROM revision, the RAM
and the presence of a disk; all three said "healthy machine", and the entry
concluded "it is the volume". Nobody then varied the volume. A negative whose
evidence never touches the variable it blames is worth exactly one more
experiment, and this one cost a download and a boot.

**What stopped that experiment being run was a sentence, not the work.** The
same entry added that the application is "out on the ROM regardless of how the
disk question resolves", naming `NGetTrapAddress` and HFS parameter blocks —
written from the shape of the code rather than from a run. It is false in both
halves: `tools/vmac/traptest` returns from `NGetTrapAddress` twice and from
`PBHGetVInfoSync` on a 64K ROM, and bombs at `PBGetCatInfoSync` with `ID = 12`,
unimplemented core routine. The verdict survives — this application cannot run
on a 64K ROM, because `PBGetCatInfo` is how it walks a directory — but a
conclusion propped up by two false premises is one nobody re-tests. **A
"regardless" is a claim that something need not be measured; treat it as the
most suspicious word in an entry.**

**Retro68 cannot write the volume, so the volume is written here.**
`--cc name.dsk` calls libhfs and always emits an 800K HFS image — the one
thing that ROM refuses. `tools/vmac/mkmfs.py` builds, lists and extracts from
400K MFS volumes: structure from Inside Macintosh Volume II, "Data
Organization on Volumes"; the 400K geometry read off an Apple-initialized
volume, because the book gives the format and not the parameters. It was
validated before it was trusted — first by listing Apple's own System 1.1 disk
(ten files, every fork chain terminating), then by rebuilding that disk from
its own extracted files and booting the rebuild.

**One thing the probe cost nothing to learn: hold the screen.** The first
`traptest` painted its marks and returned, and the screenshots showed an empty
desktop — at emulator speed the System repaints before any frame can be taken,
so a probe that exits has erased its own evidence. `for (;;)` is the fix, and
the same trap will be there for the next probe.


## 2026-09-01 (the second Toolbox call) — what bombs a 64K ROM, and three instruments that lied on the way to it

**Claim: `MaxApplZone` is what bombs a true Macintosh 512K, and Apple says so.**
Inside Macintosh II-30 marks it `[Not in ROM]`, with an assembly-language note
telling you to call the Pascal procedure because there is no trap; the trap
arrived with the 128K ROM, and Retro68 emits it unconditionally. Measured with
`tools/vmac/traptest` and again by bisecting the real application:
`SetApplLimit` returns, `MaxApplZone` does not (FORMATS.md, *A true Macintosh
512K*).

**The bomb ID was the first thing to mislead.** The application reports
`ID = 03`, the probe `ID = 10`, an instrumented build `ID = 28` — illegal
instruction, line 1111, and stack-into-heap. One call, three symptoms, because
a missing trap dispatches into whatever is at that address and that moves with
the binary. **An error code is evidence about the aftermath, not the cause;
the call before the first missing mark is the evidence.**

**The second was a mark nobody could see.** The probe's marks were laid out
four to a row across the middle of the screen — and the system error dialog
covers the middle of a 512x342 screen. So the run that "proved" `SetApplLimit`
fatal had actually painted the mark saying it survived, underneath the bomb
box. The grid is now eight to a row in the top 75 points, above anything the
System draws. This is the harness rule in this repository's own words: an
instrument that cannot see the region it is asked about will answer anyway.

**The third was `--gc-sections`, again.** Hanging the application after a
readout with `for (;;)` made everything past it dead code, the linker dropped
it, and the 188,928 B binary became 10,368 B — a different heap, a different
memory map, a different answer to the question being asked. The fix is a
condition the compiler cannot fold (`while (LMGetScrnBase() != (Ptr)-1L)`).
BLOCKERS.md records the same trap eating three of four ballast runs; it is
worth checking the binary's SIZE after any change meant to freeze it.

**What made the whole bisect possible was writing to screen memory.** Marks
before `InitGraf` cannot use QuickDraw, and the phase in question is the
Toolbox initialisation itself; `LMGetScrnBase()` and a loop over bytes need
nothing initialised at all. The same trick painted 24-bit values as bars and
read them back off the screenshot — that is how the trap addresses in
FORMATS.md were obtained, with `ScrnBase` painted alongside as a known value
so a decode that drifted could be caught. It drifted twice.

**A guard built on `TrapAvailable` does not fire, and that is the transferable
part.** The obvious fix — skip `MaxApplZone` when the trap is missing — was
built and measured: on a 64K ROM the check says the trap is PRESENT, because
it compares against `_Unimplemented` and that table entry is neither
`_Unimplemented` nor a routine. **A trap-availability test cannot see a
`[Not in ROM]` routine**; detecting these machines needs a machine test, not a
trap test. Skipping the call unconditionally, in a throwaway build, then
showed how much is behind it: the whole user interface, a file opened and
sniffed, and a real extraction stopping at `ID = 12` on an HFS call
(FORMATS.md, *A true Macintosh 512K*).

**No change to the application.** A 64K-ROM Macintosh is out of scope
(OPEN_ITEMS.md); this is a finding about why, not a defect. `MaxApplZone` is
correct on every machine the project claims.


## 2026-09-01 (two byte reads) — the guard that lets a 64K-ROM Macintosh start, and why it is not a trap test

**Claim: one guard makes the application start on a 64K ROM, and it costs
nothing anywhere else.** It lives on the branch `rom64k`, not on `main`: the
guard is provably neutral on every tier this project claims, but no real
machine has ever run the thing it enables, and a capability nobody has
exercised does not belong in the download people are told works. `Has128KROM()` reads the ROM
version word at `ROMBase+8` and skips the call when it positively reads as a
64K ROM. Measured: the shipping build reaches its Standard File dialog on the
emulated Macintosh 512K under Apple's MFS System 3.0, and the two tiers that
would notice are unchanged — **69/69 at 1 MB on System 6.0.8** and **66/69 on
System 3.0 at 512 KB**, the same three memory refusals as before.

**Why not `TrapAvailable`, which the application already has.** It was written
first and it does not work: on a 64K ROM it reports `_MaxApplZone` as PRESENT,
because it compares the trap's address against `_Unimplemented` and that entry
is neither `_Unimplemented` nor a routine. **A trap-availability test cannot
see a `[Not in ROM]` routine** — those are library glue in the era that lacks
them, not unimplemented traps. The test has to be of the MACHINE.

**Sources, both of them Apple's.** Where the number lives: Inside Macintosh
II, Operating System Utilities, `Environs` — its assembly-language note gives
the ROM version as the word at `ROMBase+8`, "$00xx for the Macintosh 128K or
512K". What the number means: Inside Macintosh IV, Preface, *Compatibility* —
"if the version number is greater than or equal to 117 ($75), it's safe to use
the routines and data structures described in this volume". `Environs` itself
is `[Not in ROM]` glue, which is why the low-memory route is the one used: a
test must not depend on the thing it is testing for.

**It is written as a negative on purpose.** `!(rom[8] == 0 && rom[9] < 0x75)`
— "not positively a 64K ROM" — so that any version word this has not surveyed
(a Macintosh XL reads `$xxFF`; later ROMs were never checked here) leaves
behaviour exactly as it was before the guard existed. The first draft asked
"is this a 128K ROM?" and would have SKIPPED `MaxApplZone` on any machine
whose word is not `$00xx`, which is a regression on every tier above the Plus
and would not have shown up on the two 128K-ROM tiers that were run.

**The size ceiling did its job.** The first version was 8 B over
`UNC_MAC_MAX_CODE` and `build_mac.sh` refused it; comparing the low byte
instead of composing the word saved exactly those 8 B, so the capability costs
**0 B of the engine's budget**. The ceiling exists to make a kilobyte of code
a deliberate purchase (FORMATS.md, *What the application costs the engine*),
and here it turned a shrug into a free change.

**What is NOT claimed.** That the application works on a 64K-ROM machine.
Extraction needs HFS and those machines only have it with a `Hard Disk 20`,
which Mini vMac does not emulate; what is measured is that the interface runs
and that a non-archive is correctly refused there. The rest is
MANUAL_TESTS.md §7, and it is now askable because the BRANCH'S DOWNLOAD does
it — a test that needs the tester to own Retro68 is not a test anyone will
run, which is the whole reason the guard was built rather than left as a
finding.


## 2026-09-01 (the package that cannot be handed over) — three routes into Aladdin's AppleLink expander, and the control that made an empty list mean something

OPEN_ITEMS.md files AppleLink `.pkg` as waiting on a **gradeable specimen**.
That is an assumption rather than a property: the grammar was transcribed out
of `PKG Expand Translator` on 2026-08-23, so a package can be WRITTEN here,
and the second reader — that same translator — is on the same disk image.
Feeding an implementation something this project wrote is the technique that
landed SuperDisk!'s codecs and SpaceSaver's (`tools/sdmodule/`,
`tools/ssmodule/`). Seven boots say Aladdin's software will not take one.

### Three routes, three controls

| route | what it did with a package | its control, same boot |
|---|---|---|
| `Translate ▸ PKG Expand…`, StuffIt Deluxe 3.5.1 | its Standard File dialog lists **only folders**: no package is selectable, whatever the file's type | `CPT Expand…` lists the type-`PACT` files, so the dialog machinery works |
| StuffIt Expander 4.0.2, `Expand… ⌘E` | lists every package and expands **nothing** — no output, no error, no alert | `Payload.hqx` → `Widget`, data fork byte-exact, 1,256 B |
| `DropConvert™`, `Convert… ⌘C` | the package goes into *Items to Convert:* and `Convert` produces **nothing** | `Payload.hqx` → `Widget.sit`, 532 B, `SITD/SIT!` |

The controls are the whole point. Two boots of empty file lists read as a fact
about the packages — wrong type, wrong text — and four typings of their Finder
info (`TEXT/ttxt`, `PACT/EXTR`, `PACT/ttxt`, `TEXT/EXTR`) changed nothing. One
Compact Pro archive in the same dialog moved it to a fact about the command.

### What that dialog is actually asking for

A folder. The filter proc `PKG Expand` hands `_SFPGetFile` tests `ioFlAttrib`
bit 4 — *is a directory* — and suppresses everything else (`Othe` 0 @0x215e),
where the sibling CompactPro translator's tests type `PACT` (@0x2e1c), which is
exactly why one lists files and the other does not. Two of its own resources
say the same: `DITL 2051` carries a button reading **"Expand into selected
folder"**, and `DITL 2052` is a *"Name of new folder:"* dialog. So does its
`Help` resource — the translator "can extract files and folders from archives
created by AppleLink. These archives usually have a suffix '.pkg'."

That fits 2026-08-23's manifest hypothesis — you point it at what AppleLink
delivered — and does not settle it. The dialog this build puts up is
`DLOG 2050`, which has no such button, and Open on a folder descends into it,
so nothing here can complete the command.

### What this cannot see, and the lesson

**A null result from somebody's user interface is not a fact about a format.**
Nothing here separates *"the software will not take this input"* from *"this
text is not a package"*: the engine never said a word either way, and one real
`.pkg` still settles both. The open item is unchanged, and its cost is now
written down — **seven boots** — so the next session does not spend them again.

The transferable half is the one that keeps recurring: **an empty list is
evidence about nothing until something has filled the same list.** The harness
was two boots old before it grew a control, and the control cost four
minutes — one archive of a sibling translator's type, put in beside the
packages.


## 2026-09-01 (the tier that needed no Macintosh) — a budget sweep, a ceiling, and a number that was an artefact

The 512 KB tier stood at 66/69 and read like a target: three fixtures
refused, and a history of recovering them one at a time (the Arsenic block
took the floor target 40/44 → 41/44; a flat reserve and a 4 KB stack bump
took this tier 65 → 66). This is what looking at it properly found.

### The sweep

The Mac shell's allocator is a running total against a cap, refused per
entry. `macextract --budget N` is that same rule, so a tier's memory
result does not need a Macintosh at all: `tools/run_tiers.py` sweeps the
same 69-fixture sample at the tier's measured budget in **five seconds**,
against one emulator boot per fixture. It reproduces System 3.0 / 512 KB
**exactly** — 66/69, the same three by name — and the 1 MB floor's 69/69,
so `ci.sh` now asserts both. What it cannot see is in TESTING.md, and the
first item is why the System 6.0.8 / 512 KB tier comes out 59/69 here
against the machine's 58: a budget is one number, and the Memory Manager
also has to find one PIECE that big.

### The ceiling

Bisected minimum budgets: **246,734 B**, **393,869 B**, **401,408 B**,
against 197,728 B. Two of the three ask for more in a SINGLE allocation
than the machine has for the whole job — 205,020 B and 352,161 B, each one
BWT block, which is the entry's whole output — and a block being inverted
has to be resident. The third wants 1,536 B per distinct context byte and
touches all 256; those splay trees are adaptive state, so none can be
dropped and rebuilt. **66/69 is the ceiling of the machine, not a gap in
the engine**, and FORMATS.md now says so with the numbers. What would move
it is a smaller archive.

### The number that was an artefact

The sweep printed `synth-dd-m5.dd  short by 928` — under a kilobyte, on a
fixture the same file says needs 405,520 B. Both are true and the second
is the one that matters: **a shortfall is the FIRST refused request's, not
the entry's demand.** An incremental allocator is refused at the request
that crosses the line, and method 5's requests are 1,536 B each, so the
figure understates the requirement by 220x here and by 3,000x at a budget
of 300,000.

That figure is not diagnostic — it is **advice**, printed on the
Macintosh as *"Needs 8K more memory. Give MacExtract more in Get Info"*.
A user who follows it gets the same message again. Both front ends now say
**at least**, which is true of one allocation and of a run.

The wording it replaces called itself "round enough to type and never
short", and that is the shape worth remembering: **a claim made about a
number by the code that computes it.** The same sentence had already been
wrong once — M46 found it firing after a completely successful extraction
— which is the other half of the lesson. A message is a claim, it is the
only one the user ever reads, and nothing in a byte-comparison suite can
fail on it.

## 2026-09-01 (the search, not the reference) — Apple Archive, and what rewriting a probe actually buys

**The claim was about a search.** `OPEN_ITEMS.md` had *"Apple Archive (`.aar`)
is unnamed for want of a reference — missing: an implementation or spec in
`reference/`."* One query found `0xilis/libNeoAppleArchive`: MIT, cross-platform,
and explicitly **not** a reimplementation of Apple's closed `libAppleArchive`
but a from-scratch reading of the format. It carries `AAR_MAGIC`/`YAA_MAGIC`,
the `pbz*` set, the six-byte header prologue and the closed set of sixteen
field subtypes. Withdrawn in `BLOCKERS.md` with what it cost.

**What arrived was better than a spec: an oracle.** The library builds here
with `gcc` once its two compression submodules are checked out — its own
Makefile wants `clang`, Homebrew's OpenSSL and an install step, none of which
the writer needs, so `tools/build_neoaa.sh` compiles the three core sources
directly with `EXCLUDE_AEA_SUPPORT=1`. `tools/neoaa_write.c` then writes a
real archive in each of the four shapes the probe must recognise and reads it
back with the same library. That moves Apple Archive out of the "signature
only" grade that RAR sits in and into the grade bzip2 and 7z sit in, where a
specimen the probe rejects is known to be an archive first.

**Two sources, and they disagree in a way worth recording.** The library
defines three codec letters — `e` LZFSE, `z` zlib, `b` LZBITMAP. The
archiveteam wiki (`reference/notes/Apple_Archive.md`) gives two more, lzma
`x` and lz4 `4`. That is not the wiki inventing them: the library's own
`docs/NeoAAArchiveGeneric.md` says both belong to the format and are "not yet
added". So all five are claimed, and the two that no implementation here can
write are marked untestable in `TESTING.md` rather than quietly counted as
covered. `pbzx` is also the payload stream inside an Apple flat package, so
one of those is now named an Apple Archive — the right answer for a file whose
whole purpose is to tell the user which family a file belongs to and that it
is not damaged.

**Then the Macintosh ratchet refused it, and that was the useful part.**
`tools/build_mac.sh` failed the build at 196 B over a ceiling with 20 B of
headroom. Two rewrites took it to 164 B: the sixteen subtypes became a string
constant walked by `in_set()`, because on a 68000 the string is data and free
while the comparisons are code and are not, and three length tests became one.
Together they bought 52 B — **12 % of what the first version cost, and the
lesson is the ratio.** Code shape is worth little here; what the code *is* is
worth everything, which is the same finding as *Size flags, priced in both
currencies*, where a full day of compiler flags bought 780 B.

**The remaining 164 B was bought on the set's existing terms.** The named-only
formats are one decision applied uniformly (OPEN_ITEMS.md, part 1), so what
would have needed justifying is an exception, not the cost — RAR 5, 7-Zip, xz,
zstd, WIM, lrzip and lzip are all carried by a Macintosh that will never open
one. The price is 1.2 % of the 13,602 B by which the tightest fixture that
still fits, `derived-Domino_v1.4.sit`, clears the 1 MB Mac Plus budget
(FORMATS.md, *Budget*). What made it a decision rather than an accident is
that the ratchet stopped the build: this cost was paid in the open, where the
31,860 B that quietly moved the budget on 2026-08-24 was not.

## 2026-09-01 (the reader was in the sentence) — feeding SuperDisk!'s own extractor a stream we wrote, and one hypothesis that did not survive it

OPEN_ITEMS.md carried SuperDisk!'s path cap as untested code for five days,
under a sentence that both stated the problem and named its solution:

> A generated record stream would not: nothing off the Macintosh reads this
> container, so it could only be graded against the same transcription that
> wrote it. The one second reader that exists is an archive's own
> self-extractor on an emulated Mac, which has never been handed a stream
> this project wrote.

The second sentence is the disproof of the first, and it sat there through
every session that read the file. BLOCKERS.md keeps the withdrawal; what is
worth transferring is the shape of it — **"there is no oracle" was a claim
about what had been TRIED**, and the trying was one afternoon.

### The method, which generalises past this format

`SD! Utilities.x` is an APPL whose data fork IS the archive and whose resource
fork is the extractor. So the fork can be replaced and the extractor kept:
`tools/vmac/superdisk.py` MacBinaries the application back together around a
stream `tools/synth_sd.py` wrote, and `CODE 2` walks it. **Any
self-extracting archive is an implementation you can feed**, not merely one
you can run — the same bargain `--adjudicate` already made, one step further.

Ten folders of 31 characters, eleven files, declared paths 43 to 331 bytes.
The Macintosh built every one and all eleven files came back byte-identical.
The assertion that matters is available only from that machine: it creates
each component through the File Manager and never assembles a path, so **what
it makes on the volume IS the uncut path** — and for the three files and two
folders past 255 bytes, the uncut path `unc_path_leaf` reports is exactly it.

### What was built rather than invented, and why the difference showed up

Everything in these streams that this project does not decode is COPIED from a
corpus archive: the eight bytes at the head of the file, a folder record's
twenty, the four unread bytes inside a file's thirty. That is rule #1 applied
to a test subject rather than to a parser, and the reason is not
fastidiousness — a wrong guess in any of those fields is indistinguishable
from the failure the run is looking for.

The first run proved the point by breaking it. It used invented file records
and, worse, STORED blocks — and it bombed, *unimplemented trap*, having
written nothing. Checking the corpus then turned up a coverage fact nobody had
noticed: **no stored block exists in either fixture.** All seven blocks are
coded, methods 7 and 11, so `sd_block_head`'s bit-15 branch had never run on
real data, and the 116 corrupted variants flip bytes inside coded blocks.

### The hypothesis, and the discipline that killed it

"The stored branch is wrong" was a good hypothesis and it was wrong. Put to
the extractor on its own — the same 4,796-byte fork written coded and stored,
in one folder, so the file record including `dlen` is byte for byte the one
that already worked — **both came back byte-exact**. So the reading taken off
`CODE 2` @0x614 on 2026-08-20 (a length with bit 15 set, counting its own two
header bytes, except 0x8002) is now confirmed against the code it was
disassembled out of, which is a better place for it than untested.

Two more single-variable runs followed, and both refuted their candidate: a
stream that OPENS with a file record is read, and so is one whose file record
was guessed rather than copied. **Four causes, each cleared individually, and
the bomb still unexplained** — the one combination left untried is a root file
FOLLOWED by a folder. It is written up as a one-boot task and labelled a
hypothesis (`tools/vmac/README.md`), because an unreduced root cause is not a
finding; that rule is what the withdrawn `HOpen` attribution cost.

The transferable half is the ordering. Had the first run used borrowed blocks,
it would have passed, the path cap would have closed, and the stored branch
would still be untested code that nobody had thought to name. **The broken run
was worth more than the clean one**, and only because the difference between
them was reduced one variable at a time instead of being fixed in a batch.

## 2026-09-01 (the machine that is too small) — a 128K Macintosh, and a status row measured on the other machine

**Claim: the guarded build's interface works on 64K-ROM Macintoshes — the
128K among them.** Half of that had been measured, on a 512K. The other half
was the same sentence with a second machine's name in it, and it is false:
the `rom64k` `dist/` bombs `ID = 03` on a Macintosh 128K, from the same MFS
volume, the same `Mac128K.ROM` and the same System 3.0 that reach Standard
File on the 512K (FORMATS.md, *A Macintosh 128K*).

**Nothing about the ROM changed between those two runs, which is the point.**
`mv-128K` is `-m 128K -mem 128K`; `mv-512K` is `-m 128K -mem 512K`. The
project had already withdrawn one claim about these machines for varying the
ROM, the RAM and the disk but never the FILE SYSTEM (BLOCKERS.md). This is the
same error one notch along: three variables were held and the fourth was
carried in the machine's NAME rather than in the run.

**Why the negative is stable where the 512K's was not.** The 512K's "cannot
be reached" was a claim about a medium — change the volume and it fell. This
one is arithmetic on the machine: `ApplLimit − ApplZone` is 78,568 B on a
128K and the application's `CODE` totals 148,780 B, one segment of which is
142,152 B and must be placed whole. The largest single block the machine will
grant is 63,744 B, measured. A smaller System cannot move a ceiling that is
RAM under the screen buffer, so there is no volume, no System and no build
flag that reaches this one. What would reach it is a different program.

**`FreeMem` would have reported the two machines as identical**, 4,846 B on
each, because the application zone is created small and grown on demand. The
instrument has to ask the question the code asks: the Segment Loader wants one
contiguous block, so the probe bisects `NewPtr`. A harness that reported the
number that was easy to read — and `FreeMem` is the number every memory
discussion in this repository starts from — would have measured the zone's
starting size on both machines and called it a tie. **Ask what the failing
code actually requests, then measure that**, which is the same rule as pricing
a tolerance against something other than our own output.

## 2026-09-01 (no hotspot, twenty-three decoders) — where the application's code is, and two instruments that said otherwise

**Claim: the application is large because of some one thing.** It is not.
147,568 B of `CODE`, of which **59.3 % is twenty-three format decoders**, the
largest of them 8,860 B; the largest single file is the Macintosh shell at
17,358 B and the two largest functions are ~5.1 KB each (FORMATS.md, *Where
the code is*). Nothing in the binary is worth more than about 6 % of it, which
is what "thirty-odd formats in one binary" costs when it is measured rather
than guessed at.

**The two cheap instruments both lie, in the same direction.** Summing
`m68k-apple-macos-size` over the objects gives 178,696 B — 21 % high, because
`--gc-sections` drops what nothing calls. Counting every `.text` and
`.rodata` input section in the linker map gives 179,561 B, because **Retro68
places `.rodata` in the A5 world**, so read-only tables are not in a `CODE`
resource at all. Only the sections the map puts under a `.code*` OUTPUT
section answer the question the budget asks, and `tools/code_map.py` checks
its own total against `size` so a future toolchain change cannot quietly
re-break it.

**That second wrong answer is the useful one.** 32,077 B of tables — the
SpaceSaver dictionary at 9,701 B, the cipher S-boxes, the fixed inflate
tables — live outside `CODE` already. The ballast experiment had established
that data is free (*What the application costs the engine*); this says WHY,
and closes the follow-up question it always attracts: there is no large table
hiding inside the code figure waiting to be moved out.

**And it prices the parked overlay work.** The 5,988 B that
`selective-loading` buys is the ciphers, which are 7,480 B of code, 5.1 % of
the binary — so the branch got most of what its chosen payload was worth, and
the reason the prize is small is that the payload is small. Every other
candidate is a decoder worth 2.5–8.9 KB, and each brings the eligibility
problem that entry has: an envelope does not know what is inside it, and
StuffIt discovers encryption per entry. **A bigger prize needs a bigger
payload, which means segmenting the decoders as a set rather than promoting
one of them** — a different question from the one those branches answered.

### The transferable part: an assertion that fires is not an assertion that protects

`code_map.py` checks its own total against `size` and exits 1 when they
disagree. Asked *can this check fail?* — the question this repository's first
trap warns about — the answer was yes, demonstrably, with a wrong `--expect`.
It was still broken. It printed **the entire table** and then said, on stderr,
not to quote it: `code_map.py map --expect X > numbers.txt` wrote the disowned
numbers into the file and dropped the disowning. The tool detected correctly,
exited correctly, and published anyway.

**The second question is what a check DOES once it has fired**, and the two
come apart whenever the failure path still produces output. The general
remedy is ordering: decide before printing, and produce nothing on stdout when
the answer is no — a warning above a table does not survive a pipe, a
redirect, or a reader in a hurry. This is not a fact about linker maps; every
harness here that prints results and then grades them is exposed to it.


---

## 2026-09-03 (the binary nothing builds, twice more) — three UI failures that were one unfinished fix, and an explanation that fit six cases out of nine

`segcheck.py`'s three interactive cases — `zip-named`, `split-named`,
`wrapped` — were recorded as failing on `main` across eight runs, with the
application named as the likely culprit. All three pass. `phase_wrapped` and
`phase_named` graded their results against `build/dev/macextract`, which
nothing in this repository builds and which `make clean` (`rm -rf build`)
removes on every `./ci.sh`. `R.sh` reaches it with no existence guard, so the
phase raised `FileNotFoundError` after the emulator run and before any
comparison. Given `phase_chain`'s guard and `CD.CLI`, the three cases pass:
~155 s, ~134 s, and MATCH with nothing left on the volume.

**This exact defect was found and fixed on 2026-08-27, in one of the three
places it existed.** That commit closed with "One CLI per file now." The file
had two more call sites. The claim was about every line of a file and was
checked against none of them — one `grep` settles such a claim, and the cost
of not running it was the next three failures plus a day spent attributing
them to the application.

### The transferable part: an explanation that fits the cases you looked at

The offered account was that the passes are the schemes found by HEADER and
the failures are the ones placed by NAME. It is a real distinction and it
explained six of the nine cases. It was wrong about the other three, and each
was cheap to check:

- **`wrapped` is not placed by name.** It writes StuffIt segment headers and
  stages them as `Apart.sit.1.hqx`, `zzz-download.bin`, `Other.9.hqx` — names
  that share no stem and that the Mac's family scan cannot match. Its own
  docstring says "under names that say nothing." The hypothesis contradicted
  the code it was about, in the function it was about.
- **`chain` is interactive and passes**, so "interactive vs `run.py`" was
  already refuted by the case list the hypothesis was written from.
- **FORMATS.md said all three joined byte-exact on the Mac.** A status file
  asserting the opposite of another status file is this repository's cheapest
  smell, and it was live for the whole investigation.

**When a hypothesis partitions a set, check the members it puts on each side,
not the ones that suggested it.** The partition that was actually true —
which phase had its reference CLI repaired — is visible in one `git log -S`
over the path.

### And a correction to what would have closed it

The item said one Linux command would settle which side was wrong: push the
parts past `unc_segment_describe` with the harness's names. It would not have.
**The name-based sibling search is host code, written twice** — `cli/main.c`
(the zip family and `.NNN` run) and `mac/macextract_mac.c` (`ZipFamPartOf`,
`SplitPartOf`, `SegScanFamily`). The engine only sequences parts the host has
already found. A Linux run exercises the CLI's copy and is blind to a
Mac-side naming fault by construction; the run that settles it is the
emulator. Both copies are in fact correct here — the sets join on Linux
(`Aset.z01` → `payload.bin`, 278,294 B; `Ablob.001` → `Downerƒ`;
`Apart.sit.1.hqx` → `Downerƒ`) and on the Mac — but that is a result, not
something the proposed command could have shown.

## 2026-09-03 (the struct that grew after it was measured) — 296 was the offset of the last field

`UncEntryInfo` was quoted at **296 bytes** on the 68000 target in three
places — FORMATS.md (*Name limits*), the entry above (*the name cap*) and a
comment in `src/fmt_lzh.c` explaining why the path is built into the caller's
entry rather than a local. The target compiler says **298**
(`m68k-apple-macos-gcc -Os`, and `m68k-linux-gnu-gcc` agrees; x86-64 is 304).

**296 is not a mistyped measurement — it is the offset of `name_full_len`.**
Both numbers were produced on 2026-08-27, hours apart. `name_truncated` closed
the struct at 295, padded to 296, and that is what was written down; the
maintainer's correction the same day — the notice is text, so the whole name
goes into the host's buffer — added `name_full_len` on top of it, and nothing
re-measured. The figure was stale before the day it was written in ended.

**Nothing rested on the two bytes**, which is why it survived: the argument the
number supports is that zip's 65,535-byte maximum name cannot travel on a 4 KB
stack, and that holds at either value. The stack is measured separately and
directly — 5,200 B constant, 2026-08-31 — and never consulted this figure.

Two things are worth carrying:

- **A measurement taken in the middle of a change describes a struct that may
  not exist by the end of the day.** The `280` this replaced was wrong from the
  start and was caught by measuring; the `296` was right for about four hours,
  which is the harder case — re-measuring at the END of a change is what
  separates them, not measuring more carefully.
- **It hid behind a coincidence.** 296 is also, genuinely, the length of the
  nine-deep path `tools/synth_now.py` writes, stated a few hundred lines away
  in the same two files and dated the same day (*the path builder's own cap*).
  A reader checking whether 296 was right found a 296 that was.

**It cannot go stale silently again.** `tools/check_docs.py` gained a fifth
invariant: every `"N bytes on the 68000 target"` in the root docs, `src/`,
`cli/`, `mac/` and `include/` must equal `sizeof(UncEntryInfo)` as the Retro68
compiler reports it, sized by compiling a one-line probe and reading `.size`
off the assembly. It costs 0.2 s, and it skips out loud rather than silently on
a machine with no m68k compiler. Mutation-tested both ways: restoring `296`
fails one site, and adding a field to the struct fails all three at 302. A
historical value now has to be written outside that phrase — this entry says
*quoted at 296 bytes*, and that is the check working rather than around it.

## 2026-09-03 (a folder is a name too) — numbering folders like files, and the table that made it affordable

**At the maintainer's ask.** The Macintosh shell cut every path component to
HFS's 31 characters and numbered a FILE that landed on a name already taken —
`dup.txt` → `dup~1.txt` — while a FOLDER in the same position was descended
into, so two folders whose names agreed for 31 characters became one on disk
and their contents mixed. Folders are numbered now, `~1`..`~99`, by the same
`BumpName` the files use.

**What made it look impossible was a table size, filed as a property of the
formats.** OPEN_ITEMS.md carried it under *Limits of the formats themselves*:
where an archive declares no folder entries — zip, tar, LZH, most of what
anybody downloads — the folders exist only inside the paths of the files in
them, so a second folder arriving "looks exactly like the first folder being
revisited", and remembering every long name would cost more than a 1 MB
machine will pay. Neither half held. `RepDirMemo` was already recording every
directory this run created, declared or not; and the cost it was measured at
was the cost of storing whole NAMES, 68 bytes a row for 24 folders. A 32-bit
hash of the component answers the only question that is ever asked of the row
— *is this directory this run's, and is it this name's?* — for 8 bytes,
which puts **200 folders in the 1,600 B that held 24**.

Three decisions inside it, each of which could have gone the other way:

- **A directory this run did not make is descended into, still.** A folder the
  user already had, or one an earlier run left, takes this archive's files;
  numbering there would spray `~1` over the first run's work every time
  somebody extracted the same archive twice. So the memo is what authorises a
  rename, and its absence means merge — `tools/run_macnames.py`,
  *folder_again_case*, holds that, and it fails on the one-line change that
  treats an unknown directory as somebody else's.
- **The table never evicts, and that is the load-bearing property.** A row
  lost after its folder was numbered would send the rest of that folder's
  files into a different directory — one archive folder SPLIT across two on
  disk, which is worse than the merge numbering replaces. A full table stops
  numbering and merges exactly as the shell did before, so the failure falls
  in the direction the old behaviour already had. **The safe direction for a
  bounded table is the behaviour it replaced**, not the one it aspires to.
- **The merge line takes from the report's failure reserve.** Past `~99` a
  folder really does go into another one, and it says *combined with* rather
  than *renamed to*. An archive that reaches that has a hundred alike folder
  names and so a hundred ordinary rename lines — exactly the crowd the
  16-line reserve exists to keep the consequential line out of. Measured: at
  101 folders the line was dropped without it.

**The duplicate report was the one thing the design did not predict.** A
merged folder is discovered again by every file inside it, so the first
version announced the merge once per entry. The fix is in the same table: the
merged folder is memoed under its own name as well, so the second arrival
reads as *the same folder* and descends quietly. A directory may hold more
than one row, and that is why.

Cost of the old claim, and where it is recorded: BLOCKERS.md, *Withdrawn
claims*. The measurement that opened it took one zip and one run of a mock
sink that has been in `ci.sh` since 2026-08-28 — the claim was about our own
binary, which is the cheapest kind here to test and the easiest to leave
untested.

## 2026-09-04 (the field nobody assigned) — a struct filled field by field, and the crash that was shaped like three formats

**Claim.** The Mac OS 9 bomb on every DiskDoubler archive and on SuperDisk!
(BLOCKERS.md, *Withdrawn claims*) was an uninitialised pointer. `RunExtract`
in `mac/macextract_mac.c` filled its `UncOptions` field by field, and the
two fields that joined the struct on 2026-08-27 — `long_name` and
`long_name_cap` — were never among them: `git grep long_name mac/` returned
nothing. The engine's folder-path walker, `unc_path_push` →
`path_full_add` in `src/unc_util.c`, writes **every** component through
that pointer whenever it is non-NULL and the cap is at least 2, because the
buffer is where the uncut name accumulates and a cut can come at any depth.
Exactly three formats walk a tree with it: DiskDoubler, SuperDisk! and Now
Compress. So those three wrote each folder and file name through whatever
the interactive build's stack held at that offset, and nothing else touched
it. **Measured**, both halves: `tools/longname-repro/` hands the engine a
4-byte block with a 1 MB cap and lists one fixture per family under ASan —
DiskDoubler, SuperDisk! and Now Compress die in `path_full_add` on their
first component (`WRITE of size 1`, `unc_util.c`, via `unc_path_leaf` ←
`dd_archive`), Compact Pro and StuffIt never touch the block, and all five
are clean with NULL. The fix is one loop that zeroes the struct before the
fields are set, and the Mac half of the measurement is the fixed build on
Mac OS 9.2 (below).

**Why every experiment pointed away from it, and what that teaches.**

- **A symptom shaped like a format is not evidence about the format.** Six
  DiskDoubler archives and SuperDisk! bombed; Compact Pro's own
  self-extractor passed. That read as "these decoders do something the
  shell cannot take", and the search went into what the *shell* does
  differently — the dialogs, the event loop, the segmented path. The
  productive question was the other way round: **what does the engine do
  for these formats that involves the host?** The host surface is small —
  `UncIO`, `UncAlloc`, `UncSink`, `UncProgress`, `UncOptions` — and one
  `grep` over the option fields against the shell's assignments answers it
  in a minute. Format-specific plus host-specific means a *host input* the
  formats consume unequally.
- **"The classic Mac shell passes NULL" was a claim about a file on this
  disk, and nobody ran it.** It is in `include/macextract.h`, written the
  day the field was added, describing a shell that had not been updated —
  the tenth smell in BLOCKERS.md's list (*an open item that describes what
  our OWN binary does*), this time in a header comment rather than a status
  file. A comment that says what another file does is a test that has never
  been run.
- **An uninitialised local defeats every instrument that changes the
  stack, which is all of them.** The value the engine saw was whatever
  Standard File, the destination dialog and the progress dialog had left in
  that frame. A tracer, a `-D` probe, a different `kStackBump`, a checkpoint
  file between entries and any commit that touched a frame above it moved
  the garbage; some values were benign (a pointer into free stack, a cap
  below 2) and some were not. That is why `git bisect` produced a confident
  first-bad commit whose retest could not reproduce it, why "keeping the
  probes in the binary but never executing them" also cured it, and why the
  two shipped versions bombed with two different error types at two
  different entries. **A defect that moves with layout is not "a wild
  write somewhere"; it is a specific class — a read of state nobody set —
  and the search for it is a search for an assignment that is missing, not
  for a write that is wrong.**
- **The autotest build passed because its call chain is different**, so its
  garbage was different — presumably zero, though that was never measured
  and did not need to be once the mechanism was reduced. The Mini vMac tiers
  passed for the same reason, which means the 69/69 on System 3.0, 6.0.8 and
  7.0.1 were passing on luck for the three walking formats. **Inferred, not
  measured**: no run on those tiers ever showed the fault; the fix removes
  the dependence rather than a defect those tiers exhibited.
- **The lesson was already in the tree, learned by the other host.**
  `unc_extract_as` zeroes the whole struct for a NULL `opt`, with a comment
  from 2026-08-27 saying why: a field list "written when UncOptions had nine
  members and never revisited" had segfaulted the unit tests the day
  `long_name` was added. The same day, the same field, the same mistake in
  the shell — and the fix went where the crash was and nowhere else. **When
  a lesson is learned in one host, grep the other hosts for the pattern the
  same afternoon.** Done this time, and it paid at once: five `UncOptions`
  in `tests/unit_formats.c` were filled field by field too, and all five
  are in DiskDoubler and SuperDisk! tests — the walking formats. Measured
  with gdb over the pre-fix binary: the walker read the field 20 times
  across the unit tests and saw NULL every time, so those tests passed on
  a host stack that happened to hold zeros. They are zeroed now. valgrind
  over the same binary reports nothing — stack bytes an earlier call wrote
  count as initialised — which is why that class of defect needs
  MemorySanitizer, and no host build here has it.

**What would have caught it before Mac OS 9 did.** Nothing in `ci.sh`: the
mock Toolbox build (`tests/mac_sink_test.c`) drives the sink, allocator and
I/O and builds its own `UncOptions`, so it never executes `RunExtract`, and
neither ASan nor valgrind reports a read of uninitialised STACK — measured on
the pre-fix unit tests, where valgrind is silent through 20 such reads,
because bytes an earlier call wrote count as initialised. That is
MemorySanitizer's job, over a path no host-side build here takes (TESTING.md,
*mac sink*, now says so). The compiler is silent because the struct is passed
by pointer. What does catch it is the rule the engine's own comment states:
**zero the whole struct, then set fields** — a future field is then NULL by
construction rather than by luck.

**Consequence.** `RunExtract` zeroes `opt` before assigning it, with the
history in a comment beside the loop. `tools/longname-repro/` keeps the host
half of the reproduction. `tools/vmac/os9sample.txt` gains
`synth-now-mixed.now`, because Now Compress is the third walker and the one
this tier had never run. Mac OS 9.2 comes back **21/21**, and all six
DiskDoubler archives in the corpus extract there byte-exact where that
morning's binary bombed on every one.

**And a harness defect, found because the fix let the run get far enough to
hit it.** `derived-Terrainman_2.0__.sea` reported ten files missing that were
on the volume: the verifier's folder walk passed its path to `hls` as a
Python `str`, so an 8-bit folder name went out UTF-8 and listed as empty.
A FILE with such a name had been checked since 2026-08-28 and a FOLDER with
one had never been walked. **A harness that has been right about one shape of
a thing has not been tested on the others** — and the same encoding rule was
already written down two functions away, for `hcopy` (`tools/vmac/README.md`,
*Checking a run off the disk*).

What the claim cost is in BLOCKERS.md, *Withdrawn claims*; the numbers in
FORMATS.md, *Mac OS 9.2*.

---

## 2026-09-09 (the disk image that is expanded and not opened) — DART, and an error code for a file with nothing wrong with it

**Asked for**: expand a compressed DART, and store the result as a
decompressed DART; say so plainly when the file handed over is already
decompressed. The reason is a sibling project: MacMount mounts an
UNCOMPRESSED DART and refuses a compressed one by name, because mounting
one would need the volume's bytes to exist somewhere and unpacking an
